# Pediatrics

## Child Development & Milestones

**Q:** A 2-year-old does not respond to his name, avoids eye contact, has no words, and engages in repetitive play. What is the diagnosis and appropriate referral?

**A:** Autism Spectrum Disorder (ASD); refer immediately to developmental pediatrics + speech therapy; do not wait

CPS (Canadian Paediatric Society) recommends immediate referral for any child with language regression, no words by 12 months, no 2-word phrases by 24 months, loss of any skills at any age, or persistent social communication concerns. Early intervention improves outcomes dramatically.

**Q:** What are the typical developmental milestones for a 12-month-old?

**A:** Gross motor: pulls to stand, cruises; Fine motor: pincer grasp; Language: says mama/dada (specific), 2-3 other words; Social: waves bye-bye, plays peek-a-boo, stranger anxiety

CPS developmental surveillance at all well-child visits. Red flags at 12 months: no babbling, no pointing/waving, no words, no social smile. Development follows cephalocaudal and proximal-distal sequence.

**Q:** What developmental milestones should a 2-year-old achieve?

**A:** Gross motor: runs, kicks ball, climbs stairs with 2 feet per step; Fine motor: scribbles, towers of 6 blocks; Language: ≥50 words, 2-word phrases, 50% intelligible to strangers; Social: parallel play

CPS developmental assessment at 18 months and 24 months. Red flags at 2 years: <50 words, no 2-word combinations, failure to follow 2-step commands. Regression is always a red flag.

**Q:** What language milestones are expected at 3 years and 4 years?

**A:** 3 years: 200+ words, 3-word sentences, 75% intelligible; asks who/what/where; 4 years: 1000+ words, 4-6 word sentences, 100% intelligible to strangers; tells stories, asks why

CPS speech milestones: refer to speech-language pathology if ≤50 words at 24 months, not combining 2 words by 24 months, or <75% intelligible at 36 months. Bilingual children may have slightly slower single-language acquisition but total vocabulary across both languages is normal.

**Q:** What is the significance of loss of previously acquired milestones (regression)?

**A:** Any developmental regression is a red flag requiring urgent assessment — may indicate Rett syndrome (girls), autism, epileptic encephalopathy (Landau-Kleffner), metabolic disorders, or mass lesion

CPS guidelines: parental concern about developmental regression must always be taken seriously. Rett syndrome: normal development to 6-18 months then regression with loss of purposeful hand use, development of hand stereotypies. Metabolic conditions may present with regression.

**Q:** What screening tools are used in Canada for developmental surveillance?

**A:** ASQ (Ages and Stages Questionnaires): 2, 4, 6, 9, 12, 18, 24, 30, 36 months; M-CHAT-R (Modified Checklist for Autism): 18 and 24 months; Denver Developmental Screening Test II

CPS recommends developmental surveillance at every well-child visit using standardized tools. ASQ is a validated parent-completed questionnaire. Positive M-CHAT-R (score ≥3) requires follow-up; ≥8 = immediate referral to developmental pediatrics.

**Q:** A 7-year-old has difficulty concentrating, hyperactivity, and impulsivity both at home and school. DSM-5 criteria are met. Diagnosis and management?

**A:** ADHD; multimodal treatment: behavioral therapy first (age <6), then methylphenidate (first-line stimulant medication for age ≥6) + behavioral therapy; school accommodations

CPS ADHD guidelines: ADHD requires symptoms in ≥2 settings (home and school), before age 12, for ≥6 months. Stimulants (methylphenidate, amphetamines) are most effective pharmacotherapy. Non-stimulants (atomoxetine, guanfacine) for stimulant intolerance or substance abuse concern.

**Q:** What is the developmental assessment for a 5-year-old starting school?

**A:** Gross motor: hops, skips, catches ball; Fine motor: prints letters, draws person with 6 parts; Language: 5+ word sentences, full sentences, grammar; Social: cooperative play, understands rules; Cognitive: counts to 10, knows colors

School readiness requires assessment of cognitive, language, motor, social-emotional, and self-care domains. CPS recommends 4-5 year visit focusing on school readiness. Phonological awareness (rhyming, letter sounds) predicts reading development.

**Q:** What is cerebral palsy (CP) and how is it classified?

**A:** CP: permanent disorder of movement and posture from non-progressive brain injury early in development; classified by: motor type (spastic most common 85%, dyskinetic, ataxic), topography (hemiplegia, diplegia, quadriplegia), GMFCS level

CP diagnosis: clinical, no single test. Risk factors: prematurity (highest), perinatal asphyxia, infection. MRI brain confirms lesion (periventricular leukomalacia in preterm). Management: multidisciplinary — physio, OT, speech, orthotics, medications (baclofen for spasticity, botulinum toxin).

**Q:** What is the expected gross motor development in the first year of life?

**A:** 2 months: lifts head prone; 4 months: head control, rolls; 6 months: sits with support; 9 months: sits unsupported, stands with support; 12 months: pulls to stand, cruises, possibly walking

CPS developmental surveillance: if not walking by 18 months → referral. 10% of children walk by 9 months, 50% by 12 months, 90% by 15 months. Independent walking after 18 months is a developmental delay requiring evaluation.

**Q:** What are the features and management of a child with intellectual disability (ID)?

**A:** ID: IQ <70 + adaptive behavior deficits in ≥2 areas (communication, self-care, home living, social skills); etiological workup: chromosomal microarray (first-line for unexplained ID), metabolic screen, MRI

CPS recommends chromosomal microarray as first-line for unexplained developmental delay/ID — identifies cause in 15-20%. Fragile X testing in males with ID. Management: early intervention services, school support, behavioral therapies, treat comorbidities (epilepsy, ADHD, ASD).

**Q:** What social-emotional milestones are expected in infancy?

**A:** 0-2 months: social smile; 2-3 months: coos, vocalizes; 4-6 months: laughs, reaches for caregiver; 9 months: stranger anxiety, separation anxiety, object permanence; 12 months: joint attention, pointing

Joint attention (following another person's gaze to an object and sharing that attention) develops at 9-12 months and is critical for language development. Absence of joint attention by 12 months is a red flag for autism spectrum disorder.

**Q:** What is the role of lead screening in pediatric developmental assessment?

**A:** Blood lead level testing at age 1-2 years in high-risk children: urban living, older housing (pre-1978), pica, immigrant from endemic country — elevated lead (>5 μg/dL) causes cognitive impairment

No safe level of blood lead exists. Sources: old paint (most common in Canada), imported ceramics, certain traditional medicines, water pipes. Treatment: chelation therapy (succimer DMSA) for blood lead >45 μg/dL. Primary prevention: blood lead level check, education.

**Q:** What is normal developmental variation versus delay in walking?

**A:** Normal: most children walk independently by 14-15 months; variation up to 18 months is normal if other milestones normal, no neurological signs, and positive family history of late walking; refer if not walking by 18 months

Bottom shufflers and commando crawlers often walk later (up to 18 months) and have a family history. CPS: investigate late walking with developmental assessment, hip X-ray, and creatine kinase (DMD). Duchenne muscular dystrophy: X-linked, toe-walking, Gowers sign.

**Q:** What is the management of a child with suspected learning disability?

**A:** Formal psychoeducational assessment, vision and hearing screening, ADHD evaluation; school accommodation plan (IEP); evidence-based educational interventions (reading: phonics-based, Orton-Gillingham for dyslexia)

Learning disabilities affect 5-15% of school-age children. Most common: dyslexia (reading disability). CPS recommends standardized psychoeducational testing by psychologist to diagnose learning disability and guide accommodations. Early identification and intervention are critical.

**Q:** What are the Denver II developmental milestones at 12 months?

**A:** Gross motor: walks with support. Fine motor: pincer grasp. Language: 1-3 words, follows simple commands. Social: waves bye, separation anxiety

Red flags: no babbling by 12mo, no gestures by 12mo, no single words by 16mo, no 2-word phrases by 24mo. Refer for developmental assessment if >=2 domains delayed.

**Q:** What are the key Denver developmental milestones at 2, 4, 6, 9, and 12 months?

**A:** 2mo: social smile, lifts head prone. 4mo: reaches for objects, rolls front-to-back, laughs. 6mo: sits unsupported, transfers objects, babbles. 9mo: pulls to stand, pincer grasp developing, stranger anxiety, says mama/dada nonspecific. 12mo: walks with one hand held, 1-3 words, pincer grasp mature.

The Denver Developmental Screening Test (DDST-II) assesses gross motor, fine motor-adaptive, language, and personal-social domains. Canadian Paediatric Society recommends developmental surveillance at every well-child visit with standardized screening at 18 and 24 months.

**Q:** What are the developmental milestones at 18 months, 2 years, 3 years, and 4 years?

**A:** 18mo: walks alone, stacks 2-4 blocks, 10-25 words, feeds with spoon, points. 2yr: runs, kicks ball, stacks 6 blocks, 2-word phrases, 50+ words, parallel play. 3yr: tricycle, copies circle, 3-word sentences, knows name/age, group play. 4yr: hops, copies cross, tells stories, cooperative play, dresses independently.

Language development is the most sensitive indicator of overall development. Failure to produce single words by 18 months or 2-word phrases by 24 months warrants referral. CPS recommends M-CHAT-R/F screening for autism at 18 and 24 months.

**Q:** What are the red flags for autism spectrum disorder and when should screening occur?

**A:** Red flags: no babbling by 12 months, no pointing/gesturing by 12 months, no single words by 16 months, no 2-word phrases by 24 months, loss of previously acquired skills at any age, lack of joint attention, no pretend play by 18 months. Screen with M-CHAT-R/F at 18 and 24 months.

CPS and AAP recommend universal ASD screening at 18 and 24 months. Early intervention (before age 3) significantly improves outcomes. DSM-5 criteria: persistent deficits in social communication AND restricted/repetitive behaviors, present from early childhood.

**Q:** What is the approach to a child with global developmental delay?

**A:** Defined as significant delay (>=2 SD below mean) in >=2 developmental domains. Workup: detailed history (prenatal, birth, family), physical exam (dysmorphology, neurocutaneous signs), genetic testing (chromosomal microarray first-line), metabolic screen (TSH, amino acids, organic acids), MRI brain, hearing and vision testing.

Chromosomal microarray has replaced karyotype as first-line genetic test (diagnostic yield 15-20%). Fragile X testing in all males with unexplained GDD. Inborn errors of metabolism are rare but treatable causes. Canadian guidelines recommend early referral to developmental pediatrics.

**Q:** What is the differential diagnosis for speech delay in a toddler?

**A:** Hearing loss (most important to exclude), autism spectrum disorder, intellectual disability, environmental deprivation/bilingualism (expressive only, receptive normal), expressive language disorder, oral motor dysfunction (dysarthria), selective mutism, genetic syndromes (Down, Fragile X).

CPS recommends audiological assessment for any child with speech delay. Universal Newborn Hearing Screening (UNHS) is standard in Canadian provinces. Bilingualism does NOT cause language delay-total vocabulary across both languages should be considered.

**Q:** What are the primitive reflexes, when do they appear, and when should they disappear?

**A:** Moro: birth-4mo. Palmar grasp: birth-6mo. Rooting: birth-4mo. Tonic neck (ATNR): birth-6mo. Parachute reflex: appears 8-9mo, persists. Babinski: birth-12mo (up to 2yr normal). Persistence beyond expected age suggests upper motor neuron pathology.

Persistence of primitive reflexes beyond normal timeframes suggests cerebral palsy or other CNS pathology. Absence at birth suggests CNS depression. The parachute reflex (protective extension) is unique in that it appears and persists-its absence by 12 months is concerning.

**Q:** What is cerebral palsy, its classification, and management approach?

**A:** CP: non-progressive motor disorder from injury to developing brain. Classification by motor type: spastic (70-80%), dyskinetic, ataxic, mixed. By distribution: hemiplegia, diplegia, quadriplegia. Management: multidisciplinary (PT, OT, speech), botulinum toxin for spasticity, orthopedic surgery, baclofen pump for generalized spasticity.

CP is the most common physical disability in children (2-3 per 1000). Prematurity is the strongest risk factor. MRI shows periventricular leukomalacia in premature and hypoxic-ischemic injury in term infants. Canadian CP registries track outcomes. Early intervention improves function.

**Q:** What growth parameters are monitored in well-child visits and when are they concerning?

**A:** Weight, length/height, head circumference (until 2 years), BMI (after 2 years). Concerning: crossing 2 major percentile lines, weight <3rd or >97th percentile, head circumference >97th (hydrocephalus, megalencephaly) or <3rd (microcephaly). Weight faltering: weight-for-age <2nd percentile or crossing down 2 lines.

Canadian Paediatric Society recommends WHO growth charts for children 0-5 years and CDC charts for 5-19 years. Failure to thrive is a clinical sign, not a diagnosis-workup includes feeding history, caloric intake, organic causes (celiac, CF, GERD), and psychosocial assessment.

**Q:** What is the Rourke Baby Record and what does it cover?

**A:** The Rourke Baby Record is a Canadian evidence-based health supervision guide for well-baby/child visits from birth to 5 years. It covers: growth monitoring, nutrition, developmental milestones, immunizations, physical exam, screening (hearing, vision, dental, anemia, lead), anticipatory guidance, and parental concerns.

The Rourke Baby Record is updated regularly and used across Canada as the standard well-child visit template. It integrates CPS, Dietitians of Canada, and CFPC recommendations. Key screening: hemoglobin at 9-12 months, dental assessment by 12 months, vision screening at 3-5 years.

**Q:** What are the screening recommendations for iron deficiency in Canadian infants?

**A:** Screen hemoglobin at 9-12 months for all infants. Risk factors: prematurity, low birth weight, exclusive breastfeeding beyond 6 months without iron supplementation, cow milk before 9-12 months, low socioeconomic status, Indigenous populations. Iron supplementation 1mg/kg/day recommended for exclusively breastfed infants starting at 4-6 months.

Iron deficiency is the most common nutritional deficiency in Canadian infants. CPS recommends iron-fortified cereals as first complementary food. Iron deficiency (even without anemia) affects neurodevelopment. Cow milk before 9 months increases risk due to low iron content and occult GI blood loss.

**Q:** What are the key features of fetal alcohol spectrum disorder (FASD)?

**A:** Sentinel facial features: short palpebral fissures, smooth philtrum, thin upper lip. CNS: microcephaly, structural brain abnormalities, cognitive impairment, behavioral issues (ADHD-like, executive dysfunction). Growth: prenatal and/or postnatal growth deficiency. Confirmed maternal alcohol exposure. Canadian Diagnostic Guidelines use 4-digit code.

FASD prevalence in Canada is estimated at 1-5% (higher in some Indigenous communities). The 2016 Canadian FASD Diagnostic Guidelines require multidisciplinary assessment. No safe level of alcohol in pregnancy. Prevention through public health messaging is the primary strategy.

**Q:** When should a child be referred for developmental assessment vs monitored?

**A:** Refer immediately: any loss of milestones (regression), no babbling/gestures by 12 months, no words by 18 months, no 2-word phrases by 24 months, not walking by 18 months. Monitor with closer follow-up: mild delay in one domain, family history of late talkers, environmental factors identified.

CPS recommends a low threshold for referral. Developmental regression at any age is a red flag for neurodegenerative disease, ASD, or Rett syndrome and warrants urgent investigation including metabolic and genetic workup. Wait-and-see approach is inappropriate for regression.

**Q:** What anticipatory guidance should be provided at key well-child visits?

**A:** Newborn: safe sleep (back to sleep, firm mattress, no co-sleeping), breastfeeding support. 2-6mo: tummy time, car seat safety. 6-12mo: choking hazards, water safety, introduction of solids. 1-2yr: dental care, limit screen time, poison prevention. 3-5yr: helmet use, stranger safety, school readiness.

CPS Caring for Kids provides evidence-based anticipatory guidance. Screen time recommendations: avoid for <2 years, limit to <1 hour/day ages 2-5. Safe sleep practices reduce SIDS risk. Canadian car seat guidelines: rear-facing as long as possible (minimum to 2 years/weight limit).

**Q:** What is the approach to a child with short stature?

**A:** Evaluate growth velocity (most important), bone age, mid-parental height. Investigations: CBC, ESR, electrolytes, creatinine, TSH, celiac screen (TTG-IgA), IGF-1, bone age X-ray. Most common causes: familial short stature (normal growth velocity, bone age = chronological age) and constitutional delay (delayed bone age, delayed puberty, catches up).

Pathological short stature: growth velocity <25th percentile, height >2 SD below mid-parental target, crossing percentiles downward. Consider: GH deficiency, Turner syndrome (karyotype in all short girls), hypothyroidism, celiac disease, chronic disease. Canadian Pediatric Endocrine Group guidelines address GH therapy indications.

**Q:** What are the key differences between ADHD, ASD, and intellectual disability in presentation?

**A:** ADHD: inattention, hyperactivity, impulsivity; normal social skills; age-appropriate cognitive function. ASD: social communication deficits, restricted/repetitive behaviors; may have normal or impaired IQ; onset <3 years. ID: significant cognitive impairment (IQ <70) with adaptive function deficits; may have social interest. Comorbidity is common (30% overlap).

DSM-5 allows dual diagnosis of ADHD + ASD (previously exclusive). Canadian guidelines recommend comprehensive psychoeducational assessment. ADHD affects 5-7% of Canadian children. First-line treatment: methylphenidate or amphetamine for ADHD; behavioral interventions for ASD.

**Q:** What is the M-CHAT-R screening tool for autism?

**A:** Modified Checklist for Autism in Toddlers, Revised. Screen at 18 and 24 months. 20 yes/no questions on social/communication behaviors. Score >=3 positive: follow-up interview then referral for diagnostic assessment

Red flags: no pointing by 12mo, no single words by 16mo, no 2-word phrases by 24mo, loss of previously acquired skills. DSM-5 ASD: persistent deficits in social communication + restricted/repetitive behaviors.

**Q:** Define failure to thrive in pediatrics and outline the initial approach.

**A:** Failure to thrive (FTT): weight <3rd percentile, weight crossing down 2 or more major percentile lines, or weight-for-length <5th percentile. Initial approach: thorough dietary history (caloric intake), feeding observation, psychosocial assessment, growth chart review (WHO charts for 0–2 years, CDC for 2–19 years in Canada), basic labs (CBC, electrolytes, urinalysis, celiac screen, TSH).

The CPS uses WHO growth standards for children 0–5 years. FTT is most commonly caused by inadequate caloric intake (environmental/psychosocial) rather than organic disease. Weight is affected first, then length, then head circumference. The pattern helps distinguish nutritional deficiency from genetic conditions or endocrinopathies.

**Q:** Which growth charts should be used in Canada for children, and what is catch-up versus catch-down growth?

**A:** WHO growth standards for ages 0–5 years (based on breastfed infants as norm); CDC growth charts for 5–19 years. Catch-up growth: a small-for-gestational-age infant crossing percentiles upward in the first 12–18 months to reach genetic potential. Catch-down growth: a large infant crossing percentiles downward to reach genetic potential. Both are normal if following a consistent trajectory after adjustment.

The CPS endorsed WHO charts in 2010, replacing CDC charts for under-5s, because WHO charts represent optimal growth in breastfed infants. Pathologic growth should be distinguished from physiologic catch-up/catch-down by assessing the child's genetic potential (mid-parental height) and monitoring for crossing more than 2 major percentile lines.

**Q:** List the key developmental milestones that, if absent, are red flags at 2 months, 9 months, 18 months, and 24 months.

**A:** 2 months: no social smile, no visual fixation/tracking. 9 months: no babbling, no sitting independently, no stranger anxiety. 18 months: no single words, no pointing, no walking independently. 24 months: no 2-word phrases, no imitative play, vocabulary <50 words.

The CPS Rourke Baby Record and Nipissing District Developmental Screen (NDDS) are widely used in Canadian practice for developmental surveillance. Missing these milestones warrants referral for developmental assessment. Early identification and intervention (especially for autism spectrum disorder) significantly improves outcomes.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 diagnostic criteria for ADHD, and what is the first-line pharmacologic treatment?

**A:** DSM-5 requires ≥6 symptoms of inattention and/or ≥6 symptoms of hyperactivity-impulsivity (≥5 for age ≥17), present for ≥6 months, onset before age 12, symptoms in ≥2 settings (home + school), with functional impairment, and not better explained by another disorder. Subtypes: predominantly inattentive, predominantly hyperactive-impulsive, or combined. First-line treatment: psychostimulants — methylphenidate (Ritalin, Concerta) or amphetamine-based (Adderall, Vyvanse).

The CPS recommends a multimodal approach including behavioural strategies, classroom accommodations (preferential seating, extended time), and medication. In Canada, ADHD affects 5–7% of school-age children. Second-line agents include atomoxetine (non-stimulant) and guanfacine. Medication monitoring should include height, weight, blood pressure, and heart rate.

**Q:** What is the M-CHAT-R/F and how is it used for autism screening?

**A:** Modified Checklist for Autism in Toddlers, Revised with Follow-up. 20-item parent questionnaire administered at 18 and 24 months. Score >=3: positive screen, proceed to follow-up interview. Score >=8: high risk, refer directly for diagnostic evaluation. Sensitivity ~85%, specificity ~95% with follow-up.

Canadian Paediatric Society recommends developmental surveillance at every well-child visit and autism-specific screening at 18-24 months. A positive M-CHAT requires follow-up, not immediate diagnosis. Diagnostic assessment uses DSM-5 criteria with multidisciplinary team (ADOS-2 is the gold standard diagnostic tool).

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 diagnostic criteria for ADHD?

**A:** >=6 symptoms (age <17) or >=5 (age >=17) of inattention AND/OR hyperactivity-impulsivity, present for >=6 months, onset before age 12, present in >=2 settings, causing functional impairment, not better explained by another disorder. Presentations: predominantly inattentive, predominantly hyperactive-impulsive, or combined.

Canadian ADHD Practice Guidelines (CADDRA) recommend multi-informant assessment (parent, teacher, self). Rating scales (Conners, SNAP-IV) support but do not replace clinical diagnosis. Girls are underdiagnosed due to predominantly inattentive presentation. ADHD persists into adulthood in 60-70% of cases.

**Q:** How do you evaluate a child with isolated speech delay?

**A:** Red flags: no babbling by 12 months, no words by 18 months, no 2-word phrases by 24 months. Workup: audiometry (hearing loss is the most common treatable cause), developmental assessment, consider referral to SLP. Evaluate for ASD (joint attention, social reciprocity, play skills), intellectual disability, and bilingual exposure.

Canadian Paediatric Society emphasizes that hearing assessment is mandatory in all children with speech delay. Late talkers (18-24 months) have 50-70% chance of catching up but should be monitored. Expressive language delay alone has better prognosis than combined receptive-expressive delay, which warrants urgent referral.

**Q:** What is the classification system for cerebral palsy?

**A:** By motor type: spastic (most common, 70-80%), dyskinetic (dystonic/choreoathetoid), ataxic, mixed. By distribution: hemiplegia (one side), diplegia (legs > arms), quadriplegia (all limbs). By function: GMFCS levels I-V (I = walks independently, V = transported in wheelchair).

CP is a non-progressive motor disorder caused by brain injury before age 2. Most common cause in term infants is perinatal hypoxic-ischemic encephalopathy. In preterm infants, periventricular leukomalacia predominates (causing spastic diplegia). MRI is indicated to identify etiology. GMFCS guides therapy goals and prognosis.

**Q:** What are learning disabilities and how are they evaluated?

**A:** Neurodevelopmental disorders causing specific difficulty in reading (dyslexia), math (dyscalculia), or written expression (dysgraphia) despite adequate intelligence and instruction. Evaluation: psychoeducational assessment (IQ testing + achievement testing showing significant discrepancy), rule out ADHD, anxiety, vision/hearing problems, and intellectual disability.

Learning disabilities affect 5-10% of school-age children and are a common comorbidity with ADHD (30-50% overlap). Canadian education systems provide individualized education plans (IEPs). The physician's role is to rule out medical causes, identify comorbid conditions, and advocate for appropriate educational support.

**Q:** What developmental milestones should a 2-year-old typically achieve?

**A:** Gross motor: runs, kicks ball, walks up stairs with support. Fine motor: stacks 6 blocks, turns pages, scribbles. Language: 50+ words, 2-word phrases, follows 2-step commands. Social: parallel play, imitates household tasks, points to show interest. Cognitive: symbolic play (pretend feeding doll).

Canadian Paediatric Society Rourke Baby Record tracks milestones at each well-child visit. Absence of 2-word phrases by 24 months or loss of previously acquired skills at any age warrants urgent referral. Developmental screening tools (ASQ-3, NDDS) supplement clinical surveillance.

**Q:** What are the early signs of autism spectrum disorder in a toddler?

**A:** Reduced eye contact, absent or reduced joint attention (not pointing to share interest), delayed or absent pretend play, lack of social smile/response to name by 12 months, language delay, repetitive behaviours (lining up toys), restricted interests, sensory sensitivities, lack of social reciprocity.

CPS recommends screening at 18 and 24 months. Early intensive behavioural intervention (IBI/ABA) improves outcomes when started before age 4. Provincial wait times for diagnosis and therapy vary significantly across Canada. Family-centred care and parent coaching are integral to management.

**Q:** What is the first-line pharmacotherapy for ADHD in children and what monitoring is required?

**A:** First-line: long-acting methylphenidate (Concerta, Biphentin) or amphetamine (Vyvanse). Start low, titrate to optimal dose. Monitoring: height, weight, BP, HR at baseline and every 3-6 months. Assess for appetite suppression, sleep disturbance, mood changes, tics. No routine ECG needed unless cardiac risk factors.

CADDRA guidelines recommend stimulant medication as first-line combined with behavioural strategies. Growth monitoring is essential as stimulants may slow height velocity (typically by 1-2 cm over first 1-3 years, with catch-up). Drug holidays may be considered if growth is significantly affected. Non-stimulants (atomoxetine, guanfacine) are second-line.

**Q:** At what age should global developmental delay be investigated with genetic testing?

**A:** When a child <5 has significant delays in >=2 developmental domains. First-tier genetic testing: chromosomal microarray (CMA) is the recommended first-line genetic test (replaces karyotype). Consider fragile X testing (especially in boys). Additional: metabolic screen if regression or specific features, MRI brain, thyroid function.

CMA identifies pathogenic copy number variants in 15-20% of children with unexplained GDD/intellectual disability. Canadian Medical Genetics guidelines recommend CMA as first-tier. Whole exome sequencing is increasingly used when CMA is non-diagnostic. An identifiable genetic cause is found in ~40% of cases with comprehensive workup.

**Q:** What is developmental coordination disorder (DCD) and how is it diagnosed?

**A:** Motor skills significantly below expected for age, interfering with daily activities and academic performance. Not explained by intellectual disability, visual impairment, or neurological condition. Diagnosis requires standardized motor assessment (MABC-2). Onset in early developmental period. Affects 5-6% of school-age children.

DCD (previously dyspraxia) is often underdiagnosed. Canadian guidelines recommend occupational therapy as primary intervention. DCD commonly co-occurs with ADHD (50%), learning disabilities, and anxiety. Children with DCD are at risk for obesity and reduced physical activity. Task-oriented approaches to OT are most effective.

## Fever in Children & Pediatric Infections

**Q:** A 2-month-old presents with fever of 38.5°C rectally. What is the management?

**A:** Fever ≥38°C in infant <2 months requires full sepsis workup (CBC, blood culture, UA + urine culture, LP) and empiric IV antibiotics (ampicillin + gentamicin ± acyclovir) regardless of clinical appearance

CPS guidelines: fever in neonates (<28 days) and young infants (28-90 days) is a medical emergency. All infants <28 days with fever require hospitalization. 28-60 day infants: risk stratification with Rochester/PECARN criteria. Well-appearing low-risk 2-3 month infants may be managed with observation.

**Q:** A 2-year-old has fever 40°C, bulging tympanic membrane, and purulent ear discharge. Diagnosis and treatment?

**A:** Acute otitis media (AOM); first-line treatment: amoxicillin 90 mg/kg/day (divided TID) x10 days for children <2 years or severe disease — 5-7 days for older children with mild disease

CPS AOM guidelines recommend high-dose amoxicillin as first-line (covers penicillin non-susceptible S. pneumoniae). Watchful waiting for 48-72h acceptable for mild AOM in children ≥6 months with unilateral disease. Amoxicillin-clavulanate for treatment failure or recurrent AOM.

**Q:** What are the most common causes of fever without localizing source in children aged 3-36 months?

**A:** Occult bacteremia (less common post-PCV13 vaccine era), UTI (most common serious bacterial infection), viral illness (most common overall), pneumonia; S. pneumoniae most common organism in bacteremia

CPS guidelines for fever without source: age 3-36 months with fever ≥39°C — if well-appearing and vaccinated, watchful waiting is appropriate. Urinalysis and urine culture for all febrile girls <2 years and boys <6 months. Blood culture if appearing ill.

**Q:** What clinical features suggest serious bacterial infection (SBI) in a febrile child?

**A:** High temperature (≥39°C in <3 months or ≥40°C in 3-36 months), toxic appearance (lethargy, poor perfusion, inconsolable irritability), ill-appearing despite antipyretics, petechial/purpuric rash, neck stiffness

NICE traffic-light system: green (low risk), amber (intermediate), red (high risk) features for SBI. CPS guidelines: petechiae + fever = meningococcemia until proven otherwise — give IV ceftriaxone immediately and admit.

**Q:** A 3-year-old has fever, sore throat, and a beefy red pharynx with exudates and swollen anterior cervical nodes. Rapid antigen test for Group A Strep is positive. Treatment?

**A:** Group A Streptococcal pharyngitis; treat with amoxicillin 50 mg/kg/day x10 days (or penicillin V) to prevent rheumatic fever — penicillin/amoxicillin is first-line; azithromycin if penicillin allergy

CPS guidelines: antibiotics for GAS pharyngitis confirmed by rapid antigen test or throat culture. Centor/McIsaac criteria for clinical probability. Treat to prevent acute rheumatic fever (ARF). ARF prevention requires 10 days of penicillin-class antibiotics.

**Q:** What is the management of a 6-year-old with suspected epiglottitis presenting with drooling, dysphagia, and respiratory distress?

**A:** Do NOT examine throat or agitate child; call anesthesia and ENT immediately; maintain airway in OR with intubation; IV ceftriaxone (H. influenzae type b and S. pneumoniae coverage)

Epiglottitis is rare post-Hib vaccine but still occurs. Classic: sudden onset, high fever, drooling, tripod position, stridor (may not be present). Do NOT attempt to examine oropharynx — may precipitate complete obstruction. Lateral neck X-ray: "thumbprint sign" (edematous epiglottis).

**Q:** A 5-year-old has a fever, strawberry tongue, diffuse erythematous rash that feels like sandpaper, and desquamation of palms and soles. What is the diagnosis and treatment?

**A:** Scarlet fever (Group A Streptococcal infection with erythrogenic toxin); treat with amoxicillin or penicillin x10 days

Scarlet fever is caused by erythrogenic toxin-producing GAS. Pastia lines (accentuation of rash in skin folds). Sandpaper rash is characteristic. Complications without treatment: ARF, glomerulonephritis. Throat culture or rapid antigen test confirms diagnosis.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic criteria for Kawasaki disease and how is it treated?

**A:** Fever ≥5 days + ≥4 of 5: bilateral conjunctivitis, mucous membrane changes (strawberry tongue, cracked lips), polymorphous rash, extremity changes (erythema/desquamation), cervical lymphadenopathy; treat with IVIG 2g/kg + ASA

CPS Kawasaki guidelines: treat within 10 days of fever onset. IVIG reduces coronary artery aneurysm from 25% to 3-5%. High-dose ASA for anti-inflammatory effect, then low-dose for antiplatelet effect. Coronary artery imaging (echocardiogram) at diagnosis, 2 weeks, and 6-8 weeks.

**Q:** A 9-month-old develops fever then rapid defervescence followed by a rose-colored rash on the trunk. Diagnosis?

**A:** Roseola infantum (HHV-6); self-limited viral illness — fever 3-5 days, then rash after fever resolves; no specific treatment required

Roseola is caused by HHV-6 (human herpesvirus 6). Most common exanthem in infants 6-24 months. The rash appears as fever resolves — this pattern is diagnostic. Febrile seizures can occur during the high fever phase. HHV-7 also causes roseola. Reassurance and supportive care.

**Q:** What is the approach to fever management in pediatrics — when should antipyretics be given?

**A:** Antipyretics for comfort (not to prevent febrile seizures); acetaminophen 15 mg/kg q4-6h OR ibuprofen 10 mg/kg q6-8h (≥6 months); do NOT use aspirin (Reye syndrome risk)

CPS guidelines: fever is a beneficial host response to infection. Antipyretics improve child comfort but do not reduce febrile seizure risk. Ibuprofen is more effective than acetaminophen for fever reduction. Tepid sponging is not recommended. Never use ASA in children due to Reye syndrome.

**Q:** A 4-year-old with high fever and a petechial rash has rapid clinical deterioration with hypotension. What is the diagnosis and immediate treatment?

**A:** Meningococcemia (Neisseria meningitidis); IV ceftriaxone 100 mg/kg immediately (do not wait for LP), aggressive fluid resuscitation, ICU admission

Meningococcal septicemia is the most dangerous pediatric infection — death within hours. Non-blanching petechiae + fever = meningococcemia until proven otherwise. Give ceftriaxone BEFORE any other investigation if patient is deteriorating. Rifampin or ciprofloxacin prophylaxis for close contacts.

**Q:** What is the workup and management of UTI in a febrile child under 2 years?

**A:** Urine sample (catheter or suprapubic for <2 years); UA + culture; treat empiric trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole (TMP-SMX) or nitrofurantoin — 7-14 days for febrile UTI; renal ultrasound for all first febrile UTIs

CPS UTI guidelines: clean-catch urine is unreliable in infants — use catheter or SPA. Febrile UTI (pyelonephritis) requires 10-14 day treatment. First febrile UTI: renal ultrasound. VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram) for abnormal ultrasound or recurrent UTI to detect VUR.

**Q:** What is hand, foot, and mouth disease (HFMD) and how is it managed?

**A:** HFMD: Coxsackievirus A16 or Enterovirus 71; painful vesicles on hands, feet, mouth; low-grade fever; self-limited 7-10 days — supportive care, analgesia for mouth pain

HFMD is highly contagious (fecal-oral, droplet). School exclusion until child is well and vesicles have crusted. No vaccine available in Canada. EV71 associated with neurological complications (brainstem encephalitis, pulmonary edema) — more common in Asia.

**Q:** A 10-year-old presents with fever, joint pain/swelling (knee, ankle) that migrated from the ankles to the knees, and a new heart murmur. What is the diagnosis?

**A:** Acute rheumatic fever (ARF); Jones criteria: 2 major OR 1 major + 2 minor criteria with evidence of prior GAS infection (elevated ASO titer, throat culture)

Major Jones criteria: carditis, migratory polyarthritis, Sydenham chorea, erythema marginatum, subcutaneous nodules. Minor: fever, elevated ESR/CRP, prolonged PR interval. ARF prevention: treat GAS pharyngitis. Secondary prophylaxis: monthly benzathine penicillin G for 10 years (or until age 25 if carditis).

**Q:** What are the indications for hospitalization of a febrile child?

**A:** Age <28 days (all), age 28-60 days if not meeting low-risk criteria, toxic appearance, petechiae, signs of sepsis, failed outpatient treatment, immunocompromised, parental inability to monitor

CPS fever guidelines: all neonates (<28 days) with fever require hospitalization. For 28-60 day age group: Rochester/PECARN low-risk criteria must be fully met for outpatient management. Ensure reliable follow-up and parent education for all discharged febrile infants.

**Q:** What is the management of febrile seizure?

**A:** Simple febrile seizure (<15min, generalized, single in 24h): reassurance, antipyretics. No antiepileptic drugs needed. Complex: investigate with LP if <12mo or if meningitis suspected

Recurrence risk: 30% overall, higher if age<18mo, lower temp threshold, family history. No increased epilepsy risk for simple FS. EEG and neuroimaging not routinely indicated.

**Q:** What is the approach to fever in infants <3 months (step-by-step)?

**A:** 0-28 days: ALL febrile neonates (>=38°C rectal) need full septic workup (blood culture, urine, LP, CXR if respiratory symptoms) + empiric IV antibiotics (ampicillin + gentamicin or cefotaxime). 29-60 days: septic workup, consider low-risk criteria (Rochester/Philadelphia) for observation. 61-90 days: risk stratify-well-appearing with normal urinalysis may be managed with close follow-up.

Serious bacterial infection (SBI) rate in febrile neonates <28 days is 10-15%, making full workup mandatory. UTI is the most common SBI in febrile infants. CPS guidelines recommend LP for all febrile neonates <30 days. HSV should be considered (acyclovir if risk factors).

**Q:** What are the Rochester criteria for identifying low-risk febrile infants?

**A:** Low risk if ALL present: previously healthy term infant, no focal infection on exam, well-appearing, WBC 5,000-15,000, band count <1,500, urinalysis normal (<=10 WBC/HPF), stool <=5 WBC/HPF if diarrhea. Low-risk infants (29-60 days) may be managed as outpatient with close follow-up in 24h.

Rochester criteria have 98-99% NPV for SBI. These criteria do NOT apply to neonates <28 days. Canadian practice varies-some centres still admit all febrile infants <60 days. Newer biomarkers (procalcitonin) are being integrated into risk stratification algorithms.

**Q:** What is the management of simple vs complex febrile seizures?

**A:** Simple: generalized, <15 min, single episode in 24h, age 6mo-5yr. No workup needed (no EEG, no imaging, no LP unless meningitis suspected). Reassurance, fever management education. Complex: focal, >15 min, recurrent in 24h, or age <6 months. Requires investigation: LP (especially if <12mo), consider EEG and MRI.

CPS guidelines state simple febrile seizures do not increase epilepsy risk above baseline (1%). Risk of recurrence: 30% overall, 50% if first seizure <12 months. Antipyretics do NOT prevent febrile seizures. Intermittent diazepam can be considered for recurrent febrile seizures with high parental anxiety.

**Q:** What are the common causes of fever and rash in pediatric patients and their key distinguishing features?

**A:** Measles: cough/coryza/conjunctivitis, Koplik spots, cephalocaudal maculopapular rash. Roseola (HHV-6): high fever 3-5 days then rash appears as fever breaks. Scarlet fever: sandpaper rash, pastia lines, strawberry tongue, GAS pharyngitis. Varicella: crops of vesicles in different stages. Kawasaki: fever >5 days, conjunctivitis, rash, extremity changes, oral changes, lymphadenopathy.

Kawasaki disease is the most important not to miss due to coronary artery aneurysm risk (25% untreated). Treatment with IVIG within 10 days reduces aneurysm risk to <5%. Canadian Paediatric Society guidelines outline diagnostic criteria (fever >=5 days + 4/5 features, or incomplete KD with echo findings).

**Q:** What is the definition and management of Kawasaki disease?

**A:** Diagnostic criteria: fever >=5 days + >=4 of 5: bilateral non-exudative conjunctivitis, oral changes (strawberry tongue, cracked lips), polymorphous rash, extremity changes (edema, erythema, desquamation), cervical lymphadenopathy (>=1.5cm unilateral). Treatment: IVIG 2g/kg single dose + high-dose ASA (80-100mg/kg/day until afebrile, then 3-5mg/kg/day for 6-8 weeks).

Incomplete Kawasaki (fever >=5 days + 2-3 criteria) is diagnosed with supporting lab/echo findings. Echocardiogram at diagnosis, 2 weeks, and 6-8 weeks. Coronary aneurysm risk: 25% untreated, <5% with IVIG. CPS guidelines recommend retreatment with IVIG or IV methylprednisolone for IVIG-resistant cases.

**Q:** What is the approach to the child with recurrent fever?

**A:** Differential: periodic fever syndromes (PFAPA: periodic fever, aphthous stomatitis, pharyngitis, adenitis-most common in children), cyclic neutropenia, familial Mediterranean fever, immunodeficiency, occult infections, malignancy. Workup: CBC with differential, ESR, CRP during episodes, genetic testing for autoinflammatory syndromes.

PFAPA is the most common periodic fever syndrome in childhood, with clockwork febrile episodes every 3-6 weeks responding dramatically to single-dose corticosteroids. CPS recommends referral to pediatric rheumatology or immunology for recurrent unexplained fevers.

**Q:** What are the indications for blood cultures in febrile children?

**A:** All febrile neonates <28 days. Febrile infants 1-3 months with risk factors. Children with toxic appearance, petechial rash, indwelling lines/devices, immunocompromised, sickle cell disease, unimmunized, prolonged fever >5 days without source, or suspected endocarditis/osteomyelitis.

Blood culture yield in well-appearing febrile children >3 months is low (<2%) and not routinely indicated if immunized. Occult bacteremia has declined dramatically with pneumococcal and Hib conjugate vaccination. Canadian microbiology guidelines recommend appropriate volume collection.

**Q:** How does the approach to fever differ in immunocompromised children?

**A:** Febrile neutropenia (ANC <500 + temp >=38.3°C once or >=38°C sustained 1h): EMERGENCY. Immediate blood cultures (peripheral + central line if present), empiric broad-spectrum antibiotics within 1 hour (piperacillin-tazobactam or cefepime). Do NOT wait for culture results. Add vancomycin if line infection suspected.

Febrile neutropenia carries 5-10% mortality if antibiotics are delayed. Canadian Paediatric Cancer guidelines mandate antibiotic administration within 60 minutes of triage. Fungal coverage (caspofungin or amphotericin) added if fever persists >96h on broad-spectrum antibiotics.

**Q:** What is the recommended approach to UTI in febrile infants and children?

**A:** Febrile infants <2 years with no clear fever source: catheterized urine sample (bag specimens are unreliable). Diagnosis: positive urinalysis (LE, nitrites, pyuria) + positive culture (>50,000 CFU/mL single organism catheter specimen). Treatment: IV antibiotics for toxic/young infants, oral cephalosporin for well-appearing >2 months. Renal ultrasound after first febrile UTI. VCUG for recurrent UTI or abnormal US.

UTI is the most common SBI in febrile infants. CPS guidelines recommend catheterized specimen for diagnosis. E. coli causes 80% of pediatric UTIs. RIVUR trial showed antibiotic prophylaxis reduces recurrence in VUR but with antibiotic resistance concerns. DMSA scan for renal scarring assessment.

**Q:** What are the clinical features and management of meningitis in children?

**A:** Bacterial meningitis: fever, headache, neck stiffness, altered consciousness, petechial rash (meningococcal). Infants: irritability, bulging fontanelle, poor feeding. LP: elevated WBC (PMN predominant), low glucose (<2.2 or <40% serum), elevated protein. Empiric treatment: ceftriaxone + vancomycin (add ampicillin if <3 months for Listeria). Dexamethasone before/with first antibiotics (reduces hearing loss for H. influenzae).

Common pathogens by age: neonate (GBS, E. coli, Listeria), 1-3mo (GBS, E. coli, S. pneumoniae), >3mo (S. pneumoniae, N. meningitidis). Dexamethasone 0.15mg/kg q6h x 4 days given before or with first dose of antibiotics. CPS guidelines recommend close contacts receive chemoprophylaxis for meningococcal disease.

**Q:** What is the differential diagnosis of prolonged fever (>7 days) in a child?

**A:** Infection (30%): endocarditis, osteomyelitis, abscess, TB, EBV, CMV, cat-scratch disease. Autoimmune (20%): juvenile idiopathic arthritis (systemic), SLE, Kawasaki, inflammatory bowel disease. Malignancy (10%): leukemia, lymphoma, neuroblastoma. Other: drug fever, factitious.

Fever of unknown origin in children (>8 days, no diagnosis after initial workup) is more commonly infectious than in adults. Systemic JIA (Still disease) presents with quotidian fever and salmon-colored evanescent rash. CPS recommends systematic stepwise investigation rather than shotgun approach.

**Q:** What antipyretic dosing is recommended in children?

**A:** Acetaminophen: 15 mg/kg/dose q4-6h (max 75 mg/kg/day or 4g/day). Ibuprofen: 10 mg/kg/dose q6-8h (max 40 mg/kg/day), approved for age >=6 months. Do NOT alternate routinely (risk of dosing errors). ASA is CONTRAINDICATED in children (Reye syndrome).

CPS emphasizes that fever itself is not harmful and antipyretics are for comfort, not to normalize temperature. Alternating acetaminophen and ibuprofen is not recommended due to caregiver confusion and dosing errors. Tepid sponge baths are not recommended.

**Q:** What are the key features differentiating viral from bacterial pharyngitis in children?

**A:** Bacterial (GAS): sudden onset, fever >38.5°C, tonsillar exudates, tender anterior cervical lymphadenopathy, absence of cough. Centor/McIsaac score guides testing. Viral: cough, rhinorrhea, conjunctivitis, hoarseness, diarrhea, oral ulcers. Rapid strep test + throat culture for confirmation.

Modified Centor (McIsaac) score: age 3-14 (+1), fever >38°C (+1), no cough (+1), tender anterior cervical nodes (+1), tonsillar exudates (+1). Score >=3: test and treat. Score <3: no testing needed. Canadian guidelines recommend treating confirmed GAS with penicillin V or amoxicillin x 10 days.

**Q:** What is the evaluation and management of a child with petechiae and fever?

**A:** Petechiae + fever = meningococcemia until proven otherwise. Immediate: IV access, blood cultures, CBC with differential, coagulation studies, empiric IV ceftriaxone. If well-appearing with petechiae above nipple line only (mechanical) and normal labs-may observe. Below nipple line or diffuse petechiae with ill appearance mandates full sepsis workup.

N. meningitidis is the most feared cause. Purpura fulminans indicates DIC and carries high mortality. Canadian public health guidelines require reporting of meningococcal disease and contact tracing with chemoprophylaxis (rifampin, ciprofloxacin, or ceftriaxone).

**Q:** What are the common causes of cervical lymphadenopathy in children?

**A:** Reactive (most common): viral URI, EBV, CMV. Bacterial: GAS pharyngitis, Staph/Strep lymphadenitis, cat-scratch (Bartonella). Mycobacterial: TB or non-tuberculous mycobacterial. Malignancy: lymphoma (firm, fixed, supraclavicular, >2cm persistent >4-6 weeks). Autoimmune: Kawasaki, SLE, JIA.

Most pediatric cervical lymphadenopathy is reactive and self-limited. Red flags for malignancy: supraclavicular location, firm/fixed, >2cm, persistent >4-6 weeks, associated B symptoms (weight loss, night sweats). Canadian guidelines recommend observation for 4-6 weeks before biopsy unless red flags present.

**Q:** What are the Rochester criteria for fever without source in neonates?

**A:** Low risk if ALL: previously healthy term infant, non-toxic appearing, no focal infection, WBC 5-15k, band count <1.5k, normal UA, if diarrhea <5 WBC/hpf in stool

Fever in neonate <28 days: full septic workup (blood cx, urine cx, LP) and empiric antibiotics (ampicillin + gentamicin) regardless of appearance. 29-60 days: risk stratify with Rochester/Philadelphia/Boston criteria.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic criteria for Kawasaki disease?

**A:** Fever >=5 days + >=4 of 5: bilateral conjunctival injection, oral mucosal changes (strawberry tongue), extremity changes (edema/desquamation), polymorphous rash, cervical lymphadenopathy (>=1.5cm)

Incomplete Kawasaki: fever + 2-3 criteria with supportive labs (CRP>=30, ESR>=40). Treatment: IVIG 2g/kg + high-dose ASA within 10 days. Coronary artery aneurysm risk 25% if untreated, <5% with IVIG.

**Q:** A 4-year-old presents with fever, coryza, conjunctivitis, cough, and Koplik spots on the buccal mucosa. Two days later a maculopapular rash starts on the face and spreads cephalocaudally. What is the diagnosis and key complications?

**A:** Measles (rubeola). The prodrome of the 3 Cs (cough, coryza, conjunctivitis) plus Koplik spots is pathognomonic. Complications: pneumonia (most common cause of death), encephalitis (acute or SSPE years later), otitis media, croup, diarrhea. Vitamin A supplementation reduces morbidity and mortality.

Measles is a reportable disease in Canada. Two doses of MMR vaccine are recommended by the CPS at 12 months and 18 months (or school entry). Canada has experienced outbreaks in undervaccinated communities. Post-exposure prophylaxis with MMR (within 72 hours) or immunoglobulin (within 6 days) can prevent or modify disease.

**Q:** Differentiate varicella, roseola, and hand-foot-mouth disease by rash characteristics and clinical features.

**A:** Varicella (chickenpox): vesicular rash in different stages (macule→papule→vesicle→crust), pruritic, centripetal distribution (trunk > extremities), fever. Roseola (HHV-6): high fever for 3–5 days that breaks, THEN maculopapular rash appears on trunk as fever resolves. Hand-foot-mouth (Coxsackie A16/enterovirus 71): painful oral ulcers plus vesicular lesions on palms, soles, and buttocks; low-grade fever.

Varicella can be prevented with 2 doses of varicella vaccine (CPS schedule: 12–15 months and 4–6 years). Roseola is the classic cause of febrile seizures in infants 6–24 months. Hand-foot-mouth is highly contagious and typically self-limited, but enterovirus 71 can cause severe neurologic complications.

**Q:** A 6-year-old develops pharyngitis followed 1–2 days later by a sandpaper-like erythematous rash starting in the groin/axillae, pastia lines, and a strawberry tongue. What is the diagnosis and treatment?

**A:** Scarlet fever, caused by Group A Streptococcus (GAS) producing erythrogenic toxin. Treatment: penicillin V or amoxicillin for 10 days (or IM penicillin G benzathine single dose). Rash desquamates after 1 week. Treatment prevents acute rheumatic fever.

Scarlet fever is essentially strep pharyngitis with a toxin-mediated rash. The CPS recommends treating GAS pharyngitis with antibiotics primarily to prevent acute rheumatic fever. Pastia lines (accentuated rash in skin folds) and circumoral pallor are classic features. Post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis is not prevented by antibiotics.

**Q:** A 9-month-old presents with colicky abdominal pain, vomiting, and currant jelly stools. Ultrasound shows a target sign. What is the diagnosis and definitive management?

**A:** Intussusception (typically ileocolic). Most common in infants 6–36 months. The classic triad is intermittent colicky pain, vomiting, and currant jelly stools (bloody mucus), though the full triad is present in only ~20%. Management: air or hydrostatic (saline) enema reduction under fluoroscopy or ultrasound guidance. Surgery if reduction fails, peritonitis, or perforation.

Intussusception is the most common cause of intestinal obstruction in infants. A lead point (Meckel diverticulum, lymphoma, polyp) should be considered in children >2 years. The CPS notes that recurrence after enema reduction occurs in 5–10% of cases. Post-reduction observation for 24 hours is recommended.

**Q:** A 4-week-old presents with projectile non-bilious vomiting after feeds, a palpable olive-shaped mass in the right upper quadrant, and a hypochloremic hypokalemic metabolic alkalosis. What is the diagnosis and treatment?

**A:** Pyloric stenosis (hypertrophic pyloric stenosis). Diagnosed by ultrasound showing pyloric muscle thickness >3 mm and length >15 mm. Treatment: first correct dehydration and electrolyte abnormalities (the alkalosis), then pyloromyotomy (Ramstedt procedure).

Pyloric stenosis affects approximately 2–5 per 1000 live births, with a 4:1 male predominance. The metabolic alkalosis results from loss of HCl in vomitus, with renal compensation (paradoxical aciduria). Surgery must wait until electrolytes are corrected, as anesthesia with uncorrected alkalosis risks apnea.

**Q:** How does Hirschsprung disease present, and what is the diagnostic workup?

**A:** Presentation: failure to pass meconium within 48 hours of birth, abdominal distention, bilious vomiting in neonates. In older children: chronic constipation refractory to treatment, failure to thrive, enterocolitis. Diagnosis: contrast enema (transition zone), followed by rectal suction biopsy (gold standard) showing absence of ganglion cells in the submucosal plexus and nerve hypertrophy. Treatment: surgical resection of aganglionic segment (pull-through procedure).

Hirschsprung disease affects approximately 1 in 5000 births, with 4:1 male predominance. It is associated with Down syndrome (2–15% of cases) and other neurocristopathies. Hirschsprung-associated enterocolitis (HAEC) is the most serious complication and can be life-threatening, requiring urgent decompression and antibiotics.

**Q:** What is the most common childhood cancer, and what are its typical presenting features?

**A:** Acute lymphoblastic leukemia (ALL) accounts for ~25% of all childhood cancers and ~75% of childhood leukemias. Presenting features: pallor, fatigue, fever, bruising/petechiae (bone marrow failure), bone/joint pain, hepatosplenomegaly, lymphadenopathy. CBC may show anemia, thrombocytopenia, and leukocytosis or leukopenia with blasts on peripheral smear.

ALL peak incidence is 2–5 years of age. Canadian survival rates now exceed 90% with modern multi-agent chemotherapy protocols. Prognostic factors include age (1–10 years favorable), WBC at diagnosis, cytogenetics (hyperdiploidy good, Philadelphia chromosome poor), and minimal residual disease response. The CPS recommends urgent referral for children with unexplained cytopenias.

**Q:** Match the following pediatric solid tumors with their classic features: Wilms tumor, neuroblastoma, retinoblastoma.

**A:** Wilms tumor (nephroblastoma): asymptomatic abdominal mass in 3–5 year old, does NOT cross the midline, associated with hemihypertrophy and aniridia (WAGR syndrome). Neuroblastoma: abdominal mass that DOES cross the midline, arises from neural crest (adrenal medulla), elevated catecholamines (VMA/HVA), can present with periorbital ecchymoses (raccoon eyes), opsoclonus-myoclonus. Retinoblastoma: leukocoria (white pupillary reflex), strabismus, associated with RB1 gene mutation (chromosome 13q).

Wilms tumor is the most common renal malignancy in children; a key clinical pearl is to avoid palpating the mass aggressively to prevent tumor rupture. Neuroblastoma is unique among pediatric cancers in its ability to spontaneously regress (stage 4S). Retinoblastoma screening relies on the red reflex exam at every well-child visit per CPS recommendations.

**Q:** How should a neonate (<28 days) with fever >=38.0C be managed?

**A:** Full septic workup: CBC with differential, blood culture, urinalysis + urine culture (catheter specimen), LP with CSF analysis and culture, chest X-ray if respiratory symptoms. Start empiric IV antibiotics immediately (ampicillin + gentamicin or ampicillin + cefotaxime). Admit to hospital. HSV PCR and acyclovir if any concern for HSV.

CPS guidelines recommend that ALL febrile neonates <28 days undergo full septic workup and empiric IV antibiotics regardless of clinical appearance, as serious bacterial infection (SBI) rate is 10-15%. HSV must be considered especially with vesicles, seizures, elevated LFTs, or maternal history. Never defer LP unless contraindicated.

**Q:** What are the Rochester criteria for identifying low-risk febrile infants 0-60 days?

**A:** Low-risk criteria (ALL must be met): previously healthy full-term infant, non-toxic appearance, no focal bacterial infection on exam, WBC 5,000-15,000, band count <1,500, urinalysis <10 WBC/hpf, stool <5 WBC/hpf (if diarrhea). Low-risk infants have <1% SBI risk but still require close follow-up.

Rochester criteria help stratify febrile young infants but do NOT replace clinical judgment. They do not include inflammatory markers (CRP, procalcitonin) which newer protocols incorporate. Canadian practice generally still admits all febrile neonates <28 days regardless of risk stratification. Protocols vary for 29-60 day infants.

**Q:** What are the complete and incomplete Kawasaki disease diagnostic criteria?

**A:** Complete: fever >=5 days PLUS >=4 of: bilateral non-exudative conjunctivitis, oral mucous membrane changes (strawberry tongue, cracked lips), cervical lymphadenopathy (>=1.5 cm, usually unilateral), polymorphous rash, extremity changes (edema, erythema, desquamation). Incomplete: fever >=5 days + 2-3 criteria with supportive lab findings (CRP >=30, ESR >=40).

Kawasaki disease is the leading cause of acquired heart disease in children in developed countries. CPS guidelines recommend echocardiography at diagnosis, 2 weeks, and 6-8 weeks. Treatment: IVIG 2 g/kg single dose + high-dose ASA, then low-dose ASA for 6-8 weeks. Coronary artery aneurysm risk is 25% if untreated vs 5% with treatment.

**Q:** What are the serious bacterial infection markers that help guide management in febrile children?

**A:** Procalcitonin >=0.5 ng/mL (most accurate single marker for SBI), CRP >=40 mg/L, WBC >15,000 or <5,000, absolute neutrophil count >10,000, urinalysis positive, positive blood/urine culture. Clinical signs: toxic appearance, poor perfusion, inconsolability, bulging fontanelle, petechiae.

Procalcitonin is emerging as the most useful biomarker for SBI in Canadian pediatric EDs — it rises earlier than CRP (within 2-4 hours of infection) and is more specific for bacterial infection. Step-by-step protocols (like PECARN febrile infant rule) combining clinical appearance, age, and biomarkers optimize safe management.

**Q:** How does the management approach differ for febrile infants aged 29-60 days vs 61-90 days?

**A:** 29-60 days: full septic workup including LP, empiric IV antibiotics, admission (most Canadian centres). 61-90 days: if well-appearing and low-risk (negative UA, normal inflammatory markers), may observe without LP or antibiotics with reliable follow-up in 24 hours. High-risk features at any age mandate full workup.

Risk of SBI decreases with age: ~10% at 0-28 days, ~5% at 29-60 days, ~3% at 61-90 days. Newer protocols (PECARN, Step-by-Step) use procalcitonin and CRP to safely identify low-risk infants 29-60 days who may be managed without LP. Canadian implementation varies by centre.

**Q:** A 4-year-old has fever for 7 days, bilateral conjunctivitis, strawberry tongue, swollen hands, and macular rash. Initial IVIG was given but fever persists at 48 hours. What is next?

**A:** IVIG-resistant Kawasaki disease. Administer second dose of IVIG 2 g/kg. If still refractory, consider IV methylprednisolone pulse (30 mg/kg x 3 days) or infliximab (5 mg/kg). Continue high-dose ASA until afebrile for 48-72 hours. Urgent cardiology consultation.

10-20% of Kawasaki patients are IVIG-resistant. CPS and AHA guidelines recommend repeat IVIG as first step. Corticosteroids and infliximab are second/third-line options. These patients have higher coronary artery aneurysm risk and require more intensive cardiac follow-up.

**Q:** What is the appropriate approach to occult bacteremia in a well-appearing febrile child 3-36 months with no focus of infection?

**A:** In the post-PCV13 era, occult bacteremia risk is very low (<0.5%). If well-appearing, fully immunized, and temperature <39.0C: no bloodwork needed, symptomatic care, anticipatory guidance, follow-up in 24-48 hours. If >=39.0C or under-immunized: consider CBC, blood culture, urinalysis.

Pneumococcal conjugate vaccines have dramatically reduced occult bacteremia rates. Canadian Immunization Guide schedules PCV13 at 2, 4, 12 months. The most common pathogen now is E. coli (from UTI). CPS guidelines emphasize that well-appearing immunized children rarely have bacteremia.

**Q:** What features of a febrile rash in a child should raise concern for meningococcemia?

**A:** Petechiae or purpura (especially trunk/lower extremities), rapidly progressive, ill/toxic appearance, fever, tachycardia, hypotension, altered mental status, neck stiffness. Petechiae below the nipple line in a febrile child has high specificity for meningococcemia.

Meningococcal disease can progress from well to critically ill within hours. Canadian guidelines recommend empiric ceftriaxone immediately if suspected, before any investigations. Chemoprophylaxis (ciprofloxacin or rifampin) for close contacts within 24 hours. Public health notification is mandatory in all provinces.

**Q:** When should you suspect urinary tract infection as the source of fever in young children?

**A:** Consider UTI in any febrile child <2 years without obvious source. Higher risk: female, uncircumcised male <6 months, previous UTI, vesicoureteral reflux, fever >=39C for >48 hours. Diagnosis requires catheter or suprapubic specimen for culture (bag specimens are unreliable for culture).

UTI is the most common SBI in febrile children. CPS and AAP guidelines recommend catheter urine in all febrile infants <2 months and in older febrile children without a source. Renal ultrasound is recommended after first febrile UTI. VCUG is reserved for recurrent UTI, abnormal US, or atypical organisms.

**Q:** What is the differential diagnosis for prolonged fever (>5 days) in a child?

**A:** Infectious: EBV, CMV, occult abscess, osteomyelitis, endocarditis, TB, cat scratch disease. Autoimmune/inflammatory: Kawasaki disease, juvenile idiopathic arthritis (systemic type), inflammatory bowel disease. Malignancy: leukemia, lymphoma, neuroblastoma. Other: drug fever, factitious fever.

Kawasaki disease must be considered in any child with fever >5 days, as delayed diagnosis increases coronary aneurysm risk. Systemic JIA presents with quotidian (daily spiking) fever pattern, salmon-coloured rash, and hepatosplenomegaly. CPS recommends systematic workup including blood smear to exclude malignancy.

**Q:** What is the Modified Glasgow Coma Score (GCS) for infants, and how does the verbal response differ from the adult GCS?

**A:** Eye opening and motor responses are similar to adult GCS. Verbal response for infants: 5 = coos/babbles, 4 = irritable cry, 3 = cries to pain, 2 = moans to pain, 1 = no response. For children <2 years/non-verbal: 5 = oriented, social, speaks, interacts; 4 = confused/disoriented/consolable; 3 = inappropriate words/not consolable; 2 = incomprehensible/agitated/restless; 1 = no response.

The modified GCS adapts verbal scoring for preverbal children by using developmentally appropriate behaviors. AVPU (Alert, Verbal, Pain, Unresponsive) has better inter-rater reliability and is preferred for initial mental status assessment in pediatric trauma patients.

**Q:** Compare croup, bacterial tracheitis, and epiglottitis: age, etiology, radiographic findings, and key distinguishing features.

**A:** Croup: age 0.5–4 yr, parainfluenza, steeple sign on X-ray, barky cough, low-grade fever, NOT toxic, usually no intubation needed. Bacterial tracheitis: age 5–10 yr, S. aureus/GAS, exudates in trachea on X-ray, barky cough, high fever, APPEARS TOXIC, requires intubation/ICU + IV antibiotics. Epiglottitis: age 2–8 yr, H. influenzae type b, thumb sign on X-ray, drooling, NO barky cough, high fever, APPEARS TOXIC, no oral exam, immediate anesthesia/ENT call required.

Epiglottitis is now rare due to Hib vaccination. Bacterial tracheitis is the most dangerous cause of croup-like illness and is unresponsive to epinephrine/steroids. The thumb sign (swollen epiglottis on lateral neck X-ray) is pathognomonic for epiglottitis. Never attempt to visualize the oropharynx in suspected epiglottitis outside the OR.

**Q:** What are the 4 D's of epiglottitis, and what is the immediate management?

**A:** 4 D's: Drooling, Dyspnea, Dysphagia, Dysphonia + tripod sitting. Management: do NOT examine oropharynx or agitate patient. Immediate anesthesia/ENT call — intubate in OR. Then IV fluids, antibiotics (for H. influenzae), and blood cultures.

Agitation in epiglottitis can cause complete airway obstruction. The safest airway management is controlled intubation in the OR by experienced providers. Heliox may temporize while preparing for definitive airway. Antibiotics target H. influenzae type b (now rare due to Hib vaccine) and cover S. aureus.

**Q:** What is the management of bacterial tracheitis in the ED?

**A:** Airway maintenance — usually requires intubation, ENT consult, and ICU admission. Start broad-spectrum IV antibiotics: ceftriaxone + vancomycin, pending cultures and sensitivities.

Bacterial tracheitis (most commonly S. aureus including MRSA) causes pseudomembrane formation in the trachea, leading to severe respiratory distress unresponsive to epinephrine. MRSA coverage with vancomycin is essential. Rigid bronchoscopy may be needed to remove tracheal membranes.

**Q:** How is a febrile infant aged 28–90 days risk-stratified in the ED?

**A:** Use validated tools: PECARN, Step-by-Step, or Aronson rule (when procalcitonin is unavailable). High risk for invasive bacterial infection (IBI): full septic workup + antibiotics (ceftriaxone or cefotaxime ± acyclovir/vancomycin). Low risk, normal labs, well-appearing: can defer LP and antibiotics; admit for observation OR discharge with follow-up in 24–48 hours.

Risk stratification tools incorporate clinical appearance, inflammatory markers (WBC, CRP, procalcitonin), and urinalysis to identify infants at low risk for IBI who can be safely observed without lumbar puncture. This reduces unnecessary procedures while maintaining safety.

**Q:** What are the Rochester Criteria for identifying serious bacterial infection in febrile infants 28–90 days old?

**A:** Low risk (previously well): term gestation (≥37 weeks), no prior antibiotics or hospitalizations, no chronic/underlying illness. Normal physical exam: no skin, soft tissue, bone, joint, or ear infection. Normal labs: WBC 5000–15,000, bands <1500, urine <10 WBC/hpf.

The Rochester Criteria were developed to identify febrile infants who are at low risk for serious bacterial infections and may be safely managed with observation rather than full septic workup. They have been largely replaced by newer tools (PECARN, Step-by-Step) that incorporate biomarkers.

**Q:** What distinguishes a typical from an atypical febrile seizure?

**A:** Typical: duration <5 min, generalized type, occurs once in 24 hours. Atypical: duration >5 min, focal features, occurs >1 time in 24 hours. Febrile seizures occur in children 6 months to 6 years with fever or recent fever, normal neurological exam afterward, no evidence of intracranial infection.

Typical febrile seizures carry an excellent prognosis and require only fever treatment and identification of the fever source. Atypical febrile seizures warrant further investigation (EEG, neuroimaging) and closer follow-up as they carry higher risk of epilepsy. LP should be considered if meningitis cannot be excluded.

**Q:** What are the physical exam findings and injury patterns associated with child abuse (non-accidental trauma)?

**A:** Head injuries (HI): torn frenulum, bilateral black eyes, traumatic hair loss, diffuse severe CNS injury, retinal hemorrhage. Shaken baby syndrome: diffuse brain injury, subdural/SAH, retinal hemorrhage, minimal external trauma, associated bony fractures. Skin: bruises/burns in shape of an object, glove/stocking distribution burns, bruises of various ages, bruises in protected areas. Bones: rib fractures without major trauma, femur fractures <1 yr, spiral fractures in non-ambulatory children, metaphyseal fractures in infants, multiple fractures of various ages, complex/multiple skull fractures.

Metaphyseal 'corner fractures' and posterior rib fractures in infants are highly specific for non-accidental trauma. Injuries inconsistent with developmental stage or caregiver explanation, multiple injuries at different healing stages, and patterned bruising are key red flags.

## Neonatal Conditions & Newborn Care

**Q:** A newborn has Apgar scores of 5 at 1 minute and 7 at 5 minutes. What does this mean and what interventions are needed?

**A:** Apgar 5 at 1 min = intermediate (interventions needed); 7 at 5 min = reassuring; interventions: dry, stimulate, position airway, suction if needed, supplemental O2

Apgar score: Appearance (color), Pulse, Grimace (reflex), Activity (tone), Respiration — scored 0-2 each (max 10). Score <7 at 5 minutes requires intervention and repeat scoring. Score <3 requires neonatal resuscitation. CPS NRP (Neonatal Resuscitation Program) guidelines.

**Q:** A term newborn requires resuscitation. After drying, stimulating, and positioning, HR is 60 bpm and infant is not breathing. What is the next step?

**A:** Positive pressure ventilation (PPV) with room air (21% O2 initially for term infants) using bag-valve-mask — if HR does not improve within 30 seconds, assess for proper mask seal and chest rise

CPS NRP 2021: initial resuscitation with air (21%) for term infants; titrate O2 up if HR not improving. Effective PPV: rate 40-60 breaths/min, look for chest rise, HR should increase within 30 seconds. Endotracheal intubation if PPV ineffective or prolonged.

**Q:** What is neonatal hypoglycemia and how is it defined and managed?

**A:** Neonatal hypoglycemia: blood glucose <2.6 mmol/L in term infants in first 48h; at-risk: IDM, SGA, LGA, preterm, post-asphyxia; treat with early feeding (BF or formula) or IV dextrose 10% (2 mL/kg bolus if symptomatic)

CPS neonatal glucose guidelines: all at-risk newborns should have glucose screening in first 30-60 minutes. Asymptomatic hypoglycemia: trial of feeding first. Symptomatic or glucose <1.8 mmol/L: IV dextrose immediately. Persistent hypoglycemia (>48h): investigate for hyperinsulinism, pituitary, or metabolic causes.

**Q:** A term newborn has yellowing of skin at 24 hours of age. What is the concern?

**A:** Jaundice in first 24 hours is ALWAYS pathological — most likely hemolytic (ABO incompatibility, Rh incompatibility, G6PD deficiency); urgent bilirubin measurement and investigation

Physiological jaundice appears at 48-72h, peaks day 3-5, resolves by day 2 weeks (term). Pathological jaundice: onset <24h, rate >85 μmol/L/day, total bilirubin >200 μmol/L (or zone-specific threshold), direct hyperbilirubinemia, prolonged (>2 weeks term, >3 weeks preterm). Phototherapy or exchange transfusion depending on levels.

**Q:** What are the current Canadian recommendations for vitamin K and vitamin D supplementation in newborns?

**A:** Vitamin K 1 mg IM (or 2 mg PO x3) immediately after birth to prevent hemorrhagic disease; Vitamin D 800-1000 IU/day oral for exclusively breastfed infants (breast milk has insufficient vitamin D)

CPS recommends universal vitamin K prophylaxis for all newborns — hemorrhagic disease of the newborn (VKDB) is preventable. Vitamin D supplementation for breastfed infants: 800 IU/day (standard) or up to 2000 IU/day for infants in northern communities.

**Q:** A 30-week premature infant develops increasing oxygen requirement, grunting, and intercostal retractions at 2 hours of life. CXR shows diffuse ground-glass opacity. Diagnosis and treatment?

**A:** Respiratory distress syndrome (RDS) from surfactant deficiency; treat with exogenous surfactant (poractant alpha) + CPAP or mechanical ventilation; antenatal corticosteroids (betamethasone) given to mother to promote surfactant synthesis

RDS is the most common cause of respiratory distress in preterm infants. Severity proportional to prematurity. INSURE strategy (INtubation, SURfactant, Extubation to CPAP) reduces ventilation complications. CPS guidelines: antenatal steroids at 24-34 weeks gestation for anticipated preterm delivery.

**Q:** A term newborn born to a GBS-positive mother develops fever, poor feeding, and grunting at 18 hours of age. What is the diagnosis and management?

**A:** Early-onset neonatal sepsis (EOS) — most likely Group B Streptococcus; blood culture, CBC, CRP, CSF; empiric IV ampicillin + gentamicin; admit to NICU

SOGC GBS guidelines: universal screening at 35-37 weeks. Intrapartum antibiotic prophylaxis (penicillin G) reduces EOS by 80%. Despite prophylaxis, EOS can still occur. EOS symptoms: poor feeding, temperature instability, respiratory distress, lethargy within 72h of birth.

**Q:** What is necrotizing enterocolitis (NEC) and in which patients does it occur?

**A:** NEC: ischemic/inflammatory bowel necrosis in preterm infants; presents at 2-6 weeks of age with feeding intolerance, abdominal distension, bloody stool, and pneumatosis intestinalis on AXR

NEC is the most common GI emergency in preterm infants. Risk increases with lower gestational age. Pneumatosis intestinalis (gas in bowel wall) and portal venous gas are radiographic signs. Treatment: bowel rest, NG decompression, IV antibiotics, surgery for perforation. Breast milk is protective.

**Q:** What is the newborn screening program in Canada and what does it test for?

**A:** Varies by province; typically: PKU, congenital hypothyroidism (most common treatable cause of intellectual disability), galactosemia, MSUD, fatty acid oxidation disorders, hemoglobinopathies, CF, SMA — heel prick at 24-48h of age

CPS recommends universal newborn screening. Congenital hypothyroidism: TSH heel prick; treat with levothyroxine within 2 weeks to prevent intellectual disability. PKU: phenylalanine-restricted diet prevents ID. Early detection through newborn screening is cost-effective.

**Q:** A preterm infant at 28 weeks develops apnea and bradycardia. What is the management?

**A:** Apnea of prematurity: caffeine citrate (respiratory stimulant) IV/oral; monitor with pulse oximetry; CPAP for recurrent or severe apnea

Apnea of prematurity results from immaturity of respiratory control centers. CAFCA trial: caffeine reduces BPD, neurodevelopmental impairment, and reduces duration of mechanical ventilation. Apnea >20 seconds or associated with bradycardia/desaturation requires intervention.

**Q:** A term newborn has passed meconium in utero. At delivery, the baby is depressed with decreased tone. What are the key steps?

**A:** Vigorous newborn: routine care; Non-vigorous (poor tone, poor resp effort, HR <100): immediate laryngoscopy and suction of trachea if meconium seen, then PPV if needed

Meconium-stained amniotic fluid (MSAF) occurs in 12% of deliveries. NRP 2021: routine suctioning of non-vigorous infants with MSAF recommended. Meconium aspiration syndrome: respiratory distress + patchy consolidation on CXR — may require surfactant, nitric oxide, or ECMO for severe cases.

**Q:** What is intraventricular hemorrhage (IVH) in premature infants and how is it graded?

**A:** IVH: bleeding from germinal matrix into ventricles; Grade I: germinal matrix only; Grade II: IVH without ventricular dilation; Grade III: IVH with ventricular dilation; Grade IV: periventricular hemorrhagic infarction — Grades III-IV associated with poor prognosis

IVH occurs in 25% of infants <28 weeks. Antenatal corticosteroids reduce IVH risk. Head ultrasound in all infants <30 weeks at 7 days and 4 weeks. Hydrocephalus may require VP shunt. Grade I-II: good prognosis; Grade III-IV: increased risk of CP and developmental delay.

**Q:** What is congenital hypothyroidism and how is it screened and treated?

**A:** Congenital hypothyroidism: TSH elevation on newborn screen (heel prick); treat immediately with levothyroxine 10-15 μg/kg/day — must start within 2 weeks to prevent irreversible intellectual disability

CH is the most common preventable cause of intellectual disability. Most cases (75%) are sporadic (thyroid agenesis or dysgenesis). Symptoms in newborn are often subtle: prolonged jaundice, large fontanelle, poor feeding, constipation, hypotonia. Levothyroxine replacement is life-long.

**Q:** What are the key differences in fluid requirements in term vs. preterm neonates?

**A:** Term newborn: start 60-80 mL/kg/day D10W; Preterm (<32 weeks): 80-100+ mL/kg/day due to increased insensible losses through immature skin; adjust based on weight, electrolytes, and urine output

Preterm infants have immature renal concentrating ability and high insensible water losses (especially <28 weeks). Monitor serum sodium daily (goal 135-145 mEq/L). Early weight loss of 5-15% is normal. Hypernatremia = insufficient fluids; hyponatremia = excess free water.

**Q:** What is the management of a newborn with suspected congenital heart disease presenting with cyanosis at birth?

**A:** Hyperoxia test (100% O2 for 10 min): PaO2 remains <150 mmHg = cardiac cyanosis; management: prostaglandin E1 (PGE1/alprostadil) to maintain ductal patency until definitive diagnosis and repair
