Prostaglandin E1 maintains ductus arteriosus patency in duct-dependent CHD (TGA, HLHS, critical pulmonary stenosis, coarctation). CXR, ECG, and echocardiography establish diagnosis. Tetralogy of Fallot: boot-shaped heart, decreased pulmonary vascular markings. TGA: egg-on-a-string.

**Q:** What is the APGAR scoring system?

**A:** Appearance(color), Pulse, Grimace(reflex), Activity(tone), Respiration. Each 0-2. Assessed at 1 and 5 minutes. <7 at 5min requires intervention

Scores: 7-10 normal, 4-6 moderately depressed, 0-3 severely depressed. Does not predict long-term neurologic outcome. Resuscitation should not wait for APGAR.

**Q:** What is the approach to neonatal jaundice?

**A:** Physiologic: onset day 2-3, peaks day 3-5, resolves by 2 weeks. Pathologic: <24h onset, rapid rise (>85umol/L/day), conjugated fraction >20%. Plot total bilirubin on Bhutani nomogram

Treatment: phototherapy based on gestational age and risk factors. Exchange transfusion if bilirubin reaches critical threshold. Causes: ABO incompatibility, G6PD, breastfeeding, Crigler-Najjar.

**Q:** What are the components of the APGAR score and the scoring intervals?

**A:** A: Appearance (0=blue, 1=acrocyanosis, 2=pink). P: Pulse (0=absent, 1=<100, 2=>=100). G: Grimace/reflex (0=none, 1=grimace, 2=cough/sneeze). A: Activity/tone (0=limp, 1=some flexion, 2=active). R: Respirations (0=absent, 1=weak cry, 2=strong cry). Scored at 1 and 5 minutes (continue q5min if <7). Max score 10.

APGAR score guides neonatal resuscitation decisions but does NOT predict long-term outcomes. Score 7-10 is normal. <3 at 5 minutes is concerning. Neonatal resuscitation should not be delayed for APGAR scoring. Canadian NRP (Neonatal Resuscitation Program) certification is mandatory for delivery attendants.

**Q:** What is the approach to neonatal jaundice and when is it pathological?

**A:** Physiological: appears day 2-3, peaks day 4-5, resolves by day 14 (21 in preterm). Pathological: jaundice within first 24 hours, total serum bilirubin (TSB) >95th percentile on Bhutani nomogram, rising >85 umol/L/day, conjugated bilirubin >34 umol/L, jaundice >2 weeks. Management: phototherapy (blue-green light, 430-490nm) based on CPS hour-specific nomogram. Exchange transfusion for extreme hyperbilirubinemia.

CPS guidelines provide hour-specific phototherapy and exchange transfusion thresholds. Risk factors for severe jaundice: ABO incompatibility, G6PD deficiency, prematurity, exclusive breastfeeding with poor intake, cephalohematoma. Universal bilirubin screening (transcutaneous) before discharge is recommended in Canada.

**Q:** What are the causes of neonatal jaundice by timing of onset?

**A:** <24h: hemolytic (ABO/Rh incompatibility, G6PD deficiency, spherocytosis). Day 2-5: physiological, breastfeeding jaundice (inadequate intake), infection/sepsis. >2 weeks: breast milk jaundice (unconjugated, benign), biliary atresia (conjugated-pale stools, dark urine), hypothyroidism.

Jaundice in the first 24 hours is always pathological and requires urgent evaluation. Conjugated hyperbilirubinemia (direct >34 umol/L or >20% of total) is always pathological and suggests biliary atresia, which requires Kasai portoenterostomy before 60 days of age. CPS recommends urgent referral.

**Q:** What is the Neonatal Resuscitation Program (NRP) algorithm?

**A:** Initial steps (30 sec): warm, dry, stimulate, position airway, clear secretions if needed. If HR <100 or gasping: PPV with 21% O2 (term) or 21-30% (preterm) at 40-60 breaths/min for 30 sec. If HR <60: intubate if not already, chest compressions 3:1 ratio with ventilation. If HR still <60: epinephrine 0.01-0.03 mg/kg IV/UVC.

NRP certification is standard in Canadian delivery rooms. Initial FiO2 is 21% for term (no longer 100%). Most neonates respond to effective ventilation alone. Canadian Paediatric Society updated NRP guidelines emphasize delayed cord clamping (>=30 seconds for vigorous term infants).

**Q:** What is the newborn metabolic screening panel in Canada?

**A:** Varies by province but typically includes: PKU, congenital hypothyroidism, galactosemia, maple syrup urine disease, medium-chain acyl-CoA dehydrogenase deficiency (MCADD), sickle cell disease, congenital adrenal hyperplasia, cystic fibrosis, biotinidase deficiency. Blood spot (Guthrie card) collected at 24-72 hours of life.

Ontario screens for ~30 conditions via the Newborn Screening Ontario program. Congenital hypothyroidism is the most common treatable condition detected (~1/3000). Early treatment of PKU with dietary restriction prevents intellectual disability. Parents should be informed about the screening program.

**Q:** What are the common congenital heart defects presenting in the neonatal period and their presentations?

**A:** Cyanotic (duct-dependent): TGA (cyanosis within hours, single loud S2), tetralogy of Fallot (cyanotic spells), TAPVR, truncus arteriosus, hypoplastic left heart. Acyanotic: VSD (murmur at 2-6 weeks), ASD, PDA (continuous murmur), coarctation (differential BP upper/lower extremities, weak femoral pulses).

Duct-dependent lesions require prostaglandin E1 (PGE1) infusion to maintain PDA. Critical congenital heart disease screening with pulse oximetry is recommended for all newborns in Canada (pre- and post-ductal SpO2 at 24-48h). Echocardiography is the diagnostic standard.

**Q:** What is the management of neonatal hypoglycemia?

**A:** Definition: plasma glucose <2.6 mmol/L in symptomatic neonate. Risk factors: SGA, LGA, IDM, prematurity, perinatal stress. Asymptomatic at-risk: feed within 1h, check glucose at 2h, target >2.6 mmol/L. Symptomatic (jitteriness, seizures, lethargy): IV dextrose bolus (D10W 2mL/kg = 200mg/kg) followed by D10W infusion at 5-8 mg/kg/min.

CPS screening guidelines target at-risk newborns (IDM, SGA, LGA, preterm). Persistent hypoglycemia (>48h) warrants investigation for hyperinsulinism, cortisol deficiency, or metabolic disease. Bedside glucose meters may underestimate true glucose; confirm with lab value if borderline.

**Q:** What are the components of the routine newborn examination?

**A:** General: weight, length, head circumference, gestational age assessment. Head: fontanelles, sutures, cephalohematoma, caput. Eyes: red reflex (retinoblastoma). Heart: murmurs, femoral pulses. Abdomen: organomegaly, umbilicus (2 arteries, 1 vein). Hips: Barlow (dislocatable) and Ortolani (reducible) maneuvers for DDH. Genitalia: testes descended. Spine: sacral dimple, tuft of hair. Reflexes: Moro, grasp, suck.

CPS guidelines recommend complete newborn exam within 24 hours. Red reflex testing detects retinoblastoma (most common childhood intraocular malignancy). DDH screening with Barlow/Ortolani maneuvers-risk factors: female, breech, family history. Ultrasound hip screening at 6 weeks if risk factors present.

**Q:** What are the causes and management of neonatal seizures?

**A:** Causes: hypoxic-ischemic encephalopathy (most common, 60%), intracranial hemorrhage, metabolic (hypoglycemia, hypocalcemia, hyponatremia), infections (meningitis, encephalitis, TORCH), developmental malformations, pyridoxine-dependent seizures. Management: treat underlying cause, phenobarbital (20mg/kg IV loading) is first-line AED.

Neonatal seizures are often subtle (lip smacking, eye deviation, cycling, apnea) and may require EEG for detection. HIE is the most common cause in term neonates. Therapeutic hypothermia (33.5°C for 72h) for moderate-severe HIE reduces mortality and disability. Canadian neonatal cooling protocols are standardized.

**Q:** What are the TORCH infections and their key neonatal manifestations?

**A:** T: Toxoplasmosis (intracranial calcifications, hydrocephalus, chorioretinitis). O: Other (syphilis, varicella, parvovirus). R: Rubella (cataracts, deafness, PDA, blueberry muffin rash). C: CMV (most common congenital infection; periventricular calcifications, microcephaly, hepatosplenomegaly, sensorineural hearing loss). H: Herpes (vesicular lesions, encephalitis, disseminated disease).

CMV is the leading infectious cause of sensorineural hearing loss and neurodevelopmental disability. Congenital CMV testing (urine or saliva PCR within 21 days of birth) is recommended for infants who fail newborn hearing screening. Treatment: IV ganciclovir or oral valganciclovir for 6 months for symptomatic disease.

**Q:** What are the types of respiratory distress in the newborn and their management?

**A:** Transient tachypnea (TTN): term/near-term, cesarean delivery, resolves 24-72h, supportive. Respiratory distress syndrome (RDS): preterm, surfactant deficiency, ground-glass CXR-surfactant replacement + CPAP. Meconium aspiration (MAS): post-term, meconium-stained fluid, patchy CXR-supportive ± surfactant. Pneumothorax: consider in sudden deterioration-needle decompression then chest tube.

Antenatal betamethasone (2 doses, 12mg IM 24h apart) for mothers 24-34 weeks gestation reduces RDS by 50%. CPS/NRP no longer recommend routine endotracheal suctioning for vigorous meconium-stained neonates. CPAP is first-line for preterm RDS with selective surfactant administration.

**Q:** What are the key aspects of prematurity management?

**A:** <28 weeks: extreme prematurity-RDS, IVH, NEC, BPD, ROP. 28-32 weeks: moderate prematurity-surfactant may be needed, less IVH risk. 32-37 weeks: late preterm-feeding difficulties, jaundice, temperature instability. Key interventions: antenatal steroids (24-34w), magnesium sulfate for neuroprotection (<32w), delayed cord clamping, kangaroo care, breast milk promotion.

Canada-wide perinatal networks (e.g., CNN-Canadian Neonatal Network) track outcomes. Antenatal magnesium sulfate before 32 weeks reduces cerebral palsy (NNT 63). ROP screening begins at 31 weeks postmenstrual age or 4 weeks chronological age. CPS provides follow-up guidelines for preterm infants.

**Q:** What are the causes and evaluation of neonatal cyanosis?

**A:** Central cyanosis DDx: cardiac (cyanotic CHD), pulmonary (RDS, pneumonia, PPHN, pneumothorax), neurological (CNS depression, apnea), hematological (methemoglobinemia). Hyperoxia test: give 100% O2 for 10 min-PaO2 >150mmHg suggests pulmonary cause; PaO2 remains <100mmHg suggests cardiac cause. Echocardiography is definitive for CHD.

The hyperoxia test helps differentiate cardiac from pulmonary causes of cyanosis. Critical congenital heart disease pulse oximetry screening in Canadian nurseries detects duct-dependent lesions before clinical deterioration. PGE1 infusion is a life-saving bridge to definitive management.

**Q:** What are the key features of necrotizing enterocolitis (NEC)?

**A:** Presentation: preterm infant with feeding intolerance, abdominal distension, bloody stools, temperature instability, apnea/bradycardia. X-ray: pneumatosis intestinalis (pathognomonic), portal venous gas, pneumoperitoneum (perforation). Modified Bell staging. Treatment: NPO, gastric decompression, IV antibiotics (ampicillin + gentamicin + metronidazole), surgical consultation.

NEC is the most common GI surgical emergency in neonates with 20-30% mortality. Breast milk feeding is the strongest protective factor. Pneumoperitoneum indicates perforation requiring surgical intervention. Canadian neonatal units use standardized feeding advancement protocols to reduce NEC risk.

**Q:** What is the evaluation of a newborn with ambiguous genitalia?

**A:** EMERGENCY: risk of salt-wasting CAH crisis. Do NOT assign gender immediately. Urgent investigations: 17-hydroxyprogesterone (CAH is most common cause), karyotype, electrolytes (hyponatremia, hyperkalemia in salt-wasting CAH), pelvic ultrasound (mullerian structures), cortisol, testosterone, LH/FSH.

Congenital adrenal hyperplasia (21-hydroxylase deficiency) accounts for >90% of ambiguous genitalia. Salt-wasting crisis presents at 1-2 weeks with hyponatremia, hyperkalemia, and shock. CPS recommends multidisciplinary team (endocrine, urology, genetics, psychology) for gender assignment and management.

**Q:** What are the features of Down syndrome and associated conditions?

**A:** Trisomy 21: flat facies, epicanthal folds, single palmar crease, hypotonia, intellectual disability. Associated: AV canal defect (40%), duodenal atresia, Hirschsprung, ALL, hypothyroidism, atlantoaxial instability

Screening: first trimester (NT + serum markers), NIPT (cell-free DNA) most sensitive. Maternal age risk: 1/350 at age 35, 1/100 at age 40. Confirm with amniocentesis or CVS (karyotype).

**Q:** What is cystic fibrosis and how is it diagnosed?

**A:** Autosomal recessive, CFTR mutation (F508del most common). Diagnosis: newborn screening (IRT) + confirmatory sweat chloride >=60mmol/L. Features: recurrent lung infections, pancreatic insufficiency, meconium ileus, male infertility

Lung disease: Pseudomonas colonization, bronchiectasis. Management: airway clearance, inhaled DNase, hypertonic saline, CFTR modulators (ivacaftor/lumacaftor-elexacaftor-tezacaftor for eligible genotypes). Median survival now >50 years.

**Q:** What conditions are included in the standard Canadian newborn screening panel, and why is early detection important?

**A:** Core conditions include: PKU (phenylketonuria), congenital hypothyroidism, cystic fibrosis, galactosemia, sickle cell disease and other hemoglobinopathies, congenital adrenal hyperplasia, biotinidase deficiency, and MCAD deficiency (medium-chain acyl-CoA dehydrogenase). Expanded panels vary by province (Ontario screens for 29+ conditions). Early detection allows treatment before irreversible damage (e.g., dietary restriction for PKU, thyroid replacement for hypothyroidism).

Newborn screening is performed via heel prick blood spot at 24–48 hours of life. In Ontario, Newborn Screening Ontario manages the program. Additionally, critical congenital heart disease screening via pulse oximetry is now recommended by the CPS in all Canadian provinces to detect duct-dependent cardiac lesions before discharge.

**Q:** Describe the initial steps of the Neonatal Resuscitation Program (NRP) algorithm for a term newborn who is not breathing at birth.

**A:** 1) Warm, dry, stimulate, position airway (head in sniffing position), clear airway if needed. 2) If not breathing or HR <100 after 30 seconds: start positive pressure ventilation (PPV) with 21% O₂ (room air) at 40–60 breaths/min. 3) If HR <60 after 30 seconds of effective PPV: start chest compressions (3:1 ratio with ventilations). 4) If HR still <60: administer IV epinephrine (0.01–0.03 mg/kg via UVC). Reassess HR every 30 seconds.

The CPS and NRP guidelines emphasize that effective ventilation is the single most important step in neonatal resuscitation. Term infants should start with 21% O₂ (not 100%) per 2021 NRP guidelines. Intubation is indicated if PPV via mask is ineffective, for prolonged ventilation, or for specific situations (e.g., congenital diaphragmatic hernia).

**Q:** A newborn has thick meconium-stained amniotic fluid but is vigorous (crying, good tone). What is the current NRP recommendation for meconium management?

**A:** For a vigorous newborn (good respiratory effort, tone, and HR >100): proceed with routine initial steps (warm, dry, stimulate, clear airway as needed). Do NOT perform routine endotracheal suctioning. If the infant is not vigorous (apneic, limp, HR <100), initiate PPV and consider intubation and tracheal suctioning only if the airway is obstructed.

The 2021 NRP guidelines changed the approach to meconium: routine tracheal suctioning of non-vigorous neonates is no longer recommended. The emphasis is on initiating ventilation promptly. Evidence showed that routine suctioning did not improve outcomes and delayed resuscitation. The CPS aligns with these updated international recommendations.

**Q:** Describe how critical congenital heart disease (CCHD) screening is performed in newborns and what constitutes a positive screen.

**A:** Pulse oximetry screening is performed on the right hand (preductal) and either foot (postductal) at ≥24 hours of age. A positive (failed) screen: any SpO₂ <90%, or SpO₂ 90–94% in both extremities on 3 measurements each separated by 1 hour, or >3% difference between right hand and foot on 3 measurements. A positive screen requires urgent echocardiography.

CCHD screening detects 7 primary targets including hypoplastic left heart, transposition of the great arteries, and tetralogy of Fallot. The CPS supports universal pulse oximetry screening. False positives can occur with pulmonary hypertension or sepsis. The screen has ~75% sensitivity for critical CHD, complementing but not replacing clinical examination.

**Q:** List the classic features of Down syndrome (trisomy 21) and its major associated medical conditions.

**A:** Features: hypotonia, flat facies, upslanting palpebral fissures, epicanthal folds, single palmar crease (simian crease), Brushfield spots (iris), short neck with excess skin, sandal gap toes. Associated conditions: congenital heart disease (AV canal defect most characteristic, 40–50%), Hirschsprung disease, duodenal atresia, ALL (10–20x increased risk), hypothyroidism, atlantoaxial instability, early-onset Alzheimer disease.

Down syndrome occurs in approximately 1 in 700 live births and is the most common chromosomal cause of intellectual disability. The SOGC recommends offering prenatal screening to all pregnant women. The CPS has published specific health supervision guidelines for children with Down syndrome including echocardiogram, thyroid screening, and audiologic assessment.

**Q:** A 16-year-old girl presents with short stature, primary amenorrhea, shield chest, and widely spaced nipples. Cardiac exam reveals a systolic murmur in the left infraclavicular area. What is the most likely diagnosis, karyotype, and expected cardiac finding?

**A:** Turner syndrome (45,X monosomy). Classic features: short stature (most consistent finding), primary amenorrhea with streak ovaries, shield chest, widely spaced nipples, webbed neck, lymphedema (especially in neonates), low posterior hairline. Cardiac: bicuspid aortic valve (most common, 15–30%) and coarctation of the aorta (7–12%). The murmur described suggests coarctation.

Turner syndrome affects approximately 1 in 2500 female births. Management includes growth hormone therapy for short stature (Health Canada approved) and estrogen replacement at age 12–13 for puberty induction. Lifelong cardiac surveillance is essential due to risk of aortic dilation and dissection. Fertility may be possible via oocyte donation.

**Q:** A tall 20-year-old male presents with gynecomastia, small firm testes, and infertility. Labs show elevated FSH and LH with low testosterone. What is the diagnosis and karyotype?

**A:** Klinefelter syndrome (47,XXY). Features: tall stature with long limbs (eunuchoid proportions), small firm testes (<4 mL), gynecomastia, sparse body hair, infertility (azoospermia), learning difficulties (especially language-based). Labs: hypergonadotropic hypogonadism (elevated FSH/LH, low testosterone).

Klinefelter syndrome is the most common sex chromosome aneuploidy in males, affecting approximately 1 in 600 male births. Many are diagnosed late or not at all. Treatment includes testosterone replacement starting at puberty for virilization, bone density, and psychosocial well-being. Fertility may be possible with testicular sperm extraction and ICSI in some cases.

**Q:** A boy with intellectual disability, long face, prominent ears, and macroorchidism after puberty is being evaluated. What is the most likely diagnosis and the underlying genetic mechanism?

**A:** Fragile X syndrome, caused by CGG trinucleotide repeat expansion (>200 repeats = full mutation) in the FMR1 gene on the X chromosome, leading to silencing of FMRP protein. It is the most common inherited cause of intellectual disability.

Fragile X follows X-linked inheritance with variable penetrance. Female carriers (premutation: 55–200 repeats) may have mild features or be unaffected but are at risk for premature ovarian insufficiency and fragile X-associated tremor/ataxia syndrome (FXTAS). Genetic counselling is essential as the repeat can expand across generations (anticipation).

**Q:** Describe the pathophysiology, diagnosis, and multisystem management of cystic fibrosis.

**A:** Pathophysiology: autosomal recessive mutation in CFTR gene (most common: F508del) causing defective chloride channel → thick, dehydrated secretions in lungs, pancreas, GI tract, and reproductive system. Diagnosis: newborn screening (IRT), confirmed by sweat chloride test (≥60 mmol/L diagnostic, 30–59 intermediate) or genetic testing. Management: airway clearance (chest physiotherapy, hypertonic saline, dornase alfa), inhaled antibiotics (tobramycin for Pseudomonas), pancreatic enzyme replacement (PERT), fat-soluble vitamin supplementation (ADEK), CFTR modulators (ivacaftor, elexacaftor/tezacaftor/ivacaftor = Trikafta).

CF is the most common life-limiting autosomal recessive disorder in Canadians of European descent (~1 in 3600 births). Trikafta has been a transformative therapy, approved by Health Canada, and is effective in ~90% of CF patients with at least one F508del allele. Cystic Fibrosis Canada supports comprehensive multidisciplinary care at accredited CF centres.

**Q:** Describe the pathophysiology of sickle cell disease, types of crises, and key management strategies.

**A:** Pathophysiology: point mutation in β-globin gene (Glu→Val at position 6) → HbS polymerizes under low O₂ → sickled RBCs causing vaso-occlusion and hemolysis. Crises: 1) Vaso-occlusive (pain crisis): most common, 2) Splenic sequestration: acute splenic enlargement + anemia, 3) Aplastic crisis: parvovirus B19 → reticulocytopenia, 4) Hemolytic crisis. Management: hydroxyurea (increases HbF), penicillin prophylaxis from 2 months to 5 years (functional asplenia), pneumococcal vaccination, folic acid, transfusions for acute chest syndrome/stroke, pain management.

Newborn screening for sickle cell disease is standard across Canadian provinces via hemoglobin electrophoresis. Acute chest syndrome (fever, chest pain, new infiltrate) is the leading cause of death. The CPS recommends comprehensive care including transcranial Doppler screening from age 2 to identify stroke risk, with chronic transfusion therapy for abnormal results.

**Q:** What is phenylketonuria (PKU), and why is newborn screening essential?

**A:** PKU is an autosomal recessive disorder caused by deficiency of phenylalanine hydroxylase, leading to accumulation of phenylalanine. Without treatment, it causes severe intellectual disability, seizures, musty body odor, eczema, and fair skin/hair (decreased melanin). Newborn screening detects elevated phenylalanine on day 1–2 heel prick. Treatment: lifelong phenylalanine-restricted diet, medical formula supplementation, and monitoring of blood phenylalanine levels. Newer therapy: sapropterin (BH4) for responsive patients.

PKU was the first condition added to newborn screening programs and demonstrates the principle that early detection prevents irreversible disease. Treatment must begin within the first weeks of life. Maternal PKU (uncontrolled phenylalanine during pregnancy) causes congenital heart defects, microcephaly, and intellectual disability in the fetus even if the fetus does not have PKU.

**Q:** A 3-day-old neonate presents with poor feeding, lethargy, vomiting, and metabolic acidosis with an elevated anion gap. What is the differential diagnosis and initial workup for suspected inborn error of metabolism?

**A:** Differential: organic acidemias (propionic, methylmalonic acidemia), urea cycle defects (hyperammonemia without acidosis), maple syrup urine disease, galactosemia, fatty acid oxidation defects. Initial workup: blood gas, electrolytes (anion gap), ammonia, lactate, blood glucose, urine ketones, urine organic acids, plasma amino acids, acylcarnitine profile, liver function tests.

Inborn errors of metabolism should be considered in any acutely ill neonate without an obvious infectious cause. The CPS emphasizes the importance of sending metabolic samples BEFORE starting treatment, as results guide specific therapy. Urgent management includes stopping protein feeds, providing high glucose infusion (10% dextrose), and correcting acidosis while awaiting results.

**Q:** A neonate with suspected urea cycle defect has an ammonia level of 800 μmol/L. What is the acute management?

**A:** Emergency management: 1) Stop all protein intake, 2) High-calorie IV glucose (D10W at 1.5× maintenance) to prevent catabolism, 3) Nitrogen scavenger therapy: sodium benzoate and sodium phenylacetate (IV Ammonul), 4) Arginine supplementation (except in arginase deficiency), 5) If ammonia >500 or rising despite medical therapy: hemodialysis (more effective than peritoneal dialysis or CVVH for ammonia removal), 6) Consult metabolic genetics urgently.

Hyperammonemia is a medical emergency as ammonia is directly neurotoxic. The duration and peak of hyperammonemia correlate with neurological outcome. In Canada, metabolic genetics centres (e.g., SickKids, CHU Sainte-Justine, Alberta Children's Hospital) provide 24/7 consultation. Urea cycle defects are X-linked (OTC deficiency, most common) or autosomal recessive.

**Q:** Explain the inheritance patterns and recurrence risks for autosomal dominant, autosomal recessive, and X-linked recessive conditions.

**A:** Autosomal dominant: 50% risk each pregnancy if one parent affected, male and female equally affected, vertical transmission. Autosomal recessive: 25% affected, 50% carrier, 25% unaffected each pregnancy if both parents are carriers; often consanguinity or same ethnic background. X-linked recessive: carrier mother has 50% chance each son is affected, 50% chance each daughter is a carrier; no male-to-male transmission; affected father transmits carrier status to all daughters, no sons affected.

Understanding inheritance patterns is fundamental to genetic counselling. In Canada, genetic counsellors are certified by the Canadian Association of Genetic Counsellors (CAGC). Key concepts to remember: de novo mutations can cause autosomal dominant conditions without family history, and germline mosaicism means recurrence risk is higher than expected even with apparently de novo mutations.

**Q:** Two carrier parents for an autosomal recessive condition wish to know the probability that their next THREE children will all be unaffected.

**A:** Each child has a 3/4 (75%) chance of being unaffected. For three independent pregnancies: (3/4)³ = 27/64 ≈ 42.2% probability that all three will be unaffected.

This applies the multiplication rule for independent events. Each pregnancy is an independent event with the same 1/4 risk. Genetic counselling should present risks in multiple formats (fractions, percentages, natural frequencies) as families process numerical information differently. Preimplantation genetic testing (PGT) and prenatal diagnosis offer options for carrier couples in Canada.

**Q:** What is the significance of trinucleotide repeat expansion disorders? Give three examples with their associated repeats.

**A:** Trinucleotide repeat disorders involve unstable DNA repeat sequences that expand across generations (anticipation = earlier onset and increasing severity). Examples: 1) Fragile X (CGG repeat in FMR1), 2) Huntington disease (CAG repeat in HTT — autosomal dominant), 3) Myotonic dystrophy type 1 (CTG repeat in DMPK — autosomal dominant, congenital form with maternal transmission).

Anticipation is a hallmark of trinucleotide repeat disorders. The number of repeats correlates with disease severity and age of onset. Premutation carriers may have intermediate repeat lengths with milder or different phenotypes (e.g., FXTAS in Fragile X premutation carriers). Genetic testing and counselling are essential for family planning in affected families.

**Q:** A couple has a child with Down syndrome (trisomy 21). The mother's karyotype reveals a Robertsonian translocation t(14;21). What is the recurrence risk?

**A:** If the mother carries the Robertsonian translocation t(14;21): theoretical risk is 1/3 affected, but empiric recurrence risk is approximately 10–15% for a female carrier and ~2–3% for a male carrier. If the translocation is t(21;21) (same chromosome): 100% recurrence risk (all viable offspring will have trisomy 21). Prenatal diagnosis should be offered in subsequent pregnancies.

Robertsonian translocations account for approximately 4% of Down syndrome cases. Unlike standard trisomy 21 (which increases with maternal age), translocation Down syndrome can occur at any maternal age and has a significant recurrence risk. Parental karyotyping is recommended for all children with Down syndrome to guide genetic counselling.

**Q:** Describe the features of congenital adrenal hyperplasia (CAH) due to 21-hydroxylase deficiency and why it is included in newborn screening.

**A:** Classic CAH: 1) Salt-wasting form (most severe): presents in first 1–2 weeks of life with adrenal crisis (hyponatremia, hyperkalemia, dehydration, shock), ambiguous genitalia in females (virilization from excess androgens). 2) Simple virilizing form: virilization without salt-wasting. Diagnosis: elevated 17-hydroxyprogesterone on newborn screen. Treatment: hydrocortisone + fludrocortisone (salt-wasting form).

CAH is the most common cause of ambiguous genitalia in 46,XX newborns. It is included in newborn screening because salt-wasting crisis can be fatal if unrecognized. In Canada, 17-OHP is measured on the newborn screening blood spot. The incidence is approximately 1 in 15,000 births, with higher rates in some populations (Yupik Eskimo, La Réunion).

**Q:** A mother with myotonic dystrophy gives birth to a floppy infant with respiratory distress. What condition should be suspected and why is maternal transmission important?

**A:** Congenital myotonic dystrophy. The infant presents with severe hypotonia, respiratory failure, feeding difficulties, facial diplegia, and talipes. Congenital myotonic dystrophy almost exclusively occurs through maternal transmission because the CTG repeat expansion preferentially increases during female meiosis (anticipation). The mother may have only mild symptoms.

Myotonic dystrophy type 1 is an autosomal dominant condition, but the severe congenital form only occurs with very large CTG expansions (>1000 repeats) transmitted maternally. Neonatal mortality is significant (up to 25%), primarily from respiratory failure. Survivors have intellectual disability and progressive myotonia. Genetic counselling should address the risk of anticipation in future pregnancies.

**Q:** How does neonatal skin differ from adult skin at birth, and when does it mature?

**A:** Full-term neonates have all five epidermal layers (similar to adults) but have smaller corneocytes, a thinner stratum corneum, and coarser texture. The stratum corneum matures by ~2 years. Dermal thickness increases from infancy through puberty.

Neonatal skin differences are clinically important: thinner stratum corneum increases percutaneous drug absorption (risk of topical drug toxicity), and increased surface area-to-body weight ratio amplifies this risk. Facial skin matures faster than trunk/limb skin.

**Q:** What is a milium and who does it typically affect?

**A:** Milium: 1–2 mm superficial white-to-yellow subepidermal papules on eyelids, cheeks, forehead. Small epidermoid cyst. In newborns (40–50% of infants), resolves spontaneously in first 4 weeks.

Milia can also be secondary to blistering disorders, trauma, topical corticosteroids, or cosmetic procedures. Treatment when needed: incision and expression, electrodessication, or topical retinoids.

**Q:** What is dermal melanocytosis (historically 'Mongolian spot') and what is its clinical significance?

**A:** Dermal melanocytosis: congenital grey-blue macules on the lumbosacral area from ectopic dermal melanocytes. Occurs in 99% of Asian and Indigenous infants. Usually fades in early childhood. Must be distinguished from ecchymosis (bruising) to avoid false child abuse allegations.

Recognizing dermal melanocytosis is important to prevent misidentification as non-accidental injury. It is benign and usually resolves by age 4–5 years, but may persist into adulthood.

**Q:** How should a febrile infant <28 days old be managed in the ED?

**A:** Admit all febrile infants <28 days with temperature >38°C. Perform full septic workup: CBC and differential, CRP or procalcitonin, blood C&S, urinalysis + urine C&S, LP (based on risk stratification), ± stool C&S, ± CXR. Treat empirically with broad-spectrum IV antibiotics: ampicillin + ceftazidime or cefepime or cefotaxime (if available) or gentamicin. Add acyclovir or vancomycin when indicated.

Neonates have immature immune systems and cannot localize infection, making them at high risk for serious bacterial infections (SBI) and herpes simplex virus. The full septic workup is mandatory. Ampicillin provides coverage for Listeria and Group B Streptococcus, which are important neonatal pathogens not covered by cephalosporins alone.

## Pediatric Asthma & Respiratory

**Q:** A 6-year-old has recurring episodes of wheezing, cough, and shortness of breath that improve with salbutamol. Spirometry is not possible. How is pediatric asthma diagnosed?

**A:** Diagnosis based on: characteristic episodic symptoms + response to bronchodilator (≥12% FEV1 improvement) or positive bronchoprovocation test; trial of ICS can confirm diagnosis in children <5

CTS pediatric asthma guidelines: young children cannot reliably perform spirometry. For preschoolers (<5): clinical diagnosis based on recurrent wheeze, response to bronchodilator, exclusion of alternative diagnoses. ICS trial for 3 months; if response confirms asthma.

**Q:** What is the first-line controller therapy for persistent asthma in children?

**A:** Low-dose inhaled corticosteroids (ICS) — e.g., fluticasone 88-176 μg/day or budesonide 200-400 μg/day; add LABA for step-up if needed (age ≥5 years)

CTS and GINA pediatric guidelines: ICS is the cornerstone of asthma control in children. Montelukast (LTRA) is an alternative but less effective. LABAs should always be used with ICS in children — never as monotherapy. Long-term ICS at recommended doses does not significantly affect growth.

**Q:** What are the management steps for a moderate acute asthma exacerbation in a 10-year-old in the ED?

**A:** SpO2 >94%, speaking in phrases: salbutamol 4-8 puffs via spacer q20min x3, ipratropium bromide 4-8 puffs q20min x3 (first hour), oral prednisone 1-2 mg/kg/day (max 40 mg) x3-5 days, supplemental O2

CPS/CTS guidelines: salbutamol + ipratropium combination in first hour reduces hospitalization. Oral corticosteroids equivalent to IV. Magnesium sulfate IV 40 mg/kg (max 2g) for severe exacerbations. Reassess after initial bronchodilators with oxygen saturation and clinical score (PRAM).

**Q:** What is the PRAM (Pediatric Respiratory Assessment Measure) score?

**A:** PRAM: 0-12 scale assessing ScaleneO2 saturation, suprasternal retraction, scalene muscle activity, air entry, and wheeze; 0-3: mild, 4-7: moderate, 8-12: severe asthma exacerbation

CPS validated the PRAM score for Canadian pediatric ED management of asthma. Higher PRAM scores correlate with increased risk of hospitalization. Serial PRAM scores guide treatment decisions and discharge readiness. PRAM reliably reflects clinical response to bronchodilator therapy.

**Q:** How is asthma control assessed in children using GINA criteria?

**A:** Well-controlled: daytime symptoms ≤2x/week, no night symptoms, SABA use ≤2x/week, no activity limitation; Partially controlled: 1-2 of these not met; Uncontrolled: ≥3 not met

CTS Canadian Pediatric Asthma Consensus Guidelines: assessment at every visit using patient/caregiver report of daytime symptoms, nighttime symptoms, activity limitation, and rescue inhaler use. Spirometry and FeNO add objective assessment. Step up if uncontrolled x4 weeks.

**Q:** What trigger avoidance measures are recommended for asthmatic children?

**A:** Environmental tobacco smoke (most important — parental smoking), dust mites (mattress/pillow covers), pet dander (remove pet if sensitized), mold remediation, exercise warm-up, avoid cold air, treat allergic rhinitis

CTS guidelines emphasize environmental control as part of asthma management. Second-hand smoke exposure doubles asthma exacerbation risk in children. Specific allergen immunotherapy (allergy shots) considered for allergic asthma sensitized to a single allergen.

**Q:** What spacer/inhaler device should be used in children by age?

**A:** <4 years: MDI + spacer with face mask; 4-6 years: MDI + spacer with mouthpiece; >6 years: MDI + spacer (preferred) or DPI; nebulizers only for severe exacerbations or if spacer unavailable

CTS guidelines: spacers are as effective as nebulizers for asthma exacerbations in children when used correctly. All children should have an MDI + spacer for home use. Regular cleaning of spacer. Technique must be reviewed at every visit.

**Q:** What is the role of montelukast in pediatric asthma?

**A:** Montelukast (LTRA): second-line controller for mild persistent asthma; also useful for exercise-induced bronchoconstriction and allergic rhinitis comorbidity; less effective than ICS; Health Canada warns about neuropsychiatric side effects

CTS guidelines: LTRAs are alternatives to low-dose ICS for mild persistent asthma. Neuropsychiatric side effects (nightmares, behavioral changes, depression, suicidal ideation) require counseling and prompt reporting. Step-down to montelukast after ICS achieves control not recommended.

**Q:** What is bronchiolitis and how is it distinguished from asthma in infants?

**A:** Bronchiolitis: RSV-mediated viral lower respiratory tract infection in infants <24 months; first episode of wheeze + respiratory distress — NOT treated as asthma; supportive care only (O2, fluids)

CPS bronchiolitis guidelines: bronchodilators, corticosteroids, and antibiotics are NOT effective and should NOT be used for bronchiolitis. Diagnosis is clinical. Hospitalization for O2 requirement <90% or significant work of breathing. RSV monoclonal antibody (nirsevimab) now recommended for infants <6 months.

**Q:** What are the indications for referral to pediatric respirologist for asthma?

**A:** Uncontrolled asthma despite step 3 therapy (medium ICS + LABA), frequent exacerbations (≥2 steroid courses/year), ICU admission, uncertain diagnosis, suspicion of another etiology (GERD, laryngomalacia, foreign body, CF)

CTS guidelines recommend specialist referral for difficult asthma management. Comprehensive evaluation at a specialist level includes fractional exhaled nitric oxide (FeNO) measurement, bronchoprovocation testing, allergy testing, and investigation of asthma mimics.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic criteria for asthma in children <6 years vs >=6 years?

**A:** <6 years: clinical diagnosis based on pattern of symptoms (recurrent wheeze, cough, dyspnea triggered by viral infections, exercise, allergens), response to bronchodilators, and exclusion of alternatives. >=6 years: confirmed by spirometry showing reversible airflow obstruction (FEV1 increase >=12% post-bronchodilator or positive methacholine challenge).

CPS and Canadian Thoracic Society guidelines note that formal diagnosis with spirometry is difficult in children <6 years. The Asthma Predictive Index (API) helps predict persistence: major criteria (parental asthma, eczema) or minor criteria (allergic rhinitis, wheezing apart from colds, eosinophilia >=4%).

**Q:** What is the stepwise approach to asthma management in children?

**A:** Step 1: SABA as needed. Step 2: low-dose ICS (e.g., fluticasone 100mcg/day). Step 3: medium-dose ICS or low-dose ICS + LABA (>=6yr) or LTRA. Step 4: medium-dose ICS + LABA. Step 5: high-dose ICS + LABA, consider biologic (omalizumab). Before stepping up: verify technique, adherence, trigger avoidance, and diagnosis.

CTS Pediatric Asthma guidelines recommend ICS as cornerstone of controller therapy. LABA monotherapy is contraindicated in children. Montelukast (LTRA) is second-line to ICS. Step-down therapy attempted after 3 months of good control. Action plans should be provided to all families.

**Q:** How is acute pediatric asthma exacerbation classified and managed?

**A:** Mild: talks in sentences, SpO2 >94%. Moderate: talks in phrases, SpO2 90-94%. Severe: talks in words, SpO2 <90%, accessory muscles, HR >200 (<3yr) or >180 (4-5yr). Management: salbutamol MDI + spacer (or nebulized) q20min x 3, ipratropium for moderate-severe, systemic corticosteroids (prednisone/prednisolone 1-2mg/kg, max 50mg x 3-5 days). Add IV MgSO4 50mg/kg for severe refractory.

CPS and CTS guidelines emphasize MDI + spacer over nebulization for most children (equivalent efficacy, better drug delivery). Oral steroids given early reduce hospitalization (NNT 8). Chest X-ray is NOT routine-only if suspecting complication. Discharge with action plan and ICS if not already prescribed.

**Q:** What are the ICS dose equivalences in children and the side effects to monitor?

**A:** Low dose: fluticasone 100-200mcg/day, budesonide 200-400mcg/day, beclomethasone 200-400mcg/day. Medium: fluticasone 200-500mcg, budesonide 400-800mcg. High: fluticasone >500mcg, budesonide >800mcg. Side effects: oral thrush (use spacer, rinse mouth), growth suppression (~1cm in first year, catch-up later), adrenal suppression at high doses.

CTS guidelines recommend the lowest effective ICS dose. Growth monitoring is essential. ICS at low-moderate doses cause minimal growth effect (~0.5-1cm reduction in final adult height per CAMP trial). Benefits of asthma control far outweigh this risk. Always prescribe spacer with MDI.

**Q:** What is the approach to exercise-induced bronchospasm (EIB) in children?

**A:** Diagnosis: FEV1 drop >=10% post-exercise challenge. Prevention: SABA 15-20 minutes before exercise (most effective). Alternatives: daily LTRA, warm-up exercises. If EIB occurs despite pre-treatment SABA, indicates uncontrolled underlying asthma requiring controller therapy assessment.

EIB affects 40-90% of children with asthma. CTS guidelines recommend that children with asthma should be encouraged to exercise. Persistent EIB despite pre-treatment SABA should prompt reassessment of overall asthma control and controller therapy optimization.

**Q:** What are the differential diagnoses for wheezing in infants and young children?

**A:** Viral-induced wheeze (most common), bronchiolitis (RSV), asthma, foreign body aspiration (sudden onset, unilateral), tracheomalacia/bronchomalacia, cystic fibrosis, GERD, vascular ring, congenital heart disease, primary ciliary dyskinesia.

Not all that wheezes is asthma. CPS guidelines emphasize that wheezing in infants <12 months is most commonly bronchiolitis (RSV). Foreign body aspiration should be suspected with sudden onset and unilateral wheeze. CXR (inspiratory/expiratory) and bronchoscopy for suspected foreign body.

**Q:** What is the management of bronchiolitis?

**A:** Supportive care only: nasal suctioning, supplemental O2 if SpO2 <90%, hydration (oral or IV). Hypertonic saline nebulization may be used in admitted patients. NOT recommended: bronchodilators, corticosteroids, antibiotics, chest physiotherapy. Admit if: age <3 months, significant respiratory distress, SpO2 <90%, dehydration, apnea history, prematurity.

CPS bronchiolitis guidelines emphasize minimal intervention. Trial of bronchodilator is no longer routinely recommended. Palivizumab (RSV monoclonal antibody) for prophylaxis in high-risk infants (preterm <33 weeks, hemodynamically significant CHD, chronic lung disease) during RSV season.

**Q:** What are the indications for referral to a pediatric respirologist for asthma?

**A:** Uncontrolled asthma despite medium-dose ICS + LABA, frequent severe exacerbations, diagnostic uncertainty, age <2 years with recurrent wheeze, suspected comorbid conditions (VCD, allergic bronchopulmonary aspergillosis), consideration of biologic therapy, life-threatening exacerbation.

Canadian Thoracic Society recommends specialist referral when asthma is not controlled at Step 3 therapy. Biologic therapies (omalizumab for IgE-mediated, mepolizumab for eosinophilic) require specialist initiation. Most pediatric asthma can be managed in primary care with proper education.

**Q:** What is an asthma action plan and what should it include?

**A:** Written plan with zones: GREEN (well controlled)-continue maintenance therapy. YELLOW (worsening)-increase SABA frequency, double ICS dose or start prednisone 1mg/kg x 3-5 days. RED (emergency)-SABA q4h, start oral steroids, seek immediate medical attention if no improvement. Include triggers, medications with doses, and when to go to ED.

CPS and CTS mandate written action plans for all pediatric asthma patients. Studies show action plans reduce ED visits by 70% and hospitalizations by 40%. Plans should be reviewed at every visit. School copies should include permission for self-administration of SABA.

**Q:** What are the risk factors for fatal asthma in children?

**A:** Previous near-fatal exacerbation (intubation/ICU), frequent ED visits/hospitalizations, poor adherence to controller therapy, overuse of SABA (>=1 canister/month), food allergy (especially peanut), psychosocial factors (depression, family dysfunction), lack of written action plan, recent oral corticosteroid use.

These risk factors identify children needing intensive follow-up and asthma education. CTS recommends flagging high-risk patients in medical records. SABA overuse (dispensing >=3 canisters/year) is a marker of uncontrolled asthma and should trigger reassessment.

**Q:** Compare croup, epiglottitis, and bronchiolitis in terms of age, etiology, presentation, and management.

**A:** Croup: age 6mo–3yr, parainfluenza virus, barking cough + inspiratory stridor + hoarseness, steeple sign on X-ray; management: single dose dexamethasone (0.6 mg/kg) ± nebulized epinephrine if severe. Epiglottitis: age 2–7yr (rare now with Hib vaccine), H. influenzae type b (or other bacteria); toxic-appearing, drooling, tripod position, muffled voice, thumb sign on lateral neck X-ray; management: do NOT examine throat, secure airway in OR, IV antibiotics (ceftriaxone). Bronchiolitis: age <2yr (peak 2–6mo), RSV most common; wheezing, crackles, tachypnea, nasal flaring; management: supportive (O₂, suctioning, hydration), NO routine bronchodilators or steroids.

The CPS recommends dexamethasone for all children with croup regardless of severity, as it reduces return visits and hospitalizations. Epiglottitis has become rare since universal Hib vaccination in Canada. For bronchiolitis, the CPS 2014 practice point emphasizes supportive care only — evidence does not support salbutamol, epinephrine, or corticosteroids for routine bronchiolitis.

**Q:** A 2-year-old presents with a barking cough and stridor at rest. He is not in respiratory distress and is well-appearing. What is the severity classification and management?

**A:** Moderate croup (stridor at rest without significant distress). Management: single dose oral dexamethasone 0.6 mg/kg (max 10 mg). Observe for 2–4 hours. If stridor worsens or respiratory distress develops, administer nebulized epinephrine (0.5 mL/kg of 1:1000, max 5 mL) and observe for at least 2 hours post-epinephrine for rebound.

The CPS croup scoring uses stridor, retractions, air entry, and level of consciousness. Mild croup (stridor only with agitation) still warrants dexamethasone. Budesonide nebulization (2 mg) is an alternative if the child cannot take oral medication. Most children can be discharged after dexamethasone with return precautions.

**Q:** What is the current evidence-based management of bronchiolitis in infants, and which commonly used treatments are NOT recommended?

**A:** Evidence-based management: supplemental oxygen if SpO₂ <90% (CPS threshold), nasal suctioning, adequate hydration (NG or IV if needed), and monitoring. NOT recommended: salbutamol/albuterol, inhaled epinephrine (routine use), systemic corticosteroids, antibiotics (unless secondary bacterial infection), chest physiotherapy, and hypertonic saline (insufficient evidence for emergency department use).

The CPS and Canadian Bronchiolitis Epinephrine Steroid Trial (CanBEST) demonstrated that combined epinephrine + dexamethasone reduced admissions, but this has not been adopted as standard practice. Palivizumab (Synagis) prophylaxis is recommended for high-risk infants (premature, hemodynamically significant CHD, chronic lung disease) during RSV season.

**Q:** A child with croup develops severe respiratory distress with decreased air entry, cyanosis, and altered consciousness. What is the immediate management?

**A:** Severe/impending respiratory failure from croup. Immediate management: 1) Nebulized epinephrine (0.5 mL/kg of 1:1000, max 5 mL), 2) Dexamethasone 0.6 mg/kg IM/IV if unable to take orally, 3) Minimize agitation (keep child with caregiver), 4) High-flow oxygen, 5) Prepare for possible intubation (use ETT 0.5–1 size smaller than predicted). Call anesthesia/ENT for difficult airway management.

Severe croup with impending respiratory failure is a medical emergency affecting <1% of croup cases. The CPS emphasizes that nebulized epinephrine provides temporary relief (onset 10 minutes, lasts ~2 hours) and rebound can occur. Heliox (helium-oxygen mixture) may be used as a bridge in tertiary centres. These children require ICU admission.

**Q:** What are the components of a pediatric asthma action plan and how are zones defined?

**A:** Green zone (well-controlled): maintenance therapy, no rescue inhaler needed. Yellow zone (worsening): increase SABA use, may double ICS or add oral prednisone. Red zone (emergency): SABA q20min x3, oral prednisone, seek emergency care immediately. Action plan must include triggers, medications, and when to call 911.

Canadian Thoracic Society and Canadian Paediatric Society recommend written asthma action plans for all children with asthma. Studies show action plans reduce ED visits by 70%. Plans should be reviewed at every visit and updated with changes in therapy.

**Q:** What inhaler device is appropriate for each pediatric age group?

**A:** Age <4: pMDI + spacer with mask. Age 4-6: pMDI + spacer with mouthpiece. Age >6: pMDI + spacer (preferred), or DPI if adequate inspiratory flow. Nebulizers reserved for acute exacerbations or children unable to use spacer devices.

Canadian Paediatric Society emphasizes that pMDI + spacer is as effective as nebulizer for bronchodilator delivery in children. Incorrect technique is the most common cause of treatment failure. Technique should be demonstrated and reassessed at every visit.

**Q:** How is exercise-induced bronchoconstriction (EIB) diagnosed and managed in children?

**A:** Diagnosis: exercise challenge test showing >=10% fall in FEV1 post-exercise. Management: SABA 15 minutes pre-exercise (first-line), daily low-dose ICS if frequent symptoms, LTRA as alternative. Warm-up exercises reduce severity. Do not restrict physical activity.

EIB affects up to 90% of children with asthma and 10-15% without asthma. Canadian guidelines emphasize that physical activity should be encouraged, not restricted. Cold dry air worsens EIB. Swimming is generally better tolerated due to warm humid air.

**Q:** What are the preschool wheeze phenotypes and their clinical significance?

**A:** Episodic viral wheeze: only wheezes with viral URTIs, symptom-free between episodes, good prognosis. Multi-trigger wheeze: wheezes with viruses AND other triggers (exercise, allergens, cold air), more likely to persist as asthma. Atopic status, eczema, and family history help predict persistence.

The modified Asthma Predictive Index (API) helps identify preschoolers likely to develop persistent asthma: recurrent wheezing + 1 major (parental asthma, eczema) or 2 minor criteria (allergic rhinitis, wheezing apart from colds, eosinophilia >=4%). Canadian guidelines recommend ICS trial for multi-trigger wheeze.

**Q:** A 3-year-old has recurrent wheeze episodes only with viral infections and is symptom-free between episodes. Parents ask about daily controller therapy. What do you recommend?

**A:** For episodic viral wheeze with infrequent episodes, PRN SABA with spacer and mask is sufficient. If episodes are frequent (>= once monthly) or severe (requiring oral steroids or hospitalization), consider intermittent high-dose ICS at onset of viral symptoms or daily low-dose ICS.

Canadian Paediatric Society recommends against routine daily ICS for infrequent episodic viral wheeze in preschoolers, as most will outgrow symptoms. The INFANT study supports step-up approaches. Intermittent ICS at symptom onset is an evidence-based alternative to daily therapy for this phenotype.

**Q:** What is the stepwise approach to pediatric asthma management per Canadian guidelines?

**A:** Step 1: PRN SABA. Step 2: low-dose ICS daily (or LTRA if ICS refused). Step 3: medium-dose ICS or low-dose ICS + LABA (age >=6) or LTRA. Step 4: medium-dose ICS + LABA. Step 5: high-dose ICS + LABA, refer to specialist, consider biologics (omalizumab age >=6).

CTS guidelines mirror GINA stepwise approach adapted for Canadian practice. Control assessment uses the Childhood Asthma Control Test (C-ACT). Step down after 3 months of good control. ICS-formoterol as reliever therapy (SMART approach) is gaining acceptance in children >=12.

**Q:** What features in a wheezing child should prompt investigation for an alternative diagnosis other than asthma?

**A:** Neonatal onset, failure to thrive, digital clubbing, persistent wet/productive cough, focal lung findings, stridor, dysphagia, cardiac murmur, lack of response to bronchodilators, and fixed airflow obstruction on spirometry.

Differential diagnosis of recurrent wheeze includes foreign body aspiration, cystic fibrosis, tracheomalacia, vascular ring, primary ciliary dyskinesia, and congenital heart disease. Canadian Paediatric Society recommends chest X-ray and spirometry (when age-appropriate) before confirming asthma diagnosis.

**Q:** How is acute severe asthma exacerbation managed in a pediatric emergency department?

**A:** Salbutamol pMDI+spacer (or nebulizer) q20min x3, ipratropium bromide x3 doses in first hour, systemic corticosteroids (oral prednisone/prednisolone 1-2 mg/kg, max 50 mg) within first hour, oxygen to maintain SpO2 >=94%. If severe/life-threatening: continuous nebulized salbutamol, IV magnesium sulfate 50 mg/kg.

Canadian Paediatric Society guidelines emphasize early systemic steroids (within 1 hour) and ipratropium in the first hour for moderate-severe exacerbations. IV magnesium sulfate is reserved for impending respiratory failure. Aminophylline is rarely used. Reassess frequently using PRAM score.

**Q:** What is the PRAM score and how is it used in pediatric asthma?

**A:** Pediatric Respiratory Assessment Measure (PRAM): scores 0-12 based on suprasternal retractions, scalene muscle contraction, air entry, wheezing, and oxygen saturation. Mild (0-3), moderate (4-7), severe (8-12). Used to assess severity and guide treatment in the ED.

PRAM is validated for ages 2-17 and widely used in Canadian pediatric EDs. It provides objective, reproducible severity assessment and tracks response to treatment. A validated Canadian tool, it has been incorporated into national asthma guidelines and clinical pathways.

**Q:** When should spirometry be performed in a child suspected of having asthma?

**A:** Spirometry should be attempted in all children age >=6 with suspected asthma. Findings: FEV1/FVC <0.90 suggests obstruction in children, bronchodilator reversibility (>=12% improvement in FEV1 post-SABA). If spirometry normal but suspicion high, methacholine challenge or exercise challenge testing.

CTS guidelines emphasize objective lung function testing to confirm asthma diagnosis when possible. In children <6, diagnosis relies on clinical features and therapeutic trial. Serial peak flow monitoring at home can supplement spirometry. Normal spirometry does not exclude asthma.

**Q:** What is the management of moderate-to-severe croup?

**A:** Dexamethasone x 1 dose (for all severity levels). For moderate-to-severe croup: add nebulized or MDI epinephrine. Consider bacterial tracheitis or epiglottitis if unresponsive to croup therapy. Humidified O2 has no evidence for efficacy.

Dexamethasone reduces subglottic edema via anti-inflammatory effects. Epinephrine provides rapid but transient mucosal vasoconstriction. The Westley croup score is used to grade severity (mild, moderate, severe). Failure to respond to therapy should prompt reassessment of the diagnosis.

## Immunization Schedule & Vaccines

**Q:** What is the routine childhood immunization schedule for Ontario/Canada at 2 months?

**A:** 2 months: DTaP-IPV-Hib (diphtheria, tetanus, acellular pertussis, polio, Haemophilus influenzae b), pneumococcal conjugate (PCV13), meningococcal C (MenC-C), rotavirus (oral)

NACI (National Advisory Committee on Immunization) sets the Canadian immunization schedule. Provincial schedules vary slightly. All routine childhood vaccines are publicly funded. 2-month vaccines may cause fever and irritability — acetaminophen can be given.

**Q:** What is the MMR vaccine and when is it given?

**A:** MMR (measles-mumps-rubella) live attenuated vaccine: first dose at 12-15 months, second dose at 18 months or 4-6 years — 95% efficacy for measles prevention

NACI: 2 doses of MMR achieve >97% protection. MMR is contraindicated in immunocompromised patients and pregnancy. Post-vaccination measles-like illness may occur 7-12 days after vaccination. Herd immunity threshold for measles: 95% vaccination coverage.

**Q:** What is the HPV vaccine and who should receive it in Canada?

**A:** HPV vaccine (Gardasil 9 — 9-valent): 2 doses for ages 9-14 (≥6 months apart); 3 doses for ages 15-26 and immunocompromised; prevents 70-90% of cervical cancers, genital warts, oropharyngeal cancers

NACI recommends routine HPV vaccination for all adolescents at age 9-14, ideally before sexual debut. Gender-neutral programs implemented across Canada. Health Canada-approved up to age 45. HPV vaccination does not replace cervical cancer screening (Pap test).

**Q:** What vaccines are contraindicated in immunocompromised children?

**A:** Live attenuated vaccines are contraindicated: MMR, varicella (live), MMRV, BCG, oral typhoid, yellow fever, live oral rotavirus (relative), live influenza (FluMist) — inactivated vaccines are safe

CPS/NACI guidelines for immunocompromised children (cancer, HIV, immunosuppressive therapy): avoid live vaccines. Inactivated influenza vaccine recommended. Household contacts should receive inactivated flu vaccine. Varicella post-exposure prophylaxis with VZIG if exposed.

**Q:** What is the BCG vaccine and who receives it in Canada?

**A:** BCG (Bacillus Calmette-Guérin) TB vaccine: NOT routinely recommended in Canada; given at birth in high TB-prevalence countries; recommended in Canada only for infants in high-risk communities (First Nations, Inuit) with high TB rates

BCG is highly effective in preventing severe TB (meningitis, miliary TB) in infants but has variable efficacy against adult pulmonary TB. It causes false-positive TST reactions. NACI recommendation: selective use in high-risk groups; IGRA preferred for TB screening in BCG-vaccinated individuals.

**Q:** What is the recommended catch-up vaccination strategy for a 5-year-old who has never been vaccinated?

**A:** Catch-up with all age-appropriate vaccines according to provincial schedule; minimum intervals between doses must be respected; measles and varicella can be given at any age to unvaccinated children

NACI catch-up schedules allow completion of missed vaccines with minimum intervals. Start from the beginning if vaccine series not previously started. Accelerated schedule respects minimum intervals between doses. Meningococcal ACWY, HPV, and adult Tdap when age-appropriate.

**Q:** A parent refuses vaccines due to concerns about autism. How should this be addressed?

**A:** Acknowledge concerns respectfully; explain evidence disproving vaccine-autism link (Wakefield study retracted, multiple large studies); motivational interviewing approach; provide written resources; document discussion — do not dismiss or threaten

NACI and CPS strongly support routine immunization. The purported vaccine-autism link was based on a fraudulent, retracted study. Thiomersal has been removed from most childhood vaccines. Studies involving millions of children show no association between MMR and autism.

**Q:** What vaccines are recommended at the 12-month well-child visit?

**A:** 12 months: MMR, varicella, meningococcal C conjugate (MenC-C), PCV13 (4th dose); hepatitis A in provinces where included

Provincial immunization schedules vary. Most Canadian provinces have added hepatitis A vaccine to routine schedule. NACI recommends MenACWY for 12-month olds in provinces that use it as the primary MenC dose (replacing MenC-C). Review for any missed vaccines at each visit.

**Q:** What is the recommended influenza vaccine strategy for children in Canada?

**A:** Annual influenza vaccination for all children ≥6 months; children 6 months to 8 years receiving influenza vaccine for the first time need 2 doses (4 weeks apart) in the first season; LAIV (FluMist intranasal) preferred for 2-17 years if healthy (not appropriate for immunocompromised)

NACI recommends annual influenza vaccination for all Canadians starting at 6 months. LAIV has superior efficacy in children vs. inactivated vaccine. LAIV contraindicated in immunocompromised, severe asthma, salicylate therapy, pregnancy. Pharmacies can administer influenza vaccines in all provinces.

**Q:** What is vaccine-preventable disease reporting in Canada?

**A:** All vaccine-preventable diseases are nationally notifiable to PHAC; measles, mumps, rubella, pertussis, invasive Hib, invasive meningococcal/pneumococcal, polio, tetanus, and others — must be reported by physicians and laboratories

Notifiable disease reporting is provincially mandated. PHAC monitors national trends. Measles outbreaks: all cases require public health investigation and contact tracing. Imported measles from unvaccinated travelers drives most Canadian outbreaks. Vaccine-preventable disease outbreaks trigger catch-up campaigns.

**Q:** What is the Canadian immunization schedule for infants?

**A:** 2,4,6 months: DTaP-IPV-Hib-HB, PCV13, rotavirus. 12 months: MMR, varicella, PCV13, meningococcal C. 18 months: DTaP-IPV-Hib booster

NACI recommendations. HPV at grade 4-6 (age 9-13). Tdap booster in pregnancy (each pregnancy, 27-32 weeks). Annual influenza >=6 months.

**Q:** What is the Canadian publicly funded immunization schedule for infants (0-12 months)?

**A:** Birth: HepB (some provinces). 2mo: DTaP-IPV-Hib + PCV13 + rotavirus. 4mo: DTaP-IPV-Hib + PCV13 + rotavirus. 6mo: DTaP-IPV-Hib + (PCV13 in some provinces) + influenza (>=6mo annually). 12mo: MMR + varicella (or MMRV) + PCV13 + meningococcal C conjugate + HepB (if not given at birth).

The National Advisory Committee on Immunization (NACI) provides recommendations, but schedules are implemented provincially. The schedule varies slightly between provinces. DTaP-IPV-Hib is a combination vaccine reducing injection burden. Rotavirus is oral, not injectable.

**Q:** What are the live vaccines and why are they important to distinguish?

**A:** Live attenuated: MMR, varicella, MMRV, rotavirus, BCG, live attenuated influenza (LAIV/FluMist), oral polio (OPV-not used in Canada), yellow fever. Key implications: contraindicated in immunocompromised patients, must wait 28 days between two live vaccines (or give simultaneously), may need to delay after blood products.

NACI guidelines specify that live vaccines are contraindicated in severe immunodeficiency (HIV with CD4 <15%, chemotherapy, high-dose steroids >=2mg/kg/day x >=14 days). Household contacts of immunocompromised patients SHOULD receive live vaccines (except OPV). IPV is used exclusively in Canada.

**Q:** What are the true contraindications vs common misconceptions about vaccines?

**A:** True contraindications: anaphylaxis to previous dose or vaccine component, encephalopathy within 7 days of pertussis vaccine. NOT contraindications: mild illness with/without fever, current antibiotic therapy, mild local reaction to previous dose, prematurity, breastfeeding, family history of adverse events, egg allergy for MMR.

Vaccine hesitancy is a growing concern in Canada. NACI and CPS emphasize that missed opportunities due to false contraindications are more harmful than the theoretical risks. Egg allergy is not a contraindication for MMR (grown in fibroblast culture) or current influenza vaccines.

**Q:** What is the catch-up immunization approach for a child with unknown or incomplete vaccination history?

**A:** Assume unvaccinated unless documented proof exists. Start age-appropriate schedule from the beginning. Minimum intervals between doses (not maximum-no need to restart series). Simultaneous administration of multiple vaccines at different sites is safe and recommended. Serologic testing may be helpful for certain antigens (Hep B, measles, varicella).

NACI provides detailed catch-up schedules. This is common with immigrant/refugee children in Canada. Each province has a catch-up guide. Key principle: every visit is an opportunity to vaccinate. School immunization requirements vary by province (Ontario mandates documentation).

**Q:** What are the vaccines recommended for adolescents in Canada?

**A:** Grade 7-8 (age 12-13): HPV (2-dose series, Gardasil 9), Meningococcal ACWY conjugate, HepB (if not completed), Tdap booster. Annually: influenza. Additional: varicella (2nd dose if not received), catch-up of any missing childhood vaccines.

NACI recommends gender-neutral HPV vaccination (Gardasil 9 covers types 6,11,16,18,31,33,45,52,58). Two-dose schedule for ages 9-14, three-dose for >=15. Meningococcal B vaccine (Bexsero) is recommended by NACI but not publicly funded in all provinces.

**Q:** What are the special immunization considerations for preterm infants?

**A:** Immunize at chronological age (NOT corrected age) with full doses. All routine vaccines given on schedule. Additional: palivizumab for RSV prophylaxis in eligible preterm infants, annual influenza from 6 months. HepB at birth if mother HBsAg+. Monitor for apnea/bradycardia 48h after first vaccination in hospitalized preterm <31 weeks.

CPS and NACI emphasize that prematurity is NOT a reason to delay vaccination. Immune response may be slightly lower but is still protective. All routine vaccines including rotavirus should be given at 2 months chronological age even if still in NICU.

**Q:** What are the post-exposure prophylaxis protocols for key vaccine-preventable diseases?

**A:** Measles: MMR within 72h or Ig within 6 days for susceptible contacts. Varicella: vaccine within 5 days or VZIG for high-risk (immunocompromised, pregnant, newborns). Pertussis: azithromycin for close contacts regardless of immunization status. Hepatitis B: HBIG + vaccine within 12h for neonates of HBsAg+ mothers. Rabies: wound care + HRIG + vaccine series.

Post-exposure protocols are critical for public health in Canada. Measles outbreaks require rapid contact tracing. Healthcare workers with uncertain immunity should receive post-exposure vaccination. CPS and NACI provide detailed post-exposure algorithms for each pathogen.

**Q:** What adverse events following immunization (AEFI) should be reported?

**A:** Report: anaphylaxis, seizures, encephalopathy, intussusception (post-rotavirus), Guillain-Barre, thrombocytopenia, severe local reaction, any hospitalization or death temporally associated with vaccination. Reporting is through the Canadian Adverse Events Following Immunization Surveillance System (CAEFISS). Anaphylaxis rate: ~1/million doses.

CAEFISS collects national AEFI data. Healthcare providers are legally required to report AEFIs in most provinces. The National Vaccine Injury Compensation Program was established in Canada in 2021. Vaccine-associated anaphylaxis requires 15-minute observation post-vaccination.

**Q:** What is the meningococcal vaccination strategy in Canada?

**A:** Routine: Meningococcal C conjugate at 12 months, Meningococcal ACWY conjugate in adolescence. Meningococcal B (Bexsero, Trumenba): recommended by NACI for high-risk groups, funded in some provinces. High-risk requiring both MenACWY + MenB: asplenia, complement deficiency, outbreaks, travel to endemic areas.

N. meningitidis serogroups B and W are emerging concerns in Canada. MenB vaccine is not yet universally publicly funded despite NACI recommendation. Healthcare providers should discuss MenB with parents as it may require private purchase in some provinces.

**Q:** How should COVID-19 vaccination be approached in Canadian pediatric populations?

**A:** NACI recommendations evolve with evidence. Currently: mRNA vaccines recommended for children >=6 months. Primary series + boosters based on age and risk. Higher priority for high-risk children (immunocompromised, chronic conditions). Can be co-administered with other routine vaccines.

NACI updates COVID-19 pediatric vaccine recommendations as evidence evolves. Myocarditis/pericarditis is a rare adverse event more common in males 12-29 years after mRNA vaccines (especially after 2nd dose). Benefits exceed risks at the population level per NACI risk-benefit analysis.

**Q:** What are the PHE vaccine recommendations for adults in Canada per the periodic health exam table?

**A:** Tetanus booster every 10 years (if had primary series, Grade A). One Tdap booster for adults <65 (as Adacel® or Boostrix®, Grade A). Herpes zoster vaccine for adults ≥50 (Grade A). Varicella vaccine for susceptible adolescents/adults ages 1-12 and above (Grade A). Rubella vaccine for non-pregnant women of childbearing age without immunity (Grade B). Pneumococcal vaccine (Pneumovax®23) for all ≥65 or high-risk; add Prevnar®13 if >65 and high-risk (Grade A).

Immunizations are a critical preventive health measure in primary care. High-risk groups include immunocompromised patients, those >65, patients with COPD/asthma/CHF/asplenia/liver disease/renal failure/DM who require pneumococcal vaccination. Annual influenza vaccine is recommended for all adults.

**Q:** What vaccinations are required after splenectomy and when should they be given?

**A:** Pneumococcal, Haemophilus influenzae type b (Hib), and meningococcal vaccines, ideally 2 weeks pre- or postoperatively.

Post-splenectomy sepsis from encapsulated organisms (Streptococcus pneumoniae, H. influenzae, Neisseria meningitidis) occurs in 4% of splenectomized patients; risk is highest in children under 16. Children <6 years should also receive prophylactic penicillin.

**Q:** What is Gardasil 9's dosing schedule and how does the number of doses relate to age at vaccination?

**A:** Gardasil 9 dosing: 2-dose schedule for individuals who receive the first dose before age 15 (second dose given 6-12 months after first dose). 3-dose schedule (0, 2, 6 months) for those aged ≥15 years and for immunocompromised individuals regardless of age. Approved for both females and males ages 9-45. Contraindicated in pregnancy and breastfeeding (limited data).

The 2-dose schedule for younger adolescents is effective because the immune response to HPV vaccine is more robust in pre-adolescents compared to older individuals, achieving similar immunogenicity with fewer doses. Vaccination before sexual debut provides maximum protection but remains beneficial even in sexually active individuals as they are unlikely to be infected with all HPV strains simultaneously.

## Peds General

**Q:** What defines 'presumed cardiac' arrest in children and young adults?

**A:** Cardiac arrest without identifiable non-cardiac cause in young patients

Allan et al. questioned whether 'presumed cardiac' arrest in young patients is truly cardiac in origin, suggesting this may be a misnomer.

**Q:** When is a biopsy indicated for a lipoma?

**A:** Only if atypical features are present (painful, rapid growth, firm consistency)

Most lipomas are asymptomatic and can be observed. Biopsy or excision is warranted when features suggest malignancy, such as rapid growth, pain, or firm texture.

**Q:** In a prepubertal child with acne, what should you consider?

**A:** Underlying hormonal abnormality, such as late-onset congenital adrenal hyperplasia

Prepubertal acne, especially before age 7-8, warrants evaluation for androgen excess from adrenal or gonadal sources.

**Q:** When is biopsy indicated for lipoma?

**A:** Only if atypical features are present (painful, rapid growth, firm consistency)

**Q:** What is the management for nevus flammeus (port-wine stain)?

**A:** Laser treatment or cosmetic makeup. Lesions never spontaneously regress but grow proportionally with the child. May be associated with Sturge-Weber syndrome if in V1/V2 distribution.

Port-wine stains are congenital vascular malformations that persist and require active treatment for cosmesis and functional concerns.

**Q:** When is biopsy indicated for a suspected lipoma?

**A:** Biopsy is indicated only if atypical features are present: painful lipoma, rapid growth, or firm consistency.

Most lipomas are asymptomatic and require no investigation. Atypical features warrant biopsy to rule out liposarcoma.

**Q:** What hormonal abnormality should be considered in a prepubertal child with acne?

**A:** Late-onset congenital adrenal hyperplasia

Acne in prepubertal children (before age 8 in girls, before age 9 in boys) is abnormal and warrants investigation for androgen excess. Late-onset congenital adrenal hyperplasia is a key differential diagnosis.

**Q:** What is the management for lipoma?

**A:** Reassurance is typically sufficient. Excision or liposuction only if desired for cosmetic purposes. Biopsy/ultrasound only if atypical features (painful, rapid growth, firm).

Lipomas are benign and do not require removal unless symptomatic or for cosmetic reasons.

**Q:** What investigations are indicated for lipoma?

**A:** Biopsy only if atypical features (painful, rapid growth, firm)

Lipomas are typically diagnosed clinically. Biopsy or ultrasound is warranted only when atypical features raise concern for malignancy.

**Q:** What hormonal abnormality should be considered in prepubertal children with acne?

**A:** Late onset congenital adrenal hyperplasia

Prepubertal acne may indicate androgen excess from adrenal causes like congenital adrenal hyperplasia, warranting endocrine evaluation.

**Q:** When can children with head lice return to school?

**A:** After the first treatment with permethrin

Current guidelines permit children to return to school after the initial treatment, reflecting that lice are primarily spread through direct head-to-head contact rather than fomites.

**Q:** When may children return to school after head lice treatment?

**A:** Children are medically cleared to return to school after the first treatment with permethrin.

Current guidelines emphasize that exclusion from school is not necessary once treatment has been initiated, as transmission primarily occurs from living lice rather than nits.

**Q:** What is the treatment for head lice?

**A:** Permethrin 1% cream rinse (Nix), R&C or Kwellada-P shampoo. Comb hair with fine-toothed comb using dilute vinegar solution to remove nits. Repeat treatment 7 days after first treatment. Children may return to school after first treatment.

The 7-day repeat treatment is critical because permethrin is not fully ovicidal. Vinegar helps dissolve the nit cement.

**Q:** What are the 3 stages of the hair growth cycle?

**A:** Anagen (growth stage), Catagen (transitional stage), Telogen (resting stage)

Most scalp hairs are in anagen phase at any given time. The duration of anagen determines maximum hair length and varies by body site.

**Q:** What is the expected timeline for recovery from Telogen Effluvium?

**A:** Regrowth may begin in a few months, but meaningful regrowth can take 6-12 months

The slow recovery reflects the time needed for follicles to re-enter anagen and produce visible hair. Full recovery depends on resolution of the underlying cause.

**Q:** What is the key distinguishing feature between scarring and non-scarring alopecia?

**A:** In non-scarring alopecia, stem cells are intact so spontaneous or treatment-induced regrowth may be helpful; in scarring alopecia, permanent destruction of stem cells prevents regrowth

The stem cells reside in the bulge region of the hair follicle. Loss of this region (as in scarring alopecia) is irreversible.

**Q:** What hormonal changes during menopause contribute to perceived decreased scalp hair in women?

**A:** Reduced estrogen leads to decreased hair diameter, growth rate, and percentage of hairs in anagen phase; compounded by chronological age effects on hair density

Estrogen normally prolongs anagen and promotes hair growth. Postmenopausal estrogen decline unmasks androgenetic effects and reduces overall hair density.

**Q:** What are the three stages of the hair growth cycle?

**A:** 1. Anagen (growth stage) - stem cells at upper part of follicle, 2. Catagen (transitional stage) - bulge region, 3. Telogen (resting stage)

Hair grows cyclically through these three phases, with most scalp hairs normally in the anagen phase.

**Q:** What causes hair loss in postmenopausal women?

**A:** Reduced estrogen leads to decreased hair diameter, growth rate, and percentage of hairs in anagen phase; compounded by chronological aging effects on hair density

Estrogen normally prolongs anagen phase and promotes hair growth; its loss during menopause unmasks androgen effects and accelerates age-related thinning.

**Q:** Why cannot eyebrow, eyelash, and axillary hairs regrow after being lost?

**A:** These hairs have a short anagen (growth) phase relative to their telogen (resting) phase, meaning their hair follicles cannot regenerate in time to replace lost hair.

The anagen:telogen ratio differs by body site. Areas with short anagen phases cannot grow hair long and cannot regrow after cycling.

**Q:** What hormonal changes during menopause contribute to perceived decreased scalp hair density?

**A:** Reduced estrogen leads to decreased hair diameter, growth rate, and percentage of hairs in anagen phase. Combined with chronological aging effects on hair density.

Estrogen normally prolongs anagen and protects against androgen effects. Postmenopausal estrogen decline unmasks androgen sensitivity.

**Q:** What hormonal factors regulate hair growth?

**A:** Testosterone and dihydrotestosterone (DHT) regulate hair follicle growth response, which is genetically controlled

DHT causes miniaturization of hair follicles in androgenetic alopecia, particularly in genetically susceptible individuals.

**Q:** What hormonal changes during menopause affect hair?

**A:** Reduced estrogen leads to decreased hair diameter, growth rate, and percentage of hairs in anagen phase; combined with chronological aging effects, this causes perceived decreased scalp hair density

Estrogen normally prolongs the anagen phase and promotes hair thickness, so its decline affects hair quality and density.

**Q:** What is the hypogmented subtype of mycosis fungoides and who does it affect?

**A:** A variant of mycosis fungoides most commonly seen in children with deeper skin tones, presenting with hypopigmented patches rather than the typical erythematous lesions
