This subtype can be mistaken for vitiligo or tinea versicolor, leading to delayed diagnosis in darker-skinned individuals.

**Q:** What age restriction exists for permethrin in scabies treatment?

**A:** Do not use in children <2 years old

Permethrin 5% cream is applied once for scabies and repeated after 1 week. It is contraindicated in infants under 2 years and those with chrysanthemum sensitivity.

**Q:** What is the preferred topical treatment for scabies in children under 2 years?

**A:** Sulfur in petroleum (permethrin is not recommended in children <2 years)

While permethrin is first-line for scabies in older children and adults, it is contraindicated in children under 2 years, requiring alternative agents like precipitated sulfur.

**Q:** What is the treatment protocol for permethrin (Kwellada® P) in scabies?

**A:** Apply 5% cream to all skin areas from neck down, leave on for 8-10 hours (overnight), wash off; repeat application after 1 week; do not use in children <2 years

Permethrin is a pyrethroid insecticide that is first-line treatment for scabies and pediculosis. It has low toxicity and excellent efficacy with rapid resolution of symptoms.

**Q:** What are the symptoms of primary HSV-1 gingivostomatitis?

**A:** Entire buccal mucosa involved with erythema and edema of gingiva; often in children and young adults

Primary HSV-1 infection is usually symptomatic in young patients with high fever and lymphadenopathy.

**Q:** What is the compression-to-ventilation ratio for child CPR (1-8 years)?

**A:** 15 compressions to 2 ventilations

For children ages 1-8 years, the ratio is 15:2, differing from adults who use 30:2.

**Q:** What is the compression-to-ventilation ratio for child CPR (ages 1-8 years)?

**A:** 15:2 (15 compressions to 2 ventilations)

Children receive 15 compressions followed by 2 ventilations, reflecting their higher ventilatory needs compared to adults.

**Q:** What is the legal obligation when parents refuse life-saving treatment for a child?

**A:** Act in the best interest of the child while involving local child protection services and continuing to engage with parents

Healthcare providers must ensure the child's welfare while following legal protocols for child protection.

**Q:** What is telogen effluvium?

**A:** Uniform decrease in hair density due to premature shifting of hairs from anagen (growth) to telogen (resting) phase

Hair loss typically occurs 2-4 months after the precipitating event, with regrowth beginning in months but taking 6-12 months for meaningful recovery.

**Q:** What is the hair growth cycle and which phase are most scalp hairs in?

**A:** Three stages: anagen (growth), catagen (transitional), telogen (resting). Most scalp hairs are in anagen phase.

Anagen lasts 2-6 years for scalp hair, catagen 2-3 weeks, and telogen 2-4 months. The proportion in each phase determines hair density.

**Q:** What hormonal changes occur in menopause that affect hair?

**A:** Reduced estrogen leads to decreased hair diameter, growth rate, and percentage of hairs in anagen phase

Estrogen prolongs the anagen phase; its decline during menopause, combined with aging, leads to perceived decreased hair density in middle-aged women.

**Q:** What is the typical timeline for hair regrowth after telogen effluvium?

**A:** Regrowth may begin in a few months but meaningful regrowth takes 6-12 months

The delayed timeline reflects the need for follicles to cycle back through telogen and re-enter anagen phase sequentially.

**Q:** What is the duty to report in emergency medicine?

**A:** Legal obligation to report certain conditions: gunshot wounds, suspected child abuse, communicable diseases, medical unsuitability to drive, risk of harm to others

Duty to report laws vary by province/state but typically require reporting conditions that pose public safety risks.

**Q:** When can parents refuse treatment for their child based on religious beliefs?

**A:** They can refuse but physician must evaluate risk, ensure informed decision, and if life-saving treatment is refused, involve child protection services immediately

The child's best interest prevails when refusal would result in serious harm - CAS must be involved.

**Q:** What are the contraindications for permethrin in scabies treatment?

**A:** Children <2 years old and hypersensitivity to chrysanthemums

Permethrin is contraindicated in children under 2 years and in patients with known sensitivity to chrysanthemums or pyrethroids

**Q:** According to 2020 AHA CPR Guidelines, what is the compression-to-ventilation ratio for adults vs. children vs. infants?

**A:** Adults: 30:2, Children (1-8yr): 15:2, Infants (<1yr): 15:2

The 2020 AHA guidelines emphasize higher ventilation ratios for children and infants to provide more oxygen delivery during resuscitation.

**Q:** What are the compression landmarks for adults vs. children vs. infants?

**A:** Adults: lower half of sternum, Children: just below nipple line, Infants: just below nipple line

Correct hand placement ensures effective compressions while minimizing injury risk.

**Q:** What is the role of thyroid hormones in fetal growth and development?

**A:** Required for normal CNS development and growth in the fetus and child via stimulation of GH release in synergism with cortisol

Thyroid hormone deficiency during critical developmental periods can cause irreversible cognitive impairment.

**Q:** What are the three major hormones hypersecreted by pituitary adenomas?

**A:** Prolactin (PRL), Growth Hormone (GH), and Adrenocorticotropic Hormone (ACTH)

These are the most common functioning pituitary adenomas: PRL causes galactorrhea/hypogonadism, GH causes acromegaly/gigantism, and ACTH causes Cushing's syndrome.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of GH deficiency?

**A:** Short stature in children; adults have increased fat mass, decreased lean body mass, decreased BMD, fatigue

GH deficiency affects growth in children and metabolic functions in adults, including lipolysis, protein synthesis, and bone mineralization.

**Q:** What clinical features do GH-secreting pituitary adenomas cause?

**A:** Acromegaly in adults, gigantism in children

GH excess before epiphyseal plate closure causes gigantism (linear growth). In adults, excess GH causes acromegaly (enlarged hands/feet, facial changes).

**Q:** What is the role of thyroid hormones in fetal growth?

**A:** Required for normal growth in fetus and child, including CNS development, via stimulation of GH release in synergism with cortisol

Thyroid hormones are crucial for normal brain development and work with GH and cortisol to promote growth.

**Q:** What are the inhibitory effects of excess cortisol?

**A:** Inhibit bone formation (stimulate resorption), inhibit fibroblasts causing collagen loss, suppress inflammation and cell-mediated immunity, inhibit growth hormone and reproductive axes, increase PTH release and urine calcium excretion

These effects are typically only seen with cortisol excess (Cushing's syndrome).

**Q:** What are the clinical features of gigantism?

**A:** GH excess in children before epiphyseal closure - excessive linear growth

**Q:** What is adrenarche?

**A:** The growth of axillary and pubic hair, driven by adrenal androgens

**Q:** What are the treatment goals for testosterone replacement therapy in men with hypogonadism?

**A:** Improve libido, muscle mass, strength, body hair growth, and bone mass

Testosterone replacement aims to reverse the effects of testosterone deficiency on sexual function, body composition, and skeletal health.

**Q:** What is the goal of testosterone replacement therapy in men with hypogonadism?

**A:** To improve libido, muscle mass, strength, body hair growth, and bone mass

Testosterone replacement addresses the symptoms of androgen deficiency by supplementing endogenous testosterone production.

**Q:** What are the inhibitory effects of cortisol?

**A:** Inhibits bone formation, stimulates bone resorption; inhibits fibroblasts causing collagen loss; suppresses inflammation impairing cell-mediated immunity; inhibits growth hormone and reproductive axes

These inhibitory effects are more pronounced with cortisol excess (Cushing's syndrome).

**Q:** What are the inhibitory effects of glucocorticoids?

**A:** Inhibit bone formation, stimulate bone resorption; inhibit fibroblasts causing collagen loss; suppress inflammation impairing cell-mediated immunity; inhibit growth hormone and reproductive axes

These effects typically occur with cortisol excess (Cushing's syndrome).

**Q:** What are the goals of testosterone replacement therapy in men with hypogonadism?

**A:** Improve libido, muscle mass, strength, body hair growth, and bone mass

Testosterone replacement addresses the symptoms of androgen deficiency to improve quality of life and prevent complications of hypogonadism.

**Q:** What are the goals and main formulations of testosterone replacement therapy in men with hypogonadism?

**A:** Goals: improve libido, muscle mass, strength, body hair growth, and bone mass. Formulations: IM/SC injection, transdermal testosterone, or gel.

Testosterone replacement addresses symptoms of hypogonadism but does not treat infertility and may worsen it.

**Q:** What are the goals of testosterone replacement therapy?

**A:** Improve libido, muscle mass, strength, body hair growth, and bone mass

Testosterone replacement addresses the symptoms of hypogonadism by restoring physiological testosterone levels.

**Q:** What are the goals and methods of testosterone replacement therapy?

**A:** Goals: improve libido, muscle mass, strength, body hair growth, bone mass. Methods: IM/SC injection, transdermal testosterone or gel.

Testosterone replacement is the primary treatment for symptomatic hypogonadism, with multiple delivery routes available including injections and transdermal preparations.

**Q:** What are the rewards (benefits) of smoking cessation to discuss with patients?

**A:** Improved health, save money, food tastes better, good example for children

Personal benefits like financial savings and health improvements are strong motivators.

**Q:** What vaccinations are recommended for diabetes patients?

**A:** Influenza annually, pneumococcal vaccination

Diabetes patients are at increased risk for infections and should receive recommended vaccinations.

**Q:** What vaccines are recommended for diabetic patients?

**A:** Influenza annually and pneumococcal vaccination

Diabetic patients should receive annual influenza vaccination and pneumococcal vaccination.

**Q:** What is the accurate method to measure temperature in a child <2 years old?

**A:** Rectal temperature

Rectal temperature is required for accuracy in children under 2 years as other sites are unreliable.

**Q:** In children under 2 years, which temperature site is required for accurate fever measurement?

**A:** Rectal temperature

Young children require rectal temperature for accuracy as other sites (axillary, temporal) are unreliable in this age group.

**Q:** What is the recommended pneumococcal vaccination schedule for diabetics?

**A:** Pneumococcal vaccination is recommended

Diabetics should receive pneumococcal vaccination to prevent respiratory infections.

**Q:** What is the recommended influenza vaccination frequency for diabetics?

**A:** Annually

Diabetics should receive influenza vaccination annually to prevent respiratory complications.

**Q:** What is the gold standard site for temperature measurement in children <2 years?

**A:** Rectal temperature

Rectal provides most accurate core temperature in young children - other sites unreliable.

**Q:** When is tympanic membrane (TM) temperature accurate for children?

**A:** TM not accurate until child >5 years old

Younger children have narrow ear canals making TM measurement unreliable.

**Q:** How should temperature be measured in children <2 years old?

**A:** Rectal temperature is required for accuracy

Rectal temperature is the gold standard in young children because other sites (tympanic, oral, axillary) are unreliable in this age group.

**Q:** What annual investigations are recommended for diabetes patients?

**A:** Fasting lipid profile, random urine ACR and Cr, annual retinopathy screening, influenza vaccination

Annual monitoring for diabetes complications includes lipid testing, kidney function, eye exams, and vaccinations.

**Q:** In children under 2 years, which site is required for accurate temperature measurement?

**A:** Rectal temperature

Rectal temperature is the gold standard for children under 2 years as other sites (oral, axillary, tympanic) are not accurate in this age group.

**Q:** What should be done when an STI is detected in a child?

**A:** Evaluation for sexual abuse is mandatory

STIs in children require mandatory reporting and assessment for possible sexual abuse per child protection guidelines.

**Q:** What is the age range for Gardasil vaccination recommendation?

**A:** Females aged 9-27, and women 27+ if at risk of ongoing exposure

Gardasil protects against HPV types 6, 11, 16, 18 causing genital warts and cervical cancer.

**Q:** What is the recommended management approach when an STI is detected in a child?

**A:** Evaluation for sexual abuse is mandatory

STI in a child raises serious concerns for possible sexual abuse and requires mandatory reporting and assessment.

**Q:** What is the rectal temperature range defining fever in children?

**A:** 37.5°C - 38.3°C

Rectal temperature between 37.5°C and 38.3°C defines fever in children.

**Q:** How should temperature be measured in children <2 years of age?

**A:** Rectal temperature is required for accuracy

Fever in children under 2 years must be measured rectally as axillary and tympanic membrane measurements are inaccurate in this age group.

**Q:** Whattympanic membrane (TM) temperature is considered accurate?

**A:** Only accurate for children >5 years old

Tympanic membrane temperature measurement is not accurate until the child is greater than 5 years of age.

**Q:** When is evaluation for sexual abuse mandatory in children with STIs?

**A:** Always - when an STI is detected in a child, evaluation for sexual abuse is mandatory

This is a mandatory reporting requirement for healthcare providers.

**Q:** What rewards of quitting smoking should be discussed with patients?

**A:** Improved health, save money, food tastes better, good example for children

The 4th R (Rewards) in motivational interviewing helps patients identify personal benefits of quitting.

**A:** Annual influenza vaccination and pneumococcal vaccination

Diabetic patients should receive annual influenza vaccination and pneumococcal vaccination to prevent infections.

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment for uncomplicated cellulitis in children?

**A:** Cephalexin 50-100 mg/kg/day divided QID x 10-14 days

Pediatric dosing is weight-based. Second-line options include cloxacillin or clindamycin for penicillin-allergic patients.

**Q:** Who should receive HPV vaccination (Gardasil)?

**A:** Females aged 9-27, and women 27+ if at risk of ongoing exposure

Gardasil is recommended for females up to age 27 and older women at ongoing risk, protecting against HPV-related disease.

**Q:** What is the appropriate action when an STI is detected in a child?

**A:** Evaluation for sexual abuse is mandatory

In cases of STI in children, mandatory reporting and evaluation for abuse is required by law.

**Q:** What is first-line treatment for cellulitis in children?

**A:** Cephalexin 50-100mg/kg/day divided QID x 10-14 days

Cephalexin covers both S. aureus and Group A Streptococcus, the most common causes of cellulitis in children.

**Q:** What are the second-line antibiotics for cellulitis?

**A:** Cloxacillin (children: 50mg/kg/d divided QID; adults: 500mg PO QID) OR Clindamycin (children: 25mg/kg/d; adults: 300mg PO) - both x 10-14 days

Second-line options include cloxacillin for penicillin-allergic patients or clindamycin when anaerobes are suspected.

**Q:** What are the indications for HPV vaccination (Gardasil)?

**A:** Females between ages 9 to 27, and women 27+ if at risk of ongoing exposure

Gardasil protects against HPV types 6, 11, 16, 18 and is most effective when given before sexual debut.

**Q:** What are the second-line treatments for uncomplicated cellulitis in children?

**A:** Cloxacillin 50 mg/kg/day divided QID for 10-14 days OR clindamycin 25 mg/kg/day for 10-14 days

Alternatives for patients allergic to penicillins or when atypical organisms are suspected.

**Q:** What are the indications for HPV vaccination with Gardasil?

**A:** Females aged 9-27, women 27+ if at risk of ongoing exposure

Gardasil protects against HPV types 6, 11, 16, 18 and is most effective when given before sexual debut.

**A:** Females ages 9-27, and women 27+ if at risk of ongoing exposure

Gardasil 9 protects against HPV types 6, 11, 16, 18, 31, 33, 45, 52, and 58, preventing cervical cancer and genital warts.

**A:** Cephalexin 50-100 mg/kg/day divided QID x10-14 days

Cephalexin covers both S. aureus and group A streptococcus, the most common causes of cellulitis.

Second-line options for penicillin-allergic patients or treatment failures.

Second-line options are used for penicillin-allergic patients or treatment failure.

**A:** Rectal temperature

Rectal temperature is the gold standard for accuracy in children under 2 years; axillary and tympanic membrane measurements are unreliable in this age group.

**A:** Rectal temperature is required for accuracy in children <2 years

Axillary and tympanic membrane temperatures are unreliable in this age group due to measurement site limitations.

**Q:** When is evaluation for sexual abuse mandatory in a child with STI?

**A:** Always - when an STI is detected in a child, evaluation for sexual abuse is mandatory

STIs in children require mandatory reporting and assessment for possible abuse.

**Q:** What is first-line treatment for uncomplicated cellulitis in children?

**A:** Cephalexin 50-100 mg/kg/day divided into 4 doses daily for 10-14 days

Cephalexin covers both S. aureus and Group A Streptococcus. Alternatives include cloxacillin or clindamycin.

**Q:** At what age do most children get infected with Rotavirus?

**A:** By 3 years of age, virtually all children are infected

Rotavirus causes severe dehydration in children; oral vaccine is given at 2 and 4 months of age.

**Q:** At what age do most children get rotavirus infection?

**A:** Virtually all children are infected by 3 years of age

Rotavirus is nearly universal in early childhood; the oral vaccine is given at 2 and 4 months of age.

**Q:** When is evaluation for sexual abuse mandatory in a child with an STI?

**A:** When an STI is detected in a child

Any STI in a child requires mandatory assessment for potential sexual abuse per legal requirements.

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment for cellulitis in children?

**A:** Cephalexin 50-100 mg/kg/day divided QID x 10-14 days

First-generation cephalosporins cover both S. aureus and group A streptococcus effectively in pediatric patients.

**Q:** What are second-line treatments for cellulitis in children?

**A:** Cloxacillin 50 mg/kg/day divided QID x 10-14 days, OR clindamycin 25 mg/kg/day x 10-14 days

Second-line options include penicillinase-resistant penicillins or clindamycin for penicillin-allergic patients.

**Q:** What are risk factors for Giardia lamblia infection?

**A:** Daycare children, untreated water ('beaver fever'), MSM, immunodeficiency

Giardia is transmitted fecal-orally through contaminated water or food, common in daycares.

**Q:** A 5-year-old child presents with watery diarrhea and vomiting. The patient has a history of unimmunized status. What virus is the most common cause of severe pediatric gastroenteritis?

**A:** Rotavirus

Rotavirus is the most common cause of severe dehydrating gastroenteritis in children; virtually all children are infected by age 3 years.

**Q:** What are the risk factors for Giardia lamblia infection?

**A:** Daycare children, consumption of untreated water ('beaver fever'), men who have sex with men, immunodeficiency

Giardia lamblia (beaver fever) is transmitted fecal-orally and has higher risk in daycare settings, untreated water consumption, MSM, and immunodeficiency states.

**Q:** At what age are virtually all children infected with rotavirus?

**A:** By 3 years old

Rotavirus infects virtually all children by 3 years of age, causing severe dehydration. The oral vaccine is given at 2 and 4 months of age.

**Q:** What vaccinations should PBC patients receive?

**A:** Updated HAV and HBV vaccines

PBC patients should avoid alcohol, smoking, and should be vaccinated against hepatitis A and B to prevent additional liver injury.

**Q:** What is the most common cause of severe dehydration in children with viral gastroenteritis?

**A:** Rotavirus

Rotavirus causes severe dehydration in children, with virtually all children infected by age 3. Oral vaccine is given at 2 and 4 months.

**Q:** What is the most common viral cause of diarrhea in children?

**A:** Rotavirus

Rotavirus is the most common cause of severe viral gastroenteritis in children, causing 3-8 days of diarrhea with significant dehydration.

**Q:** What is the blue dot sign in torsion of testicular appendix?

**A:** Blue infarcted appendage visible through scrotal skin, typically in children. Cremaster reflex is preserved and testicle has vertical lie.

This finding helps differentiate from true testicular torsion where the reflex is absent and testicle lies horizontally.

**Q:** What are the initial investigations for a child with suspected vesicoureteral reflux (VUR)?

**A:** Focused history (voiding dysfunction, UTI symptoms), growth parameters, blood pressure, serum Cr, urinalysis with C&S, renal ultrasound, and VCUG to confirm diagnosis and grade

Initial evaluation includes assessing renal status, growth, and blood pressure. VCUG is the gold standard for diagnosing and grading VUR.

**Q:** What is the DMSA renal scan used for in pediatric urology?

**A:** High-risk detection of structural defects (dysplasia, renal scarring, pyelonephritis); higher sensitivity than ultrasound but entails radiation exposure

DMSA scan is used in children at high risk for renal scarring or structural abnormalities associated with UTIs.

## General

**Q:** What is the definition of fever based on oral temperature measurements?

**A:** Oral temperature >37.2°C (AM) or >37.7°C (PM)

Fever definitions vary by measurement site; rectal temperature is 37.5°C-38.3°C and axillary is >37.2°C.

**Q:** What are the temperature thresholds that define fever for oral (AM/PM), rectal, and axillary measurements?

**A:** Oral: >37.2°C (AM) or >37.7°C (PM); Rectal: 37.5°C-38.3°C; Axillary: >37.2°C

Fever definition varies by measurement site and time of day. Rectal temperature is most accurate for children <2 years.

**Q:** What is the definition of fever by temperature measurement site?

**A:** Oral >37.2°C (AM) or >37.7°C (PM); Rectal 37.5°C-38.3°C; Axillary >37.2°C

Fever definition varies by measurement site and time of day. Rectal is gold standard for children <2 years.

**Q:** What is the definition of fever by oral temperature?

**A:** Oral temperature >37.2°C in the AM or >37.7°C in the PM

Fever is defined by specific temperature thresholds measured at different times of day, with higher thresholds for evening measurements.

**Q:** What is the definition of fever by temperature site?

**A:** Oral >37.2°C (AM) or >37.7°C (PM), rectal 37.5-38.3°C, or axillary >37.2°C

Fever definitions vary by measurement site and time of day; rectal temperature is required for children <2 years for accuracy.

**Q:** What is the definition of fever by site of measurement?

**A:** Oral >37.2°C (AM) or >37.7°C (PM), rectal 37.5-38.3°C, or axillary >37.2°C

Fever definitions vary by measurement site and time of day; rectal temperature is required for children <2 years for accuracy.

**Q:** What is the universal recommendation for infant hearing screening?

**A:** Universal newborn hearing screening program

Infants should undergo universal newborn hearing screening programs for early detection.

**Q:** What is the definition of fever based on temperature measurement site?

**A:** Oral >37.2°C (AM) or >37.7°C (PM), rectal 37.5-38.3°C, or axillary >37.2°C

Fever definitions vary by measurement site, with rectal being most accurate in children under 2 years.

**Q:** What are the temperature thresholds that define fever?

**A:** Oral >37.2°C (AM) or >37.7°C (PM), rectal 37.5-38.3°C, or axillary >37.2°C

Fever definition varies by measurement site; rectal temperature is required for children <2 years for accuracy

**Q:** What are the diagnostic criteria for fever by site of measurement?

**A:** Oral >37.2°C (AM) or >37.7°C (PM), rectal 37.5-38.3°C, or axillary >37.2°C

Fever definitions vary by measurement site; rectal temperature is most accurate in children <2 years.

**Q:** What is the oral temperature threshold for fever in adults?

**A:** Oral temperature >37.2°C (AM) or >37.7°C (PM)

Fever is defined as oral temperature exceeding these thresholds. Different sites have different normal ranges.

**Q:** What are the accepted temperature thresholds for defining fever?

**A:** Oral >37.2°C (AM) or >37.7°C (PM), rectal 37.5°C-38.3°C, or axillary >37.2°C

Fever definitions vary by measurement site and time of day. Rectal temperatures are most accurate in young children.

**Q:** What is the spontaneous resolution rate for primary vesicoureteral reflux?

**A:** 60% spontaneous resolution, particularly in lower grades (I-II)

Primary VUR has a relatively high incidence of spontaneous resolution, especially in lower grades, making conservative management appropriate initially.

## Pediatrics

**Q:** What is the contraindication for rotavirus (Rot-1) vaccine?

**A:** History of intussusception, immunocompromised status, and abdominal disorders (e.g., Meckel's diverticulum).

Rotavirus vaccine is a live oral vaccine. Since intussusception is a known rare adverse event, prior history is an absolute contraindication. Immunocompromised infants cannot receive live vaccines.

**Q:** What is the contraindication for MMR vaccine?

**A:** Pregnancy, immunocompromised infants (except healthy HIV-positive children), anaphylactic reaction to gelatin, and cannot give within 4 weeks of TB skin test.

MMR is a live attenuated vaccine and is contraindicated in pregnancy and immunocompromised patients. It can interfere with tuberculin skin test results if given within 4 weeks.

**Q:** Which live vaccines can be given to patients with asplenia, isolated IgA deficiency, or complement deficiencies?

**A:** Live attenuated vaccines can be given to those with isolated IgA, IgG subclass, or complement deficiencies, or asplenia.

These conditions represent limited immune defects that do not significantly impair the response to live vaccines. However, severely immunocompromised patients should avoid live vaccines.

**Q:** What are the average growth parameters at birth for weight, length, and head circumference?

**A:** Weight: 3.25 kg (7 lbs), Length: 50 cm (20 in), Head circumference: 35 cm (14 in).

These are standard values that serve as baselines. Birth weight doubles by 4-5 months, triples by 1 year, and quadruples by 2 years. Half of adult height is achieved by age 2.

**Q:** At what age does the Moro reflex disappear?

**A:** 3-6 months.

The Moro reflex involves abduction and extension of arms followed by flexion and adduction when head support is suddenly withdrawn. Persistence beyond 6 months suggests neurological abnormality such as cerebral palsy.

**Q:** At what age should a child be able to walk without support, use a fine pincer grasp, and say one word with meaning?

**A:** 12 months.

At 12 months, children typically walk a few steps with wide gait, use fine pincer grasp with fingertips, say 1 word besides mama/dada, and can follow 1-step commands with gesture.

**Q:** What are the developmental red flags that should prompt further evaluation?

**A:** No social smile at 4 months, no pointing at 15-18 months, fewer than 6 words at 18 months, hand preference before 18 months, and loss of previously acquired skills at any age.

These red flags suggest possible developmental delay, cerebral palsy, or autism spectrum disorder. Early identification allows for early intervention which improves outcomes.

**Q:** What is the recommended age for exclusive breastfeeding, and what supplementation is required?

**A:** Exclusive breastfeeding for the first 6 months, with vitamin D supplementation (400 IU/day, or 800 IU/day if risk factors present).

Breastfeeding provides optimal nutrition and immunological benefits. Vitamin D supplementation is needed because breast milk has insufficient vitamin D, and deficiency leads to rickets.

**Q:** Why should honey be avoided in infants under 12 months?

**A:** Risk of infant botulism from Clostridium botulinum spores.

The immature infant gut cannot prevent colonization by C. botulinum spores found in honey. The bacteria produce toxin in the GI tract causing descending flaccid paralysis.

**Q:** What are the absolute maternal contraindications to breastfeeding?

**A:** HIV, HTLV type I and II, and infant galactosemia.

HIV and HTLV are transmissible through breast milk. Galactosemia is an inborn error of metabolism where the infant cannot metabolize galactose in breast milk, leading to liver failure and sepsis.

**Q:** What is the definition of infantile colic and when does it typically resolve?

**A:** Unexplained paroxysms of irritability and crying >3 hours/day, >3 days/week, for >3 weeks (rule of 3s/Wessel criteria) in a healthy, well-fed baby. Resolves by 3-6 months.

Colic is a diagnosis of exclusion with onset at 10 days to 3 months, peaking at 6-8 weeks. Etiology is unknown. Management focuses on parental support and reassurance.

**Q:** What is the definition and management of primary nocturnal enuresis?

**A:** Involuntary urinary incontinence at night in a child >5 years who has never achieved bladder control. Management: reassurance (~20% resolve spontaneously/year), behavioral modification, wet alarm (70% success), desmopressin for short-term use in children >7 years.

Primary nocturnal enuresis is a developmental/maturational lag. It should not be formally treated until age 7 due to high spontaneous cure rates. Punishment should be avoided.

**Q:** What is the definition of failure to thrive (FTT)?

**A:** Weight <3rd percentile, falls across two major percentile curves, or <80% of expected weight for height and age.

Inadequate caloric intake is the most common cause. The pattern of growth parameter abnormalities helps identify etiology: decreased weight alone suggests caloric insufficiency, while all three decreased suggest intrauterine insult.

**Q:** What defines childhood obesity for ages 5-19 years?

**A:** Overweight: BMI >85th percentile. Obese: BMI >97th percentile.

For children 2-5 years, different cutoffs apply (>97th and >99.9th). Childhood obesity is associated with T2DM, dyslipidemia, HTN, slipped capital femoral epiphysis, OSA, and psychological trauma.

**Q:** What are the SIDS risk factors and prevention strategies?

**A:** Risk factors: prone sleeping, soft bedding, prematurity, maternal smoking/alcohol/drug use, bed-sharing, overheating. Prevention: 'Back to Sleep, Front to Play,' avoid bed-sharing, avoid smoke exposure, firm mattress, breastfeeding, pacifier use.

SIDS is the leading cause of death between 1-12 months with peak incidence at 2-4 months. The 'Back to Sleep' campaign significantly reduced SIDS rates. Pacifiers and immunizations appear protective.

**Q:** What physical findings in a child should raise suspicion of non-accidental injury (child abuse)?

**A:** Bruises on soft-tissue areas (abdomen, buttocks, genitalia, cheeks, ears, neck), patterned injuries, posterior rib/metaphyseal/scapular fractures, immersion burns, retinal hemorrhages, and injuries inconsistent with developmental age or provided history.

The TEN-4-FACESp tool screens for concerning bruising in children <4 years. Traumatic head injury is the leading cause of death in child maltreatment. Physicians have a legal duty to report suspected abuse.

**Q:** What are the three fetal shunts and what triggers their closure at birth?

**A:** 1) Ductus arteriosus (PA to aorta) - closes due to increased O2 and decreased prostaglandins. 2) Foramen ovale (RA to LA) - closes due to increased left atrial pressure from increased pulmonary flow. 3) Ductus venosus (umbilical vein to IVC) - closes after placental separation.

Understanding fetal circulation is key to understanding congenital heart disease. Many cyanotic heart defects depend on these shunts remaining open, which is why prostaglandin E1 is given to maintain ductal patency.

**Q:** What is the most common congenital heart defect?

**A:** Ventricular septal defect (VSD), accounting for 30-50% of all congenital heart defects.

Most small VSDs close spontaneously. Large VSDs can cause CHF by 2 months and secondary pulmonary HTN if untreated. The murmur intensity is inversely related to VSD size (loud = small).

**Q:** What is the classic murmur of a patent ductus arteriosus (PDA)?

**A:** Continuous 'machinery' murmur best heard at the left infraclavicular area, with bounding pulses and wide pulse pressure.

PDA connects the descending aorta to the left pulmonary artery. Indomethacin antagonizes prostaglandin E2 to promote closure but is only effective in premature infants. Term infants typically require catheter or surgical closure.

**Q:** What are the four components of Tetralogy of Fallot?

**A:** VSD, right ventricular outflow tract obstruction (RVOTO/pulmonary stenosis), overriding aorta, and right ventricular hypertrophy (RVH).

ToF is the most common cyanotic heart defect diagnosed beyond infancy. The degree of RVOTO determines cyanosis severity. 'Tet spells' are treated with knee-chest position, O2, fluid bolus, morphine, and propranolol.

**Q:** What is the most common cyanotic congenital heart defect in neonates, and what is the classic CXR finding?

**A:** Transposition of the great arteries (TGA). CXR shows an 'egg on a string' appearance (egg-shaped heart with narrow mediastinum).

In TGA, the systemic and pulmonary circulations run in parallel. Survival depends on mixing through PDA, ASD, or VSD. Management involves prostaglandin E1 infusion and arterial switch operation within the first 2 weeks.

**Q:** What are the 4 key features of congestive heart failure in children?

**A:** Tachycardia, tachypnea, cardiomegaly, and hepatomegaly ('2 Tachys and 2 Megalys').

Unlike adults, orthopnea, PND, and pedal edema are uncommon in children. Infants present with feeding difficulties, diaphoresis, and FTT. Management includes diuretics, ACEI, and correction of underlying cause.

**Q:** What is the most common cause of congenital heart block in neonates?

**A:** Maternal anti-Ro (SSA) or anti-La (SSB) antibodies, as seen in mothers with SLE.

These maternal IgG antibodies cross the placenta and damage the fetal cardiac conduction system. It is often diagnosed in utero and may lead to fetal hydrops. Symptomatic patients require pacemaker implantation.

**Q:** What distinguishes innocent from pathological heart murmurs in children?

**A:** Innocent: asymptomatic, systolic ejection murmur, grade <3/6, soft/vibratory, physiologic S2 splitting, varies with position. Pathological: symptoms present, diastolic/pansystolic/continuous, grade ≥3/6, harsh, fixed split or single S2.

50-80% of children have audible murmurs at some point, most of which are innocent. Murmurs become louder in high-output states like fever and anemia. Five common innocent murmurs include Still's, venous hum, and pulmonary flow.

**Q:** What is global developmental delay (GDD)?

**A:** Significant delay (≥2 SDs below mean) in at least 2 developmental domains in a child <5 years. Domains include gross motor, fine motor, speech/language, cognitive, social/personal, and ADLs.

GDD affects up to 3% of the pediatric population. First-line investigations include chromosomal microarray and Fragile X DNA testing. After age 5, the term 'intellectual disability' is used instead.

**Q:** What is the most common preventable cause of intellectual disability?

**A:** Fetal Alcohol Spectrum Disorder (FASD), with a prevalence of ~1%.

FASD sentinel facial features include short palpebral fissures, flattened philtrum, and thin upper lip. Diagnosis requires multidisciplinary assessment. No safe amount of alcohol in pregnancy has been established.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 diagnostic criteria for Autism Spectrum Disorder?

**A:** 1) Persistent deficits in social communication/interaction (social-emotional reciprocity, nonverbal communication, relationships). 2) Restricted, repetitive patterns of behavior/interests (≥2 of: stereotyped movements, insistence on sameness, fixated interests, sensory hyper/hypo-reactivity). Symptoms present in early development.

ASD prevalence is 1 in 36 children (M:F=4:1). It is not better explained by intellectual disability. Treatment is team-based with behavioral therapy, and pharmacotherapy targets comorbid symptoms, not ASD itself.

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment for ADHD in children ≥6 years?

**A:** Combination of psychosocial intervention plus stimulant medication (methylphenidate or amphetamines). For children <6 years, psychosocial intervention alone is first line.

ADHD diagnosis requires onset before age 12, symptoms in ≥2 settings, and ≥6 symptoms for ≥6 months. Second-line agents include atomoxetine and guanfacine XR. Treatment with stimulants actually decreases future substance misuse risk.

**Q:** What is the diagnostic criteria for Type 1 Diabetes in children?

**A:** Symptoms (polyuria, polydipsia, weight loss) plus random glucose ≥11.1 mmol/L, OR two of: fasting glucose ≥7.0, 2h OGTT ≥11.1, HbA1c ≥6.5% on separate occasions.

T1DM is the most common form in children with bimodal peaks at 5-9 and 10-14 years. ~30% present with DKA. HbA1c target is <7.5% to minimize hypoglycemia. Screen for microvascular complications and autoimmune comorbidities.

**Q:** What are the warning signs of cerebral edema in pediatric DKA?

**A:** Headache, bradycardia, irritability, decreased level of consciousness, incontinence, and specific neurological signs.

Cerebral edema complicates 0.5-1% of DKA cases. Management includes mannitol, IV fluid restriction, and ICU transfer. Insulin bolus should be avoided and infusion delayed until 1 hour after fluid resuscitation in pediatric DKA protocols.

**Q:** What is 21-hydroxylase deficiency, and how does it present?

**A:** Autosomal recessive enzyme deficiency causing ~95% of congenital adrenal hyperplasia (CAH). Decreased cortisol and aldosterone with increased androgens. Classic with salt-wasting: FTT, hyperkalemia, hyponatremia. Classic without salt-wasting: virilization. Most common cause of ambiguous genitalia in 46,XX females.

Elevated 17-OH progesterone is diagnostic. Cortisol deficiency causes elevated ACTH and adrenal hyperplasia. Treatment includes glucocorticoids (hydrocortisone) and mineralocorticoids (fludrocortisone) with stress dosing.

**Q:** What is the normal sequence of pubertal development in females?

**A:** 1) Thelarche (breast budding), 2) Pubarche (axillary hair, body odor, acne), 3) Growth spurt, 4) Menarche (mean age 12.5 years). Onset ages 8-13.

Early puberty in females is common and often constitutional. Precocious puberty is defined as development of secondary sexual characteristics before age 8 in females or 9 in males. Central precocious puberty is treated with GnRH agonists.

**Q:** How do you assess the degree of dehydration in a child <2 years?

**A:** Mild (5%): normal pulse, slightly dry mucosa, decreased urine output. Moderate (10%): rapid pulse, dry mucosa, sunken fontanelle/eyes, decreased skin turgor. Severe (15%): rapid weak pulse, decreased BP, anuria, tenting, markedly sunken fontanelle.

For children >2 years, the percentages are 3%, 6%, and 9%. Capillary refill >3 seconds suggests severe dehydration. IV resuscitation with NS 20 mL/kg bolus is indicated for severe dehydration.

**Q:** What is the 4:2:1 rule for maintenance IV fluids in pediatrics?

**A:** First 10 kg: 4 mL/kg/hr. Next 10 kg (11-20): add 2 mL/kg/hr. Each kg >20: add 1 mL/kg/hr.

Equivalent to the 100:50:20 rule per day. All pediatric maintenance fluids should contain dextrose due to higher risk of hypoglycemia. Standard choice is D5W/NS + KCl 20 mEq/L for children. Sodium should not be corrected faster than 12 mmol/L/day.

**Q:** What is the classic triad of pyloric stenosis?

**A:** Projectile non-bilious emesis within 30 minutes after feeding, palpable 'olive' mass in RUQ, and hypochloremic hypokalemic metabolic alkalosis.

Pyloric stenosis typically presents at 2-6 weeks of age. Ultrasound of the pylorus is diagnostic. The metabolic alkalosis results from loss of gastric HCl through vomiting. Surgical pyloromyotomy is curative.

**Q:** What is the classic triad of intussusception?

**A:** Colicky abdominal pain, vomiting, and 'red currant jelly' stools (present in <15% of patients).

Intussusception usually occurs at the ileocecal junction in children <2 years. Ultrasound is diagnostic (target sign). Air or saline enema reduces 75% of cases. Recurrence rate is 10-15%.

**Q:** What is the '4 Fs' management of toddler's diarrhea?

**A:** Adequate Fiber, normal Fluid intake, lower dietary Fat (35-40%), and discourage excess Fruit juice.

Toddler's diarrhea is the most common cause of chronic diarrhea in infancy (6-36 months). It is a diagnosis of exclusion in a thriving child. It resolves spontaneously between 2-4 years.

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment for acute otitis media in children?

**A:** High-dose amoxicillin 75-90 mg/kg/day BID for 5 days (10 days if age <2 years, perforated TM, or recurrent AOM).

Most common pathogens are S. pneumoniae and H. influenzae. Watchful waiting is appropriate for mild cases in children >6 months. Second-line is amoxicillin-clavulanate. Recurrent AOM may require tympanostomy tubes.

**Q:** What are the CSF findings in bacterial vs viral meningitis?

**A:** Bacterial: elevated neutrophils (100-10000), low glucose (CSF:blood <0.4), elevated protein (>1.0 g/L). Viral: elevated lymphocytes (10-1000), normal glucose, mildly elevated protein (0.4-1.0 g/L).

LP is required for definitive diagnosis but should not delay empiric antibiotics. Neonatal meningitis is treated with ampicillin + cefotaxime; children >90 days get ceftriaxone + vancomycin. Dexamethasone is given before antibiotics for H. influenzae meningitis.

**Q:** What are the three stages of pertussis (whooping cough)?

**A:** 1) Catarrhal (1-7 days): URTI symptoms. 2) Paroxysmal (4-6 weeks): paroxysms of cough with inspiratory whoop, post-tussive emesis. 3) Convalescent (1-2 weeks): decreasing frequency and severity of cough.

Pertussis is caused by B. pertussis. Infants <6 months may present with apnea without whoop. Treatment is macrolide antibiotics (azithromycin). Prophylaxis with macrolides for all household contacts.

**Q:** What is the Jones Criteria for diagnosing rheumatic fever?

**A:** Requires 2 major OR 1 major + 2 minor criteria PLUS evidence of preceding strep infection. Major: carditis/valvulitis, arthritis, Sydenham chorea, subcutaneous nodules, erythema marginatum. Minor: arthralgia, fever, elevated ESR/CRP, prolonged PR interval.

Rheumatic fever is an inflammatory disease from antibody cross-reactivity following GAS infection. Peak incidence is 5-15 years. Treatment is penicillin x 10 days. Secondary prophylaxis with daily penicillin prevents recurrence.

**Q:** What is the approach to the febrile neonate (<28 days)?

**A:** Admit for full septic workup (CBC, blood/urine/CSF cultures, CXR) and empiric antibiotics regardless of clinical appearance.

Febrile neonates are considered high-risk regardless of clinical features because protocols like Rochester Criteria are not as sensitive in the 1-28 day age group. The younger the child, the greater the difficulty in clinically assessing severity.

**Q:** What are the features of erythema infectiosum (fifth disease)?

**A:** Caused by parvovirus B19. 'Slapped cheek' bilateral facial erythema with circumoral sparing, followed by lacy reticular rash on trunk/extremities. Rash appears 10-17 days after prodromal flu-like illness.

The rash appears after viremia resolves (immune response), so patients are no longer infectious when the rash appears. Complications include transient aplastic crisis in patients with chronic hemolytic anemias.

**Q:** What are the classic features of Kawasaki disease?

**A:** Fever ≥5 days plus ≥4 of: bilateral conjunctival injection, oral changes (strawberry tongue, cracked lips), cervical lymphadenopathy, polymorphous rash, extremity changes (edema, desquamation).

Kawasaki disease is the most common cause of acquired heart disease in children in developed countries. Coronary artery aneurysms are the major complication. Treatment is IVIG plus high-dose aspirin.

**Q:** What is the classic presentation of infectious mononucleosis?

**A:** Classic triad: fever, generalized non-tender lymphadenopathy, and exudative pharyngitis/tonsillitis. May also have hepatosplenomegaly and rash (especially after ampicillin/amoxicillin).

Caused by EBV, peak incidence 15-19 years. Diagnosed with heterophile antibody test (Monospot, 85% sensitive in older children). All patients should avoid contact sports for 6-8 weeks due to risk of splenic rupture.

**Q:** What are the most common pediatric UTI pathogens and when should a child be admitted?

**A:** E. coli accounts for ~70%. Admit if: age <2 months, urosepsis, persistent vomiting, inability to tolerate oral medications, moderate-severe dehydration, immunocompromised.

UTI prevalence in febrile infants is 7%. Clean catch or catheterized specimen is required for diagnosis. Bag specimens can only be used to rule out (not confirm) UTI. Renal/bladder ultrasound recommended for all febrile UTIs in children <2 years.

**Q:** What is the triad of hemolytic uremic syndrome (HUS)?

**A:** 1) Non-immune microangiopathic hemolytic anemia, 2) Thrombocytopenia, 3) Acute renal injury.

STEC-HUS (90%) is caused by Shiga toxin-producing E. coli (O157:H7). Presents 3-10 days after bloody diarrhea. Avoid antibiotics, NSAIDs, and antidiarrheal agents in STEC-HUS. Blood smear shows schistocytes.

**Q:** What are the defining features of nephrotic syndrome vs nephritic syndrome?

**A:** Nephrotic (HELP): Hypoalbuminemia, Edema, Lipids elevated, Proteinuria >50 mg/kg/day. Nephritic: Hematuria (RBC casts), proteinuria (<50 mg/kg), azotemia, oliguria, HTN.

MCD causes 85% of idiopathic nephrotic syndrome in children, peaking at ages 2-6. MCD responds to prednisone in 80% of cases. Post-streptococcal GN is the most common cause of nephritic syndrome with decreased C3.

**Q:** What are the most common causes of childhood cancer by age?

**A:** Leukemia is the most common cancer in children 0-14 years (ALL 80%, AML 15%). Neuroblastoma is most common in the first year of life. Wilms' tumor peaks at 2-5 years.

Children with Down syndrome are 15x more likely to develop leukemia. Neuroblastoma arises from sympathetic tissue (adrenal most common site) with elevated urine catecholamines. Wilms' tumor presents as an asymptomatic abdominal mass in 80%.

**Q:** What syndromes are associated with Wilms' tumor?

**A:** WAGR syndrome (Wilms' tumor, Aniridia, Genitourinary anomalies, Range of developmental delays) - 50% risk. Beckwith-Wiedemann syndrome - 5-10% risk. Denys-Drash syndrome - 90% risk.

WAGR involves 11p13 deletion. Beckwith-Wiedemann features organomegaly, hemihypertrophy, and omphalocele. Denys-Drash features gonadal dysgenesis and nephropathy. Wilms' tumor has >95% 5-year survival with surgery and chemotherapy.

**Q:** What is the congenital hypothyroidism presentation and when must treatment begin?

**A:** Usually asymptomatic at birth (maternal T4 crosses placenta). Later: prolonged jaundice, feeding difficulty, lethargy, constipation, umbilical hernia, macroglossia, large fontanelles, hypotonia. Most commonly detected via newborn screening. Treatment (thyroxine) must begin within 2 weeks.

Congenital hypothyroidism occurs in 1:2000-4000 births and is one of the most common preventable causes of intellectual disability. If treatment is delayed beyond 3-6 months, permanent developmental delay results.

**Q:** What is coarctation of the aorta and how does it present?

**A:** Narrowing of the aorta at the level of the ductus arteriosus. Presents with BP discrepancy between upper and lower extremities (>20 mmHg), diminished/delayed femoral pulses (brachial-femoral delay). Associated with bicuspid aortic valve (50%) and Turner syndrome (35%).

Severe coarctation presents with shock in neonatal period when the ductus closes. Management: prostaglandins to keep ductus patent, then surgical correction. Older children may receive balloon arterioplasty.

**Q:** What are the Rochester criteria used for?

**A:** To identify febrile infants <60 days at low risk of serious bacterial infection. Low risk: clinically well, WBC 5-15 x 10⁹/L, urinalysis <10 WBC/HPF, stool <5 WBC/HPF (if diarrhea), born >37 weeks, no hospitalizations, no prior antibiotics, no chronic disease.

These criteria are not sensitive in the 0-28 day age group, so all febrile neonates <28 days should be treated as high risk. The younger the child, the greater the difficulty in clinically assessing illness severity.

**Q:** What is the celiac disease screening consideration in children with IgA deficiency?

**A:** Celiac disease has increased prevalence of IgA deficiency. Since tissue transglutaminase (tTG) is an IgA-detecting test, you must order an accompanying total IgA level to avoid false negatives.

Celiac disease in children presents at any age, often 6-24 months with gluten introduction. Symptoms include FTT, diarrhea, iron-deficiency anemia, and dermatitis herpetiformis. A gluten-free diet must avoid BROW foods (Barley, Rye, Oats, Wheat).

**Q:** What are the features of scarlet fever?

**A:** Acute onset fever, sore throat, strawberry tongue. Sandpaper rash starting in groin/axillae/neck 24-48h after pharyngitis, becomes generalized with perioral sparing. Pastia's lines in flexural areas. Desquamation after 3-4 days.

Scarlet fever is a delayed-type hypersensitivity to GAS pyrogenic exotoxin requiring prior strep exposure. Treatment is penicillin, amoxicillin, or erythromycin for 10 days. Complications include rheumatic fever and post-streptococcal GN.

**Q:** What is the HEEADSSS adolescent psychosocial assessment?

**A:** Home, Education/Employment, Eating, Activities, Drugs, Sexuality, Suicidality/Depression, Safety/Violence.

Review confidentiality and its limits with adolescents before taking history. Start with impersonal questions before personal ones. The CRAFFT screen is validated for substance use in adolescents 12-21 years. Age of sexual consent in Canada is 16.

**Q:** What are the characteristics of night terrors vs nightmares?

**A:** Night terrors: first 1/3 of night, arousal from deep (stage 4) sleep, screaming, eyes open, no memory of event, inconsolable, 15% of children. Nightmares: last 1/3 of night, REM sleep, child alert on awakening with clear recall, common ages 4-7.

Night terrors involve autonomic arousal without recall. Management for both is reassurance. For night terrors, parents can try to wake the child 15 minutes before the usual time to disrupt the pattern. Both typically resolve before puberty.

**Q:** What is the OSA management in children?

**A:** First-line: adenotonsillectomy and weight management. Intranasal corticosteroids as temporizing measure in mild OSA. CPAP if adenotonsillectomy is contraindicated or for residual OSA.

Pediatric OSA affects 1-5% of preschool children. Diagnosis: polysomnography is gold standard but not required. Adenotonsillectomy improves behavior, QOL, and polysomnographic findings but does not improve executive function/attention.

**Q:** What is Hirschsprung's disease and how is it diagnosed?

**A:** Congenital absence of ganglion cells in the distal colon causing functional obstruction. Presents with bilious emesis, abdominal distension, and failure to pass stool in neonates. Diagnosed by abdominal x-ray, contrast enema, and rectal biopsy (gold standard).

Hirschsprung's is an important organic cause of constipation in neonates. Most healthy newborns pass meconium within 24-48 hours. Failure to pass meconium should raise suspicion. Treatment is surgical resection of the aganglionic segment.

**Q:** What is the most common cause of acute renal failure in children?

**A:** Hemolytic uremic syndrome (HUS).

STEC-HUS from E. coli O157:H7 accounts for 90% of pediatric HUS. The pathophysiology involves Shiga toxin damaging endothelial cells, especially in the kidney, causing platelet-fibrin thrombi and microangiopathic hemolytic anemia. Mortality is <5% but 30% develop long-term renal damage.

**Q:** What is the normal heart rate range for a neonate (<28 days) while awake?

**A:** 100-205 beats per minute (awake); 90-160 bpm asleep.

Neonatal heart rates are significantly higher than adult ranges due to their higher metabolic rate and predominantly sympathetic tone. Bradycardia in neonates (HR <100 bpm) is concerning and requires evaluation. Normal respiratory rate for neonates is 30-53 breaths/min.

**Q:** What is the definition of fever by rectal temperature measurement in pediatric patients?

**A:** Rectal temperature ≥38°C (normal range 36.6-38°C).

Rectal temperature is the most accurate measurement method. Different measurement routes have different fever thresholds: rectal ≥38°C, ear ≥38°C, oral ≥37.5°C, axillary ≥37.5°C. In infants <3 months, fever ≥38°C by any route warrants urgent evaluation for serious bacterial infection.

**Q:** What is the recommended temperature measurement technique for children under 2 years of age?

**A:** Rectal temperature (definitive); axillary (screening).

Rectal temperature is the gold standard for accuracy in infants and young children. Tympanic and temporal artery measurements are acceptable screening tools in hospital settings for children aged 2-5 years. For children over 5 years, oral temperature is the definitive method.

**Q:** What does the Canadian Pediatric Society (CPS) recommend for the schedule of well-child visits?

**A:** Newborn (48-72h post-discharge), 2, 4, 6, 9, 12, 18 months; annually ages 2-5; every 1-2 years ages 6-18.

Well-child visits allow monitoring of growth, development, immunizations, nutrition, and anticipatory guidance. The Rourke Baby Record (birth to age 5) and Greig Health Record (ages 6-17) are recommended evidence-based clinical tools used in Canadian primary care.

**Q:** What is the BINDS mnemonic for pediatric history taking?

**A:** B - Birth history; I - Immunizations; N - Nutritional history; D - Developmental history; S - Social history.

BINDS is a structured approach to pediatric history unique to children. Each domain captures age-specific information crucial for comprehensive assessment: birth complications, vaccine status, feeding patterns, developmental milestones, and family/social environment.

**Q:** What is the HEEADSSS assessment and when is it used in pediatric practice?

**A:** HEEADSSS (Home, Education/Employment, Eating, Activities, Drugs, Sexuality, Suicide/depression, Safety/violence) is used for psychosocial screening in adolescents during well-child visits.

HEEADSSS provides a structured framework for confidential adolescent health interviews covering major risk domains. SSHADESS is an alternative framing that starts with Strengths. It is used to identify mental health concerns, substance use, and safety issues that adolescents may not volunteer spontaneously.

**Q:** What additional vaccines are required for children with asplenia or hyposplenia (e.g., sickle cell disease)?

**A:** All routine immunizations PLUS: quadrivalent meningococcal conjugate (Men-C-ACYW), meningococcal B vaccine (8 weeks apart, booster every 5 years), pneumococcal polysaccharide (Pneu-P-23), pneumococcal conjugate vaccine (Pneu-C-13), and consider single Hib booster >5 years.

Asplenic patients are at high risk for overwhelming post-splenectomy infection (OPSI) from encapsulated organisms (S. pneumoniae, H. influenzae, N. meningitidis, E. coli, Klebsiella, Salmonella, Group B Strep). Enhanced vaccination is critical for prevention. No vaccines are contraindicated in asplenia, though live vaccines should be used with caution in immunocompromised states.

**Q:** What are the true contraindications to childhood vaccination?

**A:** Moderate/severe illness with fever, and allergy to a vaccine component. (Mild illness is NOT a contraindication to vaccination.)

Common misconceptions about vaccine contraindications lead to missed vaccination opportunities. Mild illness without significant fever is NOT a contraindication. True contraindications are few: severe illness with fever and known allergy to vaccine components (e.g., egg allergy for influenza vaccines). Previous anaphylaxis to a vaccine dose is an absolute contraindication to subsequent doses.

**Q:** What maternal screening tests are included in the standard pediatric obstetrical history review?

**A:** Blood group, antibody screen, Rh status, DAT, HBsAg, rubella, syphilis, HIV, GBS (Group B Streptococcus).

These maternal screening tests directly affect neonatal risk assessment and management. GBS status determines intrapartum antibiotic prophylaxis; HBsAg determines need for neonatal hepatitis B immunoglobulin; Rh status determines need for anti-D. Each result has direct implications for the newborn's health.

**Q:** What is the normal systolic blood pressure range for a school-age child (6-9 years)?

**A:** 97-115 mmHg.

Pediatric blood pressure norms are age-dependent and significantly lower than adult values. Hypertension in children is defined as SBP/DBP above the 95th percentile for age, sex, and height on three separate occasions. Knowledge of age-appropriate norms is essential for identifying hypertension in pediatric patients.

**Q:** What are the distinguishing clinical features of bacterial gastroenteritis compared to viral gastroenteritis in children?

**A:** Bacterial gastroenteritis: severe abdominal pain, high fever, bloody diarrhea. Viral gastroenteritis: slight fever, malaise, vomiting, vague abdominal pain, resolves in 3-7 days, often associated with URTIs.

Common bacterial causes include Salmonella, Campylobacter, Shigella, pathogenic E. coli, Yersinia, and C. difficile. Viral causes (rotavirus, norovirus, adenovirus) are more common overall. Distinguishing features guide antibiotic use decisions.

**Q:** What investigations help differentiate bacterial from viral gastroenteritis in children?

**A:** Bacterial: elevated band count on CBC, positive stool cultures, presence of C. difficile toxins. Parasitic: elevated eosinophil count on CBC, stool examination for ova and parasites.

Viral gastroenteritis is typically a clinical diagnosis not requiring lab confirmation. Bacterial investigations are warranted when there is bloody diarrhea, high fever, systemic symptoms, or exposure history (travel, poorly cooked meat).

**Q:** What is the management of pediatric gastroenteritis with significant vomiting?

**A:** Ondansetron is indicated for suspected gastroenteritis with mild to moderate dehydration or failed oral rehydration therapy (ORT) with significant vomiting. Antidiarrheal medications are NOT indicated.

Prevention and treatment of dehydration is the most important management goal. Early refeeding with age-appropriate diet after rehydration is advisable. Zinc supplementation is recommended. Antibiotic/antiparasitic therapy only when indicated.

**Q:** What is toddler's diarrhea and how is it managed?

**A:** Toddler's diarrhea is the most common cause of chronic diarrhea during infancy (onset 6-36 months, resolves 2-4 years). Diagnosis of exclusion in a thriving child with 4-6 BM/day. Management: the 4 F's — adequate Fibre, normal Fluid intake, lower dietary Fat (35-40%), discourage excess Fruit juice.

Excess juice intake overwhelms small bowel resulting in disaccharide malabsorption. Stools may contain undigested food particles. No routine investigations needed if child is growing appropriately. Condition is self-limiting.

**Q:** How is lactose intolerance diagnosed in children, and what are the two main types?

**A:** Primary lactose intolerance: crampy abdominal pain with loose stool in older children (more common in East Asian and African descent). Secondary lactose intolerance: persistent diarrhea in older infants following viral/bacterial infection, celiac disease, or IBD. Diagnosis: trial of lactose-free diet; positive breath hydrogen test if >6 years after oral lactose load.

Investigations are usually not needed for diagnosis. Demonstration of lactose malabsorption should be combined with assessment of intolerance symptoms. Management includes lactose-free diet and lactase-containing supplements (Lacteeze, Lactaid).

**Q:** What is the association between celiac disease and IgA deficiency, and what test must be ordered?

**A:** Celiac disease is associated with an increased prevalence of IgA deficiency. Since tTG (tissue transglutaminase) is an IgA-detecting test, an accompanying IgA level MUST be ordered to avoid false negatives.

If IgA-deficient, tTG-IgA will be falsely negative despite celiac disease. In this case, IgG-based antibodies (tTG-IgG, DGP-IgG) should be used. Celiac disease diet must avoid gluten present in BROW foods: Barley, Rye, Oats (controversial), Wheat.

**Q:** What are the BROW foods to avoid in celiac disease?

**A:** B - Barley, R - Rye, O - Oats (controversial), W - Wheat. These foods contain gluten which triggers the immune response in celiac disease.

Celiac disease is an immune-mediated enteropathy triggered by gluten. In children it presents at any age, often 6-24 months with introduction of gluten in the diet. Non-GI manifestations include iron-deficiency anemia, dermatitis herpetiformis, osteopenia, short stature, and delayed puberty.

**Q:** What is the difference between IgE-mediated and non-IgE-mediated cow's milk allergy (CMA) in children?

**A:** IgE-mediated CMA: occurs within hours of exposure; skin (urticaria, pruritus), respiratory (wheeze, cough), and GI (pain, N/V, diarrhea) symptoms. Non-IgE-mediated CMA: occurs hours after ingestion; includes FPIAP (mild diarrhea, bloody stools), FPIES (severe vomiting/diarrhea, anemia, hematochezia), food protein-induced enteropathy (FTT, chronic diarrhea, hypoalbuminemia).

Up to 50% of children intolerant to cow's milk may also be intolerant to soy protein. Food challenge is the gold standard for diagnosis. Non-IgE-mediated CMA: stop exposure, reintroduce milk at 6-8 months; >90% will outgrow by 1 year.

**Q:** What are the Rome IV criteria for functional constipation in children >4 years?

**A:** ≥2 of the following, present at least once per week for ≥1 month: <2 defecations/week, ≥1 episode/week of fecal incontinence, history of retentive posturing or excessive volitional stool retention, history of painful or hard bowel movements, evidence of large fecal mass in rectum, history of large-diameter stools that may obstruct the toilet. Symptoms not fully explained by another medical condition.

Functional constipation accounts for 99% of constipation cases in children. Common precipitants include lack of fibre, poor fluid intake, toilet training, starting school, or pain on defecation. Treatment is maintained for at least 3-6 months.

**Q:** What is the management of functional constipation in children?

**A:** 1. Education for parents/older children. 2. Clean out (if encopresis): PEG 3350 (1-1.5 g/kg/d). 3. Maintenance: adequate fluid intake, dietary fibre, medications (PEG 3350, Senna), appropriate toilet training (dedicated 3-10 min, 1-2x/day). Treat for at least 3-6 months.

PEGlyte is used for inpatient clean out. Children should not be weaned from maintenance therapy until having regular BMs without difficulty. Complications include anal fissures, chronic dilatation, and overflow incontinence (pain-retention cycle).

**Q:** What are organic causes of constipation in children?

**A:** Endocrine/metabolic: hypothyroidism, DM, hypercalcemia, CF, lead poisoning, hypokalemia. Neurologic: spinal cord abnormalities/trauma, NF, Hirschsprung disease, cerebral palsy. Anatomic: bowel obstruction, anorectal anomalies. Drugs: lead, chemotherapy, opioids. Other: celiac disease, generalized joint hypermobility.

These organic causes must be considered when constipation does not respond to standard treatment. Hirschsprung disease presents with failure to pass meconium within 24-48 hours of birth, bilious vomiting, and abdominal distension.

**Q:** What is the 'classic triad' of intussusception, and how common is it?

**A:** Classic triad: abdominal pain, vomiting, and red currant jelly stools. Present in <15% of patients. More common presentation: sudden onset of recurrent, paroxysmal, severe periumbilical pain with inconsolable crying, legs raised toward abdomen, with pain-free intervals.

Intussusception is the telescoping of a bowel segment into a distal segment. 90% are idiopathic. Affects 80-90% before age 2, M:F = 3:2. Lethargy may be the only presenting symptom. Usually diagnosed on ultrasound.

**Q:** What is the treatment for ileocolic intussusception?

**A:** Air or saline/contrast enema can be therapeutic (reduces intussusception in 75% of cases), reduction under hydrostatic pressure. Surgery is rarely needed. Recurrence rate is 10-15%; consider pathologic lead point. Ileoileal intussusception is often not significant and usually does not require treatment.

Lead points of telescoping segment include swollen Peyer's patches, Meckel's diverticulum, polyp, malignancy, HSP-related bowel wall edema. Children with CF or GJ tubes are at significantly increased risk.

**Q:** What are the red flags for an organic etiology of chronic abdominal pain in children?

**A:** Age <5, fever, pain that localizes away from midline, anemia, evidence of GI blood loss, significant vomiting, chronic severe diarrhea, pain that wakes child at night, travel history, prominent vomiting/diarrhea, weight loss or failure to gain weight, deceleration in linear growth, joint pain, family history of IBD, abnormal physical exam findings.

Organic causes account for <10% of chronic abdominal pain. The remaining 90% is functional. Functional abdominal pain seldom awakens child from sleep and is less common on weekends — useful distinguishing feature from organic disease.

**Q:** What are the functional abdominal pain subtypes in children?

**A:** 1. Functional dyspepsia: pain in upper abdomen. 2. Irritable bowel syndrome: alternating bowel movements. 3. Abdominal migraine: paroxysmal abdominal pain with anorexia, nausea, vomiting, pallor. 4. Functional abdominal pain syndrome.

Functional abdominal pain affects 10% of school children (peak 8-10 years, F>M). Rule of 3s: 3 episodes of severe pain, over a 3-month period. Psychological factors and psychiatric comorbidity (anxiety, somatoform, mood disorders) are common.

**Q:** What are the most common malignant and non-malignant renal abdominal masses in children?

**A:** Non-malignant renal: hydronephrosis, polycystic kidney disease, hamartoma. Malignant renal: nephroblastoma (Wilms' tumour), renal cell carcinoma. Note: 50% of abdominal masses in newborns are renal in origin.

Wilms' tumour: asymptomatic abdominal swelling, abdominal pain (30-40%), hematuria (12-25%), fever, hypertension (25%). Treatment: surgery, chemotherapy, radiation. Renal cell carcinoma classic triad: flank pain, hematuria, palpable renal mass.

**Q:** What are the most common causes of lower GI bleeding in children by acuity?

**A:** Acute causes: infectious (bacterial, parasitic), antibiotic-induced C. difficile, NEC in preterm infants, malrotation/volvulus, intussusception, Hirschsprung disease, Meckel's diverticulitis, anal fissures, HSP, HUS, coagulopathy. Chronic causes: anal fissures (most common), infectious colitis, IBD, FPIAP, allergic (milk protein), polyps (mostly hamartomas).

Lower GI bleed may present as melena (if it involves the small bowel) or hematochezia. Meckel's radionuclide scan is a key investigation. Acute stabilization: ABCs, volume and blood replacement, bowel rest (NPO, NG tube).

**Q:** What are normal hemoglobin values by age in children?

**A:** Newborn: 137-201 g/L. 2 months: (lower limit). 6 months-5 years: 105-140 g/L. 7-29 years: 110-160 g/L. Adult female: 120-160 g/L. Adult male: 140-180 g/L.

General rule for lower normal limit of MCV = 70 + age (years) until 80 fL (adult standard). Understanding age-specific norms is essential to avoid over- or under-diagnosis of anemia in children.

**Q:** What is physiologic anemia of infancy and when does it nadir?

**A:** High Hb and reticulocyte count at birth (relatively hypoxic intrauterine environment). After birth, levels fall due to shorter fetal RBC lifespan, decreased RBC production (virtually no erythropoiesis in first 6-8 weeks), and increasing blood volume with growth. Nadir: Hb ~100 g/L at 8-12 weeks (earlier and more exaggerated in premature infants).

Usually no treatment required. Red flags for non-physiologic anemia: Hb lower than expected, signs of hemolysis, or symptoms of anemia. A 'normal' reticulocyte count in the context of anemia indicates inadequate bone marrow response.

**Q:** What is iron deficiency anemia in pediatrics and who is most at risk?

**A:** Most common cause of childhood anemia. Full-term infants exhaust iron reserves by 6 months; premature infants by 2-3 months. Most common between 6 months-3 years and 11-17 years. At-risk groups: premature, maternal iron deficiency, low birth weight, low SES, food insecurity, selective eating (e.g., autism), cow's milk in the first year, >500 mL/day of non-iron fortified milk (age 1-5 years).

Iron deficiency is rare in children <6 months in the absence of blood loss or low birth weight. Cow's milk is a significant risk factor in the first year. Communities facing poverty and diminished access to traditional foods are also at risk.

**Q:** What is the Mentzer index and how is it used to distinguish iron deficiency anemia from thalassemia?

**A:** Mentzer index = MCV/RBC count. Ratio <13 suggests thalassemia. Ratio >13 suggests iron deficiency anemia.

Both iron deficiency anemia and thalassemia cause microcytic anemia. In thalassemia, the RBC count tends to be normal or elevated despite low MCV. Ferritin should always be ordered with CRP since ferritin is an acute phase reactant and may be falsely normal in the presence of infection/inflammation.

**Q:** Why must ferritin always be ordered with CRP when investigating iron deficiency anemia?

**A:** Ferritin is an acute phase reactant. Therefore, normal or high ferritin does NOT rule out iron deficiency anemia if there is concurrent inflammation (e.g., infection). A CRP must be ordered alongside ferritin to determine if the ferritin is falsely elevated.

True iron deficiency presents with low ferritin, low serum iron, high TIBC, high transferrin, and low transferrin saturation. Blood smear shows hypochromic, microcytic RBCs, pencil-shaped cells, and poikilocytosis.

**Q:** What is the treatment for iron deficiency anemia in children?

**A:** Oral iron therapy: 4-6 mg/kg/day elemental iron, divided BID to TID, for 3-6 months. Response markers: increased reticulocyte count in 2-3 days (peaks day 5-7), increased hemoglobin by 30 days, repletion of iron stores in 1-3 months. Repeat Hb levels after 1 month of treatment. Limit homogenized milk to 500 mL/day.

Poor response to oral iron therapy suggests: non-adherence, medication intolerance, ongoing blood loss, IBD, celiac disease, or incorrect diagnosis. Emerging evidence for IV iron sucrose use especially when adherence is a concern. Untreated IDA can cause irreversible behavioural and intellectual deficiencies.

**Q:** What are the iron supplementation recommendations for breastfed and low birth weight infants?

**A:** Breastfed term infants: at 6 months ensure >2 iron-rich feeds/day; if unable, consider iron supplementation. Non-breastfed term infants (<50% diet): iron-fortified formula from birth. Low birth weight (2.0-2.5 kg): 1-2 mg/kg/day for first 6 months. BW <2.0 kg: 2-3 mg/kg/day for first 12 months.

No cow's milk until 12 months. Total daily iron should be 1 mg (ages 6-12 months) and 7 mg (ages 1-3 years). Screen Hb levels in infants not receiving iron-fortified formula at 9-12 months, and earlier if risk factors present.

**Q:** What is Vitamin K Deficiency Bleeding (VKDB) in neonates — when does it present and how is it prevented?

**A:** VKDB (formerly hemorrhagic disease of the newborn) has three presentations: early-onset (first 24 hours), classic (2-7 days), and late-onset (2-12 weeks up to 7 months — highest risk of intracranial hemorrhage). Prevention: Vitamin K IM injection (0.5-1 mg) at birth. Can also be given orally (at first feed, 1, 4, 8 weeks) for breastfed term infants but carries higher risk of VKDB.

Most commonly occurs in infants <6 months due to hepatic immaturity, inefficient vitamin K use, poor vitamin K stores, and low vitamin K content in milk. Coagulation profile shows elevated PT and PTT with normal platelet count.

**Q:** How is acute VKDB (Vitamin K Deficiency Bleeding) managed?

**A:** Managed urgently with IV/SC vitamin K (1-2 mg). If severe bleeding, also administer fresh frozen plasma (FFP) or prothrombin complex concentrate (PCC).

Vitamin K-dependent clotting factors are II, VII, IX, X (and proteins C and S). VKDB causes PT and PTT prolongation. Risk factors for non-classic VKDB include maternal medications (antiepileptics, certain antibiotics, warfarin), chronic malabsorption (CF, celiac, IBD, biliary atresia), liver failure.

**Q:** What is Immune Thrombocytopenic Purpura (ITP) in children — definition, epidemiology, and clinical features?

**A:** ITP: isolated thrombocytopenia (platelets <100,000/μL with normal WBC and Hb). Most common cause of symptomatic thrombocytopenia in childhood. Peak age: 2-5 years, M slightly > F. 60% present within 1 month after viral illness. Sudden onset petechiae, purpura, bleeding in an otherwise well child. No lymphadenopathy or hepatosplenomegaly.

Caused by autoantibodies binding platelet membranes → Fc-receptor mediated splenic destruction. Clinically significant bleed in only 3%; <0.5% risk of intracranial hemorrhage. Diagnosis of exclusion — made in well-appearing patients with mucocutaneous bleeding and normal WBC/Hb.

**Q:** What is the management of ITP in children based on bleeding severity?

**A:** No/mild bleeding (bruising/petechiae only): watchful waiting regardless of platelet level. Moderate bleeding (severe skin manifestations, mucosal lesions, troublesome epistaxis/menorrhagia): IVIg (1 g/kg) or steroids; anti-D if Rh-positive and DAT-negative. Severe bleeding (prolonged epistaxis, GI bleeding, ICH): IV steroids + IVIg; tranexamic acid as adjunct. Life-threatening bleed: platelet transfusion + emergency splenectomy.

IVIg provides faster increase in platelet count vs. steroids. Bone marrow aspirate only if red flags present. Anti-D is not indicated in low Hb from blood loss or autoimmune hemolysis. Avoid contact sports and ASA/NSAIDs. Persistent ITP: >3-12 months; chronic ITP: >12 months.

**Q:** What are the distinguishing lab findings in hemophilia A, hemophilia B, von Willebrand disease, and ITP?

**A:** Hemophilia A: prolonged PTT, decreased Factor VIII:C. Hemophilia B: prolonged PTT, all others normal. Von Willebrand Disease: prolonged PFA, normal/prolonged PTT, decreased VWF and Factor VIII:C. ITP: prolonged PFA, decreased platelets. DIC: prolonged everything, decreased fibrinogen. Vitamin K deficiency: prolonged PT and PTT.

PFA (platelet function assay) is prolonged in platelet disorders and vWD. PT reflects extrinsic pathway; PTT reflects intrinsic pathway. Extensive bruising in absence of lab abnormalities should raise suspicion for child maltreatment.

**Q:** What are the most common pediatric malignancies and at what age groups do they peak?

**A:** Leukemia: most common overall (30%), infancy/childhood. Brain tumours: 25%, infancy/childhood. Lymphomas: 15%, adolescence. Age-specific peaks: Newborns — neuroblastoma, Wilms' tumour, retinoblastoma. Infancy/childhood — leukemia, neuroblastoma, CNS tumours, Wilms' tumour, retinoblastoma. Adolescence — lymphoma, leukemia, gonadal/germ cell tumours, thyroid cancers, melanoma, bone tumours.

Cancer is the second most common cause of death after injuries in children >1 year. Overall cure rates >80% with modern treatment. Unique pediatric considerations: radiation, chemotherapy, and surgery can impact growth, development, endocrine function, and fertility.

**Q:** What clinical features distinguish malignant from benign lymphadenopathy in children?

**A:** Malignant features: firm, discrete, non-tender, enlarging, immobile, worrisome location (supraclavicular or generalized), abnormal imaging/bloodwork, constitutional symptoms (fever, weight loss, night sweats). Benign (infectious) features: fluctuance, warmth, tenderness.

Key investigation: early biopsy if supraclavicular nodes, nodes >4 cm, or groups of nodes with total diameter >3 cm. Late biopsy (within 4 weeks) if increasing in size, or >2 cm with unclear diagnosis or no response to treatment. Supraclavicular lymphadenopathy requires CXR to rule out mediastinal mass.

**Q:** What are the indications for biopsy in pediatric lymphadenopathy?

**A:** Early biopsy: supraclavicular nodes, nodes >4 cm, or groups of nodes with total diameter >3 cm. Late biopsy (within 4 weeks): node increasing in size, node >2 cm with unclear diagnosis, or no response to treatment. Regional lymphadenopathy: biopsy if persistent >4 weeks and/or constitutional symptoms present.

Supraclavicular lymphadenopathy requires CXR to rule out mediastinal mass — often associated with lymphoma. Generalized lymphadenopathy (≥4 body areas) requires CBC, differential, blood culture, inflammatory markers (ESR, CRP), serology, uric acid, LDH, and CXR.

**Q:** What is the most common cause of acute abdomen in children after age 5?

**A:** Appendicitis is the most common cause of acute abdomen after age 5. Clinical features: low-grade fever, abdominal pain, anorexia, N/V (after onset of pain), peritoneal signs (generalized peritonitis is a common presentation in infants/young children). Treatment is surgical. Complications include perforation (common in young children) and abscess.

Young children are at higher risk of perforation because they have difficulty localizing and describing pain, leading to delayed diagnosis. Generalized peritonitis at presentation is therefore more common in infants/young children compared to older children or adults.

**Q:** What risk factors predispose children to cancer and what are the most common predisposing syndromes?

**A:** Chromosomal syndromes (e.g., Trisomy 21/Down syndrome), cancer predisposition syndromes (e.g., Li-Fraumeni syndrome), prior malignancies, neurocutaneous syndromes, immunodeficiency syndromes, family history, exposure to radiation/chemicals/biologic agents.
