The cause of pediatric cancer is rarely known. Overall survival and cure rates have improved remarkably to >80% for many pediatric cancers. Unique treatment considerations exist because therapies can impact growth, development, endocrine function, and fertility.

**Q:** What is the approach to anemia classification based on reticulocyte count and RBC indices?

**A:** Step 1: Decreased reticulocytes = production problem → classify by MCV: Microcytic (IDA, thalassemia, lead poisoning, sideroblastic anemia, anemia of chronic disease), Normocytic (anemia of chronic disease, aplastic anemia, recent blood loss), Macrocytic (megaloblastic — B12/folate deficiency, hypothyroidism, alcohol). Step 2: Increased reticulocytes = hemolysis or blood loss → classify by DAT: DAT+ = immune hemolysis; DAT- = intrinsic (hereditary spherocytosis, SCD, thalassemia, G6PD) or extrinsic non-immune (TTP, HUS).

A 'normal' reticulocyte count in the context of anemia is indicative of an inadequate bone marrow response. True hemolysis produces an elevated absolute reticulocyte count as the marrow compensates. This framework helps identify the underlying mechanism before specific testing.

**Q:** What are the major Jones Criteria for diagnosing Acute Rheumatic Fever?

**A:** Carditis and valvulitis, arthritis, CNS involvement (Sydenham chorea), subcutaneous nodules, and erythema marginatum.

The revised Jones Criteria require 2 major OR 1 major + 2 minor criteria PLUS evidence of preceding GAS infection (history of scarlet fever, positive culture/rapid Ag test, or elevated ASOTs). Recognizing these criteria is essential for diagnosing ARF in Canadian practice.

**Q:** What are the minor Jones Criteria for diagnosing Acute Rheumatic Fever?

**A:** Arthralgia, fever, elevated ESR or CRP, and prolonged PR interval on ECG.

Minor criteria alone are insufficient; they supplement major criteria in diagnosis. Prolonged PR interval reflects subclinical carditis. Elevated acute phase reactants confirm systemic inflammation from GAS-triggered autoimmune response.

**Q:** What evidence of preceding GAS infection is required to diagnose Acute Rheumatic Fever using Jones Criteria?

**A:** History of scarlet fever, positive GAS pharyngitis culture, positive rapid antigen detection test, or elevated ASOTs (anti-streptolysin O titres).

ARF is an autoimmune sequela of GAS infection; demonstrating prior GAS exposure is mandatory for diagnosis. Without microbiological or serological evidence of GAS, the diagnosis cannot be made even if major/minor criteria are present.

**Q:** What is the treatment for acute Rheumatic Fever, including management of severe carditis?

**A:** Penicillin or erythromycin for 10 days (acute GAS eradication); prednisone if severe carditis. Secondary prophylaxis with daily penicillin or erythromycin.

Antibiotics eliminate the inciting GAS organism. Corticosteroids are reserved for severe carditis to reduce inflammation. Long-term secondary prophylaxis prevents recurrent GAS infections, which can cause cumulative valvular damage and rheumatic heart disease.

**Q:** What are the chronic complications of Rheumatic Fever and when do they typically manifest?

**A:** Valvular heart disease (mitral and/or aortic insufficiency/stenosis), infectious endocarditis, thromboembolic phenomena. Onset is usually 10–20 years after acute carditis.

Repeated or severe acute rheumatic carditis leads to scarring and fibrosis of heart valves, most commonly the mitral valve (mitral stenosis is a classic late sequela). This latency period underscores the importance of secondary prophylaxis to prevent further valve damage.

**Q:** What is Post-Streptococcal Glomerulonephritis (PSGN) and what triggers it?

**A:** PSGN is an antigen-antibody mediated, complement-activating diffuse proliferative glomerulonephritis occurring 1–3 weeks after GAS infection (skin or throat), most common in children ages 4–8 years.

Streptococcal antigens form immune complexes that deposit in the glomerulus, activating complement and causing inflammatory injury. Unlike ARF, PSGN can follow skin (impetigo) or throat infection and does not require prophylaxis, as most children recover fully.

**Q:** How is Post-Streptococcal Glomerulonephritis confirmed on investigations?

**A:** Elevated serum antibody titres against streptococcal antigens (ASOT, anti-DNAase B) and low serum complement (C3), combined with clinical features of acute nephritis and recent GAS infection.

Low C3 reflects complement consumption by immune complex deposition. ASOT may be normal after skin infections; anti-DNAase B is more sensitive for skin-associated PSGN. These findings differentiate PSGN from other causes of nephritic syndrome.

**Q:** What is the prognosis and management of Post-Streptococcal Glomerulonephritis in children?

**A:** 95% of children recover completely within 1–2 weeks. Management: fluid/sodium restriction, loop diuretics for hypertension/edema, dialysis if severely impaired. Treat persistent GAS infection with penicillin or erythromycin.

PSGN is generally self-limited in children. Antibiotics do not alter the glomerular course but eradicate ongoing GAS infection. 5–10% have persistent hematuria. Long-term renal sequelae are rare in children, unlike adults.

**Q:** What is the peak age for pediatric meningitis and what are the main causative organisms by age group?

**A:** Peak age <1 year; 90% of cases in children <5 years. 0–28d: GBS, E. coli, Listeria → Ampicillin + cefotaxime. 28–90d: overlap of neonatal + older pathogens → Ceftriaxone + vancomycin (+ ampicillin if immunocompromised). >90d: S. pneumoniae, N. meningitidis, H. influenzae → Ceftriaxone + vancomycin.

Neonates are susceptible to maternal flora (GBS, E. coli, Listeria). Beyond 3 months, encapsulated bacteria predominate. Empiric antibiotic selection must cover likely pathogens for the age group while awaiting culture results.

**Q:** What are the contraindications to lumbar puncture in meningitis, and how should management proceed if LP is contraindicated?

**A:** Contraindications: thrombocytopenia, DIC, signs of raised ICP, unstable patient, known anatomical lumbar abnormalities. If contraindicated: commence empiric antibiotics immediately — do NOT delay antibiotic therapy for LP.

LP is the gold standard for diagnosing bacterial meningitis, but clinical deterioration from performing LP outweighs diagnostic benefit in high-risk patients. Blood cultures should still be drawn. Empiric coverage should not be delayed as mortality increases with each hour of untreated bacterial meningitis.

**Q:** When is adjuvant dexamethasone indicated in pediatric bacterial meningitis?

**A:** Dexamethasone should be given BEFORE antibiotics for H. influenzae type b (Hib) meningitis; consider for children >6 weeks with pneumococcal meningitis.

Dexamethasone reduces inflammation by suppressing cytokine release, decreasing risk of sensorineural hearing loss and neurological sequelae. Timing before antibiotics is critical as antibiotic-induced bacterial lysis releases inflammatory mediators. Evidence is strongest for Hib meningitis.

**Q:** What are the signs of meningismus in children?

**A:** Nuchal rigidity, Kernig's sign (inability to extend knee with hip flexed), Brudzinski's sign (involuntary knee flexion with neck flexion), and opisthotonos (arching of the back).

These signs reflect meningeal irritation from inflammation. Nuchal rigidity occurs due to reflex muscle spasm. These signs may be absent in infants, making clinical diagnosis more challenging. In infants, bulging fontanelle and toxic appearance are more reliable indicators.

**Q:** What are the chronic complications of bacterial meningitis in children?

**A:** Hearing loss (most common), neuromotor/cognitive delay, learning disabilities, neurological deficit, seizure disorder, hydrocephalus.

Sensorineural hearing loss is the most common long-term complication of bacterial meningitis, emphasizing the importance of post-meningitis hearing assessment. Inflammatory damage to the cochlea is irreversible. Hydrocephalus results from impaired CSF absorption at the arachnoid villi.

**Q:** What is the causative agent of mumps, its incubation period, and infectivity window?

**A:** Mumps virus (RNA Rubulavirus, Paramyxoviridae family). Incubation: 14–16 days (range 12–25 days). Infectivity: 7 days before parotitis to 5 days after parotitis onset.

The pre-parotitis infectious period is why mumps spreads before symptoms appear. Droplet precautions must be maintained for 5 days after onset of parotid swelling. Two-dose MMR vaccination has dramatically reduced Ontario incidence to ~25 cases/year.

**Q:** What are the complications of mumps?

**A:** Common: aseptic meningitis, orchitis/oophoritis. Less common: encephalitis, pancreatitis, thyroiditis, myocarditis, arthritis, glomerulonephritis, ocular complications, hearing impairment.

Mumps virus spreads hematogenously to secondary sites (salivary glands, gonads, pancreas, meninges, kidneys, heart, thyroid). Orchitis occurs in post-pubertal males and can cause infertility. Aseptic meningitis is the most common CNS complication.

**Q:** How is mumps diagnosed and managed?

**A:** Clinical diagnosis; confirmed by IgM serology within 4 weeks, PCR, or viral cultures. Management: supportive (analgesics, antipyretics, warm/cold packs). Droplet precautions until 5 days after parotid swelling onset. Admit for serious complications (meningitis, pancreatitis).

Mumps is primarily a clinical diagnosis supported by serology. Management is supportive as no antiviral therapy is available. Lab confirmation is important for public health reporting. Vaccination with two-dose MMR is the key prevention strategy.

**Q:** Describe the three clinical stages of pertussis (whooping cough) and their duration.

**A:** 1. Catarrhal stage: 1–7 days; URTI symptoms (coryza, mild cough), no or low-grade fever. 2. Paroxysmal stage: 4–6 weeks; paroxysms of cough ('100-day cough'), inspiratory whoop, post-tussive emesis. 3. Convalescent stage: 1–2 weeks; decreasing cough frequency, non-infectious.

Pertussis is most contagious during the catarrhal phase when it resembles a common cold. Infants <6 months often lack the characteristic whoop and may present with post-tussive apnea — a life-threatening presentation requiring hospital admission.

**Q:** What is the causative organism of pertussis, the gold standard diagnostic test, and the recommended antibiotic treatment?

**A:** Causative organism: Bordetella pertussis (Gram-negative pleomorphic rod). Gold standard: culture on Regan-Lowe agar from NP specimen (PCR more commonly used in practice). Treatment: macrolide antibiotics (azithromycin, erythromycin, or clarithromycin).

PCR has largely replaced culture due to higher sensitivity and faster turnaround. Macrolides reduce transmission and severity if given in the catarrhal phase; benefit is less clear in the paroxysmal phase. All household contacts require macrolide prophylaxis regardless of vaccination status.

**Q:** What are the indications for hospital admission in a child with pertussis?

**A:** Admit if paroxysms of cough are associated with cyanosis and/or apnea; give supplemental oxygen. Also required for young infants <6 months given high mortality risk.

Infants <6 months have the highest pertussis mortality (~0.3% overall, higher in this age group) and are at greatest risk for life-threatening apnea and cyanosis. Pertussis mortality is highest in infants <6 months — the primary reason for universal infant immunization (Pentacel) and adolescent boosters (Adacel).

**Q:** What is the most common causative organism of pediatric UTI, and what are the most common Gram-negative and Gram-positive organisms?

**A:** Most common overall: E. coli (~70%). Gram-negative bacilli: E. coli, Klebsiella, Proteus, Enterobacter, Pseudomonas, Citrobacter. Gram-positive cocci: S. saprophyticus, Enterococcus.

E. coli dominates pediatric UTI due to its virulence factors enabling urinary tract colonization. S. saprophyticus is more common in sexually active adolescent females. Knowledge of common organisms guides empiric antibiotic selection based on local susceptibility patterns.

**Q:** What urine specimen is acceptable for culture in pediatric UTI, and what colony counts confirm diagnosis?

**A:** Acceptable sterile specimens: clean catch, catheterization (≥50,000 CFU/mL), suprapubic aspiration. Bag specimens: only for urinalysis to RULE OUT diagnosis — never send for culture. Diagnosis confirmed: ≥50,000 CFU/mL on catheter specimen OR ≥100,000 CFU/mL on clean catch.

Bag specimens have high contamination rates and cannot reliably diagnose UTI. Catheterization is preferred in infants and young children. Suprapubic aspiration is the most sterile method but rarely used. Combining urinalysis findings (leukocyte esterase, nitrites) with culture criteria improves diagnostic accuracy.

**Q:** What imaging is recommended after a first febrile UTI in children, and what are the indications for VCUG?

**A:** Renal and bladder ultrasound (RBUS) for all febrile infants <2 years and all ages with recurrent febrile UTIs. VCUG: NOT recommended after first febrile UTI unless RBUS shows hydronephrosis, obstructive uropathies, or signs suggestive of high-grade VUR.

RBUS screens for anatomical abnormalities, hydronephrosis, and renal abscesses. VCUG exposes children to radiation and catheterization, so it is reserved for cases where high-grade VUR is suspected. This approach balances diagnostic yield against procedural risk and radiation exposure.

**Q:** What are the indications for hospital admission in a child with UTI, and what are the empiric antibiotic regimens by age?

**A:** Admit: <2 months, urosepsis, persistent vomiting, inability to tolerate oral meds, moderate-severe dehydration, immunocompromised, complex urologic pathology, failed outpatient therapy. Neonates: IV ampicillin + aminoglycoside. Infants/children (inpatient): IV ampicillin + gentamicin or ceftriaxone monotherapy. Outpatient: oral antibiotics based on local E. coli sensitivity. Duration: 5–10 days.

Neonates require IV therapy due to septicemia risk and inability to tolerate oral medications. Empiric antibiotic selection should be guided by local antibiograms given increasing E. coli resistance to ampicillin. Treatment is tailored once urine culture and sensitivity results are available.

**Q:** What are the classifications of gestational age (GA) at birth?

**A:** Preterm: <37 weeks (extremely preterm <28 wk, very preterm 28–32 wk, moderate-late preterm 32–37 wk). Term: 37–42 weeks. Post-term: >42 weeks.

Classification by GA guides management and helps predict complications. Extremely preterm infants (<28 weeks) face the highest morbidity and mortality. GA can be estimated post-delivery using Dubowitz/Ballard scoring (physical and neuromuscular maturity criteria), accurate to ±2 weeks.

**Q:** What are the birth weight classifications in neonates?

**A:** SGA: <10th percentile or >2 SD below mean for GA. AGA: within 2 SD of mean for GA. LGA: >90th percentile or >2 SD above mean for GA. Low birthweight (LBW) <2500 g, Very LBW <1500 g, Extremely LBW (ELBW) <1000 g.

SGA and LGA infants face different but overlapping risks. SGA is associated with perinatal hypoxia, hypoglycemia, polycythemia. LGA (often due to maternal diabetes) is associated with birth trauma, meconium aspiration, hypoglycemia. These classifications guide monitoring and intervention in the newborn period.

**Q:** What are the common causes and problems associated with preterm birth (<37 weeks)?

**A:** Causes: maternal (HTN, DM, cardiac/renal disease), fetal (multiple pregnancy, congenital abnormalities, RBC isoimmunization), pregnancy issues (placental insufficiency, placenta previa, infection). Problems: RDS, apnea of prematurity, bronchopulmonary dysplasia, NEC, feeding difficulties, hypoglycemia, hypocalcemia, hypothermia, anemia, jaundice, retinopathy of prematurity, ICH/IVH, PDA.

Preterm infants lack sufficient surfactant (RDS), have an immature CNS (apnea), immature gut (NEC), and susceptible retinal vessels (retinopathy of prematurity). PDA is common in preterm due to oxygen-sensitive ductal closure mechanisms. Understanding these complications guides neonatal intensive care management.

**Q:** What are the problems associated with Large for Gestational Age (LGA) infants, and what is the most common cause?

**A:** Most common cause: maternal diabetes. Problems: birth trauma (shoulder dystocia), perinatal depression (meconium aspiration), RDS, transient tachypnea of newborn (TTN), jaundice, polycythemia, hypoglycemia, hypocalcemia.

Maternal hyperglycemia causes fetal hyperinsulinemia, promoting macrosomia. After birth, persistent hyperinsulinism causes hypoglycemia despite no ongoing glucose source. Polycythemia in LGA infants results from hypoxia-driven erythropoiesis. Hypocalcemia in LGA infants of diabetic mothers results from maternal-fetal calcium transfer abnormalities.

**Q:** What are the components of the Apgar score and when is it assessed?

**A:** Assessed at 1 and 5 minutes. Five components (APGAR): Appearance (color), Pulse (heart rate), Grimace (irritability/reflex), Activity (muscle tone), Respiration. Each scored 0–2; total 0–10. If <7 at 5 min, reassess every 5 minutes until >7.

The 1-minute Apgar reflects immediate transition from intrauterine life; the 5-minute score better correlates with neonatal outcome. Resuscitation must NOT be delayed to assign Apgar scores — start resuscitation based on rapid assessment of tone, color, and breathing.

**Q:** What are the steps of initial neonatal resuscitation and when is PPV initiated?

**A:** Warm/dry the newborn (remove wet blankets), stimulate (rub lower back or flick soles), position airway ('sniffing' position), suction if needed, assess breathing and HR. If apneic/ineffective respiration and HR <100: PPV with room air (30% if preterm). If HR <60: effective ventilation + chest compressions + 100% oxygen.

The 2015 neonatal resuscitation guidelines recommend starting with room air (not 100% O2) as hyperoxia can cause oxidative injury. For preterm infants, 30% O2 is used. Chest compressions are reserved for HR <60 despite effective ventilation, at a 3:1 compression:ventilation ratio.

**Q:** What is the dose and indication for epinephrine in neonatal resuscitation?

**A:** Indication: HR <60 not rising despite effective ventilation and chest compressions. IV dose: 0.02 mg/kg (0.2 mL/kg of 1:10,000 solution). Endotracheal dose (while awaiting IV): 0.05–0.1 mg/kg (0.5–1 mL/kg of 1:10,000). Can repeat every 3–5 minutes PRN.

IV access (umbilical vein) is the preferred route; endotracheal epinephrine is less reliable and only used as a bridge. Epinephrine increases cardiac contractility and peripheral vasoconstriction to restore cardiac output. Avoid rapid large-volume infusions to prevent IVH in neonates.

**Q:** What is the definition of neonatal apnea, and what are the three types?

**A:** Apnea: absence of respiratory gas flow for >20 seconds OR less if associated with bradycardia or desaturation. Types: (1) Central — no chest wall movement, no obstruction. (2) Obstructive — chest wall movement against obstructed upper airway, no airflow. (3) Mixed — combination of central and obstructive.

Distinguish from periodic breathing (normal: rapid respirations alternating with 5–10 second pauses). Mixed apnea is most common in preterm infants. Apnea in term infants always requires a full septic workup to exclude infection as the cause.

**Q:** What is apnea of prematurity and how is it treated?

**A:** Apnea of prematurity: occurs in infants <34 weeks due to CNS immaturity; resolves by 36 weeks corrected GA. Treatment: caffeine (methylxanthine) — stimulates CNS and diaphragm. Also: tactile stimulation, O2/ventilatory support. Infants require cardiorespiratory monitoring in NICU and an apnea-free period before discharge.

Caffeine is the preferred methylxanthine due to its wider therapeutic index, once-daily dosing, and evidence supporting improved long-term neurodevelopmental outcomes. Term infants with apnea should NOT receive caffeine — a full diagnostic workup is mandatory to identify treatable underlying causes.

**Q:** What is Neonatal Alloimmune Thrombocytopenia (NAIT), and how is it treated?

**A:** NAIT: maternal IgG antibodies against HPA antigens on fetal platelets (platelet equivalent of Rh disease). Affects 1 in 4000–5000 births. Presentation: petechiae, purpura, thrombocytopenia in an otherwise healthy neonate; can cause ICH. Treatment: platelet transfusion (HPA-specific preferred, random donor acceptable); IVIG to mother prenatally (starts second trimester) + steroids + fetal platelet transfusions; consider IVIG to neonate.

Unlike autoimmune thrombocytopenia, NAIT can occur in the first pregnancy because the fetal platelet antigen (usually HPA-1a) is paternally inherited. ICH is the most serious complication and can cause stillbirth or severe neurological damage. Maternal washed platelets are effective but logistically difficult.

**Q:** How does neonatal autoimmune thrombocytopenia differ from NAIT in pathophysiology, severity, and treatment?

**A:** Autoimmune thrombocytopenia: caused by maternal ITP or SLE; passive transfer of anti-platelet antibodies across placenta. Usually LESS severe than NAIT and less likely to cause bleeding. Treatment: steroids or IVIG to mother 10–14 days pre-delivery; IVIG to neonate if active bleeding, platelets <20,000 (or <50,000 in sick/premature). Platelet transfusion only in severe cases (antibodies will destroy transfused platelets).

The key distinction is that autoimmune thrombocytopenia involves antibodies against self-platelet antigens (HPA-positive in both mother and fetus), so the titer tends to be lower and disease milder. NAIT involves antibodies against foreign (paternal) antigens on fetal platelets, often causing more severe thrombocytopenia.

**Q:** What is Bronchopulmonary Dysplasia (BPD) and how is it classified?

**A:** BPD (neonatal chronic lung disease): O2 dependency persisting to 28 days of life, further classified at 36 weeks corrected GA. Mild: weaned from O2 by 36 weeks. Moderate: requires up to 30% O2 at 36 weeks. Severe: requires >30% O2, CPAP, or mechanical ventilation at 36 weeks. Caused by damage from prolonged ventilation, high O2, and infection.

BPD results from the combined injury of mechanical ventilation, oxidative stress, and inflammation to the immature developing lung. The 36-week classification standardizes severity across different gestational ages. Dexamethasone reduces inflammation but is associated with adverse neurodevelopmental outcomes — significant practice variation exists.

**Q:** What are the long-term outcomes of Bronchopulmonary Dysplasia (BPD)?

**A:** Pulmonary HTN, poor growth, right-sided heart failure (cor pulmonale). Significant respiratory impairment persisting into adolescence. Airway obstruction, airway hyper-reactivity, and emphysema may persist into adulthood. Increased risk of adverse neurodevelopmental outcomes.

BPD is one of the most significant long-term morbidities of preterm birth. Pulmonary HTN from chronic hypoxemia leads to right heart strain. The neurodevelopmental risk may be partly related to corticosteroid exposure used in BPD management, complicating treatment decisions.

**Q:** What newborn screening tests are performed in Ontario?

**A:** Metabolic disorders (amino acid disorders, organic acid disorders, fatty acid oxidation defects, biotinidase deficiency, galactosemia), blood disorders (sickle cell disease, hemoglobinopathies), endocrine disorders (CAH, congenital hypothyroidism), others (CF, SCID), congenital hearing loss, critical congenital heart disease (CCHD), spinal muscular atrophy (SMA), congenital CMV. Parent screening for biliary atresia.

Ontario's newborn screening program detects treatable conditions before symptoms appear. Early identification of congenital hypothyroidism prevents intellectual disability; early identification of SCID allows life-saving HSCT before infections occur. CCHD screening uses pulse oximetry post-ductal.

**Q:** What routine prophylaxis is given to all newborns at birth and why?

**A:** Vitamin K IM: prophylaxis against Vitamin K Deficiency Bleeding (VKDB). Erythromycin eye ointment: prophylaxis against ophthalmia neonatorum (N. gonorrhoeae) — no longer recommended by CPS but required by law in some provinces.

Newborns have low vitamin K stores and sterile gut (no bacterial vitamin K synthesis), making them susceptible to VKDB including intracranial hemorrhage. IM vitamin K is more effective than oral. Erythromycin eye prophylaxis prevents gonococcal conjunctivitis which can cause corneal scarring and blindness.

**Q:** What are the cardinal signs of orbital cellulitis (as opposed to periorbital/preseptal cellulitis)?

**A:** Ophthalmoplegia/diplopia, proptosis, decreased visual acuity, pain with extraocular eye movement.

Orbital cellulitis (postseptal) involves infection posterior to the orbital septum, affecting the extraocular muscles and compressing the optic nerve. These features distinguish it from periorbital (preseptal) cellulitis, which is anterior to the septum and does not affect eye movement or vision. Orbital cellulitis is a surgical emergency.

**Q:** When should antibiotics be prescribed for sinusitis in children, and what is the first-line antibiotic?

**A:** Antibiotics (amoxicillin) are indicated for ALL children if symptoms persist >10 days OR if symptoms are worsening/severe. Most viral sinusitis resolves spontaneously within a few weeks without antibiotics.

Sinusitis complicates 10% of pediatric URTIs. Diagnostic imaging is NOT required for diagnosis in children. CT is reserved for suspected complications, recurrent disease, or pre-surgical planning. Complications include orbital cellulitis, brain abscess, meningitis, and Pott's Puffy tumour (frontal osteomyelitis with subperiosteal abscess).

**Q:** What is Pott's Puffy Tumour and how is it managed?

**A:** Pott's Puffy Tumour: complication of sinusitis presenting with tender, soft tissue erythematous swelling of the forehead, headache, photophobia, and fever. Results from frontal sinusitis causing osteomyelitis with subperiosteal abscess. Managed with IV antibiotics and ENT consult.

Pott's Puffy Tumour is a rare but serious intracranial complication of sinusitis — osteomyelitis of the frontal bone with associated subperiosteal abscess. It can also be associated with intracranial complications (epidural abscess, subdural empyema, cerebral abscess). Surgical drainage is often required alongside IV antibiotics.

**Q:** A 6-year-old presents 2 weeks after a GAS throat infection with cola-coloured urine, facial puffiness, and hypertension. Investigations show low C3, elevated ASOT, and RBC casts on urinalysis. What is the diagnosis and expected prognosis?

**A:** Post-Streptococcal Glomerulonephritis (PSGN). Prognosis: excellent — 95% of children recover completely within 1–2 weeks; 5–10% have persistent hematuria.

The classic triad of hematuria (cola/tea-coloured urine), hypertension, and edema following GAS infection 1–3 weeks prior, combined with low C3 and elevated streptococcal antibodies, is diagnostic of PSGN. Management is supportive. This contrasts with IgA nephropathy where hematuria occurs during (not after) infection.

**Q:** A 3-week-old term infant presents with a petechial rash and platelet count of 12,000/μL but is otherwise well. Mother has no known platelet disorder. What is the most likely diagnosis and management?

**A:** Most likely: Neonatal Alloimmune Thrombocytopenia (NAIT). Management: platelet transfusion (HPA-specific platelets preferred; random donor acceptable). Consider IVIG. Obtain maternal and paternal platelet typing and platelet alloantibody identification.

NAIT presents in otherwise well neonates (unlike sepsis-associated thrombocytopenia). A mother without ITP/SLE suggests alloimmune rather than autoimmune cause. HPA-1a is the most common antigen involved. ICH risk is high with platelets <30,000 — immediate transfusion is warranted. Prenatal IVIG and steroids are given in subsequent pregnancies.

**Q:** What is the m-CENTOR score used for, and what are its components in pediatric practice?

**A:** Used to estimate probability of streptococcal pharyngitis. Components: Cough (absent = +1), Exudates or tonsillar swelling (+1), Nodes (anterior cervical lymphadenopathy = +1), Only Young (<15 years = +1), Rarely Elder (>45 years = -1).

The m-CENTOR score guides decision-making for GAS pharyngitis: score 0–1 suggests no testing or antibiotics; score 2–3 suggests rapid strep test; score ≥4 suggests rapid strep test and consider antibiotics. This prevents inappropriate antibiotic prescribing for viral pharyngitis.

**Q:** What is the hyperoxia test and how do you interpret results in a cyanotic neonate?

**A:** Obtain baseline PaO2 in room air, then PaO2 on 100% O2 after 10-15 min. PaO2 <150 mmHg suggests cyanotic CHD or PPHN; PaO2 >150 mmHg suggests respiratory or non-cardiac cause.

The hyperoxia test distinguishes cardiac from respiratory causes of cyanosis. In cyanotic CHD, the R→L shunt bypasses the lungs entirely, so supplemental O2 cannot meaningfully raise PaO2. PPHN behaves similarly due to persistent fetal circulation. Respiratory causes improve with supplemental oxygen.

**Q:** What are the classic clinical features of congenital diaphragmatic hernia (CDH) on physical examination?

**A:** Scaphoid abdomen, barrel-shaped chest, dull percussion and absent breath sounds on affected side (may hear bowel sounds instead), heart sounds shifted contralaterally (if left-sided), asymmetric chest movements, trachea deviated away from affected side.

CDH results from herniation of abdominal organs into the thorax through a developmental diaphragmatic defect, typically on the left (Bochdalek). The displaced bowel causes the characteristic scaphoid abdomen. Associated pulmonary hypoplasia and PPHN are major sources of morbidity.

**Q:** Why is bag-mask ventilation contraindicated in a newborn with suspected congenital diaphragmatic hernia?

**A:** Air will enter the stomach and herniated bowel, further compressing the already hypoplastic lungs, worsening respiratory status.

Immediate endotracheal intubation is required at birth. A large-bore orogastric tube should be placed to decompress the bowel. Bag-mask ventilation risks rapid distension of intrathoracic gut, causing critical lung compression in an infant who already has severely limited pulmonary reserve.

**Q:** What is the definition of neonatal hypoglycemia?

**A:** Blood glucose <2.6 mmol/L within the first 72 hours of life, OR <3.3 mmol/L after the first 72 hours of life.

Neonates have limited glycogen stores and immature counter-regulatory mechanisms. At-risk infants (infants of diabetic mothers, LGA, SGA, preterm) require pre-feed glucose monitoring. Symptomatic hypoglycemia manifests as jitteriness, lethargy, poor feeding, apnea, or seizures.

**Q:** A neonate with persistent hypoglycemia (>72 h, glucose <2.8 mmol/L) is being worked up. What 'critical blood work' should be sent during the hypoglycemic episode?

**A:** Confirmatory plasma glucose, ABG, ammonia, beta-hydroxybutyrate, cortisol, carnitine, acylcarnitine profiling, free fatty acids, GH, insulin, lactate, urine dipstick for ketones.

Persistent neonatal hypoglycemia beyond 72 hours requires investigation for underlying causes including hyperinsulinism (elevated insulin:glucose ratio), hormone deficiencies (low cortisol, GH), and inborn errors of metabolism (fatty acid oxidation defects, galactosemia). Critical sample must be drawn during the hypoglycemic episode for meaningful interpretation.

**Q:** What causes neonatal hypoglycemia in infants of mothers who took labetalol during pregnancy?

**A:** Labetalol is a beta-blocker; beta-blockade impairs glycogenolysis and gluconeogenesis, blunting the counter-regulatory epinephrine response and suppressing glucose mobilization.

Labetalol is commonly used for hypertension in preeclampsia. Beta-adrenergic blockade inhibits catecholamine-mediated glycogenolysis in the liver and glucagon release from the pancreas, predisposing neonates to hypoglycemia, particularly in the first hours of life.

**Q:** When is neonatal jaundice always considered pathological?

**A:** Jaundice occurring within the first 24 hours of life, and any conjugated hyperbilirubinemia at any age, are always pathological.

Physiologic jaundice does not appear until day 3-4 in term neonates. Jaundice in the first 24 hours suggests hemolytic disease (Rh incompatibility, ABO incompatibility), sepsis, or TORCH infection. Conjugated (direct) hyperbilirubinemia always indicates an underlying pathology such as biliary atresia, neonatal hepatitis, or metabolic disease.

**Q:** What are the timing characteristics of physiologic jaundice in term vs. premature neonates?

**A:** Term infants: onset 3-4 days of life, resolution by 10 days. Premature infants: higher peak bilirubin and longer duration.

Physiologic jaundice results from increased bilirubin load (high hematocrit, shortened RBC lifespan), immature hepatic glucuronyl transferase system (reduced conjugation capacity), and increased enterohepatic circulation. Premature infants have even more immature enzyme systems and higher bilirubin burdens.

**Q:** Distinguish breastfeeding jaundice from breast milk jaundice.

**A:** Breastfeeding jaundice: early onset due to inadequate milk intake → dehydration → exaggerated physiologic jaundice. Breast milk jaundice: onset day 7, peak at 2-3 weeks, resolves by 6 weeks; caused by a glucuronyl transferase inhibitor in breast milk; affects 1 in 200 breastfed infants.

These are distinct entities. Breastfeeding jaundice is a volume/hydration problem managed by improving latch and feeding frequency. Breast milk jaundice is a biochemical phenomenon where a substance in mature breast milk inhibits hepatic conjugation. In breast milk jaundice, interrupting breastfeeding for 24-48 hours leads to a rapid decline in bilirubin, confirming the diagnosis.

**Q:** What investigations are indicated in a neonate with unconjugated hyperbilirubinemia?

**A:** Hemolytic workup: CBC, reticulocyte count, blood group (mother and infant), peripheral blood smear, Coombs test (DAT). If unwell/febrile: septic workup (CBC + differential, blood/urine cultures, LP, CXR). Others: G6PD screen (especially males), TSH.

The initial goal is to identify hemolytic causes (most urgent) vs. non-hemolytic causes. ABO/Rh incompatibility and G6PD deficiency are the most common immune and enzymatic causes. TSH is checked because hypothyroidism can present with prolonged unconjugated jaundice. G6PD deficiency particularly affects males (X-linked).

**Q:** What investigations should be considered for conjugated hyperbilirubinemia in a neonate?

**A:** Liver enzymes (AST, ALT), coagulation studies (PT, PTT), serum albumin, ammonia, TSH, TORCH screen, septic workup, galactosemia screen (erythrocyte galactose-1-phosphate uridyltransferase), metabolic screen, abdominal U/S, HIDA scan, sweat chloride.

Conjugated hyperbilirubinemia must be investigated without delay. Key diagnoses to exclude include biliary atresia (urgent—Kasai procedure most successful if done before 8 weeks), neonatal hepatitis, metabolic disorders (galactosemia, alpha-1-antitrypsin deficiency), and hypothyroidism. HIDA scan assesses bile excretion.

**Q:** How does phototherapy work in neonatal unconjugated hyperbilirubinemia, and what are its contraindications?

**A:** Blue-green wavelength light converts insoluble unconjugated bilirubin to excretable form via photoisomerization. Contraindicated in conjugated hyperbilirubinemia (causes 'bronzed baby' syndrome due to accumulation of a toxic photoproduct).

Photoisomerization converts bilirubin to water-soluble lumirubin and configurational isomers excreted in bile/urine without conjugation. The effect is reversible, so bilirubin may rebound after phototherapy is stopped. Eye shields are used routinely to prevent retinal damage. Side effects include skin rash, diarrhea, and dehydration.

**Q:** What is kernicterus and at what bilirubin level does risk significantly increase?

**A:** Kernicterus is permanent brain damage (basal ganglia and brainstem necrosis) from unconjugated bilirubin deposition when it exceeds albumin binding capacity. Incidence increases significantly above 340 µmol/L.

Unbound (free) unconjugated bilirubin crosses the blood-brain barrier. Risk is increased at lower bilirubin levels in the presence of sepsis, meningitis, hemolysis, hypoxia, acidosis, hypothermia, hypoglycemia, and prematurity, all of which reduce albumin binding or disrupt the blood-brain barrier.

**Q:** What are the stages of acute bilirubin encephalopathy?

**A:** Early: lethargy, hypotonia, poor feeding, emesis. Intermediate: hypertonia, high-pitched cry, opisthotonic posturing (back arching), bulging fontanelle. Late: apnea, inability to feed, seizures, hypertonia, weak/absent cry, death.

Acute bilirubin encephalopathy is a medical emergency. Progression through the stages reflects increasing bilirubin deposition in the basal ganglia and brainstem. Chronic bilirubin encephalopathy (in survivors) manifests as hypotonia, delayed motor skills, choreoathetoid cerebral palsy, gaze palsy, and sensorineural hearing loss.

**Q:** What is biliary atresia, how does it present, and what is the definitive surgical treatment?

**A:** Progressive obliterative cholangiopathy of extrahepatic bile ducts leading to conjugated hyperbilirubinemia. Presents with dark urine, pale stools, persistent jaundice >2 weeks, hepatomegaly, abdominal distension. Treatment: hepatoportoenterostomy (Kasai procedure), most successful if performed before 8 weeks of age; 2/3 eventually require liver transplantation.

Biliary atresia affects 1 in 10,000-15,000 births and is the most common indication for pediatric liver transplantation. Early diagnosis is critical because the Kasai procedure has the best outcomes before 8 weeks. Postoperative management includes fat-soluble vitamins (A, D, E, K) and medium-chain triglyceride-enriched diet due to fat malabsorption from cholestasis.

**Q:** What is necrotizing enterocolitis (NEC) and what is its hallmark radiologic finding?

**A:** NEC is intestinal inflammation with focal or diffuse ulceration/necrosis, primarily affecting the terminal ileum and colon. The hallmark AXR finding is pneumatosis intestinalis (intramural air).

NEC predominantly affects preterm/VLBW infants (1-5% of NICU admissions). Pathophysiology involves immature gut defenses, mucosal ischemia, enteral feeding, and bacterial overgrowth. Additional AXR findings include portal venous gas, free intraperitoneal air (perforation), and fixed bowel loops. Abdominal U/S with Doppler may be more sensitive than X-rays.

**Q:** What are the risk factors for necrotizing enterocolitis?

**A:** Prematurity (most important), low birth weight, asphyxia/shock (poor bowel perfusion), hyperosmolar feeds, formula feeding (breast milk is protective), sepsis, congenital heart disease, IUGR.

Breast milk is protective in NEC, likely due to secretory IgA, oligosaccharides, lactoferrin, and beneficial microbiome modulation. The modified Bell criteria are used to stage NEC severity. Perforation carries ~40% mortality, managed with peritoneal drain or surgical resection of necrotic bowel.

**Q:** What is the empiric antibiotic regimen for NEC?

**A:** Ampicillin + gentamicin + metronidazole (metronidazole added if risk of perforation); for 7-10 days. NPO for 7-10 days, NG tube decompression, IV fluids, TPN.

Metronidazole provides anaerobic coverage important if gut perforation is suspected. Serial AXRs are essential for early detection of perforation. Surgical intervention (peritoneal drain or bowel resection) is required for perforation or failure of medical management.

**Q:** What is the pathophysiology of persistent pulmonary hypertension of the newborn (PPHN)?

**A:** Persistent elevation of pulmonary vascular resistance causes R→L shunt through PDA and patent foramen ovale, leading to decreased pulmonary blood flow and hypoxemia, which further promotes pulmonary vasoconstriction (vicious cycle).

PPHN represents failure of the normal circulatory transition at birth. In utero, the foramen ovale and ductus arteriosus are patent with high PVR. At birth, lung expansion and oxygenation normally reduce PVR. Secondary PPHN is associated with asphyxia, MAS, RDS, sepsis, pneumonia, and structural abnormalities (diaphragmatic hernia, pulmonary hypoplasia).

**Q:** How is PPHN diagnosed and what are the key treatment modalities?

**A:** Diagnosis: pre/post-ductal O2 gradient, hyperoxia test to exclude CHD, echocardiogram (confirms elevated pulmonary pressures and R→L shunt, rules out cardiac defects). Treatment: maintain SaO2 >95%, O2 supplementation, mechanical ventilation (including high-frequency oscillation), sedation, minimize handling, milrinone (pressor), inhaled nitric oxide, surfactant, ECMO if refractory.

A pre-ductal (right hand) vs. post-ductal (foot) SpO2 difference >10% confirms the right-to-left ductal shunt. Inhaled nitric oxide is a selective pulmonary vasodilator and is a cornerstone of PPHN treatment. ECMO is reserved for cases failing other therapies at specialized centres.

**Q:** Compare the CXR findings in RDS, TTN, and MAS.

**A:** RDS: homogeneous infiltrates, air bronchograms, decreased lung volumes; severe = 'white-out'. TTN: perihilar infiltrates, fluid in fissures ('wet silhouette'), normal to increased lung volumes. MAS: hyperinflation, patchy atelectasis, coarse patchy infiltrates; 10-20% have pneumothorax.

RDS (hyaline membrane disease) is due to surfactant deficiency in preterm neonates. TTN ('wet lung') results from delayed resorption of fetal lung fluid and typically resolves in 24-72 hours. MAS occurs in term/post-term infants and involves airway obstruction plus chemical inflammation from meconium, leading to air trapping and pneumothorax risk.

**Q:** What is the gestational age distribution and primary prevention for RDS vs. TTN?

**A:** RDS: preterm infants. Prevention: prenatal corticosteroids (e.g., betamethasone 12 mg q24h x2 doses) if risk of preterm delivery <34 wks; monitor L/S ratio (>2:1 indicates lung maturity). TTN: term and late preterm, associated with elective Cesarean (especially before 38 weeks GA). Prevention: avoid elective cesarean before 38 weeks.

Prenatal corticosteroids accelerate fetal lung maturation by inducing surfactant synthesis. The L/S (lecithin:sphingomyelin) ratio from amniocentesis assesses lung maturity. TTN is more common after elective cesarean because the 'thoracic squeeze' during vaginal delivery helps expel fetal lung fluid.

**Q:** What is the treatment for RDS and MAS in neonates?

**A:** RDS: resuscitation, oxygen, ventilation, exogenous surfactant (decreases alveolar surface tension, improves lung compliance, maintains FRC). MAS: resuscitation, oxygen, ventilatory support, surfactant, inhaled nitric oxide, ECMO for associated PPHN.

Exogenous surfactant is the cornerstone of RDS treatment. In MAS, meconium inactivates endogenous surfactant, providing rationale for exogenous surfactant replacement. If the infant is depressed at birth with meconium-stained fluid, intubation and tracheal suctioning can be done based on clinical judgment (not mandatory in current NRP sequence).

**Q:** What are the pathogens of early-onset vs. late-onset neonatal sepsis?

**A:** Early onset (<72 h): GBS, E. coli, Listeria monocytogenes (vertical transmission). Late onset (72 h-28 d): coagulase-negative Staphylococcus (most common in NICU), GBS, anaerobes, E. coli, Klebsiella (acquired after birth).

GBS is the leading cause of early-onset neonatal sepsis and meningitis. E. coli is significant, particularly in preterm neonates. Late-onset sepsis in NICU infants is dominated by coagulase-negative Staphylococcus (S. epidermidis) due to central-line colonization. Listeria should be considered in early-onset sepsis with maternal exposure to unpasteurized food.

**Q:** What is the empiric antibiotic regimen for early-onset neonatal sepsis vs. suspected neonatal meningitis?

**A:** Early-onset sepsis (non-meningitic): IV ampicillin + gentamicin. Suspected meningitis or late-onset sepsis: IV ampicillin + cefotaxime + vancomycin at meningitic doses. Add IV acyclovir if HSV infection is suspected.

Ampicillin covers GBS, Listeria, and many gram-negatives. Gentamicin provides synergistic gram-negative coverage. Cefotaxime achieves better CSF penetration than gentamicin for meningitis. Vancomycin covers resistant gram-positive organisms common in late-onset NICU sepsis. Acyclovir is added empirically when HSV encephalitis/sepsis is possible (skin/oral lesions, maternal HSV history).

**Q:** What CBC findings suggest neonatal sepsis?

**A:** WBC <5 x 10^9/L or >30 x 10^9/L (or left-shifted), low absolute neutrophil count (ANC) <1.5 x 10^9/L. No single CBC value reliably identifies occult bacteremia in infants <3 months.

Both leukopenia and leukocytosis can indicate sepsis in neonates. A left shift (increased band neutrophils) reflects bone marrow stress response to infection. The immature-to-total neutrophil ratio (I:T ratio) >0.2 is also used clinically. No individual CBC parameter has sufficient sensitivity and specificity alone to rule in or rule out neonatal sepsis.

**Q:** What is congenital dermal melanocytosis (Mongolian spot) and what is its clinical significance?

**A:** Slate grey patches, usually over the lower back/buttocks/lower limbs, caused by dermal melanocytes. Prevalence: Asian > Black > Hispanic > White. Typically fades within first 2 years. Benign; may be mistaken for bruising—important to document to avoid false accusations of child abuse.

Congenital dermal melanocytosis represents entrapped melanocytes in the dermis during neural crest migration. The slate-grey colour is due to the Tyndall effect. Documentation at birth is critical because the appearance can mimic inflicted injury bruising.

**Q:** What is erythema toxicum neonatorum and how is it distinguished from serious neonatal skin conditions?

**A:** Common benign rash consisting of yellow-white papules/pustules surrounded by erythema; eosinophils within lesions on smear. Resolves in 5-7 days. Distinguished from infection by the eosinophil-predominant smear and the absence of systemic illness.

Erythema toxicum neonatorum occurs in up to 50% of term newborns. The etiology is unknown. Wright stain of pustule contents showing eosinophils distinguishes it from neonatal herpes (multinucleated giant cells) and bacterial pustulosis (neutrophils). No treatment is required.

**Q:** What is hemolytic uremic syndrome (HUS) and what is the classic triad?

**A:** Simultaneous occurrence of: (1) non-immune microangiopathic hemolytic anemia, (2) thrombocytopenia, (3) acute renal injury. Most common cause of acute renal failure in children.

STEC-HUS (90% of pediatric HUS) is caused by Shiga toxin-producing E. coli O157:H7. Shiga toxin damages colonic and renal endothelial cells, triggering platelet/fibrin thrombi in the microvasculature. RBCs forced through occluded vessels become fragmented (schistocytes), seen on peripheral smear.

**Q:** Why are antibiotics contraindicated in STEC-HUS (E. coli O157:H7 infection)?

**A:** Antibiotics promote bacterial lysis, releasing large amounts of Shiga toxin, which can worsen the systemic toxin burden and increase risk of HUS development.

Several observational studies suggest antibiotic use for E. coli O157:H7 diarrhea increases HUS risk, likely through toxin release. NSAIDs and antidiarrheal agents are also avoided (NSAIDs due to renal toxicity; antidiarrheals because they prolong toxin exposure). Supportive care is the mainstay of STEC-HUS treatment.

**Q:** What is the treatment for atypical HUS vs. STEC-HUS?

**A:** STEC-HUS: supportive care (nutrition, hydration, blood products), dialysis if needed; avoid antibiotics, NSAIDs, antidiarrheal agents. Atypical HUS: plasma exchange or eculizumab (complement inhibitor).

Atypical HUS is caused by dysregulation of the complement pathway (hereditary mutations) or non-Shiga toxin infections (S. pneumoniae, HIV). Eculizumab is a monoclonal antibody against complement C5 that prevents terminal complement activation, and is now standard of care for atypical HUS. Atypical HUS has a much worse prognosis (50% death or dialysis-dependent).

**Q:** What defines nephritic syndrome and what are its key urinalysis findings?

**A:** Glomerular injury/inflammation characterized by hematuria (>5 RBCs/HPF), dysmorphic RBCs, and RBC casts on urinalysis, often with proteinuria (<50 mg/kg/d), edema, HTN, azotemia, and oliguria.

RBC casts are pathognomonic of glomerulonephritis. Dysmorphic RBCs (acanthocytes) result from RBCs being deformed as they pass through damaged glomerular basement membranes. Nephritic syndrome is most common in children ages 5-15 years. The mechanism involves humoral immune response → immunoglobulin deposition → complement activation → glomerular inflammation.

**Q:** What are the major causes of nephritic syndrome with decreased C3 vs. normal C3?

**A:** Decreased C3: post-infectious GN (most common—post-streptococcal), membranoproliferative GN (types I and II), SLE, bacterial endocarditis, shunt nephritis, cryoglobulinemia. Normal C3: IgA nephropathy, HSP (very common in pediatrics), anti-GBM disease, ANCA-associated vasculitis (granulomatosis with polyangiitis, polyarteritis nodosa), Goodpasture's syndrome.

Complement levels are a key discriminating point in the workup of nephritic syndrome. Low C3 indicates complement consumption in the pathogenesis. Post-streptococcal GN has the best prognosis among primary nephritic syndromes. IgA nephropathy (Berger disease) is the most common worldwide cause of GN and has normal complement.

**Q:** What investigations are used to confirm a recent streptococcal infection in post-infectious GN?

**A:** ASOT (anti-streptolysin O titre), anti-hyaluronidase, anti-streptokinase, anti-NAD, anti-DNAse B antibodies.

Post-streptococcal GN typically follows pharyngitis (2 weeks) or impetigo (4-6 weeks). A single negative ASOT does not exclude streptococcal etiology because it may not rise after skin infections (use anti-DNAse B for impetigo). C3 is depressed in the acute phase and normalizes within 6-8 weeks; persistent low C3 beyond 8 weeks should prompt evaluation for other causes (MPGN, SLE).

**Q:** When is renal biopsy indicated in nephritic syndrome?

**A:** Renal biopsy should be considered when there is acute renal failure, no evidence of streptococcal infection, and normal C3/C4.

When the clinical picture clearly fits post-streptococcal GN (elevated streptococcal titres, decreased C3, appropriate timeline), biopsy is generally not needed. However, if the etiology is uncertain, ARF is present, or C3 remains persistently low, biopsy is necessary to guide immunosuppressive therapy and determine prognosis.

**Q:** Why are ACE inhibitors and ARBs avoided in acute nephritic syndrome with hypertension?

**A:** In acute nephritic syndrome, ACEIs/ARBs may further decrease GFR (which is already impaired due to glomerular inflammation), potentially worsening renal function. They are appropriate for chronic persistent HTN in nephritic syndrome, not acute cases.

In the acute phase, efferent arteriolar dilation by RAAS blockade risks precipitous GFR decline in already compromised kidneys. Acute HTN management prioritizes salt and fluid restriction. In contrast, chronic nephritic syndrome with persistent HTN benefits from RAAS blockade for both BP control and renoprotection (reducing proteinuria).

**Q:** Define nephrotic syndrome and give the mnemonic for its key features.

**A:** Clinical syndrome characterized by significant proteinuria (>50 mg/kg/d), peripheral edema, hypoalbuminemia (<20 g/L), and hyperlipidemia. Mnemonic: HELP — Hypoalbuminemia, Edema, Lipids elevated, Proteinuria.

Massive urinary protein loss (primarily albumin) drives the pathophysiology: reduced oncotic pressure → edema; liver compensatory hyperlipidemia (decreased catabolism, increased VLDL synthesis) → hyperlipidemia; loss of clotting factors and immunoglobulins → increased infection and thrombosis risk. Most common in children ages 2-6 years.

**Q:** What is the most common cause of nephrotic syndrome in children and how is it confirmed?

**A:** Minimal change disease (MCD), accounting for ~85% of primary nephrotic syndrome in pediatrics. Diagnosed clinically when steroid-responsive; renal biopsy shows no glomerular changes on light microscopy (effacement of podocyte foot processes on electron microscopy).

MCD is idiopathic (primary nephrotic syndrome without systemic disease). Characterized by absence of glomerular inflammation on biopsy. The hallmark is dramatic steroid responsiveness, so most children are treated empirically with corticosteroids without initial biopsy. Biopsy is reserved for atypical presentations or steroid-resistant cases.

**Q:** What are the secondary causes of nephrotic syndrome in pediatrics?

**A:** Autoimmune: SLE, DM, juvenile idiopathic arthritis. Genetic: sickle cell disease, Alport syndrome. Infections: hepatitis B/C, post-streptococcal, infective endocarditis, HUS, HIV. Malignancies: leukemia, lymphoma. Medications: captopril, penicillamine, NSAIDs, antiepileptics. Vasculitides: HSP, granulomatosis with polyangiitis.

Secondary nephrotic syndrome accounts for <10% of pediatric cases. In contrast to MCD, these require treatment of the underlying condition. HSP (Henoch-Schönlein Purpura/IgA vasculitis) is a very common pediatric vasculitis that can cause nephritic and/or nephrotic syndrome.

**Q:** What is the annual incidence of HUS in Canada and what is the E. coli serotype most commonly responsible?

**A:** Annual incidence: 1-2 per 100,000 in Canada. Most common E. coli serotype: O157:H7 (Shiga toxin-producing E. coli, STEC).

STEC-HUS accounts for 90% of pediatric HUS in Canada. O157:H7 produces Shiga toxins 1 and 2, with Stx2 being more nephrotoxic. Outbreaks are linked to contaminated ground beef, unpasteurized apple cider, sprouts, and contaminated water. The incubation period is 3-5 days, and HUS develops 5-10 days after the onset of diarrhea.

**Q:** What neonatal skin condition appearing as firm white pearly papules on the nasal bridge is a normal finding?

**A:** Milia — 1-2 mm firm white pearly papules on the nasal bridge, cheeks, and palate; self-resolve within the first few weeks of life.

Milia are benign epidermal inclusion cysts arising from retained keratin within immature pilosebaceous follicles. They are found in up to 50% of newborns and require no treatment. They differ from Epstein pearls (similar white papules on the gingiva/palate) but both are normal and self-resolving.

**Q:** What neonatal conditions can present with a flank mass?

**A:** Hydronephrosis, polycystic kidney disease (autosomal dominant or recessive), and renal tumour (e.g., Wilms tumour).

A flank mass in a neonate is most commonly hydronephrosis (usually detected on antenatal U/S). Bilateral flank masses suggest polycystic kidney disease—ARPKD presents at birth with massively enlarged kidneys and is associated with Potter sequence (oligohydramnios, pulmonary hypoplasia, characteristic facies). A unilateral mass in an older child warrants urgent evaluation for Wilms tumour.

**Q:** What is the prognosis of STEC-HUS vs. atypical HUS in children?

**A:** STEC-HUS: <5% mortality; 30% develop long-term renal damage (HTN, proteinuria, decreased GFR). Atypical HUS: much worse prognosis; 50% of cases result in death or dialysis-dependent renal disease.

Long-term follow-up after STEC-HUS should include annual BP monitoring and urinalysis to detect chronic kidney disease. Atypical HUS has a poor prognosis because complement dysregulation causes relapsing or chronic TMA. Eculizumab has significantly improved outcomes in atypical HUS by blocking terminal complement activation.

## Neonatology

**Q:** What is the Apgar score and what does it assess?

**A:** Assessed at 1 and 5 minutes. Scores 0-2 for each of 5 parameters: Appearance (color), Pulse (heart rate), Grimace (reflex irritability), Activity (muscle tone), Respiration. Maximum score is 10.

If <7 at 5 minutes, reassess every 5 minutes until ≥7. However, do not wait for Apgar scoring before initiating resuscitation. Approximately 10% of newborns require breathing assistance after delivery.

**Q:** What newborn screening tests are included in Ontario?

**A:** Metabolic disorders (amino acid, organic acid, fatty acid oxidation), blood disorders (sickle cell, hemoglobinopathies), endocrine disorders (CAH, congenital hypothyroidism), CF, SCID, congenital hearing loss, critical CHD, SMA, and congenital CMV.

Early detection through newborn screening allows treatment before irreversible damage occurs. For example, treating congenital hypothyroidism within 1-2 months of birth yields excellent outcomes.

**Q:** What is the definition and management of neonatal hypoglycemia?

**A:** Glucose <2.6 mmol/L within 72h of birth or <3.3 mmol/L after 72h. Identify at-risk infants (IDM, LGA, SGA, preterm), begin oral feeds early, check glucose at 2h. If symptomatic: IV D10W.

Signs are non-specific: jitteriness, lethargy, poor feeding, tremors, seizures. Persistent hypoglycemia requires 'critical blood work' during an episode: glucose, insulin, cortisol, GH, lactate, ammonia, ketones, and free fatty acids.

**Q:** What is the difference between breastfeeding jaundice and breast milk jaundice?

**A:** Breastfeeding jaundice: due to insufficient milk intake causing dehydration and exaggerated physiologic jaundice (early onset). Breast milk jaundice: due to glucuronyl transferase inhibitor in breast milk, onset at 7 days, peaks at 2-3 weeks, resolves by 6 weeks.

Breastfeeding jaundice is common and related to poor latch or inadequate milk production. Breast milk jaundice affects 1 in 200 breastfed infants. Neither condition typically requires cessation of breastfeeding.

**Q:** When is neonatal jaundice always pathologic?

**A:** Jaundice within the first 24 hours of life and conjugated hyperbilirubinemia are always pathologic.

Causes of early jaundice include hemolytic disease (Rh/ABO incompatibility, G6PD deficiency), sepsis, and congenital infections (TORCH). Conjugated hyperbilirubinemia requires urgent investigation for biliary atresia, neonatal hepatitis, or metabolic disorders.

**Q:** What is kernicterus and how is it prevented?

**A:** Kernicterus is unconjugated bilirubin deposition in the brain (basal ganglia/brainstem) causing permanent damage. Prevention: phototherapy, exchange transfusion, and IVIG for severe hemolytic disease.

Risk increases above 340 μmol/L but can occur at lower levels with sepsis, hemolysis, or prematurity. Acute signs include lethargy, hypotonia, then hypertonia, high-pitched cry, and seizures. Chronic sequelae include choreoathetoid cerebral palsy and sensorineural hearing loss.

**Q:** What is biliary atresia and when should Kasai procedure be performed?

**A:** Atresia of extrahepatic bile ducts causing progressive cholestasis with conjugated hyperbilirubinemia. Presents with dark urine, pale stools, jaundice persisting >2 weeks, hepatomegaly. Kasai hepatoportoenterostomy is most successful if performed before 8 weeks of age.

Biliary atresia occurs in 1 in 10,000-15,000 births. Two-thirds eventually require liver transplant. Diagnosis involves conjugated hyperbilirubinemia, abdominal ultrasound, and liver biopsy. Fat-soluble vitamins (A, D, E, K) supplementation is essential.

**Q:** What is the hallmark radiographic finding of necrotizing enterocolitis (NEC)?

**A:** Pneumatosis intestinalis (intramural air) on abdominal x-ray.

NEC primarily affects preterm newborns and presents at 2-3 weeks of age with abdominal distension, feeding intolerance, and bloody stools. Treatment is NPO, NG decompression, IV antibiotics, and TPN. Surgery is needed for perforation (40% mortality).

**Q:** How do you differentiate RDS, TTN, and MAS in a newborn?

**A:** RDS: preterm, surfactant deficiency, homogeneous infiltrates/air bronchograms on CXR. TTN: term/late preterm, delayed fetal lung fluid resorption, perihilar 'wet silhouette.' MAS: term/post-term, meconium-stained fluid, patchy infiltrates with hyperinflation.

RDS is treated with surfactant and ventilation. TTN is self-limited (24-72h). MAS can cause chemical pneumonitis and PPHN. Prenatal corticosteroids reduce RDS risk if delivery <34 weeks.

**Q:** What is the hyperoxia test and what does it differentiate?

**A:** Baseline PaO2 in room air, then PaO2 on 100% O2. PaO2 >150 mmHg: respiratory or non-cardiac cause. PaO2 <150 mmHg: cyanotic congenital heart disease or PPHN.

The hyperoxia test helps differentiate cardiac from pulmonary causes of cyanosis. Pre- and post-ductal pulse oximetry difference >5% suggests right-to-left shunt through the ductus arteriosus.

**Q:** What is the congenital diaphragmatic hernia presentation, and what should you NOT do at delivery?

**A:** Respiratory distress, cyanosis, scaphoid abdomen, barrel chest, absent breath sounds on affected side, bowel sounds in chest. Do NOT bag-mask ventilate - immediate intubation required.

Bag-mask ventilation forces air into the stomach, further compressing the lungs. Associated with pulmonary hypoplasia and PPHN. CXR shows bowel loops in thorax with displaced mediastinum. Place large bore OG tube to decompress bowel.

**Q:** What empiric antibiotics are used for neonatal sepsis (early onset)?

**A:** IV ampicillin + gentamicin (non-meningitic coverage). If meningitis suspected: IV ampicillin + cefotaxime.

Early onset sepsis (<72h) is from vertical transmission, most commonly GBS, E. coli, and Listeria. Risk factors include maternal GBS positivity, prolonged rupture of membranes >18h, and chorioamnionitis. Add acyclovir if HSV suspected.

## Pediatrics Immunization

**Q:** What is the Ontario publicly funded immunization schedule age for the first dose of DTaP-IPV-Hib vaccine?

**A:** 2 months of age

The Ontario publicly funded schedule administers DTaP-IPV-Hib (diphtheria, tetanus, acellular pertussis, inactivated polio, Haemophilus influenzae type b) at 2, 4, and 6 months, with a booster at 4-6 years. This protects infants during their most vulnerable period for pertussis and Hib disease.

**Q:** At what age and via what route is rotavirus (Rot-1) vaccine given in Ontario's publicly funded schedule?

**A:** Orally (PO) at 2 and 4 months of age (up to 15 weeks for first dose, up to 32 weeks for last dose)

Rotavirus vaccine is given orally as it is a live attenuated oral vaccine. The first dose must be given before 15 weeks of age and the series completed before 32 weeks. It is contraindicated in children with a history of intussusception or abdominal disorders such as Meckel's diverticulum.

**Q:** Which vaccines are contraindicated in pregnancy?

**A:** MMR, Varicella (Var), MMRV, and DTaP (first trimester). Live attenuated vaccines are generally contraindicated in pregnancy.

Live attenuated vaccines (MMR, Var, MMRV) are contraindicated in pregnancy due to theoretical risk of fetal infection. DTaP is contraindicated in the first trimester. Inactivated vaccines like Tdap and influenza are safe and recommended in pregnancy. Women should avoid pregnancy for 3 months after Varicella vaccination.

**Q:** What are the contraindications to MMR vaccination?

**A:** Pregnancy, immunocompromised state (except healthy HIV-positive children), anaphylactic reaction to gelatin; cannot give MMR within 4 weeks of TB skin test (but can receive on same day)

MMR is a live attenuated vaccine and is contraindicated in pregnancy and severely immunocompromised patients. Healthy HIV-positive children are an exception and may receive MMR. Gelatin allergy is a contraindication. The TB skin test interaction is important: giving MMR can suppress tuberculin reactivity for up to 4-6 weeks, so tests should not be done within 4 weeks after MMR.

**Q:** What is the adverse effect of rotavirus vaccine that is also listed as a contraindication to future doses?

**A:** Intussusception — it is both a potential adverse effect and an absolute contraindication to Rot-1 vaccine

Intussusception is the telescoping of one segment of bowel into another. It is a rare adverse effect of rotavirus vaccine. A prior history of intussusception is an absolute contraindication to rotavirus vaccination. Other contraindications include immunocompromised state and abdominal disorders (e.g., Meckel's diverticulum).

**Q:** What are the adverse effects of TdaP-IPV vaccine, and when is it contraindicated?

**A:** Adverse effects: prolonged crying, hypotonic-hyporesponsive episode (rare), seizure on day of vaccine (rare). Contraindications: evolving unstable neurologic disease, hyporesponsive/hypotonic episode after previous dose, anaphylaxis to vaccine components (neomycin/streptomycin), encephalopathy within 7 days of prior pertussis vaccine, GBS within 8 weeks of prior tetanus vaccine.

TdaP-IPV is an inactivated combination vaccine. The hypotonic-hyporesponsive episode (HHE) is a rare but recognized adverse event — child becomes pale, limp, and unresponsive. History of HHE after a previous dose is a contraindication. Encephalopathy within 7 days and GBS within 8 weeks of prior doses are specific contraindications related to the pertussis and tetanus components, respectively.

**Q:** What are the cold chain requirements for vaccine storage?

**A:** +2 to +8°C (refrigerated, not frozen, for most vaccines)

Maintaining the cold chain is critical for vaccine efficacy. Most vaccines must be stored between +2 and +8°C. Vaccines exposed to temperatures outside this range (frozen or overheated) may lose potency. Adverse effects following immunization (AEFI) must be reported to local/regional health units when they meet reporting criteria (temporal association, no other cause, serious criteria met, or unexpected).

**Q:** Which immunizations can be given to immunocompromised patients, and which cannot?

**A:** Inactivated vaccines: generally can be given (reduced response possible). Live attenuated vaccines: generally contraindicated if severely immunocompromised. Exception: live viral vaccines OK for phagocyte/neutrophil disorders; live bacterial vaccines are contraindicated. Live vaccines OK for isolated IgA/IgG subclass/complement deficiencies or asplenia.

Immune response to vaccines varies in immunocompromised patients. Inactivated vaccines (e.g., influenza, Hepatitis B, DTaP) are safe but may have reduced immunogenicity. Live vaccines (MMR, Var, rotavirus) are contraindicated in severe immunocompromise. Extra or booster doses may be needed. Post-immunization antibody titres should be considered.

**Q:** What is the recommended approach when dealing with vaccine-hesitant parents?

**A:** Use a presumptive approach (announce the vaccine rather than asking), motivational interviewing, open-ended questions, non-judgmental validation of concerns, compelling disease stories, address pain management, discuss community protection (herd immunity). Do not dismiss families.

Healthcare professionals' vaccine advice is the most influential factor in parental decisions. The presumptive announcement approach ('Today your child will get these vaccines') is more effective than a participatory approach. Motivational interviewing, listening without judgment, and addressing specific concerns respectfully are key. Altruism (protecting others) and herd immunity are important discussion points.

## Pediatrics Growth Development

**Q:** When using WHO Growth Charts, what growth tool is used for premature infants, and until what age is it used?

**A:** Fenton Curve is used for premature infants (<37 weeks gestation), with corrected gestational age used until 2 years of age

WHO Growth Charts are used for term infants and children. The Fenton Curve is specifically designed to assess growth in preterm infants and allows classification as small for gestational age (SGA) or large for gestational age (LGA). Corrected gestational age (chronological age minus weeks premature) is used until 2 years for all developmental and growth assessments.

**Q:** What are the expected average growth parameters for weight in the first 2 years of life?

**A:** Birth weight ~3.25 kg; gain 20-30 g/day (term neonate); regain birth weight by ~10-14 days; 2x birth weight by 4-5 months; 3x birth weight by 1 year; 4x birth weight by 2 years. Up to 10% weight loss in first 7 days is normal.

Neonatal weight loss of up to 10% in the first 7 days reflects normal physiological fluid loss and is not a sign of failure to thrive as long as birth weight is regained by 10-14 days. The doubling, tripling, and quadrupling milestones are key MCCQE1 facts used to identify failure to thrive.

**Q:** At what age does the head circumference grow most rapidly, and what is the rate of growth?

**A:** Most rapidly in first 3 months: 2 cm/month; then 1 cm/month at 3-6 months; 0.5 cm/month at 6-12 months

Head circumference is measured around the occipital, parietal, and frontal prominences to obtain the greatest circumference. The mean birth head circumference is 35 cm. Rapid early head growth reflects rapid brain development. Deviation from expected growth may suggest microcephaly (e.g., TORCH, genetic) or macrocephaly (e.g., hydrocephalus).

**Q:** What are the primitive reflexes and their expected ages of disappearance?

**A:** Moro: 3-6 months; Galant: 2-3 months; Grasp: 3-4 months; ATNR (asymmetric tonic neck reflex): 4-6 months; Stepping: 2-3 months; Rooting: 2-3 months. Lateral propping does NOT disappear (appears by 6-8 months).

Primitive reflexes are mediated by the brainstem. Persistence beyond the expected age of disappearance suggests cerebral palsy or other CNS pathology. Absence during the neonatal period, asymmetry, or persistence after 4-6 months are red flags. Babinski's (upgoing plantar) is normal in infants up to 2 years.

**Q:** What are the key developmental red flags at any age for pediatric patients?

**A:** Loss of previously acquired skills (regression) is a red flag at any age. Other age-specific red flags: not smiling at 4 months; not pointing at 15-18 months; hand preference before 18 months (suggests contralateral weakness); <6 words at 18 months; not seeing or hearing normally.

Developmental regression (loss of previously acquired skills) is always concerning regardless of age and warrants urgent evaluation. Hand preference before 18 months may suggest hemiplegia contralateral to the preferred hand. Early identification of developmental delays allows for timely intervention during the critical window of neuroplasticity.

**Q:** What are the key developmental milestones at 12 months of age?

**A:** Gross motor: walks a few steps, wide gait. Fine motor: fine pincer grasp (fingertips), finger-feeds, voluntary release. Language: 1 word with meaning (besides mama/dada), responds to own name, follows 1-step command with gesture. Cognitive: uses objects functionally, cause and effect. Social: points at wanted items.

The 12-month milestone is critical for MCCQE1. The fine pincer grasp (index finger + thumb tip) replaces the raking/inferior pincer grasp seen at 9 months. One meaningful word other than mama/dada is required. Pointing to show interest (protodeclarative pointing) is a key social-communicative milestone relevant to autism screening.

**Q:** What are the developmental milestones at 18 months and 24 months of age?

**A:** 18 months: runs, stoops and recovers; tower of 4 blocks, scribbles; 15-25 words; symbolic play; parallel play; 3 body parts. 24 months: jumps on two feet; tower of 6 blocks, established handedness; 2-3 word phrases, 50% intelligible, 50+ words; new problem-solving strategies; testing limits/tantrums.

By 18 months, children should have at least 15-25 words — fewer than 6 words is a red flag. By 24 months, speech should be 50% intelligible to strangers. Lack of 2-word phrases by 24 months is a red flag. Handedness is established by 24 months — earlier preference may suggest contralateral motor deficit.

**Q:** What is the definition of failure to thrive (FTT) in children?

**A:** Weight <3rd percentile, OR weight falling across two major percentile curves on growth chart, OR <80% of expected weight for height and age. Inadequate caloric intake is the most common factor.

FTT is a sign, not a diagnosis. The growth pattern helps identify the underlying cause. Decreased weight with normal height and head circumference suggests caloric insufficiency. Decreased weight and height with normal head circumference suggests structural dystrophies or endocrine disorder. Decreased weight, height, and head circumference suggests intrauterine insult. Management is typically outpatient and multidisciplinary.

**Q:** What does the mnemonic SMALL KID represent in the clinical signs of failure to thrive?

**A:** S - Subcutaneous fat loss; M - Muscle atrophy; A - Alopecia; L - Lagging behind normal milestones; L - Loss of skin turgor; K - Kwashiorkor features (edema); I - Irritability; D - Dermatitis

The SMALL KID mnemonic summarizes the physical signs of malnutrition in a child with FTT. Kwashiorkor (protein deficiency) presents with edema, hypoalbuminemia, and dermatitis, distinct from marasmus (caloric deficiency) which shows severe wasting without edema. FTT investigations include CBC, electrolytes, T4/TSH, ferritin, calcium, celiac screen, and bone age x-ray.

**Q:** What pattern of growth parameters suggests intrauterine insult as the cause of failure to thrive?

**A:** Decreased weight + decreased height + decreased head circumference (all three growth parameters reduced) suggests intrauterine insult (e.g., TORCH infections, placental insufficiency, genetic/chromosomal abnormalities).

The FTT pattern table distinguishes causes by which growth parameters are affected. Head circumference-sparing (decreased weight/height, normal HC) suggests endocrine or structural causes. When all three parameters are affected (symmetric growth restriction), the insult occurred prenatally affecting overall growth including brain growth. This contrasts with postnatal FTT where head circumference is typically preserved until late stages.

**Q:** What are the main categories of etiology for failure to thrive?

**A:** 1. Psychosocial/parent-child interaction issues; 2. Inadequate caloric intake (poor latch, cleft palate, oromotor dysfunction); 3. Inadequate absorption (celiac, IBD, CF, milk protein allergy, biliary atresia); 4. Increased metabolism (CF, chronic infection, hyperthyroidism, asthma, malignancy, renal failure); 5. Increased losses; 6. Increased utilization (chromosomal disorders); 7. Prenatal factors (placental insufficiency, maternal disease/substance use).

FTT etiology is broadly divided into organic and non-organic, though most cases involve an interplay. Psychosocial factors (feeding environment, parent-child attachment, postpartum depression, poverty) are the most common underlying contributors in developed countries. CF is a classic cause of FTT with both malabsorption and increased metabolic demands. All causes require a multidisciplinary approach.

**Q:** What are the signs of readiness for toilet training, and at what age do most children achieve daytime bladder control?

**A:** Toilet readiness signs (usually 18-24 months): ambulating independently, stable on potty, motivated to please/be independent, sufficient expressive and receptive language (2-step commands), can stay dry for several hours, recognizes need to go, can remove clothing. 25% toilet trained by age 2; 98% by age 3. Females generally train earlier than males.

Toilet training should be child-led and begin when readiness signs are present. Most children attain bowel control before bladder control (90%). Coercive or premature toilet training is a risk factor for retentive encopresis. A stepwise approach using a potty chair with positive reinforcement is recommended.

**Q:** What is the HEEADSSS/SSHADESS assessment used for in pediatric history taking?

**A:** HEEADSSS (or SSHADESS) is a structured psychosocial history framework used for adolescents. It covers: Home, Education/Employment, Eating, Activities, Drugs, Sexuality, Suicide/Depression, Safety (and Strengths in SSHADESS). It screens for key adolescent risk factors in a systematic, confidential manner.

Adolescent health visits require a structured psychosocial assessment due to the unique risk profile of this age group. HEEADSSS allows clinicians to open conversation about sensitive topics (substance use, sexual activity, mental health, safety). The interview should include confidential time alone with the adolescent to encourage honest disclosure. ITHELPS is another tool covering social determinants: income, transportation, home, education, legal status, personal safety, support.

**Q:** What is the upper-to-lower segment ratio at birth and in adulthood, and what conditions affect it?

**A:** Newborn: 1.7 (upper > lower). Adult male: 0.9; adult female: 1.0 (lower > upper). INCREASED ratio (short limbs): achondroplasia, short limb syndromes, hypothyroidism, storage diseases. DECREASED ratio (long limbs): Marfan syndrome, Klinefelter syndrome, Kallmann syndrome, testosterone deficiency.

The upper/lower segment ratio uses the pubic symphysis as midpoint. Upper segment: crown to pubis; lower segment: pubis to floor. Infants are proportionally top-heavy due to large heads and short limbs. With growth, limbs elongate and the ratio falls. Disproportionate short stature (high ratio) vs. disproportionate tall stature (low ratio) helps classify the cause of growth abnormalities.

**Q:** What developmental milestone is achieved at 3 years of age?

**A:** Gross motor: rides tricycle, climbs stairs alternating feet. Fine motor: toilet trained, undresses, draws circle and cross (+). Language: 3-4 word phrases, 'W' questions (why), 200 words, 75% intelligible, 3-step commands. Cognitive: identifies shapes, counts to 3, simple time concepts. Social: cooperative/role play, separates easily, sharing.

At 3 years, speech should be 75% intelligible to strangers (rising to 100% by 4 years). The ability to draw a circle and then a cross (+) reflects fine motor development. Pretend/cooperative play emerges, distinguishing it from the parallel play of 18-month-olds. The ability to count to 3 and understand simple time concepts reflects cognitive advancement.

**Q:** What are the requirements for Growth Hormone (GH) therapy in pediatric GH deficiency?

**A:** 1. GH deficiency confirmed by 2 different stimulation tests (arginine, glucagon, or insulin); 2. Growth velocity <3rd percentile OR height <3rd percentile; 3. Bone age x-rays showing unfused epiphyses/delayed bone age.

GH therapy is indicated for confirmed GH deficiency (not just short stature) and must be started early to maximize adult height potential. Idiopathic short stature (height <3rd percentile without any identified etiology) is also a recognized indication for GH therapy.

**Q:** What is the most common cause of tall stature from a genetic etiology in pediatrics, and what are the key associated syndromes?
