**A:** Genetic causes of tall stature include: Klinefelter syndrome (47,XXY), Marfan syndrome, homocystinuria, and Sotos syndrome. Endocrine causes include Beckwith-Wiedemann syndrome, hyperthyroidism, hypophyseal gigantism, and precocious puberty.

Tall stature is defined as height >2 SDs above mean for a given age, sex, and race. Constitutional/familial causes are most common. Genetic syndromes like Marfan and Klinefelter are important to recognize due to associated systemic complications (cardiovascular, cognitive, etc.).

**Q:** What is the normal sequence of pubertal development in males?

**A:** Onset ages 9–14 years. Usual sequence: 1. Testicular enlargement → 2. Penile enlargement → 3. Pubarche (axillary and facial hair, body odour, mild acne) → 4. Growth spurt (occurs later in boys than girls). Early puberty is uncommon in males (rule out organic causes); late puberty is common and often constitutional.

Gynecomastia (transient breast tissue development) occurs in ~50% of males during puberty and is self-limited. Any nipple discharge or fixed mass should be investigated further. The growth spurt occurs later in boys, distinguishing male from female pubertal progression.

**Q:** What is the definition and epidemiology of precocious puberty?

**A:** Development of secondary sexual characteristics 2–2.5 SDs before population mean: <8 years in females, <9 years in males. Epidemiology: 1 in 10,000; F > M. Usually idiopathic in females (90%); more suggestive of pathology in males (50%).

The higher rate of pathological causes in males means boys with precocious puberty require more aggressive investigation. In females, idiopathic/constitutional precocious puberty is most common. Obesity is also a recognized cause of central precocious puberty.

**Q:** What is the difference between central (GnRH-dependent) and peripheral (GnRH-independent) precocious puberty?

**A:** Central: GnRH-dependent; hypergonadotropic hypergonadism; premature activation of HPG axis; LH/FSH levels as in normal puberty. DDx: idiopathic (most common in females), obesity, CNS disturbances (tumours, hamartomas, post-meningitis, increased ICP, radiotherapy), NF1, primary severe hypothyroidism. Peripheral: GnRH-independent; hypogonadotropic hypergonadism. DDx: testicular/ovarian tumours, adrenal disorders (CAH, neoplasm), exogenous steroids, McCune-Albright syndrome.

The key distinction is whether GnRH is the driver. Central precocious puberty responds to GnRH agonists (e.g., leuprolide) which suppress the HPG axis. Peripheral causes require treatment of the underlying source of sex steroids.

**Q:** What is the management of central vs. peripheral precocious puberty?

**A:** Central: GnRH agonists (e.g., leuprolide) are most effective; goals: preserve final adult height and alleviate psychosocial stress. Peripheral: medications that decrease production of a specific sex steroid or block its effects (e.g., ketoconazole, spironolactone, tamoxifen, anastrozole); surgical intervention as needed.

Indications for medical intervention: rapid advancement of puberty, very early age of onset, risk of compromised final adult height, and psychological effects. GnRH agonists paradoxically suppress gonadotropins when given continuously (versus pulsatile endogenous GnRH).

**Q:** What are the definitions of delayed puberty in males and females?

**A:** Failure to develop secondary sex characteristics by 2–2.5 SDs beyond population mean. Males: lack of testicular enlargement by age 14. Females: lack of breast development by age 13, OR absence of menarche by age 16, OR absence of menarche within 5 years of pubertal onset. Epidemiology: M > F. Usually constitutional delay in males; more suggestive of pathology in females.

The most common cause of delayed puberty overall is constitutional delay of growth and puberty (CDGP), especially in males. In females, delayed puberty is less common and more likely to represent an organic etiology such as Turner syndrome or chronic illness.

**Q:** What are the central and peripheral causes of delayed puberty?

**A:** Central (hypogonadotropic hypogonadism): constitutional delay of HPG axis activation (most common), Kallmann syndrome, hypothalamic/pituitary disorders, chronic illness, hypothyroidism, hyperprolactinemia, poor nutrition, excessive exercise. Peripheral (hypergonadotropic hypogonadism): primary gonadal failure, Turner syndrome, Klinefelter syndrome, androgen insensitivity syndrome, hormone deficiency.

FSH/LH levels distinguish central (low) from peripheral (elevated) causes. Turner syndrome (45,X0) in females and Klinefelter syndrome (47,XXY) in males are key genetic causes. Kallmann syndrome features hypogonadotropic hypogonadism plus anosmia.

**Q:** What are the Tanner staging criteria for female breast development?

**A:** Stage 1: Papilla elevation only (prepubertal). Stage 2: Breast and papilla elevated as small mound, enlargement of areola. Stage 3: Enlargement of breast and areola, no separation of contour. Stage 4: Areola and papilla form secondary mound above level of breast. Stage 5: Mature adult breast; only nipple projects; areola recessed to general contour.

Tanner (now called Maturity Rating) staging is used to standardize assessment of pubertal development in pediatrics. It guides timing of investigations for precocious or delayed puberty and is based on external primary and secondary sex characteristics.

**Q:** What are normal growth velocity parameters during puberty for males and females?

**A:** Prepubertal minimum: 4 cm/year. Growth spurt: males 10–14 cm/year; females 8–12 cm/year. (Males have a later but larger pubertal growth spurt than females.)

Growth velocity is a key parameter in the assessment of both precocious and delayed puberty. Reduced growth velocity may indicate endocrine pathology, chronic illness, or malnutrition, while accelerated velocity may indicate precocious puberty.

**Q:** What investigations are included in the initial and secondary workup for precocious puberty?

**A:** Initial: bone age, serum hormone levels (estradiol, testosterone, LH, FSH, TSH, free T4, DHEA-S, 17-OH-progesterone, prolactin). Secondary: MRI brain, pelvic ultrasound, beta-hCG, GnRH stimulation test, and/or ACTH stimulation test. All boys with precocious puberty should receive brain MRI.

Bone age advancement (on wrist X-ray) confirms the effect of sex steroids on skeletal maturation and helps predict final adult height. GnRH stimulation test differentiates central (LH surge) from peripheral (no LH surge) precocious puberty.

**Q:** What is the 'jump start' therapy for constitutional delayed puberty?

**A:** Low-dose, short-course sex hormone therapy to induce puberty in patients with constitutional delay or GnRH deficiency: cyclic estradiol and progesterone for females; testosterone for males. These patients should be referred to a pediatric endocrinologist for hormone therapy.

Constitutional delay of growth and puberty (CDGP) is the most common cause of delayed puberty, especially in males, and is a diagnosis of exclusion. Jump start therapy is offered to alleviate psychosocial distress and has no significant effect on final adult height.

## Pediatrics Nutrition

**Q:** What are the major caloric requirements for children using the Holliday-Segar method?

**A:** Weight <10 kg: 100 kcal/kg/day. Weight 10-20 kg: 1000 kcal + 50 kcal/kg for each kg over 10. Weight >20 kg: 1500 kcal + 20 kcal/kg for each kg over 20.

The Holliday-Segar method (also used for maintenance fluids) provides a practical way to estimate caloric and fluid requirements. For example, a 15 kg child needs 1000 + (50 × 5) = 1250 kcal/day. This is distinct from the actual caloric content of foods and must be adjusted for illness, prematurity, catch-up growth, and activity level.

**Q:** What is the recommended timing for introduction of solid foods and highly allergenic foods in infants?

**A:** Solid foods introduced at 4-6 months (do not delay beyond 9 months). Highly allergenic foods (eggs, milk, peanuts, seafood, sesame, soy, tree nuts, wheat, mustard) should be introduced early — around 6 months for low-risk infants, and at about 6 months (but not before 4 months) for high-risk infants (personal/family history of atopy) to prevent food allergy.

Early introduction of allergenic foods is now recommended to reduce the risk of food allergy (based on LEAP trial evidence for peanuts). Delaying introduction increases risk. Solids should begin with iron-fortified cereals/meats since iron stores nadir at 6 months. Introduce 2-3 new foods per week with at least 2 days between each new food to identify adverse reactions.

**Q:** What foods should be avoided in infants and at what ages?

**A:** Honey: avoid until >12 months (risk of infant botulism). Added sugar and salt: avoid. Excessive cow's milk >500 mL/day after 1 year (iron deficiency anemia risk). Juice: not recommended (high sugar). Choking hazards: chunks, hard candies, nuts, round foods (grapes, hot dogs). Bottles: discontinue by 12-24 months.

Honey contains Clostridium botulinum spores that can germinate in the immature GI tract of infants under 1 year, causing infant botulism (flaccid paralysis). Excessive cow's milk displaces iron-rich foods, leading to iron deficiency anemia — a common cause in toddlers. Juice provides empty calories and promotes dental caries.

**Q:** What are the absolute contraindications to breastfeeding?

**A:** HIV infection, HTLV type I and II (in the mother), and infant galactosemia (cannot metabolize galactose in breast milk)

HIV can be transmitted through breast milk; in Canada, formula feeding is recommended for HIV-positive mothers. HTLV I and II (human T-lymphotropic viruses) are also transmitted via breast milk. Infant galactosemia is an absolute contraindication on the infant's side, as galactose from lactose in breast milk causes accumulation and serious organ damage. Active untreated TB and active HSV-2 lesions on the breast are relative contraindications.

**Q:** What are the signs of inadequate breastfeeding intake in a newborn?

**A:** Less than 6 wet diapers/day after first week; fewer than 7 feeds/day; sleepy or lethargic; loss >10% of birth weight (after first week); jaundice; not regaining birth weight by 10-14 days

Adequate breastfeeding is monitored by output (wet diapers), weight gain, and infant alertness. In the first week, neonates should have 1 wet diaper per day of life. By day 5+, 3+ yellow seedy stools per day indicates mature milk. Excessive weight loss or failure to regain birth weight may warrant formula supplementation and lactation consultation.

**Q:** What is the composition of colostrum, and how does it differ from mature breast milk?

**A:** Colostrum (first few days): clear, rich in protein and immunoglobulins (especially IgA), low fat. Mature breast milk: 70:30 whey:casein ratio, fat from dietary butterfat, carbohydrate from lactose.

Colostrum is the first milk produced and is rich in secretory IgA, which provides passive mucosal immunity to the newborn. It has higher protein and lower fat content than mature milk. Mature breast milk contains lactoferrin (inhibits E. coli), lysozymes, macrophages, and active lymphocytes, providing ongoing immunologic protection. The lower pH of breast milk promotes Lactobacillus growth.

**Q:** When is soy protein formula (Isomil, Prosobee) indicated, and what is its key limitation?

**A:** Indicated for galactosemia and desire for vegetarian/vegan diet. Key limitation: 10-35% of children with cow's milk protein allergy also react to soy, making it unsuitable for most cow's milk protein allergy cases.

Soy formula uses corn syrup solids or sucrose instead of lactose. While it avoids dairy protein, significant cross-reactivity with cow's milk protein allergy means it should not be used as the primary alternative for milk protein allergy — protein hydrolysate or amino acid formula is preferred in those cases. Soy is appropriate for galactosemia as it is lactose-free.

**Q:** What formula is indicated for severe cow's milk protein allergy or malabsorption, and what is the protein source?

**A:** Protein hydrolysate formula (Nutramigen, Alimentum, Pregestimil, Portagen): protein is 100% casein hydrolysate. For severe allergy unresponsive to hydrolysate: amino acid formula (Neocate, PurAmino): free amino acids (no intact protein).

In cow's milk protein allergy, the immune system reacts to intact proteins. Extensively hydrolyzed (protein hydrolysate) formulas break proteins into small peptides, eliminating most allergenicity. If allergy persists, elemental (amino acid) formulas provide individual amino acids with no allergenic potential. These are significantly more expensive. Short gut syndrome may also require amino acid formula for absorption.

**Q:** What is the definition of childhood obesity by age group in Canada?

**A:** Ages 0-2 years: weight-for-length >97th percentile (overweight), >99.9th percentile (obese). Ages 2-5 years: BMI >97th percentile (overweight), >99.9th percentile (obese). Ages 5-19 years: BMI >85th percentile (overweight), >97th percentile (obese).

Canada uses BMI percentile cutoffs from WHO growth standards. Childhood obesity is associated with numerous complications including hypertension, dyslipidemia, T2DM, OSA, slipped capital femoral epiphysis (SCFE), asthma, gynecomastia, PCOS, early menarche, and psychological issues (bullying, depression). Childhood obesity frequently persists into adulthood.

**Q:** What are the rare organic causes of childhood obesity, and what conditions should be screened for?

**A:** Organic causes (<5%): hypothyroidism, Cushing syndrome, PCOS, Prader-Willi syndrome, Carpenter syndrome, Turner syndrome. Investigations: blood pressure, dyslipidemia screen, fatty liver disease (ALT), T2DM screen (based on risk factors).

The vast majority of childhood obesity is exogenous (excess intake, decreased activity). Organic causes are suggested by short stature, dysmorphic features, developmental delay, or other syndromic features. Prader-Willi causes hyperphagia and obesity from hypothalamic dysfunction. Cushing causes central obesity with striae and growth failure. Hypothyroidism causes weight gain with decreased linear growth.

**Q:** What is the vitamin D supplementation recommendation for breastfed infants?

**A:** 400 IU/day for all breastfed infants. 800 IU/day if infant or maternal risk factors are present. This supplementation is required because breast milk is deficient in vitamin D.

Vitamin D is synthesized in skin with UV exposure; however, infants are kept out of direct sunlight. Breast milk contains insufficient vitamin D regardless of maternal vitamin D status. Vitamin D deficiency causes rickets in infants and children. Formula is fortified with vitamin D, so formula-fed infants consuming adequate amounts do not typically require supplementation. Supplementation should begin shortly after birth.

## Pediatrics Preventive Care

**Q:** What are the leading causes of death from injury in children >1 year of age?

**A:** Motor vehicle crashes, burns, drowning, falls, choking, and infanticide

Injuries are the leading cause of death in children older than 1 year. Motor vehicle collisions are the #1 cause. Prevention counselling at each well-child visit (Rourke Baby Record) addresses age-specific hazards. Car seat use is mandatory in Ontario and is required before leaving hospital for newborns.

**Q:** What is the CPS position on routine male newborn circumcision?

**A:** The Canadian Paediatric Society (CPS) states circumcision is NOT medically indicated and does NOT recommend routine circumcision for every newborn male. It is often performed for religious or cultural reasons.

While circumcision has some potential benefits (slight reduction in UTI risk, STI including HIV, phimosis, balanitis, penile cancer), the CPS concludes these do not justify routine circumcision in otherwise healthy newborns. The procedure has a complication rate <1% (infection, bleeding, urethral injury, meatal stenosis) but complications are higher in older children vs. infants. It is contraindicated in the presence of genital abnormalities (e.g., hypospadias) or bleeding disorders.

**Q:** When does primary dentition erupt and how many teeth does a child have?

**A:** Primary dentition: 20 teeth. First tooth (lower central incisor) erupts at 5-9 months, then 1 new tooth per month. By 1 year: 6-8 central teeth. Secondary (adult) dentition: 32 teeth; first adult tooth is 1st molar at age 6, then lower incisors.

The first dental assessment should occur 6 months after the first tooth erupts and certainly by 1 year (Grade B recommendation). Early dental assessment helps prevent early childhood caries, which is multifactorial (diet, bacteria, host, social determinants). The 6-year molar is often called the 'key to the dental arch' and is the first permanent tooth to erupt.

**Q:** What are the key prevention strategies for early childhood dental caries?

**A:** No bottle at bedtime; clean teeth after last feed; no juice or sweetened pacifier; <3 yr: daily brushing with rice-grain sized fluoridated toothpaste; 3-6 yr: pea-sized fluoridated toothpaste with adult assistance; first dental visit by 1 year; no prolonged bottle feeding or putting to bed with bottle.

Early childhood caries (ECC) is the most common chronic disease of childhood. Inappropriate feeding practices are major modifiable risk factors: prolonged bottle feeding, bottle at bedtime (pools sugary liquid around teeth), excessive juice. Fluoridated toothpaste is recommended as soon as teeth emerge. Ontario's Healthy Smiles program supports low-income families' dental access.

**Q:** What are the Canadian screen time guidelines for children by age group?

**A:** Under 2 years: no screen time recommended. Ages 2-4 years: <1 hour/day. Ages 5-17 years: ≤2 hours/day. Physical activity: 1 hour/day for children with obesity.

Screen time guidelines come from the Canadian Society for Exercise Physiology. Excessive screen time displaces physical activity, disrupts sleep, and is associated with obesity, behavioral problems, and developmental delays. For childhood obesity management, small sustainable changes in energy expenditure and intake are recommended (lose approximately 1 lb/month), with a long-term goal of maintaining BMI <85th percentile.

**Q:** What household substances are most dangerous to children from a poisoning perspective, and what is the risk of alcohol?

**A:** Dangerous substances: medications, illicit drugs, drain cleaners, furniture polish, insecticides, cosmetics, nail polish remover (acetone), automotive products. Alcohol: as little as 3 oz of hard liquor can kill a 2-year-old.

Unintentional poisoning is a leading cause of injury in children. Children aged 1-4 years are at highest risk. Prevention involves storing all medications, chemicals, and household products in locked, child-resistant containers out of reach and sight. Chemicals should never be stored in beverage containers. Poison Control Centre (1-800-268-9017 in Ontario) should be contacted immediately for suspected ingestions.

**Q:** What injury prevention advice is most important for a 6-month-old infant?

**A:** Never leave unattended on bed, changing table, or in tub; install stair barriers; discourage use of walkers; keep crib rails up; check water temperature before bathing; avoid co-sleeping; always position to sleep on back with no soft objects in crib (SIDS prevention); appropriate car seats; cover electrical outlets; supervise during feeding; unplug appliances when not in use.

At 6-12 months, infants become mobile (rolling, crawling, pulling to stand) and are at risk for falls, burns, and aspiration. Infant walkers are strongly discouraged as they cause falls (especially down stairs) and delay gross motor development. Car seats are mandatory. Co-sleeping increases SIDS risk substantially. 'Back to sleep' is the most effective SIDS prevention strategy.

## Pediatrics Common Complaints

**Q:** What are breath holding spells? Describe the two types and their triggers.

**A:** Cyanotic type (more common): triggered by anger/frustration — child holds breath, becomes silent, may lose consciousness; resolves spontaneously. Pallid type: triggered by pain/surprise — vagal stimulation reduces heart rate causing cerebral hypoperfusion and loss of consciousness. Epidemiology: 0.1-5% of children aged 6 months-4 years.

Breath holding spells are benign but frightening. They typically begin in the first year and almost all resolve by age 8 (median remission age 4 years). Management focuses on parental reassurance, avoiding reinforcement of the spell, and treating associated iron deficiency anemia (which improves frequency). If episodes are prolonged/frequent, triggered by minor stimuli, or there is a family history of syncope or sudden death, cardiac evaluation for prolonged QT syndrome is warranted.

**Q:** What is the management of breath holding spells, and what condition should be screened for?

**A:** Management: reassurance, help child manage frustration, avoid reinforcing the spell. Screen for iron deficiency anemia (supplemental iron improves episodes). If prolonged/frequent or triggered by non-traumatic stimuli or family history of syncope/sudden death: cardiac evaluation for prolonged QT syndrome.

Iron deficiency anemia is associated with increased frequency of breath holding spells, and iron supplementation can reduce their occurrence. This is a treatable cause. The association with iron deficiency suggests a shared mechanism possibly related to autonomic dysregulation. Long QT syndrome must be excluded when atypical features are present, as it can also cause loss of consciousness and sudden death.

**Q:** What is infantile colic? What are the Wessel (rule of 3s) criteria?

**A:** Unexplained paroxysms of irritability and crying for >3 hours/day, >3 days/week, for >3 weeks in an otherwise healthy, well-fed baby. Affects ~10% of infants; onset 10 days to 3 months, peak at 6-8 weeks.

Colic is a diagnosis of exclusion — causes such as otitis media, cow's milk protein intolerance, GI problems, fracture, and hair tourniquet must be ruled out. The etiology is unknown. It resolves spontaneously, most in the first 3-6 months, with no long-term adverse effects. The rule of 3s (Wessel criteria) is a standard diagnostic criterion.

**Q:** What is the management of infantile colic?

**A:** Parental reassurance and support (essential); adjust feeding technique to minimize air swallowing; comfort measures (holding, soother, car ride, music); limited evidence for probiotics. For breastfed: 1-2 week trial of hypoallergenic maternal diet (no cow's milk, eggs, nuts, wheat). For formula-fed: 1-2 week trial of hydrolyzed formula.

Colic resolves in all cases, most within 3-6 months, with no long-term adverse effects. Parental reassurance is the cornerstone. Dietary trials (maternal elimination or hydrolyzed formula) are time-limited and used to exclude cow's milk protein sensitivity. Simethicone has not been shown effective. Probiotics (Lactobacillus reuteri) show limited but emerging evidence in breastfed infants.

**Q:** Define primary nocturnal enuresis and outline its management.

**A:** Primary nocturnal enuresis: bedwetting when bladder control has never been attained. Affects 10% at age 6, 3% at age 12, 1% at age 18. Management: reassurance (~20% resolve spontaneously/year), behaviour modification (fluid restriction before bed, void before sleep, no caffeine), enuresis alarm (70% success — first-line), desmopressin (DDAVP Melt 120-360 mcg) for short-term use (e.g., sleepovers) in children >7 years.

Primary nocturnal enuresis is a maturational delay in nocturnal bladder control. Additional investigations are not routinely warranted without warning signs. The enuresis (wet) alarm has the highest long-term success rate. Desmopressin (ADH analogue) reduces urine production at night but does not address the underlying cause. It is not recommended as primary treatment and should not be used in children with polydipsia. Comorbidities to screen for include constipation, sleep-disordered breathing, and ADHD.

**Q:** What distinguishes secondary enuresis from primary enuresis, and what are the common causes?

**A:** Secondary enuresis: bedwetting that develops after at least 6 months of sustained bladder control. Causes: psychosocial (stress, anxiety, birth of sibling, family discord, sexual abuse) and organic (UTI, DM, DI, sleep apnea, neurogenic bladder, cerebral palsy, seizures, pinworms).

The key distinction is that secondary enuresis follows a period of dryness (≥6 months), suggesting an acquired cause. Always screen for UTI (urinalysis) and diabetes (urinalysis for glucosuria, fasting glucose). Sexual abuse can present as secondary enuresis. Management targets the underlying cause. Diurnal enuresis (daytime wetting) requires evaluation for structural anomalies, UTI, and neurogenic bladder.

**Q:** What is encopresis, and what is the most common cause in school-aged children?

**A:** Encopresis: fecal incontinence in a child >4 years, at least once/month for 3 months. Most common cause: retentive encopresis due to chronic constipation — child holds bowel movements, leading to fecal impaction and overflow incontinence (seepage of soft/liquid stool around impaction). M:F = 6:1.

Retentive encopresis is a common but often missed diagnosis. The cycle is: painful defecation → withholding → constipation → impaction → overflow incontinence (often mistaken for diarrhea). Physical exam shows palpable stool in LLQ, large fecal mass on digital rectal exam, and anal fissures. Management requires bowel cleanout (PEG 3350, Pico-Salax) followed by maintenance therapy (PEG 3350 + Senna).

**Q:** A 3-month-old infant has been crying for >3 hours per day, >3 days per week, for the past 3 weeks. The parents are exhausted. Exam is normal. What is the diagnosis, management, and prognosis?

**A:** Diagnosis: infantile colic (Wessel/rule of 3s criteria met). Management: reassurance and parental support (essential), minimize air swallowing during feeds, comfort measures (holding, white noise, soother), time-limited trial of hypoallergenic diet (breastfed) or hydrolyzed formula (formula-fed). Prognosis: all cases resolve, most by 3-6 months, no long-term adverse effects.

This is a classic presentation of infantile colic. Before diagnosing colic, other causes of crying must be excluded: otitis media, hair tourniquet, fracture, cow's milk protein intolerance, GI issues, GERD. The diagnosis is one of exclusion. Parental exhaustion and anxiety are significant — supporting parents is a primary goal. Probiotics (L. reuteri) may have limited benefit in breastfed infants.

**Q:** A 6-year-old boy wets the bed 3-4 nights per week. He has never been dry at night. Parents ask about treatment. What is the first-line treatment?

**A:** Enuresis (wet/moisture) alarm — 70% success rate. Also: reassurance (20% resolve spontaneously each year), behaviour modification (limit fluids before bed, avoid caffeine, void before sleep). Desmopressin (DDAVP) is for short-term use (sleepovers) in children >7 years. Punishment should be avoided.

The enuresis alarm works by conditioning: the child wakes when voiding begins, reinforcing arousal from sleep for bladder fullness. It has the highest long-term success rate. Desmopressin is effective short-term but does not treat the underlying condition and has rebound after stopping. Anticholinergic medications and antidepressants are second-line. Additional investigations are not warranted without warning signs.

## Pediatrics Rashes

**Q:** What are the distinguishing features of candidal diaper dermatitis compared to irritant contact diaper dermatitis?

**A:** Candidal diaper dermatitis features erythematous macerated papules/plaques with satellite lesions and involvement of skin folds. Irritant contact dermatitis does NOT involve skin folds, appearing as shiny red macules/patches, and lacks satellite lesions.

Skin fold involvement and satellite lesions are pathognomonic for candidal infection. Irritant contact spares the folds because the folds protect skin from direct contact with urine/feces. Treatment of candidal diaper rash requires antifungal agents (clotrimazole, nystatin), while irritant dermatitis is managed with barrier creams and frequent diaper changes.

## Pediatrics Sleep

**Q:** A 6-month-old infant wakes screaming, is inconsolable, has eyes open but appears disoriented, with signs of autonomic arousal. The episode occurs in the first third of the night. The parents report no memory of the event upon awakening. What is the diagnosis and management?

**A:** Night terrors (pavor nocturnus). Management: parental reassurance, ensure child safety (especially if sleepwalking), identify pattern and wake child 15 minutes before typical episode to disrupt the sleep cycle. Condition often remits spontaneously before puberty.

Night terrors arise from arousal from deep slow-wave (stage 4) sleep in the first third of the night and are characterized by abrupt arousal, screaming, autonomic signs, and no recall of the event. They differ from nightmares (REM sleep, child recalls dream, occurs in last third of night). Stress/anxiety can aggravate night terrors.

**Q:** How do nightmares differ from night terrors in pediatrics regarding sleep stage, timing in the night, recall, and demographics?

**A:** Nightmares: REM sleep, last third of night, child is alert upon awakening and clearly recalls a frightening dream, associated with daytime stress/anxiety, more common in boys aged 4–7 years. Night terrors: deep slow-wave (stage 4) sleep, first third of night, no memory of event, child disoriented if awakened.

Distinguishing nightmares from night terrors is a classic MCCQE1 question. The sleep stage (REM vs slow-wave), timing (late vs early night), and recall (present vs absent) are the key differentiating features. Both are parasomnias but require different counselling to parents.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic criteria and epidemiology of obstructive sleep apnea (OSA) in children?

**A:** OSA definition: partial or intermittent complete airway obstruction during sleep causing disrupted ventilation and sleep pattern. Epidemiology: affects 1–5% of preschool-aged children, more common in Black children. Polysomnography is gold standard for definitive diagnosis but not required to initiate treatment.

In children, OSA most commonly results from adenotonsillar hypertrophy. Clinical features include snoring/gasping during sleep and daytime irritability, tiredness, or hyperactivity (not sleepiness as in adults). Complications include cardiovascular (HTN, LV remodelling), growth, cognitive, and behavioural problems.

**Q:** What is first-line management of OSA in children, and when is CPAP indicated?

**A:** First-line: adenotonsillectomy and weight management. CPAP is indicated when adenotonsillectomy is contraindicated (cleft palate, bleeding disorder, acute tonsillitis), when there is minimal adenotonsillar tissue, or for residual OSA post-surgery. Intranasal corticosteroids are a temporizing measure for mild OSA.

Adenotonsillectomy improves behaviour, quality of life, and polysomnographic findings in children with OSA, though it does NOT improve executive function or attention. Watchful waiting is acceptable in mild-moderate cases. Clinicians should also advise avoidance of pollutants, tobacco smoke, and allergens.

**Q:** Describe the management approach to limit-setting sleep disorder versus sleep-onset association disorder in children.

**A:** Limit-setting sleep disorder (preschool+, due to bedtime resistance/caregiver inability to set rules): positive reinforcement, always sleep in own bed in dark/quiet room, avoid screens before bedtime, set strict bedtimes and wind-down routines, do not use bedroom for timeouts. Sleep-onset association disorder (infants/toddlers, child only falls asleep with specific associations): systematic ignoring and gradual extinction (e.g., Ferber method).

Sleep-onset association disorder occurs when a child learns to fall asleep only under specific conditions (breastfeeding, rocking, parent present) and cannot self-soothe when they wake. Behavioural interventions (extinction techniques) are evidence-based. Limit-setting disorder involves oppositional behaviour at bedtime requiring consistent routine reinforcement.

## Pediatrics Sids

**Q:** What are the risk factors for Sudden Infant Death Syndrome (SIDS) and the key prevention strategies?

**A:** Risk factors — Maternal: young age (<20 yr), smoking/alcohol/drug use in pregnancy, late/no prenatal care. Infant: prematurity, low birthweight, prone sleeping, soft bedding, sibling of SIDS victim (5–6x increased risk). Environmental: second-hand smoke, bed-sharing, overheating, swaddling. Prevention: supine sleeping ('Back to Sleep'), firm mattress, avoid loose bedding/pillows, avoid bed-sharing, avoid tobacco smoke, pacifier use (protective), exclusive breastfeeding, immunizations (protective).

SIDS is the leading cause of death between 1–12 months in full-term infants, with peak incidence at 2–4 months. The 'triple-risk model' proposes an underlying vulnerability + a trigger + a critical developmental period. Infant monitors do NOT reduce incidence. The Canadian Paediatric Society endorses supine sleep positioning.

## Pediatrics Adolescent

**Q:** What is the HEEADSSS adolescent psychosocial assessment tool and what does each letter stand for?

**A:** H – Home, E – Education/Employment, E – Eating, A – Activities, D – Drugs, S – Sexuality, S – Suicide/Depression, S – Safety/Violence.

HEEADSSS is the standard framework for taking an adolescent psychosocial history in Canada. Confidentiality and its limits must be reviewed with the adolescent before taking the history. Clinicians should start with less sensitive topics (Home, Education) and progress to more sensitive ones (Drugs, Sexuality, Suicide). The CRAFFT screen is a validated tool used within the Drugs section.

**Q:** What are the Canadian age of consent rules for sexual activity, including close-age exceptions?

**A:** Legal age of consent is 16 years. A youth 16–17 years CANNOT consent if the partner is in a position of trust/authority. Close-age exceptions: youth 14–15 years can consent if partner is less than 5 years older and there is no relationship of trust, authority, dependency, or exploitation. Youth 12–13 years can consent if partner is less than 2 years older and there is no such relationship.

Understanding Canadian consent laws is mandatory for MCCQE1. These rules reflect the Criminal Code of Canada. Physicians must be able to identify when sexual activity constitutes a reportable offence, particularly when there is exploitation, a power imbalance, or the partner exceeds the close-age exception.

**Q:** Describe the cognitive, psychosocial, and sexual developmental milestones of early (10–13 yr), middle (14–16 yr), and late (17–19 yr) adolescence.

**A:** Early: concrete thinking, unable to perceive long-term consequences, preoccupied with changing body, seeks same-sex peers, increased interest in sexual anatomy but limited dating. Middle: emergence of abstract thought, 'stereotypical adolescent,' intense peer group involvement, initiation of relationships and sexual activity, questions of sexual orientation. Late: future-oriented idealism, stable body image and firm identity, peers recede, focus on intimacy and stable relationships, consolidation of sexual identity.

Knowing the stages of adolescent development helps clinicians tailor counselling, anticipatory guidance, and history-taking strategies. Middle adolescence is characterized by the most peer-driven behaviour and conflict over autonomy, while late adolescence is marked by greater independence and forward-thinking.

**Q:** What is the CRAFFT screening tool and how is it administered?

**A:** CRAFFT is a validated substance use screening tool for adolescents aged 12–21 years. Part A (last 12 months): Did you drink alcohol? Smoke cannabis/vape/inhale? Use anything else to get high? If YES to any → ask all 6 Part B questions. If NO to all → ask only Part B question 1. Part B (C=Car, R=Relax, A=Alone, F=Forget, F=Friends/Family, T=Trouble): ridden in a car driven by someone high/drunk; use to relax/fit in; use alone; forget things while using; family/friends say cut down; gotten in trouble while using.

The CRAFFT screen is the most commonly used adolescent substance use screening tool in Canada and aligns with the HEEADSSS framework. Two or more positive responses on Part B indicates high risk for substance use disorder. It is recommended for routine adolescent health visits.

## Pediatrics Child Abuse

**Q:** What are the legal obligations of Canadian physicians regarding suspected child abuse and neglect?

**A:** Upon reasonable grounds to suspect abuse or neglect, physicians are REQUIRED by legislation to contact local child protection agencies (e.g., Children's Aid Society) and personally disclose all relevant information. The duty to report overrides patient confidentiality; the physician is protected against liability. If additional grounds arise, a further report must be made (ongoing duty to report).

The duty to report is statutory in all Canadian provinces and territories. It applies to all health professionals, not just physicians, and cannot be delegated. 'Reasonable grounds to suspect' is a lower threshold than proof — suspicion alone is sufficient. Failure to report is a legal offence.

**Q:** What are red flag features in the history and physical exam that raise suspicion for physical child abuse?

**A:** History red flags: inconsistent with developmental capabilities, changes dramatically over time, delayed medical attention (unexplained), previous trauma/hospitalizations. Physical exam red flags: patterned skin injuries, bruises on abdomen/buttocks/genitalia/ears/neck/feet, posterior rib/metaphyseal/scapular/vertebral/sternal fractures, immersion burns, retinal hemorrhages, frenulum tears, traumatic alopecia (patchy hair loss).

Traumatic head injury is the leading cause of death in child maltreatment. The TEN-4-FACESp tool screens for concerning bruising in children <4 years. Clinicians must distinguish genuine injuries from 'red herrings' such as Mongolian spots (slate-grey macule/congenital dermal melanocytosis) and cultural practices like cupping.

**Q:** What investigations are required when physical child abuse is suspected?

**A:** Document injuries on a body diagram; photography preferred (police or hospital). Rule out medical causes: if fractures — Ca²⁺, Mg²⁺, PO₄²⁻, ALP, PTH, Vitamin D, albumin. If bruising — CBC, INR, PTT, von Willebrand factor, factors VIII/IX. Abdominal trauma screen: if elevated transaminases/amylase or bruising → abdominal CT. Toxicology screen. Skeletal survey in children <2 years. Neuroimaging (CT/MRI) + dilated eye exam by pediatric ophthalmologist if subdural hemorrhage detected.

A skeletal survey is mandatory in children under 2 years to detect occult fractures. Posterior rib fractures and classic metaphyseal lesions are highly specific for inflicted injury. Ruling out osteogenesis imperfecta, rickets, and bleeding disorders is essential before attributing injuries to abuse.

**Q:** What are the key features of the medical assessment for sexual abuse in a prepubertal child?

**A:** History gathered from caregiver in absence of child (do not take abuse history from young child — requires forensic interview). Anogenital exam performed by trained provider; speculum contraindicated in prepubertal girls; instrumentation not required. Most victims have a NORMAL anogenital exam — a negative exam does NOT rule out sexual abuse. Investigations: STI screen, pregnancy test, consider STI prophylaxis/emergency contraception, drug/alcohol screen if altered consciousness (concern for drug-facilitated assault).

The absence of physical findings is common in child sexual abuse and does not exclude the diagnosis. Referral to a child maltreatment pediatrician or sexual assault/domestic violence centre is appropriate for a comprehensive sexual assault examination kit. This is a legally and emotionally sensitive area requiring specialized training.

**Q:** What are the forms of child neglect and how is medical neglect defined?

**A:** Physical neglect: failure to provide adequate food, clothing, shelter, supervision, protection. Emotional neglect: failure to provide affection/love/emotional support; child ignored, rejected, prevented from interacting with others. Medical neglect: failure to obtain appropriate medical care for the child, including needed treatment for injuries, physical or mental health disorders, or delaying medical care when child is ill.

Neglect (acts of omission) is distinct from abuse (acts of commission). Neglect is the most common form of child maltreatment. It spans physical, emotional, and medical domains. Physicians have the same duty to report neglect as they do for abuse. Investigations are guided by the specific neglect concern and may include tests to rule out nutritional deficiencies (e.g., thrombocytopenia, coagulopathy).

## Pediatrics Cardiology Chd

**Q:** What are the three fetal circulatory shunts, and what happens to each at birth?

**A:** 1. Ductus arteriosus (pulmonary artery → aorta): closes with increased O₂ concentration after first breath → decreased prostaglandins → closure. 2. Foramen ovale (RA → LA): closes with increased left atrial pressure from increased pulmonic blood flow. 3. Ductus venosus (umbilical vein → IVC): closes with separation of low-resistance placenta.

Fetal circulation is designed to preferentially deliver oxygenated blood to the brain and myocardium. At birth, with the first breath, pulmonary resistance decreases, pulmonary blood flow increases, and systemic resistance rises. Understanding these transitions is fundamental to the pathophysiology of congenital heart disease.

**Q:** What is the most common congenital heart defect, and what is the most common cyanotic congenital heart defect diagnosed beyond infancy?

**A:** Most common overall CHD: ventricular septal defect (VSD) — 30–50% of all CHD. Most common cyanotic CHD beyond infancy: Tetralogy of Fallot (10% of CHD, peak incidence 2–4 months).

The most common cyanotic CHD in neonates is transposition of the great arteries (TGA). VSD accounts for the largest proportion of congenital heart defects. The epidemiology of CHD is 8 per 1000 live births overall.

**Q:** What are the characteristic CXR findings for Tetralogy of Fallot, Transposition of Great Arteries, and Total Anomalous Pulmonary Venous Return?

**A:** Tetralogy of Fallot: boot-shaped heart (coeur en sabot), decreased pulmonary vasculature, right aortic arch in 25%. Transposition of Great Arteries: egg-shaped heart with narrow mediastinum ('egg on a string'). Total Anomalous Pulmonary Venous Return: 'snowman' heart.

These are classic radiological signs tested on MCCQE1. Boot-shaped heart reflects right ventricular hypertrophy with pulmonary oligemia. The egg-on-a-string appearance in TGA is due to the narrow superior mediastinum from the parallel position of the great vessels.

**Q:** What are the three types of Atrial Septal Defect (ASD), which is most common, and what are the characteristic physical exam and ECG findings?

**A:** Types: ostium secundum (most common, 50–70%, at septum between LA and RA), ostium primum (common in Down syndrome, near mitral/tricuspid valves), sinus venosus (at SVC–RA junction). Physical exam: grade 2–3/6 pulmonic outflow murmur, widely split and fixed S2. ECG: RAD, mild RVH, RBBB.

The fixed split S2 is pathognomonic for ASD. The split is fixed because ASD equalizes preload between ventricles throughout the respiratory cycle. ASDs <8 mm have an 80–100% spontaneous closure rate. Surgical or catheter closure is done electively between ages 2–5 years if indicated.

**Q:** What is the relationship between VSD size and murmur intensity, and what are the clinical features of moderate-to-large VSD?

**A:** VSD size is INVERSELY related to murmur intensity — a smaller VSD produces a louder murmur. Moderate-to-large VSD: holosystolic murmur at LLSB, mid-diastolic rumble at apex, delayed growth, decreased exercise tolerance, recurrent URTIs/'asthma' episodes, loss of S2 splitting and loud P2 (suggests pulmonary HTN). CHF develops by 2 months if untreated.

The inverse relationship between VSD size and murmur intensity is a classic exam pearl. A restrictive (small) VSD creates a high-pressure gradient across a small defect — loud murmur. A large VSD has equalized pressures, reducing turbulence — softer murmur. Surgical closure before 1 year is required for large VSDs to prevent irreversible pulmonary vascular disease.

**Q:** What is the classic murmur of Patent Ductus Arteriosus (PDA), and how does management differ between premature and term infants?

**A:** Classic murmur: continuous 'machinery' murmur best heard at left infraclavicular area, with bounding pulses and wide pulse pressure. Premature infants: indomethacin (Indocid®) — antagonizes prostaglandin E2 that maintains ductal patency; effective only in premature infants. Term infants: indomethacin NOT effective; catheter or surgical closure if causing respiratory compromise, FTT, or persists beyond 3 months.

PDA occurs due to failure of the ductus arteriosus to close after birth. In premature infants, the ductal smooth muscle is less responsive to oxygen, so indomethacin (a prostaglandin inhibitor) is effective. In term infants, the pathophysiology is a structural/functional defect, making indomethacin ineffective. PDA accounts for 5–10% of CHD.

**Q:** What are the clinical features and associations of Coarctation of the Aorta?

**A:** Definition: narrowing of aorta at the level of the ductus arteriosus. Associations: bicuspid aortic valve (50%), Turner syndrome (35%). Physical exam: BP discrepancy between upper and lower extremities (>20 mmHg is significant), diminished/delayed femoral pulses (brachial-femoral delay), systolic murmur at apex/left axilla/left back. Severe: shock in neonatal period when ductus closes. Management: prostaglandins to keep ductus patent → surgical correction in neonates; balloon arterioplasty for older infants/children.

Coarctation of the aorta is a classic cause of hypertension in a young person with differential blood pressures. Turner syndrome (45,X) has a strong association. ECG shows RVH in early infancy and LVH later. Complications include HTN; associated with PDA/VSD → CHF.

**Q:** What are the four components of Tetralogy of Fallot, and how do 'tet spells' present and get managed?

**A:** Four components (PROVE mnemonic): Pulmonary stenosis/RVOTO, Right ventricular hypertrophy, Overriding aorta, VSD. Tet spells: triggered by exertion (crying, exercise) → increased pulmonary vascular resistance + decreased systemic resistance → increased R-to-L shunting → paroxysmal cyanosis, rapid deep breathing, irritability, decreased murmur intensity; patient squats for relief. Management: O₂, knee-chest position, IV fluid bolus, morphine sulfate, propranolol, phenylephrine. Definitive treatment: surgical repair at 4–6 months.

Squatting increases peripheral vascular resistance, which reduces right-to-left shunting and improves oxygenation. The knee-chest position achieves the same effect in infants. Tetralogy of Fallot has an embryological origin from anterior-superior deviation of the outlet septum. CXR shows boot-shaped heart with decreased pulmonary vasculature.

**Q:** What is the pathophysiology of Transposition of the Great Arteries (TGA) and how is it initially managed?

**A:** Pathophysiology: parallel pulmonary and systemic circulations — systemic: body→RA→RV→aorta→body; pulmonary: lungs→LA→LV→PA→lungs. Survival depends on mixing via PDA, ASD, or VSD. CXR: egg-shaped heart ('egg on a string'). Initial management: prostaglandin E1 infusion to keep ductus open → balloon atrial septostomy (Rashkind). Surgical repair: arterial switch operation within first 2 weeks (before LV muscle mass decreases). TGA without VSD has no murmur.

TGA is the most common cyanotic CHD in neonates (3–5% of all CHD). Without mixing, oxygenated blood circulates only through the pulmonary circuit, and deoxygenated blood only through the systemic circuit — incompatible with life. The arterial switch operation must be done early while the LV is still conditioned to handle systemic pressures.

**Q:** What is the hyperoxic (hyperoxia) test and how is it interpreted in a cyanotic neonate?

**A:** Procedure: obtain preductal (right radial) ABG in room air, then repeat after the child breathes 100% O₂ for 10 minutes. Interpretation: if PaO₂ improves to >150 mmHg → cyanosis is less likely cardiac in origin (more likely pulmonary). If PaO₂ remains low (<150 mmHg) → cardiac cyanosis likely (R-to-L shunt). Pre- and post-ductal pulse oximetry: >5% difference suggests R-to-L shunt.

The hyperoxia test exploits the fact that pulmonary causes of cyanosis (e.g., respiratory distress syndrome) respond to supplemental oxygen, whereas cardiac R-to-L shunts do not, because blood bypasses the alveoli entirely. Pre- vs post-ductal oxygen saturation differences help identify ductal-dependent lesions.

**Q:** What is Hypoplastic Left Heart Syndrome (HLHS) and how is it managed?

**A:** HLHS: LV hypoplasia ± atretic/stenotic mitral and/or aortic valve, small ascending aorta, and coarctation. Epidemiology: 1–3% of CHD; most common cause of cardiac death in first month of life. Systemic circulation is ductus-dependent. Presentation: circulatory shock + metabolic acidosis when ductus closes. Management: intubate, correct metabolic acidosis, IV prostaglandin E1 to keep ductus patent, surgical palliation (Norwood procedure, overall survival 50%) or heart transplant.

HLHS is a duct-dependent lesion — any neonate presenting with cardiogenic shock shortly after birth should raise suspicion for HLHS or critical coarctation, both of which require immediate prostaglandin E1. The 4 key features are hypoplastic LV, narrow mitral/aortic valves, small ascending aorta, and coarctation.

**Q:** What are the 5 Ts of cyanotic congenital heart disease?

**A:** 1. Tetralogy of Fallot, 2. Transposition of the great vessels, 3. Tricuspid atresia, 4. Truncus arteriosus, 5. Total anomalous pulmonary venous return (TAPVR).

The 5 Ts is a classic mnemonic for cyanotic CHD. Each involves right-to-left shunting or a mixing lesion where deoxygenated blood enters the systemic circulation. Ebstein's anomaly and Hypoplastic Left Heart Syndrome are additional cyanotic lesions that don't fit the T mnemonic. All present with cyanosis that fails to improve significantly with supplemental oxygen.

**Q:** What are the four key clinical features of pediatric congestive heart failure?

**A:** The '2 Tachys and 2 Megalys': Tachycardia, Tachypnea, Cardiomegaly, Hepatomegaly. Additional features in infants: weak cry, diaphoresis while eating/sleeping, feeding difficulties, FTT. In children: decreased exercise tolerance, frequent URTIs or 'asthma' episodes.

Pediatric CHF differs from adult CHF in that orthopnea, paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnea, and pedal edema are uncommon in children. The predominant features in infants are feeding difficulties and diaphoresis. The most common causes are CHD, cardiomyopathy, high-output states, pressure overload (aortic/pulmonary stenosis), and volume overload (L-to-R shunts).

**Q:** What is Total Anomalous Pulmonary Venous Return (TAPVR) and what is the required management?

**A:** All pulmonary veins drain into the right-sided circulation (systemic veins or RA) instead of the LA. No direct oxygenated pulmonary venous return to the LA. An ASD must be present for oxygenated blood to reach the systemic circulation. Often associated with obstruction at connection sites. CXR: 'snowman' heart. Management: surgical repair required in ALL cases; urgent surgery for severe cyanosis.

TAPVR accounts for 1–2% of CHD. It is a mixing lesion and is included among the cyanotic CHDs. The presence of obstruction at the anomalous connection determines severity — obstructed TAPVR presents with severe cyanosis and pulmonary edema in the newborn period and is a surgical emergency.

**Q:** What are the acyanotic vs. cyanotic classifications of congenital heart disease and the mechanism explaining cyanosis?

**A:** Acyanotic CHD (L-to-R shunt or obstruction beyond lungs): blood passes through pulmonary circulation → oxygenation occurs → low levels of deoxygenated Hb in systemic circulation → no cyanosis. Examples: ASD, VSD, PDA, coarctation, aortic/pulmonary stenosis. Cyanotic CHD (R-to-L shunt): blood bypasses lungs → no oxygenation → high levels of deoxygenated Hb in systemic circulation → cyanosis (absolute deoxygenated Hb ≥30 g/dL). Examples: 5 Ts + Ebstein's anomaly + HLHS.

Cyanosis becomes clinically apparent when absolute concentration of deoxygenated hemoglobin reaches 30 g/dL. This is why anemic patients may not appear cyanotic despite severe hypoxemia. Untreated L-to-R shunts can eventually reverse to R-to-L (Eisenmenger syndrome) due to progressive pulmonary vascular disease.

## Pediatrics Safety

**Q:** What is the epidemiology of drowning in Canadian children and what are the key risk factors?

**A:** Drowning is one of the leading causes of death in children aged 1–4 years. Babies and toddlers drown most often at home in bathtubs and swimming pools. Risk factors: inability to swim, inadequate fencing around pools/water, absence of approved personal flotation devices, risk-taking behaviour, alcohol and substance use (adolescents), prescription medication causing drowsiness, inadequate supervision.

Drowning is defined as the process of experiencing respiratory impairment from liquid submersion and includes both fatal and nonfatal events. Duration of submersion is the most critical factor in outcome — the rate of death or severe neurologic injury is low when submerged for less than 5 minutes. Survivors can sustain long-term pulmonary, neurologic, and cardiovascular injury.

## Pediatrics Cardiology

**Q:** What are the key ECG features that distinguish supraventricular tachycardia (SVT) from sinus tachycardia in a child?

**A:** SVT: rate >220 bpm (infants) or >180 bpm (children), NO beat-to-beat HR variability, P waves absent or abnormal, PR indeterminable, QRS usually narrow. Sinus tachycardia: beat-to-beat HR variability with activity changes, P waves present/normal, PR constant, QRS narrow.

SVT is the most frequent sustained dysrhythmia in children. Differentiating it from sinus tachycardia is clinically important. Sinus tachycardia rate can be slowed by vagal maneuvers or beta-blockers to reveal sinus P waves; SVT does not show this variability.

**Q:** What is the most common cause of congenital complete heart block in a neonate?

**A:** Maternal anti-Ro (SSA) or anti-La (SSB) antibodies — classically in a mother with SLE or other connective tissue disease. The block is often diagnosed in utero and may lead to fetal hydrops.

Maternal autoantibodies cross the placenta and damage the fetal atrioventricular conduction system. This is the most clinically important cause of congenital complete heart block. Symptomatic patients require a pacemaker.

**Q:** What ECG features are normal in a neonate but would be abnormal in an older child or adult?

**A:** Normal neonatal ECG findings: right axis deviation (RAD), large precordial R waves (due to RV dominance), shorter PR/QT intervals and QRS duration, upright T waves in right precordial leads, inferior and lateral small Q waves.

The right ventricle is larger than the left in neonates due to in utero physiology, explaining RV dominance on ECG. These findings normalize as the LV becomes dominant with postnatal systemic circulation.

**Q:** Which features distinguish a pathological heart murmur from an innocent murmur in a child?

**A:** Pathological features: symptoms/signs of cardiac disease (FTT, exercise intolerance), diastolic, pansystolic, or continuous timing (except venous hum), grade ≥3/6 or palpable thrill, harsh quality, fixed split or single S2, extra sounds/clicks, murmur unchanged with position. Innocent: asymptomatic, soft/blowing/vibratory, <3/6, physiologic S2 splitting, varies with position.

50–80% of children have audible murmurs at some point. Most are functional/innocent. Key red flags pointing to pathology include diastolic timing, a thrill, fixed S2 splitting (as in ASD), or associated symptoms. Innocent murmurs accentuate in high-output states like fever or anemia.

**Q:** Describe the five types of innocent heart murmurs in children, including their age groups and locations.

**A:** 1. Peripheral Pulmonic Stenosis — neonates, left upper sternal border, systolic ejection, radiates to axilla/back (disappears by 3–6 mo). 2. Still's Murmur — 3–6 yr, LLSB/apex, systolic ejection, high-pitched vibratory. 3. Venous Hum — 3–6 yr, infraclavicular (R>L), continuous. 4. Pulmonary Flow Murmur — 8–14 yr, left upper sternal border, systolic ejection, soft blowing. 5. Supraclavicular Arterial Bruit — any age, supraclavicular, systolic ejection, low intensity.

Each innocent murmur has characteristic features that must be distinguished from pathological counterparts. Peripheral pulmonic stenosis mimics true PS or PDA; venous hum is the only innocent continuous murmur and can mimic PDA; Still's murmur DDx includes small VSD and subaortic stenosis.

**Q:** What premature ventricular contraction (PVC) features in a child suggest a benign etiology?

**A:** Benign PVCs: single morphology (uniform), disappear with exercise, no associated structural cardiac lesions. PVCs are common in adolescents. Non-benign PVCs: multiform (polymorphic), persist or worsen with exercise, associated with structural heart disease — may degenerate into more serious dysrhythmias.

PVCs in children are generally benign if they are isolated, uniform, and exercise-related disappearance is demonstrated. Exercise stress testing can help differentiate benign from pathological PVCs. Electrolyte disturbances and digitalis toxicity can also cause PVCs.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic criteria for Kawasaki Disease, and what is the mnemonic?

**A:** Mnemonic: Warm CREAM. Fever >5 days PLUS ≥4 of: Conjunctival injection (bilateral, non-exudative); Rash (polymorphous); Edema of hands/feet (acute) or periungual desquamation (subacute); Adenopathy (cervical lymphadenopathy >1.5 cm, usually unilateral); Mucosal changes (fissured lips, strawberry tongue, injected pharynx). Exclusion of other diseases (scarlet fever, measles). Incomplete Kawasaki: fever ≥5 days + only 2-3 features → further evaluation with CRP, ESR, supplemental lab criteria.

Kawasaki disease is the most common cause of acquired heart disease in children in developed countries (Canada included). Peak age 3 months–5 years; most common in East Asian/Pacific Islander descent (especially Japanese). The diagnosis is clinical. Incomplete Kawasaki disease is important to recognize because children with fewer classic features can still develop coronary aneurysms.

**Q:** What is the treatment of Kawasaki Disease and what complication does it prevent?

**A:** Initial therapy: IVIG 2 g/kg (single dose) + low-dose ASA 3-5 mg/kg/d (max 325 mg/d). IVIG must be given within 10 days of fever onset to reduce risk of coronary artery aneurysm formation. If fever persists 24-36 h after IVIG: repeat IVIG at same dose; if second dose fails, trial a third IVIG or IV pulse methylprednisolone, or consult rheumatology. Baseline 2D echocardiogram and follow-up at 2 and 6 weeks. Anticoagulation for multiple or large coronary aneurysms.

Without treatment, coronary artery aneurysms occur in 20-25% of children with Kawasaki disease. IVIG within 10 days reduces this to <5%. 50% of aneurysms regress within 2 years. ASA is used initially at anti-inflammatory doses and then continued at antiplatelet doses once fever resolves. The combination of IVIG + ASA is the cornerstone of therapy per Canadian and international guidelines.

**Q:** How is hypertension defined in children, and how does it differ from 'elevated BP' (formerly pre-HTN)?

**A:** HTN: sBP and/or dBP ≥95th percentile for sex, age, and height on ≥3 occasions. Elevated BP (pre-HTN): sBP and/or dBP ≥90th but <95th percentile, OR BP ≥120/80 irrespective of age, sex, and height.

Childhood BP thresholds are age-, sex-, and height-specific (unlike adult cut-offs of 140/90). A 17-year-old male at the 75th height percentile has a 95th percentile BP of ~137/86 mmHg. White coat hypertension is common in all pediatric ages and should be ruled out with 24-hour ambulatory monitoring.

**Q:** What is the most common cause of hypertension in adolescents vs. young children?

**A:** Adolescents (≥10 yr): primary (essential) hypertension accounts for ~90% of cases — associated with obesity, positive family history, male sex, mild HTN. Young children (<10 yr, especially infants): secondary hypertension predominates — identifiable causes including renal parenchymal disease, renovascular abnormalities, coarctation of the aorta.

In neonates, renal artery thrombosis and coarctation of the aorta are key vascular causes. CAH and neuroblastoma are important endocrine/metabolic causes. In prepubertal children, the absence of family history and presence of renal disease suggest secondary HTN. Always consider white coat HTN and ensure appropriate cuff size.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of hypertensive emergency in children?

**A:** Neurological: headache, seizures, focal complaints, change in mental status, visual disturbances. Cardiovascular: symptoms of MI or heart failure (chest pain, palpitations, cough, SOB).

Most children with HTN are asymptomatic; hypertensive emergency represents acute end-organ damage. Non-specific symptoms (FTT, fatigue, epistaxis) may occur. The differential exam should include: four-limb BPs (coarctation), abdominal/renal bruit (renal artery stenosis), ambiguous genitalia (CAH), and signs of renal parenchymal disease (edema).

**Q:** What is the pharmacologic management of hypertension in children, including hypertensive emergency?

**A:** Gradual BP lowering using thiazide diuretics (no antihypertensives formally studied in children). For hypertensive emergency: hydralazine, labetalol, or sodium nitroprusside. Non-pharmacologic: weight reduction, exercise, salt restriction, cessation of smoking/recreational drugs.

Management also targets the underlying cause (secondary HTN) and end-organ damage (retinopathy, LVH assessed by echo). ACE inhibitors or ARBs are used for persistent HTN in nephrotic syndrome. Referral to a specialist Pediatrician or Pediatric Nephrologist is recommended.

## Pediatrics Neurodevelopment

**Q:** Define Global Developmental Delay (GDD) and its epidemiology.

**A:** GDD: significant delay (≥2 SDs below mean on standardized tests) in ≥2 developmental domains (gross motor, fine motor, speech/language, cognitive, social/personal, ADLs) in a child <5 years. Prevalence: up to 3% of pediatric population; 40–80% have a defined etiology.

GDD may predict future intellectual disability. After age 5, the term 'intellectual disability' replaces GDD since catch-up is no longer expected. Identifying the etiology guides treatment and genetic counseling.

**Q:** What are the first-line investigations recommended by the Canadian Paediatric Society (CPS) for a child with Global Developmental Delay?

**A:** First line: chromosomal microarray AND Fragile X DNA testing. Plus formal vision and hearing tests. 'Tier 1' metabolic labs (blood): CBC, glucose, blood gas, urea, creatinine, electrolytes with anion gap, AST, ALT, TSH, CK, ammonia, lactate, amino acids, acylcarnitine profile, carnitine, homocysteine, copper, ceruloplasmin, biotinidase. Urine: organic acids, creatine metabolites, purines, pyrimidines, glycosaminoglycans.

CPS guidelines recommend a stepwise approach guided by history and physical. Chromosomal microarray and Fragile X testing are the highest yield first-line genetic tests. Brain imaging is indicated for micro/macrocephaly, seizures, or abnormal neurological findings.

**Q:** What are the three defining domains for Intellectual Disability (ID) diagnosis, and how is severity classified?

**A:** Three domains: (1) deficits in intellectual function confirmed by clinical assessment and standardized testing (historically IQ <70), (2) deficits in adaptive functioning, (3) onset during the developmental period. Severity (mild, moderate, severe, profound) is based on adaptive function, NOT IQ.

The DSM-5 shifted ID severity classification from IQ to adaptive functioning, emphasizing functional impact over a single number. Adaptive function includes communication, self-care, social skills, work, leisure, health, and safety. Prevalence is 1–3%; M:F = 1.5:1.

**Q:** What are the risk factors for sensorineural hearing loss in infants?

**A:** Genetic syndromes/family history, congenital (TORCH) infections, ototoxic medications, birth weight <1500 g, hyperbilirubinemia/kernicterus, asphyxia/low APGAR scores, bacterial meningitis, and viral encephalitis.

Early identification of hearing loss is critical because language development is most sensitive in the first 3 years of life. Universal newborn hearing screening programs are standard in Canada, with audiological follow-up for high-risk infants.

**Q:** What is the epidemiology and key prognostic point for language delay in children?

**A:** Prevalence: ~10–15% of 2-year-olds have language delay, but only 4–5% remain delayed after age 3. ~6–8% of school-aged children have specific language impairment. M>F. Best prognosis for developmental speech delay. If language delay persists beyond age 5, more likely to have difficulties in adulthood, including poor academic performance and social isolation.

Most early language delays resolve spontaneously, but persistent delay requires intervention. Referral to speech-language pathology is the most important management step. Language delay beyond age 5 is a significant prognostic marker for lifelong difficulties.

**Q:** A 2-year-old boy presents with language delay. What is the most important first management step?

**A:** Referral to speech-language pathology (SLP) is the most important step. Also consider formal hearing assessment (audiology referral), as hearing impairment is a common treatable cause of language delay.

Management of language delay is etiology-specific. Hearing must always be assessed because even mild hearing loss significantly impacts language development. SLP provides formal evaluation and therapy. Dental assessment and optometry referral may also be warranted.

**Q:** Define Specific Learning Disorder (SLD) and list its three main types.

**A:** SLD: specific and persistent failure to acquire academic skills despite conventional instruction, adequate intelligence, and sociocultural opportunity — a significant discrepancy between intellectual ability and academic performance. Types: (1) Reading (dyslexia), (2) Written expression, (3) Mathematics (dyscalculia). Prevalence: 10%; dyslexia most common.

SLD is NOT caused by poor visual acuity. It is associated with psychiatric comorbidities (anxiety, dysthymia, conduct disorder, MDD, ODD, ADHD) and has a 40% school drop-out rate. Management includes individualized education plans (IEP) and curriculum accommodations.

**Q:** Poor visual acuity is a recognized cause of Specific Learning Disorder. True or False?

**A:** FALSE. Poor visual acuity is explicitly NOT a cause of specific learning disorder.

This is a common misconception. SLD arises from neurological processing differences, not sensory deficits. Known associations include genetic/metabolic conditions (Turner, Klinefelter), prematurity, CNS damage, FAS, and psychosocial deprivation — not refractive errors.

**Q:** What are the sentinel facial features of Fetal Alcohol Spectrum Disorder (FASD)?

**A:** Three sentinel facial features: (1) short palpebral fissures (<2 SD below mean), (2) flattened philtrum, (3) thin upper lip. Having all three is highly specific for prenatal alcohol exposure.

FASD ranges from most to least severe: FAS, partial FAS, ARBD (alcohol-related birth defects), and ARND (alcohol-related neurodevelopmental disorder). FASD is the most common preventable cause of intellectual disability. Prevalence of FAS: 0.1%; FASD: 1.0%.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic criteria for FASD with sentinel facial features?

**A:** FASD with sentinel facial features requires ALL THREE: (1) presence of all three sentinel facial features (short palpebral fissures, flattened philtrum, thin upper lip), (2) maternal alcohol consumption confirmed OR unknown, (3) evidence of neurodevelopmental impairment OR microcephaly in infants/young children.

FASD without sentinel features requires confirmed maternal alcohol consumption plus evidence of neurodevelopmental impairment. Neurodevelopmental dysfunction requires ≥3 domains affected: motor skills, neuroanatomy/neurophysiology, cognition, language, academic achievement, memory, attention, executive function, affect regulation, adaptive behaviour/social skills, or microcephaly.

**Q:** What factors improve prognosis in FASD?

**A:** Prognosis is improved if: (1) diagnosed before age 6, (2) social and educational supports are available, (3) nurturing living environment. Secondary disabilities include unemployment, mental health problems, legal difficulties, inappropriate sexual behaviour, disrupted school experience, and peer problems.

Early diagnosis is essential to prevent secondary disabilities by connecting children to therapies and supports early. There is no cure. Growth and nutrition must be monitored as nutritional deficiencies are common.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 diagnostic criteria domains for Autism Spectrum Disorder (ASD)?

**A:** Domain 1 — Persistent deficits in social communication and interaction across three areas: (a) social-emotional reciprocity, (b) nonverbal communicative behaviours, (c) developing/maintaining/understanding relationships. Domain 2 — Restricted, repetitive patterns of behaviour/interests (≥2 of): stereotyped/repetitive movements, insistence on sameness, highly restricted fixated interests, hyper-/hypo-reactivity to sensory input. Symptoms must be present in early developmental period and cause clinically significant impairment.

ASD prevalence is 1 in 36 children (M:F = 4:1). Severity specifiers: requiring support, substantial support, or very substantial support. Must not be better explained by intellectual disability or GDD alone.

**Q:** What pharmacotherapy is used in ASD, and what does it treat?

**A:** Pharmacotherapy does NOT treat ASD itself but addresses concomitant symptoms: atypical antipsychotics (for irritability, aggression, agitation, self-mutilation, tics), SSRIs (for anxiety and depression), stimulants (for associated inattention and hyperactivity). Comorbid ADHD, tics, OCD, anxiety, depression, and seizure disorder should be actively treated.

The mainstay of ASD treatment is team-based psychosocial intervention including behavioural therapy, social skills training, SLP, OT, and school programming. Pharmacotherapy is adjunctive. Prognosis is variable but improves with early intervention.

**Q:** What are the DSM-5 diagnostic requirements for ADHD in children?

**A:** Onset before age 12, persistent symptoms >6 months, symptoms in >2 settings (home, school, work), interference with academic/family/social functioning. Subtypes: combined type (≥6 inattention AND ≥6 hyperactivity-impulsivity), predominantly inattentive (≥6 inattention), predominantly hyperactive-impulsive (≥6 hyperactivity-impulsivity). For adolescents/adults >17 yr, only ≥5 symptoms required. Not explained by another psychiatric disorder.

ADHD prevalence is 5–12% in school-aged children (M:F = 4:1). Girls tend to present with inattentive symptoms and may be under-diagnosed. Heritability is ~75% with dopaminergic candidate genes (DAT1, DRD4). Neurobiological basis involves decreased catecholamine transmission and low PFC activity.

**Q:** What is the first-line pharmacotherapy for ADHD in school-aged children (≥6 years)?

**A:** First line: stimulant medications — methylphenidate or amphetamines. Second line: atomoxetine and guanfacine XR. Third line: clonidine, bupropion, imipramine. For children <6 years: psychosocial intervention is FIRST LINE. For children ≥6 years: psychosocial intervention PLUS medication is first line.

Stimulant treatment of ADHD in children does NOT increase the risk of substance misuse later in life (contrary to common concern). 70–80% of ADHD symptoms continue into adolescence but hyperactive symptoms usually abate. 65% continue into adulthood.

**Q:** What is the difference between ADHD presentation in girls versus boys?

**A:** Boys tend to present with hyperactive/impulsive symptoms (more visible/disruptive). Girls tend to present with predominantly inattentive symptoms, leading to under-diagnosis. Prevalence M:F = 4:1, though the true ratio may be closer due to under-recognition in girls.

The predominantly inattentive subtype is less disruptive to classroom function, leading to delayed identification in girls. This has important equity implications for diagnosis and treatment access. Clinicians should maintain a high index of suspicion for ADHD in girls with academic or social difficulties.

**Q:** A 3-year-old presents with regression in language skills. What diagnoses in the differential diagnosis should be considered?

**A:** Regression in language/developmental skills raises concern for: Rett syndrome, epileptic encephalopathy (Landau-Kleffner syndrome — acquired epileptic aphasia), ASD, childhood disintegrative disorder, metabolic/degenerative neurological disorders (Leigh encephalopathy). Also consider: hearing impairment, abuse, seizure disorder.

Language regression (loss of previously acquired skills) is a RED FLAG requiring urgent evaluation. Landau-Kleffner syndrome involves acquired epileptic aphasia and is distinct from other language disorders. ASD may present with regression, typically before age 3. EEG and neuroimaging are indicated in regression cases.

**Q:** What is the long-term prognosis of ADHD into adulthood?

**A:** 70–80% of children with ADHD continue to have symptoms into adolescence, but hyperactive symptoms usually abate. 65% continue into adulthood. Adults may develop secondary personality disorders and compensatory anxiety disorders. Stimulant treatment in childhood does NOT increase risk of substance use disorder later in life.

The persistence of ADHD into adulthood is well-documented. While hyperactivity diminishes, inattention and executive dysfunction often remain. Comorbidities in adulthood include adult ASPD (if childhood CD/ODD was present), depression, and anxiety.

**Q:** What are the etiology categories for Global Developmental Delay and their approximate diagnostic yields?

**A:** Prenatal biological (genetic abnormalities, CNS deformities, metabolic): up to 7% of total GDD cases. Prenatal environmental (teratogens, infections): ~11%. Perinatal (asphyxia/neonatal encephalopathy, prematurity, low birth weight): up to 55%. Postnatal (neglect, infections, trauma, kernicterus, toxins): ~12%.

Perinatal factors account for the largest proportion of GDD cases with an identified etiology. However, 20–60% of GDD cases have no identifiable cause. The CPS stepwise diagnostic algorithm guides investigations based on history and physical examination findings.

## Pediatrics Endocrinology

**Q:** What are the diagnostic criteria and epidemiology of Type 1 Diabetes Mellitus (T1DM) in Canadian children?

**A:** Epidemiology: most common form of DM in children (M=F), affects 32 per 100,000 children in Canada, bimodal peaks at 5–9 and 10–14 years. Diagnosis (any one): symptoms (polyuria, polydipsia, weight loss) + random glucose ≥11.1 mmol/L; OR two of: fasting glucose ≥7.0 mmol/L, 2h OGTT ≥11.1 mmol/L, HbA1c ≥6.5% on one occasion; OR each of those on two separate occasions without symptoms.

30% of children with T1DM present with DKA (higher incidence in younger children). Diabetes Canada guidelines recommend an HbA1c target of <7.5% for children with T1DM (less stringent than <7% to minimize hypoglycemia). HbA1c is not recommended as the sole diagnostic test in G6PD deficiency.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of T1DM presentation in children, and what is the cornerstone of management?

**A:** Clinical features: polyuria (may manifest as nocturia or secondary enuresis), polydipsia, weight loss (catabolic state), polyphagia, perineal candidiasis (younger children), visual disturbances, DKA (30% of presentations). Management cornerstone: family-centred pediatric multidisciplinary team providing education and support for survival skills, meal plans, and insulin injections.

Diet should maintain consistent carbohydrate levels avoiding high glycemic index foods. Approximately 60 min of aerobic exercise daily is recommended. Children are more susceptible to hypoglycemia. Complications screening includes ophthalmology, microalbuminuria, diabetic foot exam, thyroiditis, celiac disease, mental health, HTN, and dyslipidemia.

**Q:** What is the Diabetes Canada HbA1c target for children with T1DM, and why is it different from adults?

**A:** Target: <7.5% for children with T1DM (less stringent than the <7% recommended for adults and the <7% target for children with T2DM). The rationale is to minimize hypoglycemia, as children are more susceptible to hypoglycemia and its neurological consequences.

Severe hypoglycemia in children can lead to seizures, coma, and permanent neurological complications. The slightly relaxed HbA1c target balances glycemic control with hypoglycemia prevention. This contrasts with T2DM in children where <7% is targeted.

**Q:** How is pediatric DKA managed differently from adult DKA, and what is the most feared complication?

**A:** Pediatric-specific protocols: ABCs, 100% O2, admit, monitor, correct fluid losses with ISOTONIC fluids. Insulin: avoid bolus; delay infusion until 1 hour after fluid resuscitation; SC if mild DKA, IV if moderate/severe. Normalize glucose GRADUALLY. Monitor BG, electrolytes, fluids, neurological status. Maintain potassium in fluids; add glucose once BG reaches threshold. Most feared complication: cerebral edema (0.5–1% of DKA cases). If suspected: mannitol, restrict IV fluids, move to ICU; imaging only after stabilization.

~40% of children with new-onset T1DM present with DKA. Cerebral edema is the leading cause of DKA-related mortality in children. Warning signs of neurological deterioration: headache, bradycardia, irritability, decreased LOC, incontinence, focal neurological signs.

**Q:** What are the warning signs of cerebral edema during DKA treatment in children?

**A:** Headache, bradycardia, irritability, decreased level of consciousness (LOC), incontinence, and specific neurological signs. Management: give mannitol, restrict IV fluids, transfer to ICU. Do NOT obtain imaging until patient is stabilized.

Cerebral edema complicates 0.5–1% of pediatric DKA cases and is most common in younger children. Risk factors include aggressive fluid resuscitation. It is a life-threatening emergency. The clinical triad of headache, bradycardia, and altered LOC in a child being treated for DKA should immediately prompt treatment.

**Q:** What are the risk factors for Type 2 DM in children, and what is the first-line pharmacotherapy?
