**A:** Risk factors: first- or second-degree relative with T2DM, high-risk ethnic group, obesity, impaired glucose tolerance, PCOS, in utero diabetes exposure, acanthosis nigricans, hypertension, dyslipidemia, NAFLD, atypical antipsychotic medications. Breastfeeding is protective. First-line medication: metformin (can be initiated at diagnosis if HbA1c >7%). If targets not met, add liraglutide ± basal insulin (especially if HbA1c >8.5%).

T2DM in children is rare before age 10; mean age ~14 years. Rising prevalence is driven by childhood obesity. 10–20% of children with clinical T2DM diagnosis may actually have T1DM — check pancreatic autoantibodies. Initial therapy with insulin is used for severe decompensation (DKA or HbA1c >9%).

**Q:** When should screening for T2DM be initiated in asymptomatic children?

**A:** Screening at least every 3 years in overweight or obese asymptomatic children after puberty OR ≥10 years old if >1 risk factor is present. Preferred screening test: fasting plasma glucose. 2-hour OGTT has higher detection rate in severe obesity (BMI >99th percentile) and those with multiple risk factors.

Early detection of T2DM in youth is critical to prevent complications. HbA1c may underestimate T2DM in children with severe obesity. Pancreatic autoantibodies should be checked to exclude T1DM as 10–20% of apparent T2DM in children may be T1DM.

**Q:** Define short stature and list the 'ABCDEFG' differential diagnosis mnemonic.

**A:** Short stature: height <3rd percentile. ABCDEFG: A — Affected infant (IUGR/SGA), B — Bone dysplasias (rickets, collagen disorders, mucopolysaccharidoses, achondroplasia), C — Chromosomal (Turner, Down), D — Delayed growth (constitutional delay of growth and puberty, CDGP), E — Endocrine (hypopituitary, low GH, Cushing's, hypothyroidism), F — Familial (familial short stature), G — GI malabsorption (celiac, Crohn's).

Short stature is defined by height <3rd percentile. Pathological causes are associated with DECREASED growth velocity (height crossing major percentile lines downward), whereas non-pathological causes (familial short stature, CDGP) have NORMAL growth velocity. Decreased growth velocity is often more clinically worrisome than absolute height.

**Q:** How is constitutional delay of growth and puberty (CDGP) differentiated from familial short stature (FSS)?

**A:** Both have NORMAL growth velocity. CDGP is distinguished by DELAYED bone age on AP x-ray of left hand/wrist, indicating the child has more growth potential. FSS shows bone age appropriate for chronological age, with likely low mid-parental height. Mid-parental height calculation: male = (mother's + father's height + 12.5 cm)/2; female = (mother's + father's height − 12.5 cm)/2.

Bone age (left hand/wrist x-ray) is the key investigation to distinguish CDGP from FSS. Children with CDGP will eventually reach their genetic height potential but do so later. Risk factors for GH deficiency include prior head trauma, intracranial bleed/infection, head surgery or irradiation, breech delivery, and positive family history.

**Q:** What is the management approach for mild versus severe hypoglycemia in a child with T1DM?

**A:** Mild hypoglycemia: administer simple oral carbohydrate (e.g., sweetened fruit juice — '15-15 rule'). Severe hypoglycemia (seizure, coma, unable to take PO): administer IM glucagon OR IV dextrose. Symptoms of hypoglycemia: lethargy, unusual behaviour, tremor, pallor, tachycardia, diaphoresis, seizure, coma.

Children are more susceptible to hypoglycemia than adults. Severe hypoglycemia can lead to seizures, coma, and permanent neurological complications. The HbA1c target in children with T1DM is relaxed to <7.5% (vs <7% in adults) specifically to minimize hypoglycemia risk.

**Q:** What autoimmune complications should be screened for in children with T1DM?

**A:** Concurrent autoimmune diseases: thyroiditis (most common — screen with TSH) and celiac disease (screen with anti-tTG IgA). Also screen for: mental health issues (depression, eating disorders), hypertension, and dyslipidemia. Routine immunizations and annual influenza vaccine are recommended.

T1DM is an autoimmune condition associated with other autoimmune diseases due to shared genetic susceptibility (HLA associations). The prevalence of thyroiditis in T1DM children is ~15–30% and celiac disease ~5–10%, both higher than the general population.

**Q:** What is the most common cause of congenital hyperthyroidism (neonatal Graves' disease), and how is it managed?

**A:** Caused by transplacental transfer of TSH receptor antibody (TRAb) from a mother with Graves' disease. Management: methimazole + beta-adrenergic blocker (e.g., propranolol). Epidemiology: ~1 in 25,000 neonates, M=F.

Neonatal Graves' disease is transient as maternal antibodies clear, but prompt treatment is essential to prevent long-term CNS/cognitive complications. Key clinical features include low birthweight, IUGR, tachycardia, goitre, and hepatosplenomegaly.

**Q:** What is the incidence and epidemiology of congenital hypothyroidism (CH), and why is early treatment critical?

**A:** Incidence: 1 in 2,000–4,000 newborns; F:M = 2:1. It is one of the most common preventable causes of intellectual disability. Treatment (thyroxine) must normalize hormones within 2 weeks to avoid cognitive impairment. Excellent outcome if started within 1–2 months; permanent disability if delayed beyond 3–6 months.

Congenital hypothyroidism is screened universally through newborn screening (TSH most sensitive for primary CH). Most infants are asymptomatic at birth because maternal T4 crosses the placenta, making newborn screening essential for early detection.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of congenital hypothyroidism in the neonatal period?

**A:** Usually asymptomatic at birth (maternal T4 crosses placenta). Features develop over first few months: prolonged jaundice, feeding difficulty, lethargy, constipation, umbilical hernia, macroglossia, large fontanelles, puffy face, swollen eyes, hypotonia. Central CH also associated with hypoglycemia, micropenis, undescended testes, and features of diabetes insipidus.

The classic 'cretinism' features (macroglossia, umbilical hernia, hypotonia) develop gradually, reinforcing the need for universal newborn screening rather than relying on clinical detection. Central CH has additional pituitary/hypothalamic features.

**Q:** What are the primary and secondary causes of congenital hypothyroidism (CH)?

**A:** Primary CH (85%): thyroid dysgenesis (most common), inborn errors of thyroid hormone biosynthesis (15%). Secondary/central CH: pituitary or hypothalamic dysfunction. Peripheral CH: deficits in thyroid hormone transport, metabolism, or action. Transient CH causes: maternal antibody-mediated, iodine deficiency, antithyroid medications, congenital liver hemangiomas, gene mutations.

Primary CH is detected by elevated TSH on newborn screening. Secondary/central CH may have normal or low TSH with low free T4. Distinguishing the type guides further investigations (e.g., MRI and gene analysis for central CH).

**Q:** What is the most common cause of ambiguous genitalia in a genotypically normal (46,XX) female?

**A:** Congenital Adrenal Hyperplasia (CAH), specifically 21-hydroxylase deficiency, is the most common cause of ambiguous genitalia in 46,XX females (virilizing CAH).

In CAH, deficiency of 21-OH leads to decreased cortisol and aldosterone with compensatory ACTH elevation and androgen excess. In 46,XX females, this results in virilization/clitoromegaly and labioscrotal fusion at birth. The incidence of genital abnormalities is ~1 in 300 births; complex ambiguity ~1 in 5,000.

**Q:** What are the key physical exam findings in 46,XY vs. 46,XX disorders of sexual development (DSD)?

**A:** 46,XY DSD: small phallus, hypospadias, bilateral cryptorchidism (undescended testes). 46,XX DSD: clitoromegaly, labioscrotal fusion. Both: look for concurrent midline defects, dysmorphic features, and congenital abnormalities.

The physical examination must be systematic to avoid premature sex assignment. Management should avoid announcing probable sex until all investigations (karyotype, FISH for SRY gene, hormones, electrolytes, imaging) are complete. Psychosocial support is essential.

**Q:** What enzyme deficiency accounts for ~95% of Congenital Adrenal Hyperplasia (CAH) cases, and what are its biochemical consequences?

**A:** 21-hydroxylase (21-OH) deficiency accounts for ~95% of CAH. Results in: decreased cortisol AND aldosterone production, with compensatory ACTH elevation causing adrenal hyperplasia and shunting toward excess androgen production.

Rarer causes include deficiencies in 11-OH, cholesterol desmolase, 17-OH, and 3-HSD. The cortisol deficiency drives ACTH excess, which perpetuates adrenal hyperplasia and androgen overproduction—the core pathophysiology of classic CAH.

**Q:** What are the three clinical presentations of 21-OH deficiency CAH and their key features?

**A:** 1. Classic with salt-wasting (majority): inadequate aldosterone → FTT, hyperkalemia, hyponatremia, hypoglycemia, acidosis. 2. Classic without salt-wasting (simple virilization): adequate aldosterone; females have genital ambiguity, males present later with virilization. 3. Non-classic: mild androgen excess, sometimes asymptomatic, precocious puberty/virilization later; may mimic PCOS.

Diagnostic test: elevated serum 17-OH progesterone. Salt-wasting crisis can be life-threatening. Non-classic CAH is rarely detected by newborn screening. Management: glucocorticoids (hydrocortisone) + mineralocorticoids (fludrocortisone); extra glucocorticoids during stress.

**Q:** What is the diagnostic test for 21-OH deficiency CAH, and what other investigations should be ordered?

**A:** High serum 17-OH progesterone in a random blood sample is diagnostic. Additional investigations: plasma ACTH, serum electrolytes (Na+, K+), plasma glucose, plasma aldosterone, plasma renin activity, blood gas, and ultrasound (enlarged adrenal gland, presence of uterus).

21-OH deficiency screening is part of many newborn screening programs across North America. The non-classical variant is rarely detected by screening. Elevated 17-OH progesterone reflects the enzymatic block upstream of the deficient 21-hydroxylase.

**Q:** What did the TODAY trial demonstrate about glycemic management in youth with type 2 diabetes?

**A:** TODAY trial (NEJM 2012): RCT of 699 patients aged 10-17 yr with recent-onset T2DM. Assigned to: (1) metformin alone (51.7% failure rate), (2) metformin + rosiglitazone (38.6% failure rate), or (3) metformin + lifestyle modification focused on weight loss (46.6% failure rate). Loss of glycemic control = HbA1c >8% for ≥6 months. Conclusion: Metformin + rosiglitazone was significantly better than metformin alone; metformin + lifestyle intervention did NOT significantly improve control vs. metformin alone.

The TODAY trial highlighted the aggressive nature of T2DM in youth — high failure rates even with combination therapy. Youth with T2DM have more rapid beta-cell decline than adults. Rosiglitazone (a thiazolidinedione) combined with metformin showed the best glycemic control, though rosiglitazone has largely been replaced by newer agents due to cardiovascular concerns. The ELLIPSE trial showed liraglutide (GLP-1 agonist) + metformin is more effective than metformin alone in this population.

## Pediatrics Fluids Electrolytes

**Q:** How do you clinically assess the degree of dehydration in pediatric patients?

**A:** Based on % body weight loss and physical exam findings: Mild (<2yr: 5%; >2yr: 3%): normal/full pulse, normal BP, decreased urine output, slightly dry mucosa, normal fontanelle/eyes/skin turgor, normal capillary refill. Moderate (<2yr: 10%; >2yr: 6%): rapid pulse, low-normal BP, markedly decreased urine, dry mucosa, sunken fontanelle/eyes, decreased turgor, normal-increased refill. Severe (<2yr: 15%; >2yr: 9%): rapid/weak pulse, decreased BP (very late/dangerous), anuria, parched mucosa, markedly sunken fontanelle/eyes, tenting, increased refill (>3s).

BP decrease is a very late sign in pediatric dehydration and indicates impending shock—children compensate well via tachycardia and vasoconstriction until critically decompensated. Capillary refill time and skin turgor are more sensitive early markers.

**Q:** What are the indications for oral rehydration therapy (ORT) vs. IV rehydration in pediatric dehydration?

**A:** ORT: mild to moderate dehydration. Advantages: lower cost, no IV needed, no iatrogenic hyper/hyponatremia, parental involvement. IV rehydration indications: severe dehydration requiring close monitoring; inability to tolerate ORT (vomiting, altered mental status, ileus, monosaccharide malabsorption); inability to provide ORT; failure of ORT to achieve adequate rehydration.

ORT is the preferred first-line approach for mild-to-moderate dehydration per Canadian/international guidelines. Severe dehydration requires IV resuscitation with NS bolus (20 mL/kg). ORT can be initiated once the patient is stabilized from severe dehydration.

**Q:** What is the 100:50:20 rule for pediatric maintenance fluid requirements?

**A:** Daily maintenance fluids: First 10 kg: 100 mL/kg/day. Next 10 kg (11–20 kg): 1000 mL + 50 mL/kg/day for every kg >10 kg. >20 kg: 1500 mL + 20 mL/kg/day for every kg >20 kg. Hourly rate equivalent (4:2:1 rule): ≤10 kg: 4 mL/kg/h; 11–20 kg: 40 mL/h + 2 mL/kg/h per kg >10; >20 kg: 60 mL/h + 1 mL/kg/h per kg >20.

This is the Holliday-Segar method. All pediatric maintenance fluids should include a dextrose component due to higher risk of hypoglycemia (especially if NPO). Isotonic fluids (D5W/NS + KCl) are preferred in children to reduce risk of iatrogenic hyponatremia.

**Q:** What are the appropriate IV fluid formulations for pediatric maintenance therapy at different ages?

**A:** Newborn: D10W (dextrose 10% in water). First month of life: D5W/0.45 NS + KCl 20 mEq/L (only add KCl if voiding). Children without special considerations: D5W/NS + KCl 20 mEq/L (isotonic preferred to decrease risk of hyponatremia). For significant hypovolemia: IV normal saline bolus.

Hypotonic fluids in children can cause SIADH-related hyponatremia and acute cerebral edema. The shift toward isotonic maintenance fluids (D5W/NS) in children reflects evidence that hypotonic solutions carry increased risk of iatrogenic hyponatremia.

**Q:** What is SIADH in pediatrics and what are its risk factors and complications?

**A:** SIADH = Syndrome of Inappropriate ADH secretion. Clinical signs: hyponatremia + excretion of concentrated urine. Risk factors: certain medications (e.g., morphine), postoperative state, pain, mechanical ventilation, pulmonary disease (e.g., pneumonia), CNS disease (e.g., meningitis). Complication: acute hyponatremia is associated with rapid administration of hypotonic IV fluids → cerebral edema, brain herniation, or central pontine myelinolysis.

SIADH must be kept in mind when prescribing IV fluids. Sodium correction should not exceed 12 mmol/L/day to avoid central pontine myelinolysis (osmotic demyelination). Children are at particular risk due to their larger brain:skull ratio.

**Q:** What is the electrolyte content of gastric juice and its clinical significance in pediatric dehydration?

**A:** Gastric juice: Na+ 60–80 mmol/L, K+ ~1 mmol/L, Cl- ~100 mmol/L, HCO3- 0 mmol/L. Prolonged vomiting of gastric contents (e.g., pyloric stenosis) leads to loss of HCl → hypokalemic, hypochloremic metabolic alkalosis.

Knowing the electrolyte composition of various body fluids (gastric, pancreatic, bile, small/large bowel) is essential for predicting the metabolic consequence of fluid losses and guiding replacement therapy. Pancreatic juice is alkaline (HCO3- 40–100 mmol/L), so pancreatic fistulas cause metabolic acidosis.

## Pediatrics Gastroenterology

**Q:** What are the red flag symptoms of vomiting in pediatric patients?

**A:** Non-specific red flags: prolonged vomiting (>12h in neonates, >24h in children <2yr, >48h in children >2yr), profound lethargy, significant weight loss. GI obstruction/disease: bilious vomit, projectile vomit, hematemesis, hematochezia, marked abdominal distension, peritoneal signs. Neurological/systemic: bulging fontanelle, headache, positional triggers, altered consciousness, focal neurologic deficits, history/signs of trauma, hypotension disproportionate to illness, hyponatremia with hyperkalemia.

Vomiting without diarrhea is NOT always gastroenteritis—consider DKA, increased ICP, pyloric stenosis (non-bilious projectile), or inborn errors of metabolism. Bilious vomiting in a neonate is a surgical emergency until proven otherwise.

**Q:** What are the key distinguishing features and diagnostic approach for pyloric stenosis in a neonate?

**A:** Pyloric stenosis: projectile non-bilious emesis within 30 minutes after feeding; fatigue, dehydration; palpable 'olive' in RUQ; decreased stools; hungry infant after vomiting. Investigations: CBC, electrolytes, Cr, BUN, ABG (hypokalemic, hypochloremic metabolic alkalosis); ultrasound of pylorus (first-line); upper GI study if US nondiagnostic.

The classic metabolic picture is hypokalemic hypochloremic metabolic alkalosis due to loss of HCl from gastric juice. The 'olive' is felt on exam in ~70–90% of cases. Ultrasound is the diagnostic gold standard (pyloric muscle thickness ≥4mm, channel length ≥16mm).

**Q:** What is the classic presentation and diagnostic approach for intussusception in a child?

**A:** Classic triad: 1. Colicky progressive abdominal pain with drawing of legs up to chest; 2. Lethargy; 3. Bloody 'red currant jelly' stool. Investigations: abdominal ultrasound (diagnostic); AXR (rule out other etiologies and perforation).

Intussusception is most common between 6 months and 2 years of age. The red currant jelly stool is a late sign caused by mucosal ischemia. Air or hydrostatic enema via fluoroscopy or ultrasound guidance is both diagnostic and therapeutic if no peritonitis/perforation.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of neonatal GERD vs. GERD in older children/adolescents?

**A:** Infants with GERD: poor weight gain, irritability, sleep disturbance, respiratory symptoms (coughing, choking, wheezing). Older children/adolescents: abdominal pain/heartburn, dysphagia, asthma, recurrent pneumonia/upper respiratory infections (if aspirating), recurrent otitis media, upper airway symptoms (chronic cough, hoarseness), dental erosions, weight loss, nocturnal vomiting.

GER is extremely common in infancy (up to 50%) but is only GERD when it causes troublesome symptoms or complications. Respiratory symptoms in infants may be the only presentation of GERD. In adolescents, the presentation mimics adult GERD.

**Q:** What is the management of GERD in infants and children?

**A:** Conservative (infants): thickened feeds, smaller/more frequent feeds, head elevation, hydrolyzed protein or amino-acid based formula; breastfeeding mothers: sequential elimination diet (milk, beef, soy, egg). Older children: lifestyle changes + PPIs as in adults. Medical: PPI or H2-blocker when conservative measures fail, moderate-severe disease, or biopsy-proven esophagitis. NOT recommended: acid suppressants/motility agents for uncomplicated infant reflux; D2 antagonists (domperidone, metoclopramide) generally not recommended for GERD. Surgical: Nissen fundoplication; gastrojejunal tube for post-pyloric feeds (failure of medical therapy or multisystem comorbidity).

Acid suppressants and motility agents are not recommended for uncomplicated GER in infants due to lack of benefit and potential harm. Several recommendations must be used cautiously in preterm infants given higher risk of NEC.

**Q:** What is the definition of diarrhea in pediatrics, and what are the major pathophysiological mechanisms?

**A:** Definition: infants — increase in stool frequency to twice as often per day; older children — 3+ loose or watery stools/day. Acute: <2 weeks; chronic: >2 weeks. Pathophysiology: 1. Osmotic — non-absorbable solutes in GI tract (e.g., lactose intolerance); 2. Secretory — increased Cl- and water secretion into intestinal lumen (e.g., bacterial toxin); 3. Malabsorption — increased motility or fewer villi (e.g., short bowel syndrome).

Differentiating the mechanism helps guide management. Osmotic diarrhea stops with fasting; secretory diarrhea persists despite fasting. In the MCCQE1 context, recognizing chronic diarrhea red flags (poor growth, bloody stool, recurrent hospitalizations) is critical for identifying serious underlying pathology.

**Q:** What are the key differential diagnoses for chronic diarrhea in different pediatric age groups?

**A:** 0–3 months (no FTT): GI infection, toddler's diarrhea. With FTT: food protein-induced allergic proctocolitis (FPIAP), CF, Hirschsprung's disease. 3 months–3 years: GI infection, lactase deficiency, disaccharidase deficiency. 3–18 years: GI infection, celiac disease, IBD. Uncommon: short bowel syndrome, Shwachman-Diamond syndrome, HIV, autoimmune enteropathy, eosinophilic gastroenteritis, endocrine causes (thyrotoxicosis, Addison's), neoplastic (pheochromocytoma, lymphoma).

Celiac disease and IBD are high-yield causes of chronic diarrhea in older children. Celiac screen (anti-tTG IgA + total IgA) and IBD panel are important workup steps. CF should be considered in infants with FTT and malabsorption.

**Q:** What is the approach to investigation of acute versus chronic diarrhea in pediatric patients?

**A:** Acute diarrhea (well child, non-bloody): often no investigations needed. If indicated: stool C&S, O&P, electron microscopy for viruses, C. difficile toxin, stool multiplex PCR, blood/urine cultures, CBC, electrolytes, BUN, Cr, glucose, abdominal imaging. Chronic diarrhea (growing well): limited workup (stool cultures, stool reducing substances). Red flags (poor growth, bloody stool, vomiting, recurrent hospitalizations): full workup including endoscopy, small bowel biopsy, sweat chloride, celiac screen, thyroid function, urine VMA/HVA.

Microbiologic testing for gastroenteritis is indicated for immunocompromised hosts, bloody or mucusy stool, persistent diarrhea >7 days, international travel, or exposure to unsafe foods/farm animals/untreated water.

**Q:** Which antibiotics are most commonly associated with Clostridioides difficile (C. diff) infection in pediatrics?

**A:** Fluoroquinolones, clindamycin, broad-spectrum cephalosporins, and broad-spectrum penicillins (e.g., amoxicillin-clavulanate) are most commonly associated with C. difficile infection.

C. difficile should be considered when diarrhea occurs following recent antibiotic use. Diagnosis: C. difficile toxin A/B assay or stool multiplex PCR. Treatment: oral metronidazole (first episode, mild-moderate) or vancomycin (severe or recurrent cases).

**Q:** A 5-week-old male presents with projectile non-bilious vomiting after each feeding, remains hungry after vomiting, and has a palpable mass in the right upper quadrant. What metabolic abnormality would you expect on blood work?

**A:** Hypokalemic, hypochloremic metabolic alkalosis. This is the classic metabolic picture of pyloric stenosis due to repeated loss of gastric HCl.

The management of pyloric stenosis requires metabolic correction before surgical repair (pyloromyotomy). Operating on a patient with significant alkalosis increases anesthetic risk. IV fluid resuscitation with normal saline plus KCl correction is performed first.

## Pediatrics Oncology

**Q:** What is the most commonly diagnosed cancer in children aged 0-14 years in Canada?

**A:** Leukemia

Leukemia is the most common pediatric cancer, with ALL accounting for 80% of cases, AML 15%, CML <5%, and CLL <1%. Children with Down syndrome are 15× more likely to develop leukemia.

**Q:** What percentage of pediatric leukemia cases are Acute Lymphoblastic Leukemia (ALL)?

**A:** 80%

ALL is by far the most common pediatric leukemia. AML accounts for 15%, CML <5%, and CLL <1%. The mean age of diagnosis is 4 years but can occur at any age.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of leukemia resulting from bone marrow infiltration vs. tissue infiltration?

**A:** Bone marrow infiltration → bone pain, anemia, neutropenia, thrombocytopenia. Tissue infiltration → lymphadenopathy, hepatosplenomegaly, CNS manifestations, testicular disease.

Leukemic cell infiltration of bone marrow causes failure of normal hematopoiesis. Extramedullary infiltration leads to organomegaly and end-organ dysfunction. CNS symptoms include early morning vomiting, facial weakness, blurred vision, and seizures.

**Q:** What defines hyperleukocytosis and why is it a medical emergency?

**A:** Hyperleukocytosis is total WBC >100 × 10⁹/L. It is a medical emergency due to hyperviscosity causing leukostasis, leading to ICH, pulmonary leukostasis syndrome, and tumour lysis syndrome.

Management includes IV fluids, allopurinol/rasburicase, FFP/platelets, induction chemotherapy, and avoiding RBC transfusion unless symptomatic (and then only small volumes). Leukapheresis is used at some centres.

**Q:** What is the 5-year survival rate for ALL vs. AML in pediatric patients?

**A:** ALL: 80-90% 5-year event-free survival. AML: 65% 5-year survival.

ALL has excellent prognosis with combination chemotherapy. The most important prognostic variable for ALL is initial response to therapy. Other factors include WBC, age, CNS/testicular disease, immunophenotype, and cytogenetics.

**Q:** What is the most important prognostic variable in pediatric ALL?

**A:** Initial response to therapy

Patients are stratified into standard-risk and high-risk based on WBC and age. Other prognostic factors include presence of CNS/testicular disease, immunophenotype, cytogenetics, and initial response to therapy — which is the most important.

**Q:** What are the constitutional ('B') symptoms of Hodgkin lymphoma?

**A:** Fever, chills, night sweats, and unexplained weight loss ≥10% body weight over 6 months.

Constitutional B symptoms occur in ~30% of pediatric patients with Hodgkin lymphoma. They indicate systemic disease and influence staging and prognosis. Hodgkin lymphoma spreads contiguously.

**Q:** What is the most common presentation of Hodgkin lymphoma in children?

**A:** Persistent, painless, firm, rubbery cervical or supraclavicular lymphadenopathy.

Hodgkin lymphoma has a bimodal incidence peaking at 15-34 and >50 years. It may also present with persistent cough or dyspnea from a mediastinal mass, splenomegaly, or axillary/inguinal lymphadenopathy.

**Q:** How is Hodgkin lymphoma definitively diagnosed?

**A:** Histological examination of a biopsy specimen — excisional biopsy of enlarged lymph node (preferred), incisional biopsy (acceptable), or multiple core needle biopsies (acceptable).

PET scanning plays an increasing role in assessing early disease response and planning therapy. Investigations include CBC, electrolytes, LDH, ESR, CXR, CT neck/chest/abdomen/pelvis, BM aspirate, and LP.

**Q:** What is the 5-year survival rate for pediatric Hodgkin lymphoma?

**A:** >90%

Management includes combination chemotherapy and radiation. Recurrent or refractory disease is treated with chemotherapy, targeted therapy, checkpoint inhibitors, hematopoietic stem cell transplant, and involved-site radiation.

**Q:** How does pediatric Non-Hodgkin Lymphoma (NHL) differ from adult NHL?

**A:** Pediatric NHL is rapidly growing with distant metastases at presentation, unlike adult NHL. Peak incidence is 7-11 years. Includes Burkitt, diffuse large B-cell, T-cell lymphoblastic, and anaplastic large cell subtypes.

Pediatric NHL most commonly involves the abdomen (intussusception), chest (mediastinal mass), and head and neck. Radiation has no added benefit in pediatric protocols. 5-year survival is 75-90%.

**Q:** Why is a patient with a large mediastinal mass at risk during procedures, and how should they be positioned?

**A:** A mediastinal mass can compress the trachea, SVC, or ventricular outflow when supine, causing cardiac or respiratory arrest. Procedures should be performed with the patient on their side or prone.

This is a critical complication of mediastinal NHL (and Hodgkin lymphoma). The compression worsens in the supine position, so any procedure requiring sedation or anesthesia carries significant risk and positioning is life-saving.

**Q:** What metabolic abnormalities characterize tumour lysis syndrome?

**A:** Hyperuricemia, hyperkalemia, and hyperphosphatemia (from rapid breakdown of malignant cells).

Tumour lysis syndrome can occur spontaneously or after initiation of chemotherapy. Management includes aggressive IV hydration, allopurinol or rasburicase (for uric acid), and monitoring electrolytes.

**Q:** What is the most common primary renal neoplasm of childhood?

**A:** Wilms' tumour (Nephroblastoma)

Wilms' tumour is usually diagnosed between 2-5 years of age. In 5-10% of cases, both kidneys are affected. It is the most common primary renal neoplasm in children.

**Q:** What congenital syndrome is associated with ~50% risk of Wilms' tumour and involves an 11p13 deletion?

**A:** WAGR syndrome (Wilms' tumour, Aniridia, Genitourinary anomalies, Range of developmental delays)

WAGR syndrome is associated with deletion of chromosome 11p13 and carries approximately 50% risk of Wilms' tumour. Denys-Drash syndrome has the highest risk (~90%).

**Q:** What are the features of Beckwith-Wiedemann syndrome and its associated cancer risks?

**A:** Enlargement of body organs (especially macroglossia), hemihypertrophy, renal medullary cysts, adrenal cytomegaly, omphalocele, ear creases, macrosomia. Associated with 5-10% Wilms' tumour risk, hepatoblastoma, adrenocortical tumours, neuroblastomas, and rhabdomyosarcomas.

Beckwith-Wiedemann syndrome is an overgrowth syndrome due to abnormalities in imprinted genes on chromosome 11p15. Regular surveillance with abdominal ultrasound is recommended.

**Q:** What is the classic presentation of Wilms' tumour?

**A:** 80% present with an asymptomatic, unilateral abdominal mass. May also present with HTN, gross hematuria, abdominal pain, vomiting, fever, UTI, and anemia.

Pulmonary metastases may be present at diagnosis though children rarely present with respiratory symptoms. Imaging starts with abdominal ultrasound, followed by contrast-enhanced CT or MRI chest/abdomen/pelvis.

**Q:** What is the 5-year survival rate for Wilms' tumour?

**A:** >95%

Excellent prognosis is achieved with staging + nephrectomy and chemotherapy (± radiation for advanced disease). The COG approach recommends surgery first; SIOP recommends neoadjuvant chemotherapy first.

**Q:** What is the most common cancer in the first year of life?

**A:** Neuroblastoma (average age at diagnosis is 2.5 years)

Neuroblastoma arises from neural crest cells (sympathetic neuroblasts) and can originate from any site in the sympathetic nervous system. The most common site is the adrenal gland.

**Q:** What are the paraneoplastic features of neuroblastoma?

**A:** HTN, palpitations, sweating (from excessive catecholamines); diarrhea, FTT (from VIP secretion); opsomyoclonus (dancing eyes-dancing feet syndrome).

Neuroblastoma secretes catecholamines (measured as urinary HVA and VMA) and can produce VIP. Opsomyoclonus myoclonus is a paraneoplastic syndrome strongly associated with neuroblastoma.

**Q:** What investigations are used to diagnose neuroblastoma?

**A:** CBC, electrolytes, urinalysis, serum/urine catecholamine metabolites (HVA and VMA), Cr; imaging: MRI or CT, MIBG scan; biopsy for definitive diagnosis.

Definitive diagnosis requires either unequivocal histology (small round blue cells, pseudorosettes) OR bone marrow metastases with elevated urinary/serum catecholamines. MIBG scan is specific for neuroblastoma.

**Q:** What are good prognostic factors for neuroblastoma?

**A:** Age <18 months, stage I/II/IV-S disease, primary site in posterior mediastinum or neck, more differentiated histology, aneuploidy, absent MYCN oncogene amplification.

Prognosis is often poor due to late detection (>50% present with advanced disease). IV-S is a special classification for infants with widespread disease involving liver, skin, or bone marrow but without cortical bone involvement — these can spontaneously regress.

**Q:** What histological finding on neuroblastoma biopsy confirms the diagnosis?

**A:** Small round blue cells with pseudorosettes (Homer Wright rosettes).

Alternatively, the diagnosis can be made if bone marrow aspirate shows metastases along with elevated urinary or serum catecholamines (HVA/VMA). MIBG scan shows uptake in neuroblastoma tissue.

**Q:** What are the three major cancer predisposition syndromes with pediatric onset mentioned in Toronto Notes?

**A:** Li-Fraumeni syndrome (soft tissue sarcomas, osteosarcoma, CNS tumours, adrenocortical carcinoma), hereditary retinoblastoma, and Fanconi anemia (leukemias and oral cancers).

Cancer predisposition syndromes should be suspected in cases of multiple primary neoplasms, early onset cancers, or family history consistent with a known syndrome. Early recognition allows surveillance to limit required therapies.

## Pediatrics Infectious Disease

**Q:** How is fever defined in pediatric practice?

**A:** A practical definition is >38°C (100.4°F) oral or rectal.

Fever without a source is an acute febrile illness <10 days with no identifiable cause. Fever of unknown origin (FUO) is daily or intermittent fevers for ≥2 consecutive weeks of uncertain cause after history, physical, and initial labs.

**Q:** According to the Rochester Criteria, which age group of febrile neonates should always be considered high-risk for serious bacterial infection (SBI)?

**A:** Neonates aged 1-28 days — febrile neonates in this group should be considered high-risk regardless of clinical features and laboratory findings.

The Rochester Criteria are less sensitive in the 1-28 day age group. Therefore, all febrile neonates in this age range require full septic workup and empiric antibiotics. The younger the child, the greater the difficulty in clinically assessing degree of illness.

**Q:** What are the three criteria required to diagnose Acute Otitis Media (AOM)?

**A:** 1. Middle ear effusion; 2. Middle ear inflammation; 3. Acute onset of symptoms of middle ear effusion and inflammation.

Accurate diagnosis of AOM is very important to prevent antibiotic overuse. The most important diagnostic sign is a bulging tympanic membrane. Peak incidence is at 6-24 months.

**Q:** What is the most common bacterial cause of AOM and what has reduced its incidence?

**A:** Streptococcus pneumoniae — its incidence has been decreasing since the introduction of PCV7 and PCV13 vaccines. Other common causes: H. influenzae, M. catarrhalis, and GAS.

AOM commonly develops within a week after a viral URI. Less common bacterial causes include anaerobes (in newborns) and gram-negative enterics (in infants). Viral causes (RSV, coronaviruses, influenza) are more likely to spontaneously resolve.

**Q:** What is the first-line antibiotic treatment for AOM in children, and what is the dose?

**A:** High-dose amoxicillin 75-90 mg/kg/day dosed BID × 5 days (10 days if age <2 years, perforated TM, or recurrent AOM). If penicillin allergic: cefuroxime-axetil or ceftriaxone.

Antibiotic therapy is indicated if age <6 months or moderate-severe illness. Second-line options (if unresponsive after 48h): amoxicillin-clavulanic acid, cefuroxime axetil, ceftriaxone, cefaclor, or cefixime.

**Q:** When is watchful waiting (observation without antibiotics) appropriate in AOM management?

**A:** Watchful waiting for 24-48 hours is appropriate for children ≥6 months who are mildly ill (alert, responding to antipyretics, mild otalgia, fever <39°C without antipyretics, <48 hours of symptoms) with reliable follow-up.

After discussing with caregivers, observation with analgesia is appropriate for mild illness. If not clinically improved or worsening, treat with antimicrobials (10 days if 6 months to 2 years; 5 days if ≥2 years).

**Q:** What are the intracranial complications of AOM?

**A:** Meningitis, epidural and brain abscess, subdural empyema, lateral and cavernous sinus thrombosis, carotid artery thrombosis.

Extracranial complications include hearing loss/speech delay, mastoiditis, petrositis, labyrinthitis, cholesteatoma, facial nerve palsy, middle ear atelectasis, ossicular necrosis, and vestibular dysfunction.

**Q:** What are the otoscopic findings of Otitis Media with Effusion?

**A:** Discolouration (amber or dull grey), meniscus fluid level behind TM, air bubbles, retraction pockets/TM atelectasis, flat tympanogram, immobility on pneumatic otoscopy (most reliable finding).

OME presents with fluctuating conductive hearing loss, tinnitus, and ear fullness. Unlike AOM, there is no bulging TM and no acute inflammation. The most reliable diagnostic finding is TM immobility with pneumatic otoscopy.

**Q:** What is Erythema Infectiosum (Fifth Disease) and what is its characteristic rash?

**A:** Caused by Parvovirus B19. Rash appears 10-17 days after initial symptoms (immune response phase). Classic 'slapped cheek' appearance — bilateral erythematous maculopapular lacy rash on cheeks with circumoral sparing; may affect trunk and extremities.

Initial 7-10 days of flu-like illness and fever precede the rash. The rash may reappear months later with sunlight or exercise. Serious complication: transient aplastic crisis (especially in chronic hemolytic anemia). Management is supportive.

**Q:** What is the causative organism of Roseola and what is the classic presentation sequence?

**A:** Human herpesvirus 6 (HHV-6). Classic sequence: high-grade fever 3-5 days → fever breaks → blanching pink maculopapular rash appears (starts at neck and trunk, spreads to face and extremities).

Common associated features: irritability, anorexia, lymphadenopathy, erythematous TM and pharynx, Nagayama spots (erythematous papules on soft palate and uvula). Major complication: febrile seizures in 10-25% of cases.

**Q:** What are the 'Three C's' prodrome of measles, and what is the enanthem?

**A:** Prodrome: Cough, Coryza, Conjunctivitis (Three C's). Enanthem: Koplik's spots (appear 1-2 days before the rash).

Measles rash is erythematous maculopapular, begins at the hairline and spreads downwards, sparing palms and soles. Caused by Morbillivirus. Management: supportive (vitamin A evidence). Complications include secondary bacterial infections (AOM, sinusitis, pneumonia), encephalitis, and rarely subacute sclerosing panencephalitis.

**Q:** What is the distinguishing feature of Hand, Foot, and Mouth Disease (HFMD) rash and enanthem location?

**A:** Rash: vesicles and pustules on erythematous base on mouth, buttocks, acral areas. Enanthem: vesicles in the POSTERIOR oral cavity (pharynx, tongue). Caused by Coxsackie group A virus.

Contrast with Herpes simplex, which causes vesicles/erosions in the ANTERIOR oral cavity (buccal mucosa, tongue). HFMD is communicable for 17 days after symptoms and resolves within 1 week. Main complication is dehydration.

**Q:** How does the rash distribution of measles differ from rubella?

**A:** Measles: starts at hairline and spreads downwards, spares palms and soles. Rubella: starts on face and spreads to neck and trunk (not full body). Both are maculopapular.

Rubella prodrome includes low-grade fever and occipital/retroauricular lymphadenopathy (STAR complex: sore throat, arthritis, rash). Rubella has major implications for pregnant contacts due to congenital rubella syndrome. Prevention: MMR vaccine.

**Q:** What is the contagious period for varicella (chickenpox)?

**A:** 1-2 days pre-eruption and 5 days post-eruption. Transmitted by direct contact, inhalation of lesion aerosols, and aerosolized respiratory secretions.

Varicella rash is polymorphic: starts as macules → papules → vesicles → crusts (all stages present simultaneously). Management: supportive, AVOID salicylates (Reye syndrome risk), consider antivirals. Complications include bacterial superinfection, encephalitis, cerebellar ataxia, and hepatitis.

**Q:** What is the classic triad of Infectious Mononucleosis (EBV)?

**A:** Fever, generalized non-tender lymphadenopathy, and pharyngitis/tonsillitis (exudative).

EBV ('kissing disease') is transmitted mainly through infected saliva with incubation period of 1-2 months. Associated findings include hepatosplenomegaly, periorbital edema, and rash (especially after inappropriate β-lactam antibiotics). Peak incidence is 15-19 years.

**Q:** What is the sensitivity of the Monospot (heterophile antibody) test for EBV mononucleosis by age group?

**A:** 85% sensitive in adults and older children; only 50% sensitive if age <4 years.

False positives occur with HIV, SLE, lymphoma, rubella, and parvovirus. If Monospot is negative but clinical suspicion remains high, obtain EBV-specific titres (VCA IgM, EA, EBNA). CBC shows reactive lymphocytes, lymphocytosis, and Downey cells.

**Q:** What activity restriction is required in all patients with infectious mononucleosis, and for how long?

**A:** All patients must avoid contact sports for 6-8 weeks due to risk of splenic rupture (splenic enlargement is often not clinically apparent).

Splenic rupture is a serious short-term complication of EBV mononucleosis. Management is otherwise supportive: rest, hydration, saline gargles, and analgesics. If airway obstruction is present, admit and consider steroids.

**Q:** What is the main cause of pharyngitis in children, and what percentage is bacterial?

**A:** Viral causes account for ~80% (adenoviruses, enteroviruses, EBV, CMV, COVID-19). Bacterial causes account for ~20%, mainly Group A Streptococcus (GAS).

GAS pharyngitis peaks at 5-12 years and is uncommon under 3 years. It is more common in late winter/early spring. Viral pharyngitis affects all ages year-round.

**Q:** What clinical features distinguish GAS pharyngitis from viral pharyngitis?

**A:** GAS: severe sore throat, fever, malaise, headache, abdominal pain, N/V, absence of other URTI symptoms, pharyngeal/tonsillar exudates, enlarged tender anterior cervical nodes, palatal petechiae, strawberry tongue, scarlatiniform rash. Viral: mild sore throat, conjunctivitis, cough, rhinorrhea, hoarseness, diarrhea.

The modified Centor (m-CENTOR) score is used to predict likelihood of positive throat culture but is not sensitive or specific enough alone for diagnosis in children. A rapid antigen test followed by throat culture if negative is recommended.

**Q:** What antibiotic treatment is used for GAS pharyngitis and within what timeframe does it prevent rheumatic fever?

**A:** Penicillin V or amoxicillin (or erythromycin if penicillin allergic) × 10 days. Antibiotics can prevent rheumatic fever if given within 9 days of symptom onset. They do NOT alter risk of post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis.

No follow-up throat cultures are needed after uncomplicated GAS pharyngitis. Tonsillectomy may be considered for severe, recurrent streptococcal tonsillitis.

**Q:** What are the immune-mediated complications of GAS pharyngitis?

**A:** Scarlet fever, acute rheumatic fever, post-streptococcal glomerulonephritis (GN), reactive arthritis, and PANDAS (Pediatric Autoimmune Neuropsychiatric Disorders Associated with GAS).

Non-immune complications (preventable with antibiotics): AOM, sinusitis, cervical adenitis, mastoiditis, retropharyngeal/peritonsillar abscess, and sepsis. Post-streptococcal GN risk is not altered by antibiotic treatment.

**Q:** Describe the rash sequence and distribution of Scarlet Fever.

**A:** Rash begins in groin, axillae, neck, antecubital fossa (24-48h after pharyngitis) → within 24h, generalized sandpaper-like rash with perioral sparing and Pastia's lines in flexural areas → non-pruritic, non-painful, blanchable → fades after 3-4 days, followed by desquamation.

Scarlet fever is a delayed-type hypersensitivity reaction to pyrogenic exotoxin of GAS. It requires prior exposure to S. pyogenes. Features include acute fever, sore throat, and strawberry tongue. Treatment: penicillin, amoxicillin, or erythromycin × 10 days.

**Q:** What is Gianotti-Crosti Syndrome and which pathogens cause it?

**A:** A symmetric papular rash on the face, cheeks, and extensor surfaces of extremities, sparing the trunk. Caused by EBV and Hepatitis B virus (majority). Management is supportive; resolves in 3-12 weeks.

Also called Papular Acrodermatitis. It may be asymptomatic or pruritic. Associated features include viral prodrome, lymphadenopathy, and/or hepatosplenomegaly. Pain control if needed.

**Q:** Why must salicylates be avoided in children with varicella?

**A:** Risk of Reye syndrome — a rare but serious condition of acute liver failure and encephalopathy associated with salicylate use during viral illnesses (particularly varicella and influenza).

Reye syndrome presents with sudden onset vomiting, confusion, and liver dysfunction. It carries significant morbidity and mortality. Acetaminophen and ibuprofen are safe alternatives for fever and pain management in varicella.

**Q:** What distinguishes the rash timing in Roseola from other febrile exanthems?

**A:** In Roseola, the rash appears AFTER the fever subsides (defervescence). This is opposite to most other exanthems where rash appears during the febrile phase.

The pattern: high-grade fever for 3-5 days → sudden defervescence → blanching pink maculopapular rash appears starting at the neck and trunk. Febrile seizures can occur in 10-25% during the febrile phase. Caused by HHV-6.

**Q:** What are the risk factors for recurrent AOM and which populations in Canada are at particularly high genetic risk?

**A:** Risk factors include Eustachian tube dysfunction, cleft palate, genetic syndromes (DS, Crouzon, Apert), Kartagener's syndrome, CF, pacifier use, no breastfeeding, second-hand smoke, daycare attendance, crowded living conditions. Canadians at genetic risk: First Nations peoples and Inuit.

Persistent biofilm in the middle ear and low secretory IgA levels are additional risk factors. Prevention includes pneumococcal and influenza vaccines, addressing modifiable risk factors, and tympanostomy tubes for recurrent AOM.

**Q:** What is the pathogenesis of Acute Otitis Media?

**A:** Obstruction of Eustachian tube → air absorbed in middle ear → negative pressure → edema of mucosa with exudate/effusion of nasopharyngeal secretions → bacterial infection.

The negative pressure in the middle ear is an irritant to the mucosa, leading to mucosal edema. This creates conditions for colonization by nasopharyngeal bacteria (S. pneumoniae, H. influenzae, M. catarrhalis).

**Q:** What is the treatment for a perforated tympanic membrane in AOM?

**A:** Always treat with antimicrobial therapy — most commonly topical combined ciprofloxacin-dexamethasone (Ciprodex) — and examine for complications.

A perforated TM with purulent drainage indicates bacterial AOM and should always be treated with antimicrobials for 10 days. Topical Ciprodex is the preferred therapy for perforated TM with otorrhea.

**Q:** What is the management of OME (Otitis Media with Effusion) and what treatments have NO role?

**A:** Expectant management for low-risk children. ENT referral for unilateral OME >6 months or bilateral OME >3 months with hearing loss. Surgery: myringotomy + tympanostomy tubes ± adenoidectomy. NO role for: antibiotics, glucocorticoids, antihistamines, or decongestants.

90% of OME resolves by 3 months. Early ENT referral for craniofacial abnormalities or immunodeficiency. Complications if untreated: hearing loss, speech delay, learning problems, chronic mastoiditis, ossicular erosion, cholesteatoma.

## Pediatrics Rheumatology

**Q:** What are the diagnostic criteria for Growing Pains in children?

**A:** Growing pains is a diagnosis of exclusion in children aged 2-12 yr (M=F). Criteria: intermittent, non-articular pain with normal physical exam; pain at night, often bilateral, limited to calf, shin, or thigh; short-lived, relieved by heat, massage, or mild analgesics; child is well and asymptomatic during the day with no functional limitations. Lab investigations are NOT necessary if presentation is typical.

Growing pains affect 2-12 year olds and represent a benign functional pain syndrome. The key distinguishing features from pathological causes are the normal daytime exam, bilateral nocturnal location (calf/shin/thigh), and absence of joint swelling, fever, or functional limitation. No workup is needed for typical presentations — reassurance and supportive care suffice.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic criteria for Juvenile Idiopathic Arthritis (JIA)?

**A:** JIA requires ALL of: (1) arthritis in ≥1 joint(s), (2) duration >6 weeks, (3) onset age <16 years, and (4) exclusion of other causes of arthritis. Classification is defined by features and number of joints affected in the first 6 months of onset.

JIA is a heterogeneous group of conditions. The 6-week duration threshold distinguishes it from transient reactive arthritis. Classification into subtypes (systemic, oligoarticular, polyarticular RF-/RF+, enthesitis-related, psoriatic) guides prognosis and management, particularly the risk of uveitis and systemic complications.

**Q:** A 5-year-old has daily fever spikes >38.5°C with rapid return to baseline, a salmon-coloured maculopapular rash, lymphadenopathy, and hepatosplenomegaly. What is the diagnosis and key lab findings?

**A:** Systemic JIA (Still's Disease). Key lab findings: high ESR, CRP, WBC (leukocytosis), and platelet count (thrombocytosis). Arthritis may appear weeks to months later. Occurs at any age, M=F. Extra-articular features include erythematous salmon-coloured rash, lymphadenopathy, hepatosplenomegaly, serositis (pericarditis).

Systemic JIA (Still's disease) is characterized by quotidian (once or twice daily) fever spikes with temperature returning rapidly to baseline — children appear acutely unwell during fever episodes. The salmon-coloured maculopapular rash is classic and transient, often appearing with fever. Serositis (especially pericarditis) is a serious complication. High inflammatory markers (WBC, platelets, ESR, CRP) are typical.

**Q:** Which subtype of JIA is most common, and what are the key features including ocular risk?

**A:** Oligoarticular JIA (1-4 joints) is the most common type. Key features: onset early childhood (<5 yr), F>M; typically affects large joints (knees most common, ankles, elbows, wrists — hip involvement unusual); ANA positive ~60-80%, RF negative. Screening eye exams essential for asymptomatic anterior uveitis (occurs in ~30%). Complications: knee flexion contracture, leg-length discrepancy, growth disturbances.

Oligoarticular JIA is the most common JIA subtype. Asymptomatic anterior uveitis occurs in approximately 30% of patients and can lead to blindness if undetected — hence the requirement for regular slit-lamp eye exams regardless of ocular symptoms. ANA positivity (~60-80%) increases uveitis risk. The hip is characteristically spared, unlike septic arthritis.

**Q:** Compare RF-positive vs RF-negative Polyarticular JIA.

**A:** RF-negative polyarticular JIA (more common): onset bimodal at 2-4 yr and 6-12 yr, F>M; symmetrical large and small joints of hands/feet, TMJ, cervical spine; ANA positive in 50%, uveitis in 10%. RF-positive polyarticular JIA: onset late childhood/early adolescence, F>M; similar to aggressive adult rheumatoid arthritis, progresses into adulthood in most cases; severe rapidly destructive symmetrical arthritis of large and small joints; may have rheumatoid nodules at pressure points.

RF-positive polyarticular JIA is functionally the pediatric equivalent of adult RA and carries the worst joint prognosis among JIA subtypes, with rapid destructive disease and frequent adult continuation. RF-negative disease is more common and typically milder. Both types require DMARDs when NSAIDs fail; RF-positive disease often needs biologic therapy (TNF antagonists).

**Q:** What are the features of Enthesitis-Related Arthritis (ERA) in JIA, and what adult disease can it progress to?

**A:** ERA: onset late childhood/adolescence, M>F. RF-negative arthritis and/or enthesitis (inflammation at tendon/ligament insertion to bone). Affects weight-bearing joints especially hip and intertarsal joints, or sacroiliitis. Associated with HLA-B27 and development of symptomatic uveitis/iritis (as opposed to asymptomatic uveitis in oligoarticular JIA). Risk of developing ankylosing spondylitis in adulthood. Asymmetric lower limb involvement.

ERA is the JIA subtype most closely related to the spondyloarthropathy spectrum. HLA-B27 positivity and axial involvement (sacroiliitis) distinguish it from other JIA subtypes. Unlike the asymptomatic uveitis of oligoarticular JIA, ERA uveitis is symptomatic (acute anterior uveitis/iritis with pain, redness, photophobia). Ankylosing spondylitis in adulthood is the major long-term risk.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic features of Psoriatic JIA?

**A:** Psoriatic JIA: onset bimodal at 2-4 yr and 9-11 yr, F>M. Diagnosis requires arthritis AND psoriasis OR arthritis plus at least TWO of: dactylitis, nail pitting or other nail abnormalities, or family history of psoriasis in a 1st-degree relative. Joint involvement: asymmetric or symmetric small or large joints (usually knees and small joints of hands/feet). Skin: erythematous scaly lesions on scalp, post-auricular area, peri-umbilicus, or over extensor surfaces.

Psoriatic JIA mirrors adult psoriatic arthritis in its key features. Dactylitis ('sausage digit') and nail changes (pitting, onycholysis) can precede skin disease. The requirement for only two of three minor criteria (dactylitis, nail changes, family history) allows diagnosis even without obvious psoriatic skin disease — important clinically as skin disease may lag joint disease in children.

**Q:** Outline the management approach for JIA.

**A:** Goals: eliminate inflammation, prevent joint damage, promote normal growth and development, minimize medication toxicity. Multidisciplinary: OT/PT, social work, orthopedics, ophthalmology, rheumatology. Exercise: moderate-intensity aerobic, flexibility, and strengthening to maintain ROM and muscle strength. 1st line: NSAIDs, intra-articular corticosteroids. 2nd line: DMARDs (methotrexate, sulfasalazine, leflunomide), systemic corticosteroids (acute severe disease), biologic agents (IL-1/IL-6 inhibition for systemic JIA; TNF antagonists for polyarticular JIA).

JIA management is stepped. NSAIDs and intra-articular steroid injections are first-line for oligoarticular disease. Methotrexate is the most commonly used DMARD. Biologics are used when DMARDs fail: IL-1 inhibitors (anakinra, canakinumab) and IL-6 inhibitors (tocilizumab) for systemic JIA; TNF inhibitors (etanercept, adalimumab) for polyarticular JIA. Ophthalmologic surveillance for uveitis must continue throughout.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of Henoch-Schönlein Purpura (HSP) and what investigations are required?

**A:** HSP is the most common childhood vasculitis (small vessel). Peak 4-10 yr, M:F=2:1. Often preceded by URTI 1-3 weeks before onset. Clinical triad: (1) palpable purpura (non-thrombocytopenic, lower extremities and buttocks), (2) abdominal pain (GI bleeding, intussusception), (3) arthritis/arthralgia (large joints with painful edema). Renal: microscopic hematuria, IgA nephropathy, proteinuria, HTN, renal failure in <5%. Investigations: urinalysis (blood, protein:creatinine ratio), serum urea/electrolytes/creatinine/albumin, elevated IgA; skin/renal biopsy shows IgA deposition; ultrasound for intussusception.

HSP (IgA vasculitis) is diagnosed clinically — palpable purpura is required for diagnosis. The key investigations are renal function tests and urinalysis because nephritis can develop late (up to 6 months after presentation). Monthly urinalysis and BP monitoring for 6 months is recommended. Renal biopsy is reserved for progressive renal disease. Skin biopsy (IgA deposition) only for diagnostic uncertainty.

**Q:** What is the management and prognosis of HSP (Henoch-Schönlein Purpura)?

**A:** Management: mainly supportive. Anti-inflammatory medications for joint pain. Corticosteroids for severe abdominal pain. Monitor for proteinuria on urinalysis and hypertension every month for 6 months to detect renal disease (immunosuppressive therapy if severe renal involvement). Prognosis: self-limited, resolves within 4 weeks. Recurrence in about one-third of patients. Long-term prognosis depends on severity of nephritis.

HSP is generally self-limiting but the main concern is renal involvement (IgA nephropathy), which can develop weeks to months after the initial presentation and may progress to chronic kidney disease. Hence the requirement for 6 months of renal monitoring. Corticosteroids do NOT prevent renal involvement but are used for severe abdominal pain or severe joint disease. Recurrence occurs in ~33%.

**Q:** A child aged 7 presents with palpable purpura over the buttocks, colicky abdominal pain, and swollen knee. Which diagnosis must also be urgently considered as a complication, and how is it detected?

**A:** HSP (Henoch-Schönlein Purpura) with the classic triad. Intussusception is a serious GI complication that must be urgently excluded. It is detected by abdominal ultrasound (bowel-within-bowel sign). Additionally, testicular torsion/swelling can occur and should be evaluated with scrotal ultrasound if testicular pain/swelling is present.

HSP causes IgA-mediated vasculitis of the GI tract, leading to submucosal edema and hemorrhage. This can act as a lead point for intussusception — an emergency requiring air/hydrostatic enema reduction or surgery. GI bleeding can also occur. Renal involvement (IgA nephropathy) is the most important long-term complication, requiring 6 months of urinalysis and BP monitoring post-diagnosis.

**Q:** What are the key differences between childhood-onset SLE and adult-onset SLE?

**A:** Childhood-onset SLE vs. adult-onset SLE: Children have MORE ACTIVE disease, are more likely to have RENAL DISEASE, receive more INTENSIVE drug therapy, and have a POORER PROGNOSIS compared to adults. SLE: autoimmune illness affecting multiple organ systems; incidence 1 in 1000; more commonly occurs >10 yr in children; F:M = 10:1.

Childhood-onset SLE (before age 18) tends to be more severe than adult SLE, with higher rates of lupus nephritis, neuropsychiatric lupus, and haematologic involvement. The female predominance (10:1) is similar to adults. Children require more aggressive treatment and have a higher disease burden over their lifetime. Monitoring for organ damage, particularly renal, is essential.

**Q:** What organisms cause Reactive Arthritis in children, and what syndromes can it progress to?

**A:** Reactive arthritis (typically the knee) follows bacterial infection, especially Salmonella, Shigella, Yersinia, Campylobacter, Chlamydia, and most commonly Streptococcus (post-streptococcal reactive arthritis). Typically resolves spontaneously. May progress to chronic illness or Reiter's syndrome (classic triad: urethritis, conjunctivitis, arthritis — 'can't pee, can't see, can't climb a tree').

Reactive arthritis is a sterile synovitis triggered by an infection at a distant site. The arthritis appears 1-4 weeks after the triggering infection, is typically asymmetric, and affects large joints. Post-streptococcal reactive arthritis is distinct from rheumatic fever (no carditis). Reiter's syndrome (now called reactive arthritis triad) is the classic presentation in adults and adolescents with urethritis + conjunctivitis + arthritis.

**Q:** What investigations are indicated in the workup of limb pain in a child, and which are 'basic' vs. 'as indicated'?

**A:** Basic investigations: CBC and differential, blood smear, ESR, CRP, X-ray. As indicated (based on clinical suspicion): Blood tests — ANA, RF, blood culture, viral/bacterial serology, CK, PTT, sickle cell screen, immunoglobulins, complement; Urinalysis; Synovial fluid (cell count, Gram stain, culture); TB skin test; Additional imaging (MRI, bone scan); Bone marrow aspiration; Slit-lamp exam (for uveitis screening in JIA).

The basic workup for limb pain is targeted at the most common serious diagnoses: infection (CBC, blood culture), inflammation (ESR, CRP, ANA, RF), malignancy (CBC with differential, blood smear for blasts), and structural causes (X-ray). Additional tests are ordered based on clinical findings. Bone marrow aspiration is reserved for suspected leukemia. Slit-lamp exam is essential for uveitis surveillance in ANA-positive oligoarticular JIA.

## Pediatrics Musculoskeletal

**Q:** List the four Kocher criteria used to differentiate septic arthritis from transient synovitis of the hip.

**A:** Kocher criteria — septic arthritis is more likely than transient synovitis when MORE of these 4 criteria are met: (1) Non-weight bearing, (2) Temperature >38.5°C, (3) ESR >40 mm/hr, (4) WBC >12,000 cells/mm³. Probability of septic arthritis increases with each additional criterion met.

The Kocher criteria are the key clinical decision tool for differentiating septic arthritis from transient synovitis in children with hip pain. Septic arthritis is a medical emergency requiring prompt surgical drainage and IV antibiotics, so accurate differentiation is critical. CRP >2 mg/dL has been added in some updated criteria. If ≥3 criteria are met, septic arthritis probability is very high and surgical aspiration is indicated.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of Transient Synovitis of the Hip, and how does it differ from septic arthritis?

**A:** Transient synovitis: benign, self-limited inflammatory hip disorder, typically follows URTI/pharyngitis/AOM. Age 3-10 yr, M>F, more common on right side. Features: afebrile or low-grade fever, sudden hip or knee pain (referred from hip), painful limp but FULL ROM preserved, child does NOT look toxic. WBC normal; ESR/CRP mildly elevated; joint effusion on ultrasound. Resolves in 7-10 d spontaneously; treatment is supportive. Complication: may lead to Legg-Calvé-Perthes disease.

Transient synovitis is the most common cause of acute hip pain/limp in children. The key distinguishing feature from septic arthritis is the preserved range of motion (albeit painful) and the child not appearing toxic. Septic arthritis is a medical emergency: child appears unwell, febrile, refuses to move the joint, and requires urgent surgical drainage plus IV antibiotics. Use Kocher criteria to stratify risk.

**Q:** What are the red flags in a child presenting with a limp that should prompt urgent evaluation?

**A:** Red flags for limp requiring urgent evaluation: inability to weight bear; severe or localized joint/bone pain; persistent symptoms (>7 days); fever (suggests infection or malignancy); pinpoint tenderness (fracture, osteomyelitis); night pain; weight loss; erythema overlying bone/joint. Constitutional/nocturnal/systemic symptoms raise suspicion for infection, inflammation, and malignancy. Bone malignancy: intermittent pain at rest or at night ± palpable bony mass.

Most childhood limp (80% of cases associated with pain) is benign, but red flags mandate urgent workup. The most common diagnosis is transient synovitis; however, septic arthritis (Kocher criteria), osteomyelitis, leukemia, and neuroblastoma must be excluded. If a child appears generally unwell, serious systemic conditions must be considered first. Child abuse should be considered and CAS contacted if suspected.

**Q:** What physical exam finding suggests each of the following hip diagnoses in a limping child: (a) septic arthritis, (b) developmental dysplasia, (c) intra-articular pathology (Legg-Calvé-Perthes/SCFE)?

**A:** (a) Septic arthritis: flexed and externally rotated hip at rest. (b) Developmental dysplasia of the hip: limited hip abduction; positive Trendelenburg test (also seen with weak hip abductors). (c) Intra-articular hip pathology (Legg-Calvé-Perthes, SCFE): limited internal rotation of the hip.

These specific exam findings direct diagnosis in a limping child. SCFE classically presents with loss of internal rotation and obligate external rotation when the hip is flexed. Legg-Calvé-Perthes (avascular necrosis of the femoral head) also restricts internal rotation and abduction. Developmental dysplasia assessment includes the Ortolani/Barlow maneuvers in neonates and later the Trendelenburg sign. Septic arthritis positions the hip to minimize intra-articular pressure (flexion + external rotation + abduction).

**Q:** What imaging modalities are used in evaluating a limping child and what does each detect?

**A:** Radiography (AP + lateral): first-line if focal findings present; both lower limbs if no focal findings. Ultrasound: more sensitive for detecting hip effusions (useful for transient synovitis vs. septic arthritis). MRI: confirms osteomyelitis and stress fractures; visualizes malignancy. Bone scintigraphy: considered if other investigations do not reveal a diagnosis (not commonly used as first-line). Blood work if infection/inflammation/malignancy suspected: CBC, ESR, CRP, blood culture (pre-antibiotics).

Imaging selection in a limping child depends on clinical presentation. Plain X-ray is first-line for fractures and some bony pathology. Ultrasound is particularly useful for hip effusions — it can guide diagnostic/therapeutic aspiration. MRI is the gold standard for osteomyelitis, soft tissue infection, and malignancy. Bone scan is sensitive but non-specific and involves radiation. Synovial fluid analysis (cell count, Gram stain, culture) is essential if septic arthritis is suspected.

**Q:** What is the differential diagnosis of limb pain in a child >10 years old?

**A:** In children >10 years: Trauma; Infectious (septic arthritis, osteomyelitis); Inflammatory (JIA, spondyloarthritis, SLE, dermatomyositis, HSP); Anatomic/Orthopedic (Slipped Capital Femoral Epiphysis [SCFE], Osgood-Schlatter disease, Legg-Calvé-Perthes less common); Neoplastic (leukemia, bone tumour); Hematologic (hemophilia/hemarthrosis, sickle cell anemia); Pain syndromes (fibromyalgia, complex regional pain syndrome).

SCFE (Slipped Capital Femoral Epiphysis) occurs predominantly in obese adolescents, while Osgood-Schlatter (tibial tubercle apophysitis) peaks in active adolescents during growth spurts. Legg-Calvé-Perthes is more common in younger children (4-8 years). In any age group with limb pain, infection, malignancy, and acute orthopedic conditions must be ruled out first.

## Pediatrics Infectious

**Q:** What are the pathogens and antibiotic treatment for septic arthritis/osteomyelitis by age group in children?

**A:** Neonates (<1 mo): GBS, S. aureus, Gram-negative bacilli (E. coli) → Cloxacillin + gentamicin OR cloxacillin + cefotaxime. Infants (1-3 mo): Kingella kingae, Strep spp., Staph spp., H. influenzae type B → Cefazolin IV then cephalexin PO OR cloxacillin + cefotaxime OR cefuroxime. Children (3 mo–11 yr): Kingella kingae (<4 yr), S. aureus (>4 yr), S. pneumoniae, GAS → Cefazolin IV then cephalexin PO. Adolescents (>12 yr): Same + N. gonorrhoeae → Ceftriaxone OR cefixime + azithromycin.

Septic arthritis is a medical emergency requiring prompt IV antibiotics until systemically well/ambulating/blood cultures if positive become negative, followed by 4 weeks of oral antibiotics (4-6 weeks for septic arthritis of the hip). S. aureus is the dominant pathogen beyond infancy. Kingella kingae is an important pathogen in young children (3 months to 4 years). N. gonorrhoeae must be covered in sexually active adolescents. (CPS/Toronto Notes 2025)

**Q:** What is Lyme Arthritis in children, and why is doxycycline avoided in children under 8 years?

**A:** Lyme arthritis is caused by the spirochete Borrelia burgdorferi (transmitted by Ixodes tick). Incidence is highest in children aged 5-10 years. Arthritis is typically monoarticular, involving the knee. Treatment: doxycycline is NOT used in children <8 years because it may cause permanent tooth discolouration (dental enamel hypoplasia). Alternative antibiotics (amoxicillin or cefuroxime) are used in younger children.

Lyme disease is endemic in parts of Canada (Ontario, Manitoba, Nova Scotia, British Columbia). Arthritis typically presents as a large joint monoarthritis (knee most common) weeks to months after initial infection. The restriction on doxycycline in children under 8 years is due to tetracycline class effects on developing teeth and bones. Older children and adults can receive doxycycline as first-line therapy.

**Q:** What did the MELODY trial demonstrate about RSV prevention in infants?

**A:** MELODY trial (NEJM 2022): RCT of 1490 infants born at ≥35 weeks gestational age. Randomized 2:1 to nirsevimab (monoclonal antibody against RSV) vs. placebo. Result: RSV infections within 150 days occurred in 1.2% of the nirsevimab group vs. 5% of the placebo group. Conclusion: Nirsevimab provides effective protection of late-preterm and full-term infants from RSV infection.

Nirsevimab (Beyfortus) is a long-acting monoclonal antibody against RSV prefusion F protein, approved in Canada for RSV prevention in all infants in their first RSV season (unlike palivizumab which was limited to high-risk groups). RSV is the most common cause of bronchiolitis and a leading cause of infant hospitalization. Nirsevimab provides passive immunization without the need for repeated monthly injections.

## Pediatrics Pharmacology

**Q:** What is the acetaminophen dosing in pediatrics, and what is the maximum daily dose?

**A:** Acetaminophen: 10-15 mg/kg/dose PO q4-6 h PRN (analgesic/antipyretic). Maximum daily dose: 60 mg/kg/d in neonates OR 75 mg/kg/d in older children to a maximum of 4 g/d. Causes hepatotoxicity at high doses.

Acetaminophen is the most commonly used analgesic/antipyretic in children. The dose is weight-based and the maximum differs between neonates (lower maximum due to immature hepatic metabolism) and older children. Hepatotoxicity from overdose is the primary toxicity — NAC (N-acetylcysteine) is the antidote. Unlike ibuprofen, acetaminophen has no anti-inflammatory properties.

**Q:** What is the ibuprofen dose in children and what are the key contraindications?

**A:** Ibuprofen: 5-10 mg/kg/dose PO q6-8 h (analgesic, antipyretic, anti-inflammatory). Use cautiously in patients with liver/renal impairment, or history of GI bleeding or ulcers. Unlike acetaminophen, ibuprofen has anti-inflammatory properties, making it useful for JIA and musculoskeletal pain.

Ibuprofen is an NSAID with analgesic, antipyretic, and anti-inflammatory effects. It is preferred over acetaminophen when anti-inflammatory action is needed (e.g., JIA flares, soft tissue injuries). Contraindications include renal impairment, hepatic impairment, and GI ulceration. Avoid in neonates. ASA is generally avoided in children with viral illnesses due to risk of Reye syndrome.

**Q:** What are the dexamethasone doses for croup vs. acute asthma in children?

**A:** Dexamethasone dosing: Croup: 0.6 mg/kg PO x 1 dose. Acute asthma: 0.6 mg/kg/d PO for 2 days.

Dexamethasone is preferred over prednisone for croup due to its longer half-life (single dose is effective) and better palatability. For croup, a single oral dose of 0.6 mg/kg reduces the need for return visits and hospital admission. For acute asthma, a 2-day course of dexamethasone is equivalent to a 5-day prednisone course and improves adherence. For persistent asthma, inhaled fluticasone (Flovent) is used for maintenance.

**Q:** What is the prednisone/prednisolone dose for: (a) acute asthma, (b) ITP, and (c) nephrotic syndrome in children?

**A:** (a) Acute asthma: prednisone/prednisolone 1-2 mg/kg/d PO x 5 days. (b) ITP: 3-4 mg/kg/d PO then taper to 1-2 mg/kg/d PO once platelet count >30 x 10⁹/L. (c) Nephrotic syndrome: 60 mg/m²/d PO. Note: oral prednisone is bitter-tasting; consider using prednisolone instead.

Corticosteroids are used in multiple pediatric conditions. For asthma, a 5-day course of prednisone 1-2 mg/kg/d is standard for moderate-severe exacerbations. ITP (Immune Thrombocytopenic Purpura) uses higher doses (3-4 mg/kg/d) to rapidly raise platelet counts. Nephrotic syndrome uses surface area-based dosing (60 mg/m²/d). Prednisolone is preferred in young children because it is better tolerated palatability-wise.

## Pediatrics Allergy

**Q:** What did the LEAP trial demonstrate about peanut allergy prevention?

**A:** LEAP trial (NEJM 2015): RCT of 640 infants aged 4-11 months with severe eczema, egg allergy, or both. Randomized to peanut consumption vs. avoidance until age 60 months. Result: peanut allergy developed in only 1.9% of the consumption group vs. 13.7% of the avoidance group. Conclusion: Early peanut consumption significantly reduces the prevalence of peanut allergy — peanut avoidance increases allergy risk.

The LEAP trial fundamentally changed allergy prevention guidelines. Current Canadian and international guidelines now recommend early introduction of peanuts in high-risk infants (those with severe eczema or egg allergy) starting at 4-6 months to prevent peanut allergy. This is the opposite of previous 'avoidance' strategies. The IMPACT trial further showed that peanut oral immunotherapy in 1-3 year olds can induce desensitization (71%) and remission (21%).

## Pediatrics Respirology

**Q:** What did the MANDALA trial demonstrate about combination rescue inhalers for asthma in children?

**A:** MANDALA trial (NEJM 2022): RCT of 3132 patients aged 4-11 yr with uncontrolled moderate-to-severe asthma. Randomized to albuterol (180 µg) + budesonide (160 µg) vs. albuterol (180 µg) + budesonide (80 µg) vs. albuterol (180 µg) alone for 24 weeks rescue treatment. Result: Higher-dose combination therapy reduced risk of severe exacerbation by 26% vs. albuterol monotherapy. Conclusion: Albuterol + budesonide as rescue therapy reduces risk of severe asthma exacerbation vs. albuterol alone.

The MANDALA trial supports the use of ICS-formoterol (or ICS-SABA combinations) as rescue therapy rather than SABA alone. As per updated Canadian Thoracic Society guidelines, anti-inflammatory reliever therapy (AIR) strategy using ICS + SABA rescue is preferred in uncontrolled moderate-severe asthma. This provides both bronchodilation and reduces airway inflammation at the time of exacerbation.

**Q:** What are the defining features of childhood asthma and its Canadian epidemiology?

**A:** Inflammatory disorder of airways with recurrent episodes of reversible small airway obstruction from airway hyperresponsiveness to endogenous/exogenous stimuli. In Canada: lifetime prevalence 10-15% in childhood; most often presents in early childhood; associated with other atopic diseases (allergies, atopic dermatitis).

Consider asthma in ages 1-5 years with recurrent asthma-like symptoms even if triggered by viral infections. Formal diagnosis requires: documented airflow obstruction + reversibility with asthma therapy + no suspicion of alternative diagnosis. Associated atopic triad: asthma, allergic rhinitis, atopic dermatitis.

**Q:** What are the common triggers for asthma exacerbations in children?

**A:** URTI (viral or Mycoplasma), weather changes (cold exposure, humidity), allergens (pets, pollen), irritants (cigarette smoke), exercise, emotional stress, drugs (ASA, beta-blockers).

Viral URTIs are the most common trigger in children, particularly RSV, rhinovirus, and parainfluenza. ASA-induced asthma (Samter's triad: asthma + nasal polyps + ASA sensitivity) is more common in adults but can occur in children. Beta-blockers (including ophthalmic) can precipitate severe bronchospasm and are generally contraindicated in asthma.

**Q:** What is the acute management of pediatric asthma exacerbations in the emergency department?

**A:** 1. O2 (keep SpO2 >92%) and IV fluids if dehydrated. 2. Salbutamol (Ventolin): MDI + spacer, 4-5 puffs (<20 kg) or 8-10 puffs (>20 kg) q20 min x 3 doses (nebulized in very severe cases). 3. Ipratropium bromide (Atrovent) if severe: 4 puffs (<30 kg) or 8 puffs (≥30 kg) q15-20 min x 3 doses. 4. Steroids: prednisone 1-2 mg/kg/day x5 days or dexamethasone 0.6 mg/kg/day x2 days. 5. If no response: IV magnesium sulphate.

PRAM score (0-12) guides severity: mild (0-3), moderate (4-7), severe (8-12). Discharge criteria: β2-agonist needed not more than q4h, SpO2 ≥92% in room air, improved air entry, minimal WOB, and PRAM <3 sustained for 2-4h after last bronchodilator. Discharge with inhaled corticosteroids (12-week course) if having significant symptoms outside exacerbations.

**Q:** What are the chronic management steps for pediatric asthma (controller therapy)?

**A:** First line (all children): low-dose daily inhaled corticosteroids (ICS) with aerochamber. Second line for children <12 yr: moderate dose ICS. Second line for children >12 yr: leukotriene receptor antagonist (LTRA) OR long-acting β2-agonist (LABA) PLUS low-dose ICS; LTRA monotherapy as alternative. Reliever: short-acting β2-agonist (salbutamol) PRN.

Per 2021 Canadian Thoracic Society guidelines, well-controlled asthma criteria include: daytime symptoms <2 times/week, nighttime symptoms <1 time/week, normal physical activity, mild/infrequent exacerbations, no school/work absenteeism, β-agonist use <2 doses/week, FEV1 or PEF ≥90% of personal best, PEF diurnal variation <10-15%, sputum eosinophils <2-3%.

**Q:** What are the indications for hospitalization in pediatric asthma?

**A:** 1. Ongoing need for supplemental O2. 2. Persistently increased work of breathing. 3. β2-agonists needed more often than q4h after 4-8h of conventional treatment. 4. Patient deteriorates while on systemic steroids.

These criteria reflect failure of initial outpatient/emergency management. Children with severe or near-fatal asthma attacks may require ICU admission, IV methylprednisolone, continuous nebulized bronchodilators, heliox, or intubation with mechanical ventilation. BiPAP is an intermediate option before intubation.

**Q:** Define bronchiolitis: etiology, epidemiology, and clinical features.

**A:** LRTI in children <2 years with wheezing and respiratory distress. Most common LRTI in infants, affects 50% in first 2 years; peak incidence at 6 months; winter/early spring. Etiology: RSV (>50%), parainfluenza, influenza, rhinovirus, adenovirus. Features: first episode of wheeze in child <1 year, URTI prodrome (cough, rhinorrhea ± fever), feeding difficulties, irritability, wheezing, crackles, tachypnea, retractions; symptoms peak at 3-4 days.

RSV bronchiolitis in infancy is associated with increased incidence of asthma later in life (whether causative or marker of atopic predisposition is debated). Premature infants, those with CHD, chronic lung disease, or immunodeficiency are at high risk for severe disease. Apnea may be the presenting sign in young infants.

**Q:** What is the evidence-based management of bronchiolitis in children?

**A:** Mild-moderate: supportive care — PO/IV hydration, oral/nasal suctioning, antipyretics, humidified O2. Severe: humidified high-flow O2 or intubation/ventilation. Bronchodilators (salbutamol, ipratropium), corticosteroids, and antibiotics have NO therapeutic value (unless secondary bacterial pneumonia). Routine investigations NOT required (Choosing Wisely). Peak symptoms at 3-4 days; total duration 2-3 weeks.

Meta-analyses (Cochrane 2010, 30 RCTs, 1992 infants) confirm bronchodilators do not improve O2 saturation, hospital admission rates, or duration of hospitalization in bronchiolitis.
