CXR is only indicated for poor response to therapy or diagnostic uncertainty (shows air trapping, peribronchial thickening, atelectasis, increased linear markings).

**Q:** What are the indications for hospitalization in bronchiolitis?

**A:** 1. Hypoxia: O2 saturation <90% on initial presentation or increasing O2 requirements. 2. Signs of severe distress: tachypnea >80/min, tachycardia >180/min, grunting, nasal flaring, marked chest retractions, lethargy. 3. History of chronic lung disease, hemodynamically significant cardiac disease, neuromuscular problem, immunocompromised. 4. High-risk infants: prematurity <34 weeks, weight <4 kg, age <7 weeks. 5. Significant feeding problems. 6. Socioeconomic barriers to improvement.

Palivizumab (monoclonal antibody targeting RSV F-glycoprotein) is recommended as prophylaxis in high-risk infants: premature <30 weeks GA at <6 months of RSV season onset, chronic lung disease of prematurity requiring ongoing O2, hemodynamically significant CHD age <24 months, and infants from remote northern Inuit communities.

**Q:** What is the role of palivizumab in RSV prophylaxis, and which infants qualify in Canada?

**A:** Palivizumab is a monoclonal antibody targeting the F-glycoprotein of RSV, given monthly during RSV season. Canadian indications (Public Health Agency 2022): (1) premature infants ≤30 weeks GA at <6 months of age at onset of RSV season, (2) children <24 months with chronic lung disease of prematurity requiring ongoing O2 in the preceding 6 months, (3) infants <24 months with hemodynamically significant CHD, (4) infants born <36 weeks GA and age <6 months living in remote northern Inuit communities.

Cochrane review (2021): palivizumab reduces RSV-related hospitalizations (RR 0.30, 95% CI 0.21-0.44 in high-risk infants) with little-to-no difference in mortality or adverse events at 2-year follow-up. It is prophylaxis, not treatment for established RSV. Cost-effectiveness considerations limit use to high-risk groups.

**Q:** What is the etiology of cystic fibrosis (CF), and what mutation is most common?

**A:** Autosomal recessive; mutation in CFTR gene on chromosome 7, resulting in dysfunctional chloride channel on apical membrane. Most common mutation: ΔF508 (70% of cases), with >1600 different mutations identified. Incidence: 1 in 3000 live births; most common in people of Northern European descent.

CFTR dysfunction leads to relative dehydration of airway secretions (decreased Cl⁻ secretion → increased Na⁺ and H2O reabsorption), impairing mucociliary transport and causing airway obstruction. In sweat glands, CFTR malfunction leads to elevated sweat chloride. Pancreatic ducts obstruct, causing exocrine insufficiency and eventually endocrine (DM) dysfunction.

**Q:** What are the clinical features of cystic fibrosis across age groups?

**A:** Neonatal: meconium ileus, prolonged jaundice, antenatal bowel perforation. Infancy: pancreatic insufficiency with steatorrhea and FTT (despite voracious appetite), anemia, hypoproteinemia, hyponatremia. Childhood: heat intolerance, wheezing/chronic cough, recurrent chest infections (S. aureus, P. aeruginosa, H. influenzae), hemoptysis, nasal polyps, distal intestinal obstruction syndrome, rectal prolapse, clubbing. Older: COPD, male infertility (atresia/absence of vas deferens), decreased female fertility.

Mnemonic CF PANCREAS: Chronic cough and wheezing, Failure to thrive (despite voracious appetite), Pancreatic insufficiency (steatorrhea), Absence/atresia of vas deferens, Neonatal intestinal obstruction (meconium ileus), Clubbing, Rectal prolapse, Electrolyte elevation in sweat (salty skin), S. aureus or P. aeruginosa in sputum.

**Q:** How is cystic fibrosis diagnosed?

**A:** 1. Newborn screening (neonatal). 2. Sweat chloride test x2: >60 mEq/L is diagnostic. 3. CFTR gene mutation analysis (if sweat chloride equivocal or clinically suspicious for rare mutation). 4. Newborn genetic screening positive → requires DNA testing + subsequent sweat chloride testing.

False positive sweat chloride: malnutrition, atopic dermatitis, hypothyroidism, hypoparathyroidism, GSD, adrenal insufficiency, G6PD deficiency, Klinefelter syndrome, autonomic dysfunction, familial cholestasis. False negative: technical problem, malnutrition, skin edema, mineralocorticoids. Sweat chloride 40-60 mEq/L is equivocal; <40 mEq/L is normal.

**Q:** What is the management and prognosis of cystic fibrosis in Canada?

**A:** Nutritional: high-calorie diet, pancreatic enzyme replacements, fat-soluble vitamin supplements. Chest: physiotherapy, postural drainage, exercise, bronchodilators, aerosolized DNase, inhaled hypertonic saline, antibiotics (per sputum C&S: cephalosporin, cloxacillin, ciprofloxacin, inhaled tobramycin), lung transplantation. Genetic counselling. Current median survival in Canada: 46.6 years.

CFTR modulator therapy (e.g., ivacaftor, lumacaftor/ivacaftor, elexacaftor/tezacaftor/ivacaftor) has dramatically improved outcomes for eligible mutation carriers by correcting the underlying CFTR protein defect. Complications include respiratory failure, pneumothorax (poor prognosis), cor pulmonale, pancreatic fibrosis with DM, gallstones, cirrhosis with portal HTN, male infertility.

**Q:** What are the common causes and first-line antibiotics for community-acquired pneumonia in children by age group?

**A:** Neonates: GBS, E. coli, Listeria, HSV, Enterovirus → ampicillin + gentamicin. 1-3 months: S. aureus, S. pneumoniae, Chlamydia trachomatis, RSV, influenza → ampicillin OR ceftriaxone (add azithromycin if Chlamydia suspected). 3 months-5 years: S. pneumoniae, S. aureus, S. pyogenes, RSV → high-dose amoxicillin OR ampicillin OR ceftriaxone. >5 years: S. pneumoniae, Mycoplasma pneumoniae, Chlamydophila pneumoniae, S. aureus → high-dose amoxicillin OR ampicillin OR ceftriaxone (add azithromycin/clarithromycin if Mycoplasma/Chlamydophila suspected). Severe/multilobar/pneumatoceles: add vancomycin.

Viral etiologies are most common in children <5 years (RSV, influenza, adenovirus, parainfluenza). Mycoplasma pneumoniae becomes increasingly important after age 5 years ('walking pneumonia' with gradual onset, dry cough, headache). CXR in bacterial pneumonia shows lobar consolidation or pleural effusion; viral shows diffuse bilateral streaky infiltrates.

**Q:** Distinguish croup, bacterial tracheitis, and epiglottitis in children.

**A:** Croup (laryngotracheitis): subglottis; age 6-36 months; parainfluenza 75%; non-toxic appearance, barking cough, stridor, viral prodrome, hoarseness; CXR steeple sign. Management: corticosteroids ± nebulized epinephrine (severe). Bacterial tracheitis: trachea; age 3 months-6 years; S. aureus; toxic appearance, rapid progression, biphasic stridor, cough, no response to corticosteroids/epinephrine; endoscopy diagnostic. Management: intubation + IV antibiotics. Epiglottitis: supraglottis; age 2-6 years (decreased due to Hib vaccine); H. influenzae (+ S. pneumoniae, S. aureus); toxic, rapid progression, 4Ds (drooling, dysphagia, dysphonia, distress), tripod position, fever >39°C, NO cough; management: DO NOT examine (risk of complete obstruction), intubation + IV antibiotics.

The 'steeple sign' on AP neck/chest X-ray reflects subglottic narrowing in croup. In epiglottitis, the 'thumb sign' (thickened epiglottis) is seen on lateral neck X-ray. Physical exam must be done cautiously in epiglottitis to avoid precipitating complete airway obstruction — intubation equipment should be at bedside. Hib vaccination has dramatically reduced epiglottitis incidence.

**Q:** What is choanal atresia, and how does it present bilaterally vs. unilaterally?

**A:** Choanal atresia: persistence of oronasal membrane preventing nose from joining the oropharynx; 1 in 7000 live births; 2/3 are unilateral; F>M. Bilateral: presents in infancy with noisy breathing, cyanosis worsening with feeds and improving with crying (neonates are obligate nasal breathers). Unilateral: diagnosed later in life with unilateral discharge or obstruction. Diagnosis: inability to pass NG tube through nares; CT definitive. Acute management (bilateral): place oral airway, initiate gavage feedings. Long-term: referral to otolaryngology.

The key clinical pearl is that bilateral choanal atresia causes cyclical cyanosis: worse when feeding (mouth closed, must breathe through blocked nose) and better when crying (mouth open). This distinguishes it from cardiac cyanosis. CT scan defines the extent of bony/membranous obstruction for surgical planning (surgical puncture and stenting).

**Q:** What causes stridor vs. wheezing in children, and what are the most common differential diagnoses for each?

**A:** Stridor: high-pitched noisy breathing from obstruction ABOVE the thoracic inlet; may have hoarseness and suprasternal retractions; stridor at rest is an emergency. DDx: croup, bacterial tracheitis, epiglottitis, foreign body aspiration, subglottic stenosis, laryngomalacia/tracheomalacia, retropharyngeal abscess. Wheezing: whistling sound mainly during expiration from obstruction BELOW the thoracic inlet. Common DDx: asthma (>6 yr, recurrent), bronchiolitis (<1 yr, first episode), recurrent aspiration, pneumonia. Uncommon: foreign body (acute unilateral), CF, BPD. Rare: CHF, mediastinal mass, bronchiolitis obliterans.

The anatomical location of obstruction determines the sound: above the thoracic inlet → inspiratory stridor (extrathoracic); below → expiratory wheeze (intrathoracic). A fixed obstruction (foreign body, subglottic stenosis) may cause biphasic stridor. Laryngomalacia is the most common cause of chronic stridor in infants, due to collapse of the supraglottic structures on inspiration.

**Q:** A 5-month-old infant presents in winter with 3 days of rhinorrhea, increasing wheeze, and poor feeding. O2 saturation is 88% on room air, respiratory rate 85/min. What is the diagnosis and management?

**A:** Diagnosis: Bronchiolitis (likely RSV). Management: Supportive — humidified high-flow O2 (SpO2 <90% is indication for hospitalization), IV/NG hydration, nasal suctioning, antipyretics. Do NOT give bronchodilators (salbutamol, ipratropium), corticosteroids, or antibiotics. Hospitalization indicated: SpO2 <90%, RR >80/min, age <7 weeks, significant feeding problems.

This infant meets multiple hospitalization criteria (hypoxia, severe tachypnea, significant feeding difficulty, age <6 months). Bronchodilators have no proven benefit in bronchiolitis (Cochrane meta-analysis). Peak symptoms at 3-4 days; total illness 2-3 weeks. If premature (<34 wks), palivizumab prophylaxis should have been considered prior to RSV season.

**Q:** A 2-year-old presents at midnight with sudden barking cough, inspiratory stridor, and low-grade fever. The child appears non-toxic. What is the diagnosis and management?

**A:** Diagnosis: Croup (viral laryngotracheitis), most likely parainfluenza virus. Management: Mild-moderate — oral/IM dexamethasone 0.6 mg/kg (single dose) + supportive care. Severe — corticosteroids + nebulized epinephrine (racemic or L-epinephrine 1:1000) + humidified O2 + intubation if necessary.

Croup is most common in children 6-36 months, peaks in fall/winter. The barking cough, stridor, and hoarseness reflect subglottic edema. CXR (if done) shows 'steeple sign' (subglottic narrowing). Dexamethasone reduces severity and hospitalization. Nebulized epinephrine provides temporary relief (2 hours) in severe cases — observe for 2-4 hours post-epinephrine before discharge.

## Pediatrics Nephrology

**Q:** What is the definition of nephrotic syndrome (NS) in children, and what are the four cardinal features?

**A:** NS is defined by: (1) massive proteinuria (urine protein/creatinine ratio >300 mg/mmol on first morning urine), (2) hypoalbuminemia (<25 g/L), (3) edema, and (4) hyperlipidemia/hypercholesterolemia (total cholesterol >5 mmol/L).

The tetrad reflects the pathophysiology: glomerular leak of protein → hypoalbuminemia → reduced oncotic pressure → edema, with reactive hepatic lipoprotein synthesis causing hyperlipidemia. Minimal change disease (MCD) is the most common cause in children and is steroid-responsive in ~80%.

**Q:** In pediatric nephrotic syndrome, how does edema typically progress throughout the day, and what is the most severe form called?

**A:** Edema starts periorbital (often first sign, most prominent in morning) and pretibial, then becomes gravity-dependent — periorbital edema decreases and pretibial edema increases over the day. Anasarca is the most severe form: marked periorbital and peripheral edema, ascites, pleural effusions, and scrotal/labial edema.

Edema is detectable when fluid retention exceeds 3-5% of body weight. The gravity-dependent shift explains the clinical observation that periorbital puffiness is worse on waking. Anasarca requires urgent management including furosemide + albumin infusion.

**Q:** What investigations are used to diagnose nephrotic syndrome in children?

**A:** Urine: dipstick (3-4+ proteinuria ± microscopic hematuria), first morning urine protein/creatinine ratio >300 mg/mmol. Blood: hypoalbuminemia (<25 g/L), hypercholesterolemia (>5 mmol/L). Secondary: electrolytes (hypocalcemia, hyperkalemia, hyponatremia), renal function (↑BUN, Cr), coagulation profile (↑PTT). Rule out secondary causes: CBC, C3/C4, ANA, hepatitis B/C, ASOT, HIV.

The urine protein/creatinine ratio on a random or first morning urine sample is a reliable non-invasive method to quantify proteinuria, avoiding 24-hour urine collection in children. Complement levels (C3/C4) help distinguish MCD (normal) from membranoproliferative GN or lupus nephritis (low).

**Q:** What are the indications for renal biopsy in a child with nephrotic syndrome?

**A:** Indications: HTN, gross hematuria, ↓renal function, low serum C3/C4, no response to steroids after 4 weeks of therapy, frequent relapses (>2 in 6 months), presentation before first year of life (congenital nephrotic syndrome), presentation after age 12 (rule out serious pathology beyond MCD).

Most children aged 1-12 years with typical nephrotic syndrome (no hematuria, normal complement, normal BP) are empirically treated with steroids for presumed MCD without biopsy. Biopsy is reserved for atypical features or steroid resistance, which suggests focal segmental glomerulosclerosis, membranous nephropathy, or other entities.

**Q:** What is the first-line treatment for minimal change disease (MCD) in children, and what precaution must be taken before initiating?

**A:** Oral prednisone 2 mg/kg/day (or equivalent) for up to 12 weeks. Before starting, varicella immune status must be confirmed — prednisone can significantly exacerbate varicella infection.

MCD accounts for ~80-90% of nephrotic syndrome in children aged 1-8 years and has an excellent response to corticosteroids (~80% respond). Varicella exposure while on corticosteroids requires varicella immunoglobulin + acyclovir. Pneumococcal vaccine should be given at diagnosis, and varicella vaccine after remission.

**Q:** List the major complications of nephrotic syndrome in children.

**A:** 1. Infections: spontaneous bacterial peritonitis, pneumonia/empyema, cellulitis, sepsis (due to urinary loss of immunoglobulins and complement). 2. Hypercoagulability: PE, renal vein thrombosis (due to ↓intravascular volume + antithrombin III depletion). 3. Intravascular volume depletion → hypotension, shock, renal failure. 4. Anasarca. 5. Adverse effects on growth. 6. Drug side effects from long-term steroids.

Antithrombin III is lost in urine along with other proteins, combined with hemoconcentration from low intravascular oncotic pressure, creating a prothrombotic state. The GOOD CUSHINGS mnemonic captures steroid side effects: Growth impairment, Obesity, Osteoporosis, Dorsal hump, Cataracts, Ulcers, Striae, Hypertension, Immunosuppression, Necrosis (avascular), Glucose dysregulation, Salt/water retention.

**Q:** What is the prognosis of nephrotic syndrome due to MCD in children?

**A:** Generally good: ~80% respond to corticosteroids. Up to 2/3 experience relapse (often multiple times). Most achieve sustained remission with normal kidney function by adolescence.

Frequent relapsers (>2 relapses in 6 months) may require cytotoxic agents (cyclophosphamide), immunomodulators (mycophenolate, tacrolimus), or high-dose pulse corticosteroids. Long-term prognosis is excellent for MCD, unlike focal segmental glomerulosclerosis which has higher risk of progression to ESRD.

**Q:** What is the mnemonic for side effects of long-term corticosteroid use in children with nephrotic syndrome?

**A:** GOOD CUSHINGS: Growth impairment, Obesity, Osteoporosis, Dorsal hump (buffalo hump), Cataracts, Ulcers (peptic), Striae, Hypertension, Immunosuppression, Necrosis (avascular necrosis), Glucose dysregulation (DM), Salt and water retention.

Long-term corticosteroid side effects are a major concern in pediatric nephrotic syndrome given frequent relapses requiring repeated courses. Calcium and vitamin D supplementation should be given during corticosteroid therapy to mitigate bone loss. Growth velocity should be monitored; if significantly impaired, alternate-day dosing or steroid-sparing agents may be considered.

**Q:** A 3-year-old boy presents with periorbital edema, foamy urine, and fatigue. Urine protein/creatinine ratio is 350 mg/mmol, serum albumin 18 g/L, cholesterol 7.2 mmol/L. What is the diagnosis, most likely etiology, and initial treatment?

**A:** Diagnosis: Nephrotic syndrome. Most likely etiology: Minimal Change Disease (MCD) — most common cause in children aged 1-8 years. Initial treatment: Oral prednisone 2 mg/kg/day for up to 12 weeks, after confirming varicella immune status.

The classic tetrad is present (heavy proteinuria, hypoalbuminemia, edema, hypercholesterolemia). In this age group without red flags (normal BP, no gross hematuria, normal complement), empiric steroid therapy is appropriate without renal biopsy. ~80% of children with MCD respond to steroids. Renal biopsy would be considered if no response after 4 weeks.

## Pediatrics Neurology

**Q:** Define cerebral palsy (CP) and state its incidence.

**A:** CP is a non-progressive central motor impairment syndrome due to CNS anomaly or neural injury occurring at the prenatal, perinatal, or postnatal stage. Incidence: 1.5-2.5 per 1000 live births in industrialized nations.

Life expectancy depends on degree of mobility and intellectual impairment, not on severity of the CNS lesion. CP is classified functionally using the Gross Motor Functional Classification System (GMFCS) with 5 levels. One-third of cases have no identified cause.

**Q:** What are the four types of cerebral palsy, their characteristics, and associated brain areas?

**A:** 1. Spastic (70-80%): ↑tone, ↑reflexes, clonus; UMN pyramidal tract. Subtypes: hemiplegia (MCA stroke), diplegia (periventricular leukomalacia in prematurity), quadriplegia (HIE). 2. Athetoid/Dyskinetic (10-15%): athetosis ± chorea/hypotonia; basal ganglia (kernicterus). 3. Ataxic (<5%): poor coordination, wide-based gait, intention tremor; cerebellum. 4. Mixed (10-15%): >1 motor pattern.

Spastic CP has truncal hypotonia in the first year then upper motor neuron signs emerge. Dyskinetic CP involving the face and tongue causes dysarthria. Ataxic CP is least common. Quadriplegia has the highest rate of intellectual disability due to diffuse HIE involvement.

**Q:** What are the prenatal, perinatal, and postnatal causes of cerebral palsy?

**A:** Prenatal: TORCH infections, maternal disorders (e.g. epilepsy), congenital CNS anomaly, genetic causes. Perinatal: prematurity, LBW, ischemic stroke, hypoxic ischemic encephalopathy (HIE). Postnatal: infections (e.g. meningitis), asphyxia, IVH, trauma, kernicterus. No cause identified in 1/3 of cases.

Kernicterus (bilirubin toxicity to basal ganglia) is an important preventable cause of dyskinetic CP. Periventricular leukomalacia (PVL) in premature infants affects the descending motor tracts adjacent to the lateral ventricles, preferentially involving leg fibers and causing spastic diplegia.

**Q:** How is spasticity managed in cerebral palsy?

**A:** Baclofen (oral or intrathecal) for generalized spasticity; botulinum toxin (Botox) injections for focal spasticity. Multidisciplinary services: OT, PT, SLP, school supports. Orthopedic/neurosurgical management for dislocations, contractures, and selective dorsal rhizotomy.

Botulinum toxin reduces focal spasticity by blocking acetylcholine release at neuromuscular junctions; effects last 3-6 months. Intrathecal baclofen via pump is used for severe generalized spasticity unresponsive to oral therapy. Associated problems managed include: constipation (stool softeners), seizures (antiepileptics), and feeding difficulties (gastrostomy).

**Q:** What are the diagnostic criteria for simple (typical) vs. complex (atypical) febrile seizures?

**A:** Simple/Typical (70-80%): ALL of — duration <15 min, generalized tonic-clonic, no recurrence in 24h, no neurological impairment before or after. Very small epilepsy risk (~2% vs. 1% general population). Complex/Atypical (20-30%): ANY ONE of — duration >15 min, focal onset or focal features, recurrent seizures (>1 in 24h), previous neurological impairment or neurological deficit after seizure. Increased epilepsy risk.

Febrile seizures affect 3-5% of children (most common cause of seizures in children), peak age 6 months-6 years, M>F. The child must be otherwise neurotypical with no evidence of CNS infection before/after the seizure. Recurrence rate is ~33% (mostly within 1 year, especially if first seizure at age <1 year).

**Q:** What is the management of a simple febrile seizure in a child?

**A:** Counsel and reassure parents: febrile seizures do not cause brain damage, very small epilepsy risk. Antipyretics and fluids for comfort (do NOT prevent seizures). No prophylaxis with antiepileptic drugs. Treat underlying cause of fever. If high-risk for recurrent/prolonged seizures: prescribe rectal or sublingual lorazepam for home use.

LP is strongly considered if child <6 months, considered if 6-18 months, and only done if meningeal signs present if >18 months. EEG, CT/MRI are NOT warranted unless atypical febrile seizure or abnormal neurologic findings. Septic workup focuses on determining the fever source, not the seizure itself in typical cases.

**Q:** Differentiate upper motor neuron (UMN) from lower motor neuron (LMN) hypotonia in infants.

**A:** UMN hypotonia (60-80% of hypotonia): increased deep tendon reflexes and clonus, floppy but STRONG, positive Babinski, neonatal reflexes present. LMN hypotonia (15-30%): absent deep tendon reflexes, floppy AND WEAK, negative Babinski, neonatal reflexes absent.

'Floppy and strong' vs. 'floppy and weak' is the key clinical distinction. UMN causes include cerebral (genetic, metabolic, perinatal problems) and spinal cord lesions. LMN causes include motor neuron disease (spinal muscular atrophy), peripheral nerve, neuromuscular junction (myasthenia gravis, botulism), and muscle fiber disorders (muscular dystrophy, myotonic dystrophy).

**Q:** What are the central vs. peripheral causes of hypotonia (floppy infant syndrome)?

**A:** Central (60-80%): genetic (Down syndrome, Prader-Willi, Fragile X), metabolic (hypoglycemia, kernicterus), perinatal problems (HIE, ICH), endocrine (hypothyroidism, hypopituitarism), systemic illness (TORCH, sepsis, dehydration), CNS malformations. Peripheral (15-30%): motor neuron (SMA, polio), peripheral nerve (Charcot-Marie-Tooth), NMJ (myasthenia gravis, botulism), muscle fiber (mitochondrial myopathy, muscular dystrophy, myotonic dystrophy).

Causes of hypotonia that respond to rapid treatment (mnemonic PHHHISAD): Prader-Willi, Hypoglycemia, Hydrocephalus, Hypothyroidism, Infection, Seizure, Acidemia, Drugs/toxins. Investigations include electrolytes, ABG, glucose, CK, chromosomal analysis, metabolic testing, EMG, muscle biopsy, and MRI.

**Q:** What are the diagnostic criteria for Neurofibromatosis Type 1 (NF-1, von Recklinghausen disease)?

**A:** Diagnosis requires ≥2 of: (1) >6 café-au-lait spots (>5 mm prepubertal, >1.5 cm postpubertal), (2) ≥2 neurofibromas of any type OR one plexiform neurofibroma, (3) >2 Lisch nodules (iris hamartomas), (4) optic glioma, (5) axillary or inguinal freckling, (6) distinctive bony lesion (sphenoid dysplasia, cortical thinning of long bones), (7) first-degree relative with NF-1.

NF-1: autosomal dominant (50% new mutations), incidence 1:3000, NF1 gene on chromosome 17q11.2 (codes for neurofibromin). Common associations: learning disorders, abnormal speech, seizures. 'In neurocutaneous syndromes, the younger the age of diagnosis, the more severe the manifestation.' Management: regular monitoring (ophthalmology, BP), treat manifestations, genetic counselling.

**Q:** How does Neurofibromatosis Type 2 (NF-2) differ from NF-1?

**A:** NF-2: autosomal dominant (>50% new mutations), incidence 1:33,000, NF2 gene on chromosome 22 (codes for merlin protein). Characterized by bilateral vestibular schwannomas. Diagnosed if: bilateral vestibular schwannomas OR first-degree relative with NF-2 PLUS unilateral vestibular schwannoma OR any two of: meningioma, glioma, schwannoma, neurofibroma, posterior subcapsular lenticular opacities.

NF-1 affects peripheral nerves and skin (neurofibromas, café-au-lait spots), while NF-2 predisposes to CNS/spinal tumours with less skin involvement. The hallmark of NF-2 is bilateral vestibular schwannomas causing sensorineural hearing loss. Treatment is monitoring and surgery; no medical treatment alters disease course.

**Q:** What are the characteristics and EEG findings of Infantile Spasms (West Syndrome)?

**A:** Brief, repeated symmetric flexion/extension contractions of neck, trunk, and extremities lasting 10-30 seconds, occurring in clusters; onset age 4-8 months; associated with developmental delay. EEG shows hypsarrhythmia (high-voltage slow waves, spikes and polyspikes, background disorganization). West Syndrome triad: infantile spasms + developmental arrest/regression + hypsarrhythmia.

Infantile spasms are a pediatric emergency — early identification and treatment prevent developmental regression. 20% have unknown etiology (good treatment response); 80% have metabolic/developmental abnormalities or encephalopathies (poor response). Can evolve into Lennox-Gastaut syndrome. Treatment: corticosteroids, vigabatrin, benzodiazepines.

**Q:** What is the triad of Lennox-Gastaut Syndrome, and how is it managed?

**A:** Triad: (1) multiple types of drug-resistant seizures, (2) cognitive dysfunction and behavioural impairment, (3) slow generalized spike-and-wave EEG. Onset: commonly 3-5 years. Management: valproic acid, benzodiazepines, vagal nerve stimulator, ketogenic diet. Prognosis often poor.

Lennox-Gastaut is associated with underlying encephalopathy and brain malformations (including evolution from West syndrome). Seizure types include tonic, atonic (drop attacks), absence, and myoclonic. The ketogenic diet (high fat, low carbohydrate) can reduce seizure frequency by up to 50% in drug-resistant cases. Valproic acid is contraindicated with ketogenic diet due to hepatotoxicity risk.

**Q:** What are the key features and management of Juvenile Myoclonic Epilepsy (JME/Janz Syndrome)?

**A:** Features: myoclonus particularly in the morning; frequently presents as generalized tonic-clonic seizures; adolescent onset (12-16 years); autosomal dominant with variable penetrance. EEG: 3.5-6 Hz irregular spike-and-wave, increased with photic stimulation. Management: lifelong valproic acid treatment; excellent prognosis.

JME is the most common idiopathic generalized epilepsy syndrome. Sleep deprivation and alcohol are common triggers. Despite excellent seizure control with valproic acid, ~90% relapse if medication is discontinued, necessitating lifelong therapy. Valproic acid is teratogenic (neural tube defects) — important consideration in adolescent females.

**Q:** What are the features and management of Childhood Absence Epilepsy?

**A:** Multiple daily absence seizures lasting <30 seconds, without postictal state; may resolve spontaneously or become generalized in adolescence. Peak onset age 6-7 years, F>M, strong genetic predisposition. EEG: 3 Hz spike-and-wave. Management: valproic acid or ethosuximide.

Absence seizures manifest as sudden brief lapses of consciousness with blank stare; the child resumes activity immediately (no postictal confusion — distinguishes from complex partial seizures). EEG with hyperventilation provocation triggers the characteristic 3 Hz spike-and-wave. Ethosuximide is preferred in pure absence; valproic acid used if generalized tonic-clonic seizures co-occur.

**Q:** What is Benign Childhood Epilepsy with Centrotemporal Spikes (BECTS/Rolandic epilepsy), and how is it managed?

**A:** Focal motor seizures involving tongue, mouth, face, and upper extremity; usually at sleep-wake transitions; remains conscious but aphasic postictally; may secondarily generalize. Onset peaks 5-10 years; 16% of all non-febrile childhood seizures; remits spontaneously in adolescence without sequelae. EEG: repetitive spikes in centrotemporal area, normal background. Management: levetiracetam or carbamazepine for frequent seizures; no medication if infrequent.

BECTS is one of the most common epilepsy syndromes in children and is self-limiting. The hallmark is tonic or clonic activity of perioral muscles with drooling and dysarthria, often awakening the child from sleep. The benign course and spontaneous remission allow a conservative approach with treatment reserved for frequent or disruptive seizures.

**Q:** When should antiepileptic drug (AED) therapy be initiated in children, and when can it be discontinued?

**A:** Initiation: typically after >2 unprovoked afebrile seizures within 6-12 months; start with one drug appropriate to seizure type, titrate to seizure control before adding a second AED. Discontinuation: after seizure-free period >2 years, then wean over 4-6 months.

If a first AED fails, switch to another before adding a second drug (polytherapy). Ketogenic diet (high fat) is used for polytherapy-resistant cases or medication refusal; valproic acid is contraindicated with ketogenic diet due to hepatotoxicity risk. Legal obligation exists to report to the Ministry of Transportation if patient wishes to drive.

**Q:** What are the seizure mimics in children?

**A:** Benign paroxysmal vertigo, breath holding, hypersynchrony, narcolepsy, night terrors, non-epileptic seizure (pseudoseizure), syncope, tics. Cardiac causes (long QT syndrome, hypertrophic cardiomyopathy) are often misdiagnosed as epilepsy — especially when episodes occur during physical activity.

Distinguishing true seizures from mimics requires careful history (EEG and cardiac monitoring when appropriate). Syncope typically has prodromal features (lightheadedness, diaphoresis), rapid recovery, and is triggered by positional change. Breath-holding spells are provoked by upset or pain, involve cyanosis or pallor, and self-resolve. Cardiac causes should be considered when seizures occur during exercise.

**Q:** What are the red flag features of headache in children that require urgent investigation?

**A:** Red flags: first and worst headache of their life, thunderclap onset, focal neurological deficits, papilledema, worse with bending over/coughing/straining (↑ICP), pain that wakes patient from sleep, fatigue, seizures, affecting school attendance, heart problems (long QT, HCM — may be misdiagnosed as epilepsy).

An unremarkable history and physical exam in a child with recurrent headaches most likely indicates migraine or tension headache. CT or MRI is indicated only if red flags are present. Secondary headache causes include increased ICP (tumor, hydrocephalus, pseudotumor cerebri), meningitis, subarachnoid hemorrhage, and hypertension. Anxiety and life stress (bullying, divorce, abuse) can also cause recurrent headache.
